.

Shown in FIG.
8
are comparisons of the high-density
E_{\mathrm{sym}}(\rho)
predicted by different models in comparison with the upper and lower 68% confidence boundaries (thick blue curves) from directly inverting several NS observables available since GW170817. Here, clearly demonstrated is the fact that different approaches including non-relativistic energy density functionals, relativistic mean-field models, microscopic theories using realistic potentials or
ab initio
forces may predict very different high-density behaviors of
E_{\mathrm{sym}}(\rho)
.
This situation is partially because extracting symmetry energy straightforwardly from both astrophysical observations and/or terrestrial experimental data is very difficult and often rather model-dependent. Moreover, it is not always clear what observables are sensitive to the high-density behavior of nuclear symmetry energy in either astrophysics or nuclear physics. For example, shown with the thick blue curves in FIG.
8
are the upper and lower boundaries of
E_{\mathrm{sym}}(\rho)
from directly inverting several NS observables available since GW170817 by brute force in the high-density EOS parameter space
Zhang and Li (2020)
.
There is a large opening window between them at supra-saturation densities above about
2\rho_{0}
. This is mostly because the radii and/or tidal deformations of canonical NSs observed relatively accurately so far are not so sensitive to the pressure significantly above
2\rho_{0}
, see, e.g., discussions in Refs.
Li et al. (2021)
;
Zhang and Li (2023)
, as NS radii are determined by the condition
P(R)=0.
The latter can be heavily masked by the remaining uncertainties of nuclear EOS at low densities. Thus, establishing a direction connection between the NS radius R and its core EOS, made possible as we shall demonstrate in detail by solving perturbatively the scaled TOV equations, will be extremely invaluable for narrowing down the uncertainty range of high-density nuclear symmetry energy.

As another example, shown in FIG.
9
are the 68% posterior confidence boundaries of the SNM EOS

E_{0}(\rho)
and nuclear symmetry energy
E_{\mathrm{sym}}(\rho)
inferred from a comprehensive Bayesian
analysis
Xie and Li (2019)
using combined data of

R_{1.4}
from GW170817 by LIGO/VIRGO and low mass X-ray binaries by Chandra-XMM-Newton Collaborations. It is seen that using different minimum
M_{\mathrm{TOV}}
in the analysis affects significant the lower boundary of
E_{0}(\rho)
but has little effect on
E_{\mathrm{sym}}(\rho)
at supra-saturation densities. Overall, while the high-density SNM EOS is relatively well constrained once the minimum
M_{\mathrm{TOV}}
is considered, the available NS radius and mass data used in the Bayesian analyses do not constrain much the symmetry energy
E_{\mathrm{sym}}(\rho)
at high densities above about
2\rho_{0}
. We refer the interested readers to Ref.
Li et al. (2021)
for a review on the progress in constraining nuclear symmetry energy using NS observables since GW170817.

3.3 Existing constraints and critical issues on the speed of sound in NSs

We now review briefly the current constraints on the speed of sound squared (SSS)
s^{2}
in NS matter. The ultimate limit of
s^{2}
was discussed in 1960’s by Zel’dovich using a model with a vector-field
Zel’dovich (1961)
. It was shown
s^{2}\to 1
and
P\to\varepsilon
are the fundamental limits.
This issue was re-addressed in Ref.
Bedaque and Steiner (2015)
in 2015 by studying the possible tension between
s^{2}<1/3
and the observed massive NSs. They found that the number of models (in their simulation) consistent with low-density EOS and
s^{2}<1/3
abruptly decreases even to disappear for
M_{\mathrm{NS}}\gtrsim 2M_{\odot}
, see FIG.
10
, implying
s^{2}
should unavoidably be larger than 1/3 somewhere considering massive NSs. Since the quark matter at very large densities
\gtrsim 40\rho_{0}

Bjorken (1983)
has the approximate conformal symmetry and its EOS approaches that of an URFG with
P\approx\varepsilon/3
, its
s^{2}
naturally approaches
1/3
at these densities. One thus expects that the
s^{2}
as a function of density (or energy density) may develop a bump structure with
s^{2}>1/3
and then tend to
1/3
asymptotically at extremely large densities.
This expectation raises two important questions: (a) is this peaked structure in the density/radius profile of
s^{2}
realized anywhere (e.g., near the center or some other places) within NSs? (b) what is the physical origin of such peak if it happens in NSs?

FIG. 10:
Histogram of the number of models as a function of the
maximum/threshold mass supported, in these models the criteria
s^{2}<1/3
is fulfilled. Figure taken from Ref.
Bedaque and Steiner (2015)
.

Stimulated strongly by the findings in Ref.
Bedaque and Steiner (2015)
, many interesting works, see, e.g., Refs.
Tews et al. (2018)
;
Baym et al. (2019)
;
Alvarez-Castillo et al. (2019)
;
McLerran and Reddy (2019)
;
Duarte et al. (2020)
;
Ferreira et al. (2020)
;
Motornenko et al. (2020)
;
Malfatti et al. (2020)
;
Zhao and Lattimer (2020)
;
Minamikawa et al. (2021)
;
Stone et al. (2021)
;
Otto et al. (2020)
;
Motta et al. (2021)
;
Jakobus et al. (2021)
;
Sen and Sivertsen (2021)
;
Kapusta and Welle (2021)
;
Tan et al. (2022)
;
Tan et al. (2022)
;
Altiparmak et al. (2022)
;
Drischler et al. (2022)
;
Huang et al. (2022)
;
Kojo et al. (2022)
;
Ecker and Rezzolla (2022)
;
Ecker and Rezzolla (2022)
;
Fujimoto et al. (2022)
;
Fujimoto et al. (2023)
;
Han et al. (2023)
;
Marczenko et al. (2023)
;
Marczenko et al. (2024)
;
Providência et al. (2024)
;
Somasundaram et al. (2023)
;
Liu et al. (2023)
;
Brandes et al. (2023)
;
Brandes et al. (2023)
;
Takátsy et al. (2023)
;
Fan et al. (2024)
;
Jiménez et al. (2024)
;
Roy and Suyama (2024)
;
Gholami et al. (2024)
;
Kanakis-Pegios et al. (2024)
;
da Silva et al. (2024)
have been carried out to investigate the issues mentioned above.
For instance, in the quarkyonic picture
McLerran and Reddy (2019)
;
Pang et al. (2023)
;
Fujimoto et al. (2024)
;
Fujimoto et al. (2024)
, a special arrangement of nucleons and quarks in a combined Fermi sphere forces the appearance of a peak in
s^{2}
at NS densities. At high densities
Pang et al. (2024)
, the baryon Fermi momentum is large and the degree of freedom (dof) deep within the Fermi sphere is Pauli blocked. Thus, creating a particle-hole excitation from deep within the Fermi sea requires large amounts of energy and momentum. Therefore, one can treat such excitation as weakly interacting. Due to the asymptotical freedom of quark matter at very high densities
Bjorken (1983)
, one naturally expects the existence of quark matter at high densities where quarks behave as nearly free particles. Consequently, one may treat the dof deep within the Fermi sphere as quarks. On the other hand, the dof near the Fermi surface can be excited with low energy and momentum transfers, allowing it to be treated as nucleons arising from quark correlations
Pang et al. (2024)
. The baryon density
\rho
in this model increases less rapidly in the quarkyonic phase. The suppression of the susceptibility

\mathrm{d}\rho/\mathrm{d}\mu
with
\mu\equiv\mathrm{d}\varepsilon/\mathrm{d}\rho
leads to a rapid increase in
SSS
McLerran and Reddy (2019)
as
s^{2}=\rho\mathrm{d}\mu/\mu\mathrm{d}\rho
, generating a peak in
s^{2}
, see Refs.
McLerran and Reddy (2019)
;
Pang et al. (2024)
;
Fujimoto et al. (2024)
for more detailed analyses of the quarkyonic model and its applications in addressing problems associated with NS matter EOS.

FIG. 11:
(Color Online). Left panel: Density-dependence of
s^{2}
obtained using several phenomenological models, the basic conclusion is that
s^{2}
has non-monotonic structures at some densities.
Figure taken from Ref.
Tan et al. (2022)
.
Right panel: median and 90% 1-dimensional symmetric posterior
credible regions for
s^{2}\equiv c_{\mathrm{s}}^{2}

at densities smaller than 2 times saturation density. Figure taken from Ref.
Essick et al. (2021)
.

.

Shown in the left panel of FIG.
11
are examples of the predicted
s^{2}
as a function of density obtained via several phenomenological models
Tan et al. (2022)
. One interesting feature of these results is that the
s^{2}
is a non-monotonic function of density (the existence of plateaus, kinks, bumps).
However, the origin of these structures is still largely unknown and has significant model dependence.
For example, the approximate conformal symmetry of quark matter and its possible realization at extremely high densities in massive NS cores
Annala et al. (2018)
;
Annala et al. (2020)
;
Annala et al. (2023)
;
Gorda et al. (2023)
;
Gorda et al. (2021)
may induce a peak in
s^{2}
, indicating possibly the occurrence of a sharp phase transition or a continuous crossover signaled by a smooth reduction of
s^{2}
.
Similarly, Ref.
Zhang and Li (2023)
showed recently that a purely nucleonic matter EOS model may also generate a peak in
s^{2}
in dense neutron-rich matter accessible in massive NSs and/or relativistic heavy-ion collisions depending on the high-density behavior of nuclear symmetry energy. It is also interesting to note the work of Ref.
Cao and Chen (2023)
which demonstrated that the
s^{2}
in self-bound quark stars made purely of absolutely stable deconfined quark matter may not show the peaked behavior.
Furthermore, a very recent Bayesian analysis
Mroczek et al. (2023)
of X-ray measurements and GW observations of NSs
Abbott et al. (2017)
;
Abbott et al. (2018)
;
Abbott et al. (2020)
incorporating the pQCD predictions
Gorda et al. (2021)
shows that the peaked behavior in
s^{2}
is consistent with but not required by these astrophysical data and pQCD predictions.
A similar inference on the behavior of
s^{2}
as a function of energy density incorporating the presently existing data also implies that
s^{2}
may have a weak/wide peak, even including the newly announced massive black widow pulsar PSR J0952-0607
Romani et al. (2022)
.
The peak in
s^{2}
may also emerge in a hadron-quark hybrid model
with excluded volume effects of baryons and chiral dynamics
Kouno and Kashiwa (2024)
.

Both qualitatively and quantitatively, there are interesting discrepancies among the reported findings about the behavior of
s^{2}
. For illustrating the diversity of predictions, we mention a few of them below. For example, Ref.
Brandes et al. (2023)
found a peak in
s^{2}
at about
630\,\mathrm{MeV}/\mathrm{fm}^{3}
, which is very close to the central energy density about
670\,\mathrm{MeV}/\mathrm{fm}^{3}
.
Including updated
measurement derived by fitting models of X-ray emission from the NS surface to NICER
data accumulated through 21 April 2022
Dittmann et al. (2024)
has little impact on the
s^{2}
profile; specifically, the analysis still does not indicate an apparent peak in the density profile of
s^{2}
.
On the other hand, in a very recent study
Komoltsev (2024)
using a large model-agnostic ensemble via Gaussian processes conditioned with state-of-the-art astrophysical and theoretical inputs, the peaked structure in
s^{2}
was found to be stable when first-order phase transitions (FOPTs) are included in the inference.

Although there exist large uncertainties about the high-density behavior of
s^{2}
, the monotonicity of
s^{2}
at
\rho\lesssim 2\rho_{0}
(at zero temperature) is relatively well established. This conclusion benefited significantly from CEFT calculations conditioned on the relevant astrophysical data as shown in the right panel of FIG.
11
(taken from Ref.
Essick et al. (2021)
). It is seen that
s^{2}
is monotonically increasing with density for all slope parameter
L
of the symmetry energy.
Similar and consistent result on
s^{2}
at densities
\lesssim 2\rho_{0}
was also given in Refs.
Tews et al. (2018)
;
Keller et al. (2023)
. In particlar, Ref.
Keller et al. (2023)
found that the finite-temperature may induce a local minimum in
s^{2}
at low densities.
Interestingly, we can find from the right panel of FIG.
11
that if one assumes
L>100\,\mathrm{MeV}
a local maximum in
s^{2}
just below the saturation density may emerge (however the uncertainties are quite large to make definite conclusions).
This is because the EOSs that are stiff at low densities (corresponding to large

L
) probed by neutron-skin experiments need to be softened at densities above
\rho_{0}
to be consistent with the astrophysical data from GW170817
Abbott et al. (2017)
;
Abbott et al. (2018)
. It is useful to note that a number of studies on
s^{2}
used the findings of the PREX-II experiment
Adhikari et al. (2021)
as a justification for using
L\gtrsim 100\,\mathrm{MeV}
. However, as already indicated by the results shown in FIG.
7
, such choice of a very large
L
is not fully justified if one considers all published results as equally reliable within their error bars reported. In fact, a large
L
is not really necessary to account for the result from the PREX-II experiment
Adhikari et al. (2021)
based on several studies in the literature. For instance, a very recent study of Ref.
Yue et al. (2024)
found that a
L\approx 55\,\mathrm{MeV}
can describe adequately both the PREX-II
Adhikari et al. (2021)
and the CREX
Adhikari et al. (2022)
data simultaneously if a strong iso-vector spin-orbit interaction is considered. Similarly, if one treats the results of PREX-I, PREX-II and CREX experiments as equally reliable within the reported experimental error bars, a unified analysis of them within an extended liquid droplet model leads to a value of
L\approx 53\pm 13\,\mathrm{MeV}

Lattimer (2023)
. The values of
L
from these two independent analyses of PREX and CREX data are consistent with the established global systematics of
L
based on over 90 independent studies of various data of astrophysical observations and terrestrial nuclear experiments as shown in FIG.
7
.

Interestingly, a peaked
s^{2}
may also occur in heavy-ion collisions (HICs)
Omana Kuttan et al. (2023)
;
Oliinychenko et al. (2023)
;
Sorensen et al. (2021)
;
Yao et al. (2024)
.
For example, Ref.
Omana Kuttan et al. (2023)
gave a Bayesian inference of
s^{2}
using HIC data of mean transverse kinetic energy and elliptic flow of protons in the beam energy range
\sqrt{s_{\mathrm{NN}}}\approx 2\mbox{-}10\,\mathrm{GeV}
. In their studies, the peak in
s^{2}
profile is located at about
4\rho_{0}
. This peaked behavior is quite similar to that inferred from NS data.
However, if only 13 HIC data points were used in the analysis the peak in
s^{2}
disappears within the relevant energy density region. This may indicate that it is too early to make any robust conclusion on the possible bump structures in
s^{2}
from analyzing the HIC data.
We shall discuss more on
s^{2}
in Subsection
5.11
.

FIG. 12:
(Color Online). Anatomy of patterns of extrapolating the
s^{2}
in the core of NSs with
\rho_{\mathrm{c}}\approx\rho_{\max}\approx(4\mbox{-}8)\rho_{\mathrm{0}}
to high densities
\sim 40\rho_{\mathrm{0}}
(pQCD), here the double-circle on each line denotes its
s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}
and
s_{\max}^{2}
is the global maximum value of the SSS;
\rho_{0}\equiv\rho_{\mathrm{sat}}
.
Figure taken from Ref.
Cai and Li (2024)
.

Shown in FIG.
12
is an anatomy of different patterns of extrapolating the
s^{2}
in NSs with their maximum densities
\lesssim 8\rho_{0}
to high densities
\sim 40\rho_{0}
(pQCD region). Here the double-circle on each line denotes its central value and
s_{\max}^{2}
is the global maximum value of the SSS
Cai and Li (2024)
; a similar plot was given in Ref.
Tews et al. (2018)
.
The
s^{2}
in patterns (a) and (d) shows a monotonic behavior and the difference lies in whether
s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}
is greater (smaller) than
1/3
; the
s^{2}
in patterns (b) and (c) shows a peak at densities smaller than the central density of NSs where the
s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}
in pattern (b) (pattern (c)) is larger (smaller) than
1/3
.
Both pattern (b) and (c) indicate a continuous crossover behavior near the NS center.
Other possible nontrivial features in
s^{2}
(like plateau, spike or bump, etc.) are not sketched in FIG.
12
. We recommend Ref.
Tan et al. (2022)
and Ref.
Mroczek et al. (2023)
for more detailed discussions on these issues.

To summarize this section, we emphasize the following points on the dense NS matter EOS and its speed of sound: (a) the EOS at
\rho\lesssim\rho_{0}
is well constrained using CEFT; (b) the inference/restriction on NS matter EOS becomes eventually uncertain as density increases from
\rho\gtrsim\rho_{0}
to
\rho\lesssim 8\rho_{0}
, due to the lack/difficulties of first-principle calculations, model EOSs are often adopted and this unavoidably introduce model-dependence into the final constraints; (c) although pQCD at asymptotically large densities predicts a quite concise EOS (approximately an URFG), its relevance to NS matter EOS and impact on NS observations need more detailed investigations; (d) the SSS
s^{2}
is larger than 1/3 ( that of an URFG) in massive NSs and has a peaked structure at some density/energy density when the asymptotic limit of the EOS as
P\to\varepsilon/3
at extremely large densities is imposed; (e) however, such a peaked structure may or may not be realized within NSs since the maximum density of NSs is far smaller than the pQCD density; and (f) the physical origin of a peak in
s^{2}
profile in NSs has significant model-dependence and is therefore still quite elusive.
Improving the understanding of these issues is a major science driver of many ongoing research in nuclear astrophysics. It is also one of our major motivations for writing the current review.

We also emphasize here that efforts to constrain the EOS of supra-dense neutron-rich matter near NS cores using the relevant NS observational data (especially NS radius) within the conventional framework are unavoidably EOS-model dependent and also suffer from the remaining uncertainties of low-density EOS. On the other hand, as we shall discuss in the next section, NS radius and mass scaling relations from dissecting the scaled TOV equations themselves provide unique insights directly into the NS core EOS without using any nuclear EOS model.

4 Scalings of NS Mass, Radius and Compactness with its Central EOS

In this section, we use the perturbative analysis of the TOV equations of SECTION
2
as a tool to study the central EOS of NSs. We first give an intuitive argument in Subsection
4.1
on the mass-, radius-scalings based on dimensional analysis and order-of-magnitude estimate using the excellent self-gravitating and quantum degenerate nature of NSs, following a perturbative analysis directly from the TOV equations. We then give in Subsection
4.2
the EOS of the densest visible matter existing in our Universe, namely the EOS of the NS cores at the maximum-mass configuration
M_{\mathrm{TOV}}
.
Two examples (applications) of the method are given in the sequent subsections. In Subsection
4.3
, we shall estimate the central baryon density
\rho_{\mathrm{c}}
realized in NSs, and by taking the double-element expansion based on
\mu
of (
2.27
) and
\mathrm{X}
of (
2.25
) we give in Subsection
4.4
the result for
P/P_{\mathrm{c}}
and compare it with the state-of-the-art constraint available in the literature. Go beyond the NSs at TOV configuration, we study
the central EOSs in canonical NSs and a NS with
M_{\mathrm{NS}}\approx 2M_{\odot}
in Subsection
4.5
.
The key quantity
\Psi=2\mathrm{d}\ln M_{\mathrm{NS}}/\mathrm{d}\ln\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}
for describing generally stable NSs is given in Subsection
4.6
.
In Subsection
4.7
, we discuss the counter-intuitive feature of the NS mass scaling established in the previous few subsections, then in Subsection
4.8
we work out the details on the estimates of the maximum masses for generally stable NSs along the M-R curve and those at the TOV configuration.

In this section, we focus on the scalings of NS mass, radius and compactness as well as several related issues. In Section
5
and Section
6
, we shall shift our focus to the speed of sound squared and the trace anomaly, respectively.

4.1 Self-gravitating and quantum degenerate properties of dense NS matter

Since NSs are self-gravitating systems
Landau (1932)
;
Rhoades and Ruffini (1974)
;
Hartle and Sabbadini (1977)
;
Hartle (1978)
;
Lindblom (1984)
;
Lindblom (1992)
;
Kalogera and Baym (1996)
;
Lattimer and Prakash (2005)
;
Lattimer and Prakash (2010)
;
Shapiro and Teukolsky (1983)
, one expects that a larger energy density
\varepsilon
induces a smaller radius
R
.
By temporally neglecting the general relativistic effects
Chandrasekhar (2010)
, we have
\mathrm{d}P/\mathrm{d}r=-GM\varepsilon/r^{2}
for Newtonian stars and therefore
P\sim-GM\varepsilon/r\sim Gr^{2}\varepsilon^{2}
where
\mathrm{d}M/\mathrm{d}r=4\pi r^{2}\varepsilon
or
M\sim r^{3}\varepsilon
is used.
Consequently,

r\sim(P/\varepsilon)^{1/2}/\sqrt{G\varepsilon}\sim 1/\sqrt{G\varepsilon},

(4.1)

the second relation follows because
P
and
\varepsilon
have the same dimension (
c=1
).
The factor
(P/\varepsilon)^{1/2}
in (
4.1
) can also be obtained as follows: NSs are supported mainly by the neutron degenerate pressure (at zero temperature), a larger pressure
P
may lead to a larger radius
R
, i.e.,

R\sim P^{\sigma},

(4.2)

where
\sigma>0
.
In order to infer the value of
\sigma
, we notice that
P\sim\int M\varepsilon/r^{2}\mathrm{d}r
as a function of
r
is even (see Subsection
2.2
) and thus
P\sim\mathrm{const}.+Br^{2}+\cdots
(being equivalent to
\widehat{P}\approx\mathrm{X}+b_{2}\widehat{r}^{2}+\cdots
), from which one obtains
\sigma=1/2
and
R\sim P^{1/2}+\cdots
.
The absence of a linear term (
\propto r
) in the expansion of
P
over
r
could also be understood by the boundary condition
\mathrm{d}P/\mathrm{d}r=0
at
r=0
(pressure cannot have a cusp-like singularity).

By combining the self-gravitating and quantum-degenerate nature of NSs, we have
R\sim(P/\varepsilon)^{1/2}/\sqrt{G\varepsilon}+\cdots
, since
P/\varepsilon
is the relevant dimensionless quantity in combining
P
and
\varepsilon
. The NS M-R relation is conventionally obtained by integrating the TOV equations starting from a given central energy density
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}

Cai and Li (2016)
;
Li et al. (2022)
;
Cai and Chen (2012)
;
Cai and Chen (2017)
;
Cai et al. (2015)
, so the above scaling could also be written in the form of
R\sim\mathrm{X}^{1/2}/\sqrt{G\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}}+\cdots\sim\mathrm{X}^{1/2}/\sqrt{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}}+\cdots
, where
\mathrm{X}\equiv\widehat{P}_{\mathrm{c}}\equiv P_{\mathrm{c}}/\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}
.
Similar arguments give for the mass as

M_{\mathrm{NS}}\sim R^{3}\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}\sim\mathrm{X}^{3/2}/\sqrt{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}}+\cdots
.
Going one step further by putting back the general relativistic effects, NS radius
R
in GR should scale as

R\sim(\mathrm{X}^{1/2}/\sqrt{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}})\cdot\vartheta(\mathrm{X}),

(4.3)

with
\vartheta(\mathrm{X})
the general-relativistic correction.
Without doing detailed calculations, one can immediately infer that

\vartheta(\mathrm{X})<1
, since the stronger gravity in GR than Newtonian’s should effectively reduce the NS radius.

We can reveal the specific form of
\vartheta(\mathrm{X})
by considering the pressure expansion of Eq. (
2.23
) to order
\widehat{r}^{2}
; using the expression for the coefficient
b_{2}
of Eq. (
2.30
) and the basic definition of NS radius of Eq. (
2.5
), one has
Cai et al. (2023)

\widehat{R}=\left(\frac{6\mathrm{X}}{1+3\mathrm{X}^{2}+4\mathrm{X}}\right)^{1/2}\sim\left(\frac{\mathrm{X}}{1+3\mathrm{X}^{2}+4\mathrm{X}}\right)^{1/2},~~\mbox{from}~~\widehat{P}(\widehat{R})=0,

(4.4)

and so,

\boxed{\vartheta(\mathrm{X})=\left(\frac{1}{1+3\mathrm{X}^{2}+4\mathrm{X}}\right)^{1/2}.}

(4.5)

After obtaining the GR factor
\vartheta(\mathrm{X})
, we then obtain the radius-scaling
Cai et al. (2023)
;
Cai et al. (2023)
;
Cai and Li (2024)
;
Cai and Li (2024)
;
Cai and Li (2024)

\displaystyle R=\widehat{R}Q=\left(\frac{3}{2\pi G}\right)^{1/2}\nu_{\mathrm{c}}\sim\nu_{\mathrm{c}},~~\mbox{with}~~\nu_{\mathrm{c}}\equiv\frac{\mathrm{X}^{1/2}}{\sqrt{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}}}\left(\frac{1}{1+3\mathrm{X}^{2}+4\mathrm{X}}\right)^{1/2},

(4.6)

as well as the mass-scaling
Cai et al. (2023)
;
Cai et al. (2023)
;
Cai and Li (2024)
;
Cai and Li (2024)
;
Cai and Li (2024)

\displaystyle M_{\mathrm{NS}}\approx\frac{1}{3}\widehat{R}^{3}W=\left(\frac{6}{\pi G^{3}}\right)^{1/2}\Gamma_{\mathrm{c}}\sim\Gamma_{\mathrm{c}},~~\mbox{with}~~\Gamma_{\mathrm{c}}\equiv\frac{\mathrm{X}^{3/2}}{\sqrt{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}}}\left(\frac{1}{1+3\mathrm{X}^{2}+4\mathrm{X}}\right)^{3/2},

(4.7)

Dividing (
4.7
) and (
4.6
) gives the scaling for NS compactness

\boxed{\xi=\frac{M_{\mathrm{NS}}}{R}\approx\frac{2\Pi_{\mathrm{c}}}{G}\sim\Pi_{\mathrm{c}},~~\mbox{with}~~\Pi_{\mathrm{c}}=\frac{\mathrm{X}}{1+3\mathrm{X}^{2}+4\mathrm{X}}.}

(4.8)

Comparing it with Eq. (
2.29
), we find
\tau_{1}\approx 2
and all
\tau_{i}\sim\mathcal{O}(1)
.
The above scaling (
4.8
) implies that NS compactness directly probes its core EOS

\mathrm{X}
, in the sense that
\Pi_{\mathrm{c}}
is an increasing function of
\mathrm{X}
.
This means a NS with larger
\mathrm{X}
is more compact than that with a smaller
\mathrm{X}
; i.e.,
\mathrm{X}
plays the role of the compactness.
Otherwise,
\mathrm{X}
and
\xi
are basically two different quantities, e.g., Specially Relativity requires
\mathrm{X}\leq 1
while General Relativity limits
\xi\leq 1/2
.

FIG. 13:
(Color Online). The
I
-Love (left panel) and Love-
{Q}
relations for various EOSs. Figure taken from Ref.
Yagi and Yunes (2013)
.

The above scalings relate directly the radii and/or masses of NSs with their central EOS
{P}_{\mathrm{c}}(\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}})=P(\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}})
. They are direct consequences of the TOV equations themselves without assuming any particular structure, composition and/or EOS for NSs. Once the
\nu_{\mathrm{c}}
or
\Gamma_{\mathrm{c}}
is constrained within a certain range by NS observational data, it can be used to determine the NS central EOS. We notice that the radius scaling may be slightly affected by the still uncertain low-density EOS especially through the NS crust although its thickness accounts for only about 10% of the whole radius
Baym et al. (1971)
;
Iida and Sato (1997)
;
Xu et al. (2009)
.

In the expressions for
\vartheta(\mathrm{X})
,
\Gamma_{\mathrm{c}}
,
\nu_{\mathrm{c}}
and
\Pi_{\mathrm{c}}
, the term
3\mathrm{X}^{2}+4\mathrm{X}
is the GR correction, which is far smaller than 1 if the Newtonian limit with
\mathrm{X}\to 0
is taken.
Therefore, we have

\boxed{\mbox{Newtonian stars:}~~\vartheta\approx 1,~~M_{\mathrm{NS}}\sim\frac{{\mathrm{X}}^{3/2}}{\sqrt{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}}}\sim P_{\mathrm{c}}^{3/2}\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}^{-2},~~R\sim\frac{{\mathrm{X}}^{1/2}}{\sqrt{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}}}\sim P_{\mathrm{c}}^{1/2}\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}^{-1},~~\xi\approx 2\mathrm{X}.}

(4.9)

The GR correction bends the compactness
\xi
at large ratio of central pressure over energy density.
Consequently, we have for Newtonian stars:

P_{\mathrm{c}}\sim{M_{\mathrm{NS}}^{2}}/{R^{4}}.

(4.10)

This relation can also be obtained by dimension analysis on the Newtonian stellar equations.

Extensive studies on scaling relations among NS observables exist in the literature, see, e.g. Refs.
Rhoades and Ruffini (1974)
;
Hartle and Sabbadini (1977)
;
Hartle (1978)
;
Kalogera and Baym (1996)
;
Lattimer and Prakash (2005)
;
Tsui and Leung (2005)
;
Yagi and Yunes (2013)
;
Wen et al. (2019)
;
Jiang and Yagi (2020)
.
These EOS-independent universal scalings are mostly for NS bulk properties, such as the moment of inertia
I
, tidal Love number
k_{2}
, (spin-induced) quadrupole moment
{Q}
, compactness
\xi
, and frequencies (
f
or
\omega
) of various oscillation modes of NSs.
The moment of inertia (using
c=1
) is defined as
Hartle (1967)
,

\boxed{I=-\frac{2}{3G}\int_{0}^{R}\mathrm{d}rr^{3}\omega(r)\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}r}j(r)\right)=\frac{8\pi}{3}\int_{0}^{R}\mathrm{d}rr^{4}\left[\varepsilon(r)+P(r)\right]\exp\left[\lambda(r)\right]j(r)\omega(r),}

(4.11)

here
j(r)=\exp[-2^{-1}(\nu(r)+\lambda(r))]
and
\omega(r)
is the rotational drag;
\nu(r)
and
\lambda(r)
are two metric functions
Hartle (1967)
.
The rotational drag satisfies a differential equation
Hartle (1967)
,

\boxed{\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}r}\left(r^{4}j(r)\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}r}\omega(r)\right)+4r^{3}\omega(r)\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}r}j(r)=0.}

(4.12)

Consequently,
I=[{R^{4}}/{6G}][{\mathrm{d}\omega}/{\mathrm{d}r}]_{r=R}
.
Knowing one or more observables, these scalings (e.g.,
I
-Love-
{Q}
,
I
-Love-
\xi
or
M\omega
-
\xi
) enable the finding of other observables or bulk properties that have not been or hard to be measured; FIG.
13
shows the celebrated
I
-Love-
{Q}
relations for NSs
Yagi and Yunes (2013)
.
They are completely different from the scalings of NS radius and mass separately as functions of the reduced core pressure over energy density we derived above.
Among the earlier mass scalings closest to ours is the one showing
Hartle (1978)
;
Kalogera and Baym (1996)
;
Lattimer and Prakash (2005)

\boxed{M_{\mathrm{NS}}^{\max}\sim D_{\mathrm{M}}\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}^{-1/2},}

(4.13)

with the coefficient
D_{\mathrm{M}}
estimated empirically using either sometimes simplified or certain selected microscopic dense matter EOSs, often leading to sizable EOS model-dependence or lack solid/clear physical origins.
Another fundamental difference is that our scalings link directly the macroscopic observables with microscopic core variables (different combinations of the core pressure and energy density via
\nu_{\mathrm{c}}
and
\Gamma_{\mathrm{c}}
). This feature enables us to extract the core EOS
{P}_{\mathrm{c}}(\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}})=P(\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}})
once the NS mass or radius is constrained observationally, instead of just the individual value of
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}
or the pressure around
(1\mbox{-}2)\rho_{0}
in the previous studies of NS scaling observables.

FIG. 14:
(Color Online). Left panel: comparisons of NS radius scalings (
\widehat{R}
vs reduced pressure
\widehat{P}
) for both general-relativistic (magenta curve) NSs using our Eq. (
4.4
) and Newtonian (blue) stars with the empirical power law
R\sim P^{q}
(gray band) from Lattimer and Prakash
Lattimer and Prakash (2001)
, here
q\approx 0.23\mbox{-}0.26
. Figure taken from Ref.
Cai et al. (2023)
.
Right panel: the empirical radius scaling
R\sim P^{0.25}
obtained for canonical NSs from using two dozens of NS matter EOSs by Lattimer and Prakash. Figure taken from Ref.
Lattimer and Prakash (2001)
.

Very interestingly, an empirical power law for NS radii was found earlier by Lattimer and Prakash as
R\sim D_{\mathrm{R}}P^{q}
with
q\approx 0.23\mbox{$\sim$}0.26

Lattimer and Prakash (2001)
, where
P
is the pressure at about
(1\mbox{-}2)\rho_{0}
, see the right panel of FIG.
14
for the scaling
R\sim P^{0.25}
for canonical NSs using two dozens of NS matter EOSs existing at that time
Lattimer and Prakash (2001)
. A comparison between our radius scaling in Eq. (
4.4
) and theirs is shown in the left panel of FIG.
14
. The narrow width of the gray band indicates the very weak EOS model-dependence involved in their empirical power law. Firstly, it is clearly seen that the Newtonian prediction
\widehat{R}\sim\widehat{P}^{1/2}
(by neglecting the GR correction
3\widehat{P}^{2}+4\widehat{P}
) indicated by the blue dashed line deviates significantly from the empirical power law
\widehat{P}^{q}

Lattimer and Prakash (2001)
. On the other hand, our full scaling
\widehat{R}\sim[\widehat{P}/(1+3\widehat{P}^{2}+4\widehat{P})]^{1/2}
(magenta curve) is rather consistent with the empirical scaling by Lattimer and Prakash, especially for
0.1\lesssim\widehat{P}\lesssim 0.3
(which is the most relevant region for
P/\varepsilon
in NS cores). Since our scalings are directly from analyzing the TOV equations themselves without using any model EOS, they provide independently a fundamental physics basis for the NS empirical radius power law by Lattimer and Prakash.

4.2 EOS of the densest visible matter existing in our Universe

The maximum-mass configuration (or the TOV configuration) along the NS M-R curve is a special point
Cai and Li (2024)
.
Consider a typical NS M-R curve near the TOV configuration from right to left, the radius
R
(mass
M_{\mathrm{NS}}
) eventually decreases (increases), the compactness
\xi=M_{\mathrm{NS}}/R
correspondingly increases and reaches its maximum value at the TOV configuration.
When going to the left along the M-R curve even further, the stars becomes unstable and then may collapse into black holes (BHs).
So the NS at the TOV configuration is denser than its surroundings and the cores of such NSs contain the critically stable densest matter visible in the Universe.
Mathematically, we describe the TOV configuration by
Cai and Li (2024)
,

\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}M_{\mathrm{NS}}}{\mathrm{d}\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}}\right|_{M_{\mathrm{NS}}=M_{\mathrm{NS}}^{\max}=M_{\mathrm{TOV}}}=0,~~\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}M_{\mathrm{NS}}}{\mathrm{d}\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}}\right|_{M_{\mathrm{NS}}=M_{\mathrm{NS}}^{\max}=M_{\mathrm{TOV}}}<0.

(4.14)

Using the NS mass scaling of Eq. (
4.7
), we can obtain an expression for the central SSS
Cai et al. (2023)
;
Cai and Li (2024)
;
Cai and Li (2024)
,

\boxed{\mbox{for stable NSs along the M-R curve:}~~s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}={\mathrm{X}}\left(1+\frac{1+\Psi}{3}\frac{1+3{\mathrm{X}}^{2}+4{\mathrm{X}}}{1-3{\mathrm{X}}^{2}}\right),}

(4.15)

where
Cai and Li (2024)
;
Cai and Li (2024)

\boxed{\Psi=\frac{2\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}}{M_{\mathrm{NS}}}\frac{\mathrm{d}M_{\mathrm{NS}}}{\mathrm{d}\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}}=2\frac{\mathrm{d}\ln M_{\mathrm{NS}}}{\mathrm{d}\ln\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}}\geq 0.}

(4.16)

We study the factor
\Psi
in Subsection
4.6
.
For the TOV configuration where
\Psi=0
, we have
Cai and Li (2024)
;
Cai and Li (2024)

\boxed{\mbox{for NSs at the TOV configuration:}~~s^{2}_{\mathrm{c}}=\mathrm{X}\left(1+\frac{1}{3}\frac{1+3\mathrm{X}^{2}+4\mathrm{X}}{1-3\mathrm{X}^{2}}\right).}

(4.17)

Using the Principle of Causality of Special Relativity, we immediately obtain
Cai et al. (2023)
;
Cai and Li (2024)

\boxed{s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}\leq 1\leftrightarrow\mathrm{X}\lesssim 0.374\equiv\mathrm{X}_{+}.}

(4.18)

Physically, although the causality condition requires apparently
{\mathrm{X}}\leq 1
, the GR nature of strong-field gravity makes the
EOS of superdense NS core matter highly nonlinear as indicated by the nonlinear dependence of
s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}
on
{\mathrm{X}}
. Consequently, the maximum speed of sound reachable in massive NSs is much smaller than what is allowed by causality with a linear EOS
Cai and Li (2024)
.
The upper bound on
\mathrm{X}
and
\phi=P/\varepsilon
is recently reviewed in Ref.
Cai and Li (2024)
, we will in SECTION
6
briefly outline the main results.
After obtaining the
s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}
and using the expression for
R
of Eq. (
4.6
), we can calculate the derivative of NS radius
R
with respective to
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}
, the result is:

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}R}{\mathrm{d}\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}}=

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}R}{\mathrm{d}M_{\mathrm{NS}}}\frac{\mathrm{d}M_{\mathrm{NS}}}{\mathrm{d}\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}}=\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}}\left[\left(\frac{3}{2\pi G}\right)^{1/2}\nu_{\mathrm{c}}\right]=\left(\frac{R}{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}}\right)\cdot\left(\frac{\Psi}{6}-\frac{1}{3}\right).

(4.19)

This means if
\Psi
is around 2, the dependence of
R
on
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}
would be weak.
Moreover, since
\mathrm{d}M_{\mathrm{NS}}/\mathrm{d}\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}>0
for stable NSs, the signs of
\mathrm{d}R/\mathrm{d}\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}
and that of
\mathrm{d}R/\mathrm{d}M_{\mathrm{NS}}
are the same.
Assuming
\Psi\approx\mathrm{const.}
for a given NS mass, Eq. (
4.19
) further gives

\boxed{R\sim\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}^{\Psi/6-1/3}.}

(4.20)

For NSs at the TOV configuration,
\Psi=0
, Eq. (
4.19
) gives
\mathrm{d}R/\mathrm{d}\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}<0

Cai et al. (2023)
and Eq. (
4.20
) leads to
R\sim\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}^{-1/3}
, i.e., as
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}
increases, the radius
R
decreases (self-gravitating property), as expected.
We discuss the importance of
\Psi
further in Subsection
5.6
when we estimate the central SSS in a canonical NS.

At this point, we can express
M_{\mathrm{NS}}
in terms of
s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}
by inverting the relation (
4.15
) to obtain
\mathrm{X}=\mathrm{X}(s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2},\Psi)
and inserting the latter into (
4.7
), the result is

\displaystyle M_{\mathrm{NS}}\sim

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}}}\left(\frac{\mathrm{X}(s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2},\Psi)}{1+3\mathrm{X}^{2}(s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2},\Psi)+4\mathrm{X}(s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2},\Psi)}\right)^{3/2}\equiv\frac{\mathcal{M}(s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2},\Psi)}{\sqrt{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}}}\cdot\frac{3\sqrt{3}s_{\mathrm{c}}^{3}}{(4+\Psi)^{3/2}}\left[1-18\frac{5+2\Psi}{(4+\Psi)^{2}}s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}+\frac{81}{2}\frac{148+126\Psi+29\Psi^{2}}{(4+\Psi)^{4}}s_{\mathrm{c}}^{4}+\cdots\right]

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}}}\frac{3\sqrt{3}s_{\mathrm{c}}^{3}}{8}\left(1-\frac{45s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}}{8}+\frac{2997s_{\mathrm{c}}^{4}}{128}+\cdots\right),

(4.21)

where the last line follows by taking
\Psi=0
.
The function
\mathcal{M}(s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2},\Psi)
monotonically increases with
s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}
(and takes its maximum value about
\mathcal{M}(1,0)\approx 0.046
), therefore for a given
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}
, a larger
s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}
(stiffer EOS) generates a larger NS mass, which is a well-known result.

FIG. 15:
(Color Online). The dependence of the function
\mathcal{M}(s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2},\Psi)
on stiffness
s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}
.
For small stiffness
s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}
, the growth rate of
\mathcal{M}(s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2},\Psi)
is super-linear while for
s_{\mathrm{c}}\approx 1
, the rate is sub-linear.

Eq. (
4.21
) implies that the growth rate of
M_{\mathrm{NS}}
over
s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}
(for a given
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}
) is super-linear for small stiffness
s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}\approx 0
, i.e.,
\mathcal{M}(s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2},\Psi)\sim(s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2})^{3/2}
.
On the other hand, we can show that the growth rate of
M_{\mathrm{NS}}
is sub-linear for
s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}\lesssim 1
.
By introducing
\varphi=s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}-1\lesssim 0
, we can obtain from Eq. (
4.15
) that

\mathrm{X}\approx\mathrm{X}_{+}(\Psi)+L_{1}\varphi+L_{2}\varphi^{2}+\mathcal{O}(\varphi^{3}),

(4.22)

here
\mathrm{X}_{+}(\Psi)
is the upper bound on
\mathrm{X}_{+}
determined by
s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}\leq 1
, e.g.,
\mathrm{X}_{+}(0)\approx 0.374
of (
4.18
).
Without doing detailed derivation one can infer that
L_{1}<0
since
\varphi<0
and
\mathrm{X}_{+}(\Psi)
is the upper bound for
\mathrm{X}
.
Specifically,

\displaystyle L_{1}=

\displaystyle\frac{[1-3\mathrm{X}_{+}^{2}(\Psi)]\mathrm{X}_{+}(\Psi)[1+\mathrm{X}_{+}(\Psi)][1+3\mathrm{X}_{+}(\Psi)]}{1+8\mathrm{X}_{+}(\Psi)+8\mathrm{X}_{+}^{2}(\Psi)-12\mathrm{X}_{+}^{3}(\Psi)-21\mathrm{X}_{+}^{4}(\Psi)},

(4.23)

\displaystyle L_{2}=

\displaystyle-\frac{2[1-3\mathrm{X}_{+}^{2}(\Psi)]\mathrm{X}_{+}^{2}(\Psi)[1-\mathrm{X}_{+}(\Psi)][1+\mathrm{X}_{+}(\Psi)]^{2}[1+3\mathrm{X}_{+}(\Psi)]^{2}[2+9\mathrm{X}_{+}(\Psi)+18\mathrm{X}_{+}^{2}(\Psi)+9\mathrm{X}_{+}^{3}(\Psi)]}{[1+8\mathrm{X}_{+}(\Psi)+8\mathrm{X}_{+}^{2}(\Psi)-12\mathrm{X}_{+}^{3}(\Psi)-21\mathrm{X}_{+}^{4}(\Psi)]^{3}},

(4.24)

For all
\mathrm{X}_{+}(\Psi)\leq\mathrm{X}_{+}(0)\approx 0.374
,
L_{1}>0
(
L_{2}<0
) monotonically increases (decreases) with
\mathrm{X}_{+}(\Psi)
, e.g.,
L_{1}\approx 0.155
,
L_{2}\approx-0.107
for
\mathrm{X}_{+}(0)
and so
\mathrm{X}\approx 0.374+0.155\varphi-0.107\varphi^{2}
.
Putting the
\mathrm{X}
of (
4.22
) into
\mathcal{M}(s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2},\Psi)=\mathcal{M}(\varphi+1,\Psi)
gives

\mathcal{M}(s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2},\Psi)/\mathcal{M}(1,\Psi)\approx 1+T_{1}\varphi+T_{2}\varphi^{2}+\mathcal{O}(\varphi^{3}),~~\mathcal{M}(1,\Psi)=\left(\frac{\mathrm{X}_{+}(\Psi)}{1+3\mathrm{X}_{+}^{2}(\Psi)+4\mathrm{X}_{+}(\Psi)}\right)^{3/2},

(4.25)

where,

\displaystyle T_{1}=

\displaystyle\frac{3}{2}\frac{[1-3\mathrm{X}_{+}^{2}(\Psi)]^{2}}{1+8\mathrm{X}_{+}(\Psi)+8\mathrm{X}_{+}^{2}(\Psi)-12\mathrm{X}_{+}^{3}(\Psi)-21\mathrm{X}_{+}^{4}(\Psi)},

(4.26)

\displaystyle T_{2}=

\displaystyle\frac{3}{8}\frac{[1-3\mathrm{X}_{+}^{2}(\Psi)]^{2}}{[1+8\mathrm{X}_{+}(\Psi)+8\mathrm{X}_{+}^{2}(\Psi)-12\mathrm{X}_{+}^{3}(\Psi)-21\mathrm{X}_{+}^{4}(\Psi)]^{3}}\times\Big[1-24\mathrm{X}_{+}(\Psi)-282\mathrm{X}_{+}^{2}(\Psi)-820\mathrm{X}_{+}^{3}(\Psi)

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt-504\mathrm{X}_{+}^{4}(\Psi)+1344\mathrm{X}_{+}^{5}(\Psi)+1746\mathrm{X}_{+}^{6}(\Psi)-324\mathrm{X}_{+}^{7}(\Psi)-945\mathrm{X}_{+}^{8}(\Psi)\Big].

(4.27)

Then for all
\mathrm{X}_{+}(\Psi)
allowed,
T_{1}
is greater than zero; while
T_{2}>0
only for
\mathrm{X}_{+}(\Psi)\lesssim 0.03
or equivalently
\Psi\gtrsim 85
. For all realistic NSs, the
\Psi
factor is roughly smaller than 10, see FIG.
32
, so we can treat
T_{2}<0
.
A negative
T_{2}
shows that the growth rate of
M_{\mathrm{NS}}
when
s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}\approx 1
is sub-linear.
See FIG.
15
for the dependence of
\mathcal{M}(s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2},\Psi)
on stiffness
s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}
; the super- or sub-linear feature of the growth of
\mathcal{M}
over
s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}
implies that
M_{\mathrm{NS}}
increases much faster (slower) when
s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}
is small (large), e.g., we have
\mathcal{M}(0.5,0)/\mathcal{M}(0.25,0)\approx 1.42
and
\mathcal{M}(1.0,0)/\mathcal{M}(0.5,0)\approx 1.17
, when the
s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}
is doubled.
Another feature of FIG.
15
is that the curve of
\mathcal{M}(s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2},\Psi)
becomes more flat for smaller
\Psi
, indicating the stiffness
s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}
has a more obvious effect on stable NSs away from the TOV configuration (so the
\Psi
is large). For example, we have
\mathcal{M}(0.5,1)/\mathcal{M}(0.25,1)\approx 1.52
besides
\mathcal{M}(0.5,0)/\mathcal{M}(0.25,0)\approx 1.42
given previously.
The nonlinearity of the growth rate of
M_{\mathrm{NS}}
over
s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}
(for a given
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}
) near some other SSS (say,
s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}\approx 0.5
) could be analyzed similarly and would not be discussed further.

The second condition of the TOV configuration (
4.14
) can similarly induce useful information/constraint on the EOS.
After some long but straightforward derivations, we obtain

\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}M_{\mathrm{NS}}}{\mathrm{d}\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}}\sim\left(1-\frac{s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}}{{\mathrm{X}}}\right)\left[\left(\frac{s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}}{{\mathrm{X}}}-\frac{\mathrm{d}s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}}{\mathrm{d}{\mathrm{X}}}\right)+{\mathrm{X}}\left(1-\frac{s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}}{{\mathrm{X}}}\right)\frac{12{\mathrm{X}}^{2}+12{\mathrm{X}}+4}{9{\mathrm{X}}^{4}+12{\mathrm{X}}^{3}-4{\mathrm{X}}-1}\right].

(4.28)

Now, one can not directly use the expression for
s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}=\mathrm{d}P_{\mathrm{c}}/\mathrm{d}\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}
given by (
4.17
) to evaluate the derivative
\mathrm{d}s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}/\mathrm{d}{\mathrm{X}}
since (
4.17
) is obtained via the condition
\mathrm{d}M_{\mathrm{NS}}/\mathrm{d}\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}=0
, namely if (
4.17
) is put in (
4.28
) the expression is identically zero.
Instead, demanding generally
\mathrm{d}^{2}M_{\mathrm{NS}}/\mathrm{d}\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}<0
leads us to,

\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}}{\mathrm{d}{\mathrm{X}}}\right|_{M_{\mathrm{NS}}^{\max}}<\sigma_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}\equiv\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}{\mathrm{X}}}\left[{\mathrm{X}}\left(1+\frac{1}{3}\frac{1+3{\mathrm{X}}^{2}+4{\mathrm{X}}}{1-3{\mathrm{X}}^{2}}\right)\right]=\frac{2}{3}\frac{9{\mathrm{X}}^{4}-3{\mathrm{X}}^{2}+4{\mathrm{X}}+2}{(3{\mathrm{X}}^{2}-1)^{2}},

(4.29)

since the in-front factor
1-s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}/{\mathrm{X}}
in (
4.28
) is negative.
We may use this criterion in SECTION
5
(Subsection
5.7
) when discussing the radial dependence of the SSS.

We show in FIG.
16
the
R_{\max}
-
\nu_{\mathrm{c}}
(panel (a)) and the
M_{\mathrm{NS}}^{\max}
-
\Gamma_{\mathrm{c}}
(panel (b)) correlations by using 87 phenomenological and 17 extra microscopic NS EOSs with and/or without considering hadron-quark phase transitions and hyperons by solving numerically the original TOV equations, see Ref.
Cai et al. (2023)
for more details on these EOS samples.
The observed strong linear correlations demonstrate vividly that the
R_{\max}
-
\nu_{\mathrm{c}}
and
M_{\mathrm{NS}}^{\max}
-
\Gamma_{\mathrm{c}}
scalings are nearly universal.
Since the TOV configuration is very near the mass threshold before NSs collapse into BHs, and it is well known that certain properties of BHs are universal and only depend on quantities like mass, charge and angular momentum, it is not surprising that the predicted mass and radius scalings at the TOV configuration hold very well with the diverse set of EOSs. It is also particularly interesting to notice that EOSs allowing phase transitions and/or hyperon formations confirm consistently the same scalings first revealed from our analyses of the scaled TOV equations without using any EOS. By performing linear fits of the results obtained from using these EOS samples in solving the TOV equations in the traditional approach, the quantified scaling relations are determined to be
Cai et al. (2023)
;
Cai et al. (2023)
;
Cai and Li (2024)
;
Cai and Li (2024)

\boxed{R_{\mathrm{max}}/\mathrm{km}\approx A_{\mathrm{R}}^{\max}\nu_{\mathrm{c}}+B_{\mathrm{R}}^{\max}\approx 1.05_{-0.03}^{+0.03}\times 10^{3}\left(\frac{\nu_{\mathrm{c}}}{\mathrm{fm}^{3/2}/\mathrm{MeV}^{1/2}}\right)+0.64_{-0.25}^{+0.25},}

(4.30)

with its Pearson’s coefficient about 0.958 and

\boxed{M_{\mathrm{NS}}^{\mathrm{max}}/M_{\odot}\approx A_{\mathrm{M}}^{\max}\Gamma_{\mathrm{c}}+B_{\mathrm{M}}^{\max}\approx 1.73_{-0.03}^{+0.03}\times 10^{3}\left(\frac{\Gamma_{\mathrm{c}}}{\mathrm{fm}^{3/2}/\mathrm{MeV}^{1/2}}\right)-0.106_{-0.035}^{+0.035},}

(4.31)

with its Pearson’s coefficient about 0.986, respectively, here
\nu_{\mathrm{c}}
and
\Gamma_{\mathrm{c}}
are measured in
\mathrm{fm}^{3/2}/\mathrm{MeV}^{1/2}
.
In addition, the standard errors (ste’s) for the radius and mass fittings are about 0.031 and 0.003 for these EOS samples.
In FIG.
16
, the condition
M^{\max}_{\mathrm{NS}}\gtrsim 1.2M_{\odot}
used is necessary to mitigate influences of uncertainties in modeling the crust EOS
Baym et al. (1971)
;
Iida and Sato (1997)
;
Xu et al. (2009)
for low-mass NSs. For the heavier NSs studied here, it is reassuring to see that although the above 104 EOSs predicted quite different crust properties, they all fall closely around the same scaling lines consistently, especially for the
M_{\mathrm{NS}}^{\max}
-
\Gamma_{\mathrm{c}}
relation.

Using the numerical forms of Eqs. (
4.30
) and (
4.31
), we similarly have,

\frac{M_{\mathrm{NS}}^{\max}}{M_{\odot}}\approx\frac{1.65{\mathrm{X}}}{1+3{\mathrm{X}}^{2}+4{\mathrm{X}}}\left(\frac{R_{\max}}{\mathrm{km}}-0.64\right)-0.106.

(4.32)

The in-front coefficient
f\approx 1.65{\mathrm{X}}/(1+3{\mathrm{X}}^{2}+4{\mathrm{X}})
is a slow-varying function of
{\mathrm{X}}
, and it essentially explains the conventional quasi-linear correlation between
M_{\mathrm{NS}}^{\max}/M_{\odot}
and
R_{\max}
from model calculations.
In the right panel of FIG.
16
, the scatters of
M_{\mathrm{NS}}^{\max}
versus
R_{\max}
using the above 104 EOSs
Cai et al. (2023)
are shown.
The fitting lines of Eq. (
4.32
) adopt four fiducial values for
{\mathrm{X}}
(captioned near the lines with the same color), from which one finds that for
{\mathrm{X}}\gtrsim 0.3
the expression (
4.32
) could reasonably describe the EOS samples.
However, obvious dispersions can be seen for these scatters.
In addition, certain EOSs are unfavored by the causal limit
{\mathrm{X}}\lesssim 0.374
(solid black line, corresponding to
s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}\leq 1
discussed above).
We shall discuss the causality boundary for NSs in details in SECTION
6
.

Most probable values for the central energy density
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}
(fourth column), central pressure
P_{\mathrm{c}}
(fifth column) and the central SSS
s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}
(sixth column) for PSR J0740+6620, here its mass about
2.08_{-0.07}^{+0.07}M_{\odot}
and so
12.2\lesssim{10^{4}\Gamma_{\mathrm{c}}}/[{\mathrm{fm}^{3/2}/\mathrm{MeV}^{1/2}]}\lesssim 13.1
are used for five cases for the radius
Riley et al. (2021)
;

is measured in unit

,

and

\mathrm{MeV}/\mathrm{fm}^{3}

Table taken from Ref.
Cai et al. (2023)
with modifications.

Given a
\Gamma_{\mathrm{c}}
(mass observation) or
\nu_{\mathrm{c}}
(radius observation), either the mass scaling or the radius scaling can lead to a functional relation between
P_{\mathrm{c}}
and
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}
, namely the central EOS:

\displaystyle P_{\mathrm{c}}(\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}})\approx

\displaystyle f_{\mathrm{M}}^{2/3}\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}^{4/3}\cdot\left(1+4f_{\mathrm{M}}^{2/3}\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}^{1/3}+19f_{\mathrm{M}}^{4/3}\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}^{2/3}+100f_{\mathrm{M}}^{2}\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}+\cdots\right),

(4.33)

\displaystyle P_{\mathrm{c}}(\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}})\approx

\displaystyle f_{\mathrm{R}}^{2}\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}\cdot\left(1+4f_{\mathrm{R}}^{2}\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}+19f_{\mathrm{R}}^{4}\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}+100f_{\mathrm{R}}^{6}\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}^{3}+\cdots\right),

(4.34)

where
{f_{\mathrm{M}}}/[{\mathrm{fm}^{3/2}/\mathrm{MeV}^{1/2}}]=({M_{\mathrm{NS}}^{\max}/M_{\odot}+0.106})/{1730}
and
{f_{\mathrm{R}}}/[{\mathrm{fm}^{3/2}/\mathrm{MeV}^{1/2}}]=({R_{\max}/\mathrm{km}-0.64})/{1050}
.

With the scalings given above, if the mass and radius of a massive NS are observed simultaneously, then one can determine individually its central pressure and central energy density. As an example, for one of the most massive NSs observed/confirmed so far, e.g., PSR J0740+6620, it is reasonable to use the scallings at the TOV configuration. The extracted central properties shown in TAB.
2
for PSR J0740+6620 are indeed very interesting. In particular, Eq. (
4.33
) implies that a heavier NS has a smaller upper
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}
(as the ratio
P_{\mathrm{c}}/\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}
is bounded from above) and therefore is easier to collapse into a BH
Hawking and Ellis (2023)
. The observational radius about
12.39_{-0.98}^{+1.30}\,\mathrm{km}

Riley et al. (2021)
at 68% confidence level and mass about
2.08_{-0.07}^{+0.07}M_{\odot}

Fonseca et al. (2021)
of PSR J0740+6620 are also shown in the left panels of FIG.
16
.
The strong linear correlations of
M_{\mathrm{NS}}^{\max}
-
\Gamma_{\mathrm{c}}
and
R_{\max}
-
\nu_{\mathrm{c}}
then enable us to read out the corresponding values for
\Gamma_{\mathrm{c}}
and
\nu_{\mathrm{c}}
.
Their intersections with the two scaling lines indicate that
10.3\lesssim 10^{3}\nu_{\mathrm{c}}/[\mathrm{fm}^{3/2}/\mathrm{MeV}^{1/2}]\lesssim 12.4
and
12.2\lesssim{10^{4}\Gamma_{\mathrm{c}}}/[{\mathrm{fm}^{3/2}/\mathrm{MeV}^{1/2}]}\lesssim 13.1
, respectively, in PSR J0740+6620 (indicated by the orange arrows).
For a comparison, we notice that the hadron-quark hybrid EOS ALF2 predicts its
M_{\mathrm{NS}}^{\max}\approx 2.09M_{\odot}

Alford et al. (2005)
that is very close to the mass of PSR J0740+6620. It also predicts
P_{\mathrm{c}}\approx 310\,\mathrm{MeV}/\mathrm{fm}^{3}
and
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}\approx 1100\,\mathrm{MeV}/\mathrm{fm}^{3}
, therefore
\Gamma_{\mathrm{c}}\approx 12.4\times 10^{-4}\,\mathrm{MeV}^{1/2}/\mathrm{fm}^{3/2}
, being consistent with the
\Gamma_{\mathrm{c}}
extracted for PSR J0740+6620 from the mass scaling shown in FIG.
16
.
Different observational constraints on the radius may lead to different ranges for
\nu_{\mathrm{c}}
. We found that the radius constraint of
13.7_{-1.5}^{+2.6}\,\mathrm{km}

Miller et al. (2021)
gives
11.0\lesssim 10^{3}\nu_{\mathrm{c}}/[\mathrm{fm}^{3/2}/\mathrm{MeV}^{1/2}]\lesssim 14.9
; and the recent analysis including NICER’s background estimates
Salmi et al. (2022)
found a radius of
12.90_{-0.97}^{+1.25}\,\mathrm{km}
, leading to
10.8\lesssim 10^{3}\nu_{\mathrm{c}}/[\mathrm{fm}^{3/2}/\mathrm{MeV}^{1/2}]\lesssim 12.9
.
See the third column of TAB.
2
for these values for
\nu_{\mathrm{c}}
.
If we use the very recently updated constraints on the radius as a result of using more observational data accumulated
Salmi et al. (2024)
;
Dittmann et al. (2024)
, the
\nu_{\mathrm{c}}
should be updated correspondingly, see the last two rows of TAB.
2
.
In the following discussions, we mainly use the radius of Ref.
Riley et al. (2021)
.
Having obtained the
\Gamma_{\mathrm{c}}
and
\nu_{\mathrm{c}}
, the factor
\Pi_{\mathrm{c}}=\Gamma_{\mathrm{c}}/\nu_{\mathrm{c}}
defined in Eq. (
4.8
) could be obtained as

\boxed{\mbox{PSR J0740+6620:}~~{\mathrm{X}}\approx 0.24_{-0.07}^{+0.05},~~\mbox{under}~~R_{\max}\approx 12.39_{-0.98}^{+1.30}\,\mathrm{km}.}

(4.35)

From (
4.33
), we obtain then

\gamma_{\mathrm{c}}^{(\mathrm{M})}=\frac{s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}}{\mathrm{X}}\approx\frac{4}{3}\left(1+f_{\mathrm{M}}^{2/3}\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}^{1/3}+\frac{11}{2}f_{\mathrm{M}}^{4/3}\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}^{3/3}+34f_{\mathrm{M}}^{2}\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}+\cdots\right),~~f_{\mathrm{M}}\approx\left(\frac{M_{\mathrm{NS}}^{\max}+0.106}{1730}\right)\mathrm{fm}^{3/2}/\mathrm{MeV}^{1/2},

(4.36)

under the basic definition for the polytropic index of Eq. (
1.4
).
Similarly, we have from (
4.34
) that

\gamma_{\mathrm{c}}^{(\mathrm{R})}\approx 2\left(1+2f_{\mathrm{R}}^{2}\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}+11f_{\mathrm{R}}^{4}\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}+68f_{\mathrm{R}}^{6}\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}^{3}+\cdots\right),~~f_{\mathrm{R}}\approx\left(\frac{M_{\mathrm{NS}}^{\max}-0.64}{1050}\right)\mathrm{fm}^{3/2}/\mathrm{MeV}^{1/2}.

(4.37)

Eqs. (
4.36
) and (
4.37
) indicate that the
\gamma_{\mathrm{c}}
is greater than 4/3.
For example, taking
M_{\mathrm{NS}}^{\max}\approx 2M_{\odot}
,
R_{\max}\approx 12\,\mathrm{km}
and
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}\approx 800\,\mathrm{MeV}/\mathrm{fm}^{3}
gives the first-order corrections as
f_{\mathrm{M}}^{2/3}\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}^{1/3}\approx 0.106
and
f_{\mathrm{R}}^{2}\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}\approx 0.094
, respectively.
We discuss in Subsection
6.2
the polytropic index
\gamma_{\mathrm{c}}
further using the expression for
s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}
of Eq. (
4.17
) obtained via the condition
\mathrm{d}M_{\mathrm{NS}}^{\max}/\mathrm{d}\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}=0
, see information of TAB.
6
.

P_{\mathrm{c}}(\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}})
independently from the NICER measurements on the mass of

(chocolate band) for PSR J0740+6620, respectively.

\,\mathrm{MeV}/\mathrm{fm}^{3}
