The
\Gamma_{\mathrm{c}}
and
\nu_{\mathrm{c}}
extracted from NICER’s joint mass-radius observation for PSR J0740+6620 provide two independent constraints on the NS central EOS as shown in the left panel of FIG.
17
by the blue and chocolate bands, respectively.
Since the uncertainty of the radius is currently about three times larger than that of the mass measurement, the resulting constraining band of the central EOS from the radius measurement is much wider.
This means the central EOS is essentially given by the mass measurement via
\Gamma_{\mathrm{c}}
while the radius effectively sets the upper- and lower-limits for
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}
.
In the overlapping area of the two constraining bands, the most probable NS central EOS can then be written as
Cai et al. (2023)

\mbox{PSR J0740+6620:}~~P_{\mathrm{c}}(\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}})\approx 0.012\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}^{4/3}\cdot\left(1+0.047\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}^{1/3}+0.0026\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}^{2/3}+0.00016\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}+\cdots\right),

(4.38)

with roughly
640\,\mathrm{MeV}/\mathrm{fm}^{3}\lesssim\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}\lesssim 1210\,\mathrm{MeV}/\mathrm{fm}^{3}
.
The inset of the left panel of FIG.
17
compares our constraints on the central EOS (shown as magenta crossed band) with predictions on the dense NS matter EOS from several other approaches, these include (1) combination of low-density nuclear, high-density pQCD theories and the tidal deformability from GW event GW170817
Abbott et al. (2018)
shown as the grey shaded band
Annala et al. (2018)
; (2) pQCD with constraint of the SSS at very high densities shown as the yellow solid band
Komoltsev and Kurkela (2022)
; (3) similar physical inputs as (1) combining Gaussian-process (GP) inference algorithm using the red dash-dotted band
Gorda et al. (2023)
; (4) the construction of EOS ensembles consistent with nuclear theories, pQCD and astronomical observations and continuous SSS shown by the pink vertical hatched band
Altiparmak et al. (2022)
; (5) a nonparametric inference via GP adopting similar astronomical observations by the black dashed band together with (6) the prediction on the central pressure-density of PSR J0740+6620 shown by green horizontal hatched band
Legred et al. (2021)
; and (7) the constraint from an earlier model analysis of the NICER data by the blue solid band
Miller et al. (2021)
.

Our constraining band on the central NS EOS directly from the NICER data using the mass and radius scalings without using any specific input EOS model is mostly consistent with but much narrower than the NS EOS bands from the previous studies. For example, we have
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}\approx 901_{-287}^{+214}\,\mathrm{MeV}/\mathrm{fm}^{3}
and
P_{\mathrm{c}}\approx 218_{-125}^{+93}\,\mathrm{MeV}/\mathrm{fm}^{3}
(black “+” in FIG.
17
), respectively.
This value of central energy density is consistent with
P_{\mathrm{c}}\approx 312_{-169}^{+226}\,\mathrm{MeV}/\mathrm{fm}^{3}
and
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}\approx 904_{-327}^{+329}\,\mathrm{MeV}/\mathrm{fm}^{3}
and
\mathrm{X}\approx 0.35
for a
2.1M_{\odot}
NS from a recent Bayesian inference
Brandes et al. (2023)
.
Notice that the inferred radius of that work
Brandes et al. (2023)
is about 11.6 km, which is somewhat smaller than the observational radius for PSR J0740+6620, therefore leading to a much larger
\mathrm{X}
(the matter is denser).
Including the black widow pulsar PSR J0952-0607 in the same inference framework, Ref.
Brandes et al. (2023)
then found
R\approx 12.3\,\mathrm{km}
as well as
P_{\mathrm{c}}\approx 186\,\mathrm{MeV}/\mathrm{fm}^{3}
and
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}\approx 628\,\mathrm{MeV}/\mathrm{fm}^{3}
for a
2.1M_{\odot}
NS and so
\mathrm{X}\approx 0.30
, this is still larger than the
\mathrm{X}
we extracted for PSR J0740+6620.
Also notice that our constraining band has obvious deviation from the one in Ref.
Legred et al. (2021)
. One possible reason is that the inferred central baryon density in the latter work is about
3.6\rho_{0}
, which is much smaller than ours about
6\rho_{0}
, see Subsection
4.3
for discussion/estimate on it.
If different radius constraints
Miller et al. (2021)
;
Salmi et al. (2022)
;
Riley et al. (2021)
were used in the inference, we may obtain slightly different constraining band for the central EOS.
The result is shown in the right panel of FIG.
17
from which one can easily find the constraining bands for
P_{\mathrm{c}}(\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}})
in all three cases are narrow, and the uncertainties on
P_{\mathrm{c}}(\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}})
mainly reflect the observational uncertainty on the radius of PSR J0740+6620.
See TAB.
2
for the extracted
P_{\mathrm{c}}
and
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}
for these cases.

FIG. 18:
(Color Online). Mass and radius scalings considering anisotropic pressure effects. Figure taken from Ref.
Yang and Wen (2024)
.

Recently, the above NS mass and radius scalings were generalized by considering anisotropic pressure effects in Ref.
Yang and Wen (2024)
. In their analysis, there exists a tangential pressure
P_{\mathrm{t}}
besides the conventional radial pressure
P
; the relation between them is

\delta P=P_{\mathrm{t}}-P=\frac{\lambda_{\mathrm{BL}}}{3}\frac{(\varepsilon+3P)(\varepsilon+P)r^{3}}{r-2M},

(4.39)

here
\lambda_{\mathrm{BL}}
is a parameter characterizing the pressure anisotropy.
A new term
2\delta P/r
should be added to the right hand side of the
\mathrm{d}P/\mathrm{d}r
in the TOV equations.
Using a similar method as ours, the authors of Ref.
Yang and Wen (2024)
derived the following scalings

M_{\mathrm{NS}}^{\max}\sim\alpha\equiv\Gamma_{\mathrm{c}}\left(1-\frac{\lambda_{\mathrm{BL}}}{2\pi}\right)^{-3/2},~~R\sim\beta\equiv\nu_{\mathrm{c}}\left(1-\frac{\lambda_{\mathrm{BL}}}{2\pi}\right)^{-1/2},

(4.40)

where
\Gamma_{\mathrm{c}}
and
\nu_{\mathrm{c}}
are the same ones defined in Eqs. (
4.7
) and (
4.6
), respectively.
Their numerical verifications of the scalings are shown in FIG.
18
using different values for
\lambda_{\mathrm{BL}}
between 0 and 2.
They also derived the expression for
s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}
, which involves the parameter
\lambda_{\mathrm{BL}}
. In this case, the causality condition
s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}\leq 1
may induce a different upper bound for
\mathrm{X}
.

FIG. 19:
(Color Online). Scalings of the TOV mass
M_{\mathrm{TOV}}=M_{\mathrm{NS}}^{\max}
, the corresponding radius
R_{\max}
as well as the speed of sound with certain combinations of NS central quantities. Figure taken from Ref.
Ofengeim et al. (2023)
.

The TOV configuration is very important/useful for exploring properties of superdense matter, as we pointed out in the beginning of this subsection. Besides our scalings for NS mass and radius given above, another recent work in Ref.
Ofengeim et al. (2023)
found that the TOV mass
M_{\mathrm{NS}}^{\max}=M_{\mathrm{TOV}}
and radius
R_{\max}
scale empirically as
Ofengeim et al. (2023)
,

M_{\mathrm{NS}}^{\max}\approx\frac{a_{\mathrm{M}}\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}^{-1/2}\mathrm{X}^{3/2}}{b_{\mathrm{M}}+c_{\mathrm{M}}\mathrm{X}^{p_{\mathrm{M}}}\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}^{q_{\mathrm{M}}}},~~R_{\max}\approx\frac{a_{\mathrm{R}}\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}^{-1/2}\mathrm{X}^{1/2}}{b_{\mathrm{R}}+c_{\mathrm{R}}\mathrm{X}^{p_{\mathrm{R}}}\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}^{q_{\mathrm{R}}}},~~\mathrm{X}=P_{\mathrm{c}}/\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}},

(4.41)

here
a_{\mathrm{M,R}}
,
b_{\mathrm{M,R}}
,
c_{\mathrm{M,R}}
,
p_{\mathrm{M,R}}
and
q_{\mathrm{M,R}}
are fitting parameters
Ofengeim et al. (2023)
. Their numerical verifications of the scalings using a large and diverse set of model EOSs are shown in FIG.
19
. It is seen that the relative uncertainties of their fittings are generally smaller than
8\%
, though the physical origin and meaning of their fitting parameters are still somewhat elusive.
See also the very recent work in Ref.
Ofengeim et al. (2024)
.

The above analysis/constraint on the central EOS for PSR J0740+6620 is based on the assumption that it is at the TOV configuration.
In Subsection
4.5
we shall study the mass and radius scalings for NSs with arbitrary masses and extract the central EOS for two specific cases: a canonical NS with a mass of
M_{\mathrm{NS}}=1.4M_{\odot}
and a
2.1M_{\odot}
NS (e.g., PSR J0740+6620).

Very recently, Sun and Lattimer revisited the correspondence between NS M-R relation and the underlying nuclear EOS with the goal of inferring the latter more accurately and efficiently from observations compared to currently known approaches for solving the longstanding NS inverse structure problem
Sun and Lattimer (2024)
. They used two approaches: (1) the perturbative solution of the dimensionless TOV equations and (2) fitting the energy density, pressure and the speed of sound as powers of NS mass and radius. In their perturbative analysis, they truncated the dimensionless TOV equations at the first leading-order in
\widehat{r}^{2}
, i.e.,
\widehat{P}(\widehat{r})\approx\mathrm{X}+b_{2}\widehat{r}^{2}
and
\widehat{\varepsilon}(\widehat{r})\approx 1+a_{2}\widehat{r}^{2}
where
b_{2}
is given by Eq. (
2.30
), then set simultaneously the pressure and energy density to vanish at NS radius
\widehat{R}
.
This gives
\mathrm{X}+b_{2}\widehat{R}^{2}=0
and
1+a_{2}\widehat{R}^{2}=0
, thus
\widehat{R}^{2}=-\mathrm{X}/b_{2}
and
a_{2}=b_{2}/\mathrm{X}
. The resulting expression for
\widehat{R}
is the same as Eq. (
4.4
), while that for
a_{2}
is different from
a_{2}=b_{2}/s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}
using
s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}
of Eq. (
4.17
) or Eq. (
4.15
).
Consequently, they obtained a different scaling form for the SSS (compared with Eqs. (
4.17
) and (
4.15
) both having high-order terms in
\mathrm{X}
)
Sun and Lattimer (2024)
:

\boxed{\mbox{Sun and Lattimer:}~~s^{2}={\mathrm{d}\widehat{P}}/{\mathrm{d}\widehat{\varepsilon}}={b_{2}}/{a_{2}}=\mathrm{X}.}

(4.42)

It is interesting to notice that the above expression holds for arbitrary radial distance
\widehat{r}
(including
\widehat{r}=0
at NS center), leading to a polytropic index of
\gamma=s^{2}/\phi=\mathrm{X}/\phi
that equals to 1 at the center (i.e.,
\gamma_{\mathrm{c}}=1
), because
s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}=\mathrm{X}
. Moreover, since
s^{2}
in Eq. (
4.42
) is independent of
\widehat{r}
, it is equivalent to having an average SSS of
\mathrm{X}
that is exactly what Eq. (
6.44
) (in Subsection
6.9
) defining the average SSS in NSs requires even for SSS varying with
\widehat{r}
.

Going beyond the leading-order analysis given in Ref.
Sun and Lattimer (2024)
by including more terms in expanding the pressure and energy density may essentially change the expressions for
\widehat{R}
and SSS.
For example, considering the next-to-leading order correction
by keeping the
b_{4}
- and
a_{4}
-terms, neglecting the even higher order terms and requiring the pressure and energy density to vanish simultaneously on the surface at the
\widehat{R}^{4}
order, i.e.,

\mathrm{X}+b_{2}\widehat{R}^{2}+b_{4}\widehat{R}^{4}=0
and
1+a_{2}\widehat{R}^{2}+a_{4}\widehat{R}^{4}=0
, we obtain the expression below for the central SSS and its difference
s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}-\mathrm{X}
from the leading-order one in Eq. (
4.42
):

s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}=\frac{b_{2}}{a_{2}}=\frac{\mathrm{X}+b_{4}\widehat{R}^{4}}{1+a_{4}\widehat{R}^{4}},~~s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}-\mathrm{X}=\frac{(b_{4}-a_{4}\mathrm{X})\widehat{R}^{4}}{1+a_{4}\widehat{R}^{4}}.

(4.43)

Using the same approximation, the SSS on the surface of radius
\widehat{R}
is given by:

s_{\mathrm{surf}}^{2}=\frac{b_{2}+2b_{4}\widehat{R}^{2}}{a_{2}+2a_{4}\widehat{R}^{2}}=\frac{\mathrm{X}-b_{4}\widehat{R}^{4}}{1-a_{4}\widehat{R}^{4}}.

(4.44)

Consequently,

s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}-s_{\mathrm{surf}}^{2}=\frac{\mathrm{X}+b_{4}\widehat{R}^{4}}{1+a_{4}\widehat{R}^{4}}-\frac{\mathrm{X}-b_{4}\widehat{R}^{4}}{1-a_{4}\widehat{R}^{4}}=\frac{2(b_{4}-a_{4}\mathrm{X})\widehat{R}^{4}}{(1-a_{4}\widehat{R}^{4})(1+a_{4}\widehat{R}^{4})}=\frac{2(s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}-\mathrm{X})}{1-a_{4}\widehat{R}^{4}}.

(4.45)

Since
\mathrm{d}\widehat{M}/\mathrm{d}\widehat{r}>0
and
\mathrm{d}\widehat{\varepsilon}/\mathrm{d}\widehat{r}<0
, applying them at radius
\widehat{R}
gives
a_{4}\widehat{R}^{4}<1
. Therefore
1-a_{4}\widehat{R}^{4}>0
.
Under the (reasonable) assumption
s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}>s_{\mathrm{surf}}^{2}
, one then obtains
s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}>\mathrm{X}
or equivalently
\gamma_{\mathrm{c}}\equiv s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}/\mathrm{X}>1
, indicating qualitative effects of the next-to-leading order correction on the SSS.
Moreover, this analysis shows again that the NS central EOS could not be conformal signaled by
\gamma_{\mathrm{c}}\approx 1
.

FIG. 20:
(Color Online). The central SSS of Eq. (
4.17
) can be mapped onto an effective power-law form
\mathrm{X}^{k}
with
{k}\approx 2
(black dashed line). For typical NSs,
\mathrm{X}\gtrsim 0.1
.

In the power-law fitting scheme, Sun and Lattimer suggested that
Sun and Lattimer (2024)

\boxed{\mathcal{O}=a[{\mathcal{O}}]\left(\frac{M_{\mathrm{NS}}^{\max}}{M_{\odot}}\right)^{b[{\mathcal{O}}]}\left(\frac{R_{\max}}{10\,\mathrm{km}}\right)^{c[{\mathcal{O}}]},}

(4.46)

for a certain quantity
\mathcal{O}
(e.g., energy density, pressure and the speed of sound). They found that their power-law fits for the energy density, pressure and speed of sound are accurate to about 1.5%, 4.5%, and 3.6%, respectively, with respect to the exact solutions of TOV equations using the traditional approach. At the TOV configuration, the power-law exponents for fitting the central pressure are found to be
b[P_{\mathrm{c}}]\approx 2.8
and
c[P_{\mathrm{c}}]\approx-5.3
, respectively. Interestingly, an extra factor about
M_{\mathrm{NS}}^{\max}/R_{\max}
emerges compared with Eq. (
4.10
), i.e.,
P_{\mathrm{c}}\sim M_{\mathrm{NS}}^{\max,3}/R_{\max}^{5}\approx(M_{\mathrm{NS}}^{\max,2}/R_{\max}^{4})(M_{\mathrm{NS}}^{\max}/R_{\max})\sim\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}\Pi_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}
.
Additionally, by fitting the central SSS
b[s_{\mathrm{c}}]\approx 1.9
and
c[s_{\mathrm{c}}]\approx-2.4
were found
Sun and Lattimer (2024)
, implying
s_{\mathrm{c}}\sim(M_{\mathrm{NS}}^{\max}/R_{\max})^{2}
. In fact, these findings can be well explained quantitatively by the mass and radius scalings of Eqs. (
4.7
) and (
4.6
) as well as the expression for
s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}
of Eq. (
4.17
).
As shown in the left panel of FIG.
14
, the function
\mathrm{X}/(1+3\mathrm{X}^{2}+4\mathrm{X})
could be mapped onto an effective power-law form
\mathrm{X}^{q}
with
q\approx 1/2
.
Similarly, the central SSS for TOV NSs of Eq. (
4.17
) can be mapped onto the following power-law form:

s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}=\mathrm{X}\left(1+\frac{1}{3}\frac{1+3\mathrm{X}^{2}+4\mathrm{X}}{1-3\mathrm{X}^{2}}\right)\sim\mathrm{X}^{{k}},~~\mathrm{with}~~{k}\approx 2,

(4.47)

as shown in FIG.
20
.
With these effective forms, we then obtain
R_{\max}\sim P_{\mathrm{c}}^{q/2}\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}^{-q/2-1/2}
from Eq. (
4.6
) and
M_{\mathrm{NS}}^{\max}\sim P_{\mathrm{c}}^{3q/2}\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}^{-3q/2-1/2}
from Eq. (
4.7
), so

\boxed{P_{\mathrm{c}}\sim\left(M_{\mathrm{NS}}^{\max}\right)^{1+q^{-1}}R_{\max}^{-3-q^{-1}}\sim M_{\mathrm{NS}}^{\max,3}/R_{\max}^{5},~~\mathrm{with}~~q\approx 1/2.}

(4.48)

Dividing
M_{\mathrm{NS}}^{\max}
and
R_{\max}
leads to
{M_{\mathrm{NS}}^{\max}}/{R_{\max}}\sim({P_{\mathrm{c}}}/{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}})^{q}=\mathrm{X}^{q}
, or inversely
\mathrm{X}\sim\left({M_{\mathrm{NS}}^{\max}}/{R_{\max}}\right)^{q^{-1}}\approx\left({M_{\mathrm{NS}}^{\max}}/{R_{\max}}\right)^{2}
(using
q\approx 1/2
).
Then Eq. (
4.47
) gives:

\boxed{s_{\mathrm{c}}\sim\mathrm{X}^{{k}/2}\sim\left({M_{\mathrm{NS}}^{\max}}/{R_{\max}}\right)^{{k}/2q}\approx(M_{\mathrm{NS}}^{\max}/R_{\max})^{2},~~\mbox{with}~~q\approx 1/2,~{k}\approx 2.}

(4.49)

Interestingly, if one adopts Eq. (
4.42
) for the central SSS as
s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}=\mathrm{X}
and considering that
M_{\mathrm{NS}}^{\max}/R_{\max}=\widehat{M}_{\mathrm{NS}}^{\max}/\widehat{R}_{\max}\approx 2\widehat{R}_{\max}^{2}/15\sim\widehat{R}^{2}_{\max}\sim\mathrm{X}/(1+3\mathrm{X}^{2}+4\mathrm{X})

Sun and Lattimer (2024)
which still maps effectively onto
\mathrm{X}^{q}
with
q\approx 1/2
, then we obtain
s_{\mathrm{c}}\sim\mathrm{X}^{1/2}\sim(M_{\mathrm{NS}}^{\max}/R_{\max})
; it is a factor of
M_{\mathrm{NS}}^{\max}/R_{\max}
different from their power-law fittings mentioned above
Sun and Lattimer (2024)
.
Finally, for Newtonian stars (with
\mathrm{X}\to 0
),
q\approx 1
(which follows directly from Eq. (
4.7
)), so
M_{\mathrm{NS}}^{\max}/R_{\max}\sim\mathrm{X}
; and
s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}\approx 4\mathrm{X}/3\sim\mathrm{X}
(indicating
{k}\approx 1
). Consequently, one obtains
s_{\mathrm{c}}\sim\mathrm{X}^{1/2}\sim(M_{\mathrm{NS}}^{\max}/R_{\max})^{1/2}
. Thus, the leading-order approximation for SSS in Eq. (
4.42
) is more appropriate for light NSs or Newtonian stars.

4.3 Estimating the central baryon density of NSs at the TOV configuration

In this subsection, we use our mass and radius scalings to estimate the central baryon density
\rho_{\mathrm{c}}
of NSs at the TOV configuration.
In order to estimate the baryon density
\rho
, a relation between
\varepsilon
and
\rho
is necessary. An effective approximation is
\varepsilon\approx M_{\mathrm{N}}\rho
with
M_{\mathrm{N}}\approx 939\,\mathrm{MeV}
the mass of a static nucleon.
Then by using the scaling of Eq. (
4.30
) we can derive a relation between NS central baryon density
\rho_{\mathrm{c}}
(in terms of
\rho_{\mathrm{sat}}=\rho_{0}\approx 0.16\,\mathrm{fm}^{-3}
) and the radius
R_{\max}
. The result is,

\frac{\rho_{\mathrm{c}}}{\rho_{\mathrm{sat}}}\approx\frac{7.35\times 10^{3}{\mathrm{X}}}{1+3{\mathrm{X}}^{2}+4{\mathrm{X}}}\left(\frac{R_{\max}}{\mathrm{km}}-0.64\right)^{-2}.

(4.50)

We show in FIG.
21
the relation between this
\rho_{\mathrm{c}}/\rho_{\mathrm{sat}}
and the radius
R_{\max}
under two values of
{\mathrm{X}}
, namely
{\mathrm{X}}=0.16
and
{\mathrm{X}}=0.24
.
For a comparison, an algorithmic/empirical prediction from Ref.
Jiang et al. (2023)
is also shown (orange solid contour), with the original figure being shown in the right panel.
Specifically, Ref.
Jiang et al. (2023)
parameterized
\rho_{\mathrm{c}}/\rho_{\mathrm{sat}}
for the NSs at TOV configuration via a parabolic approximation,

\frac{\rho_{\mathrm{c}}}{\rho_{\mathrm{sat}}}\approx\overline{d}_{0}\left[1-\left(\frac{R_{\max}}{10\,\mathrm{km}}\right)\right]+\overline{d}_{1}\left(\frac{R_{\max}}{10\,\mathrm{km}}\right)^{2},

(4.51)

where
\overline{d}_{0}\approx 27.6
and
\overline{d}_{1}\approx 7.5
are two fitting parameters.
It is seen that the overall correlation between
\rho_{\mathrm{c}}/\rho_{\mathrm{sat}}
and
R_{\max}
from our analyses here is consistent with that from Ref.
Jiang et al. (2023)
.

FIG. 21:
(Color Online). Left panel: correlation between
\rho_{\mathrm{c}}/\rho_{\mathrm{sat}}
and radius
R_{\max}
adopting two reference values for
{\mathrm{X}}
(0.16 and 0.24).
Figure taken from Ref.
Cai et al. (2023)
.
Right panel: an empirical prediction on
\rho_{\mathrm{c}}/\rho_{\mathrm{sat}}
-
R_{\max}\equiv R_{\mathrm{TOV}}
used in Ref.
Jiang et al. (2023)
. Figure taken from Ref.
Jiang et al. (2023)
.

Interestingly, we see from Eq. (
4.50
) that the scaling of
\rho_{\mathrm{c}}/\rho_{\mathrm{sat}}
with
R_{\max}
is roughly

\rho_{\mathrm{c}}/\rho_{\mathrm{sat}}\sim R_{\max}^{-2}\cdot\left[1+\mbox{corrections of }R_{\max}^{-1}\right],

(4.52)

once a
{\mathrm{X}}
is specified.
The correlation shown in FIG.
21
may help us to estimate the maximum central (baryon) density when future radius measurements of very massive NSs are available
Tang et al. (2023)
.
Since the correlation (
4.50
) is obtained under the approximation
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}\approx M_{\mathrm{N}}\rho_{\mathrm{c}}
and principally one needs to use
E(\rho_{\mathrm{c}})+M_{\mathrm{N}}
to replace
M_{\mathrm{N}}
where
E(\rho_{\mathrm{c}})
is the mechanical energy per nucleon at
\rho_{\mathrm{c}}
has its own uncertainties.
Doing so will shift down the lines in FIG.
21
as
E(\rho_{\mathrm{c}})>0
at
\rho_{\mathrm{c}}
.
Similarly, using the mass scaling of Eq. (
4.31
), we may obtain another estimate for
\rho_{\mathrm{c}}/\rho_{\mathrm{sat}}
(still under
\varepsilon\approx\rho M_{\mathrm{N}}
)

{\rho_{\mathrm{c}}}/{\rho_{\mathrm{sat}}}\approx 2\times 10^{4}\left(\frac{{\mathrm{X}}}{1+3{\mathrm{X}}^{2}+4{\mathrm{X}}}\right)^{3}\left(\frac{M_{\mathrm{NS}}^{\max}}{M_{\odot}}+0.106\right)^{-2}\sim M_{\mathrm{NS}}^{\max,-2}\cdot\left[1+\mbox{corrections of }M_{\mathrm{NS}}^{\max,-1}\right].

(4.53)

It indicates that a heavier NS has a relatively smaller central baryon density with the same central EOS
{\mathrm{X}}
.

Based on the basic thermodynamic relation
\rho\partial\varepsilon/\partial\rho=P+\varepsilon
, one can obtain the perturbative expansion
\rho
using the coefficients
a_{k}
and
b_{k}
, see Eqs. (
2.22
) and (
2.23
).
The result is
Cai et al. (2023)
,

\boxed{\widehat{\rho}\equiv\rho/\rho_{\mathrm{c}}\approx 1+\left(\frac{b_{2}/s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}}{1+{\mathrm{X}}}\right)\widehat{r}^{2}+\frac{1}{1+{\mathrm{X}}}\left(a_{4}-\frac{{b_{2}^{2}}/{2s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}}}{1+{\mathrm{X}}}\right)\widehat{r}^{4},}

(4.54)

without any surprise,
\rho<\rho_{\mathrm{c}}
for finite
\widehat{r}\neq 0
, i.e., baryon density like the energy density is a monotonically decreasing function of
\widehat{r}
.
Combing (
4.54
) and
\varepsilon\approx 1+a_{2}\widehat{r}^{2}=1+s_{\mathrm{c}}^{-2}b_{2}\widehat{r}^{2}
gives to order
\mu^{2}
and
{\mathrm{X}}^{2}
,

\displaystyle\rho/\rho_{\mathrm{c}}\approx\widehat{\varepsilon}-\mu\left(1+\frac{4}{3}\mu\right){\mathrm{X}}\left(1-{\mathrm{X}}\right),

(4.55)

here
\mu=\widehat{\varepsilon}-1<0
, see the definition of Eq. (
2.27
).
Therefore, we have

\widehat{\rho}\approx\widehat{\varepsilon}
to leading-order near the center and
\widehat{\rho}\gtrsim\widehat{\varepsilon}
considering finite
{\mathrm{X}}
(notice that the finite-
{\mathrm{X}}
of Eq. (
4.55
) is very small).
Eq. (
4.55
) is an example of the double-element perturbative expansion based on
\mu
and
\mathrm{X}
, we study in Subsection
4.4
the central EOS using the double-element expansion.

FIG. 22:
(Color Online). The
{\mathrm{X}}
-dependence of coefficients
\mathcal{C}_{\Phi}
appeared in
\Phi\approx 1+\mathcal{C}_{\Phi}\widehat{r}^{2}+\mathcal{O}(\widehat{r}^{4})
for
\Phi=\widehat{P}/{\mathrm{X}}
,
\widehat{\varepsilon}/\widehat{\varepsilon}_{\mathrm{c}}
,
\phi/\mathrm{X}=(\widehat{P}/\widehat{\varepsilon})/({\mathrm{X}}/\widehat{\varepsilon}_{\mathrm{c}})
and
\rho/\rho_{\mathrm{c}}
. Figure taken from Ref.
Cai et al. (2023)
.

At this point, it is necessary to figure out the tendency of
\widehat{P}
,
\widehat{\varepsilon}
,
\phi
and
\widehat{\rho}=\rho/\rho_{\mathrm{c}}
for finite
\widehat{r}
.
Specifically, we have

\widehat{P}/{\mathrm{X}}\approx 1+\frac{b_{2}}{{\mathrm{X}}}\widehat{r}^{2},~~\widehat{\varepsilon}/\widehat{\varepsilon}_{\mathrm{c}}\approx 1+\frac{b_{2}}{s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}}\widehat{r}^{2},

(4.56)

and

\phi/{\mathrm{X}}\approx 1+b_{2}\left(\frac{1}{{\mathrm{X}}}-\frac{1}{s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}}\right)\widehat{r}^{2}=1+\frac{b_{2}}{\mathrm{X}}\left[1-\left(1+\frac{1}{3}\frac{1+3\mathrm{X}^{2}+4\mathrm{X}}{1-3\mathrm{X}^{2}}\right)^{-1}\right]\widehat{r}^{2},

(4.57)

as well as (
4.54
) for
\widehat{\rho}
; here
b_{2}<0
is given by Eq. (
2.30
).
To illustrate the
\widehat{r}
-dependence of several quantities all together, in FIG.
22
, we show the
{\mathrm{X}}
-dependence of the coefficient
\mathcal{C}_{\Phi}
appeared in the general approximation
\Phi\approx 1+\mathcal{C}_{\Phi}\widehat{r}^{2}+\mathcal{O}(\widehat{r}^{4})
for
\Phi=\widehat{P}/{\mathrm{X}}
,
\widehat{\varepsilon}/\widehat{\varepsilon}_{\mathrm{c}}
,
\phi/\mathrm{X}=(\widehat{P}/\widehat{\varepsilon})/({\mathrm{X}}/\widehat{\varepsilon}_{\mathrm{c}})
and
\rho/\rho_{\mathrm{c}}
, respectively, for the
M_{\mathrm{NS}}^{\max}
configuration.
By taking
{\mathrm{X}}\approx 0.24
for PSR J0740+6620
Cai et al. (2023)
, we have numerically
\widehat{P}/{\mathrm{X}}\approx 1-1.5\widehat{r}^{2}
,
\widehat{\varepsilon}/\widehat{\varepsilon}_{\mathrm{c}}\approx 1-0.8\widehat{r}^{2}
,
(\widehat{P}/\widehat{\varepsilon})/({\mathrm{X}}/\widehat{\varepsilon}_{\mathrm{c}})\approx 1-0.7\widehat{r}^{2}
, and
\rho/\rho_{\mathrm{c}}\approx 1-0.6\widehat{r}^{2}
, here
\widehat{\varepsilon}_{\mathrm{c}}=\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}/\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}=1
.
It is clearly shown that all these
\mathcal{C}_{\Phi}
’s are definitely negative for
0\leq{\mathrm{X}}\lesssim 0.374
. On the other hand, the coefficient
\mathcal{C}_{\Phi}
for
\Phi=s^{2}/s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}
may be either positive or negative as we shall demonstrate later (FIG.
61
and FIG.
63
).

The radial expansion (
4.54
) is very useful and we could give an order of magnitude estimate for the coefficient
a_{4}
appearing in the expansion of
\widehat{\varepsilon}\approx 1+a_{2}\widehat{r}^{2}+a_{4}\widehat{r}^{4}

Cai et al. (2023)
.
The starting point is that both
\widehat{\varepsilon}
and
\widehat{\rho}=\rho/\rho_{\mathrm{c}}
are decreasing functions of radial distance from the center.
We may use the following elementary result: If
y(x)=1+Ax+Bx^{2}
(
A<0
) is a decreasing function defined for positive
x
, then the first-order derivative
y^{\prime}(x)
should be negative, and consequently
B\leq-{A}/{2x_{\max}}
,
with
x_{\max}
the maximum value of
x
the
y
could take.
Applying this criterion to
\widehat{\varepsilon}\approx 1+a_{2}\widehat{r}^{2}+a_{4}\widehat{r}^{4}
and (
4.54
) and treating them as functions of
\widehat{r}^{2}
gives us two inequalities,

\displaystyle a_{4}\leq

\displaystyle-\frac{b_{2}}{2s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}}\frac{1}{\widehat{R}^{2}},~~\mathrm{and}~~a_{4}\leq\frac{b_{2}}{2s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}}\left(\frac{b_{2}}{1+{\mathrm{X}}}-\frac{1}{\widehat{R}^{2}}\right),

(4.58)

respectively, where
\widehat{R}=R/Q\approx 1
(see (
2.26
)) and
a_{2}=b_{2}/s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}
is used here.
By taking
{\mathrm{X}}\approx 0.24
, inequalities of (
4.58
) give then
a_{4}\lesssim 0.40
and
a_{4}\lesssim 0.51
, respectively.
Similarly, if
{\mathrm{X}}\approx 0.16
is adopted, then we have
a_{4}\lesssim 0.55
and
a_{4}\lesssim 0.69
.
Therefore,
a_{4}\sim\mathcal{O}(1)
; and there is no surprise on that since
\widehat{\varepsilon}\sim\mathcal{O}(1)
near the NS center and
\widehat{r}\leq\widehat{R}\sim\mathcal{O}(1)
, so all the expansion coefficients
a_{k}
are natural on the order of
\mathcal{O}(1)
.
The sign and magnitude of
a_{4}
is very relevant for the emergence of a peak in
s^{2}
, we will discuss it in more details in SECTION
5
.
On the other hand, both
\widehat{\varepsilon}
and
\widehat{\rho}
are obviously decreasing functions of
\widehat{r}
if
a_{4}<0
.

4.4 Double-element expansion for pressure of NS matter

In this review, we may encounter two types of dependence of a quantity
\mathcal{P}
on the energy density, one is the dependence of the (central)
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{c}}\equiv\mathcal{P}(\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}})
on
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}
(panel (a) of FIG.
23
) or equivalently on
{\mathrm{X}}
,
e.g., the dependence of central SSS (
4.17
) or (
4.15
) on
\mathrm{X}
.
The other is the dependence of
\mathcal{P}
on the reduced energy density
\widehat{\varepsilon}=\varepsilon/\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}
, as sketched in panel (b) of FIG.
23
.
The latter actually encapsulates the radial-dependence of the quantity, i.e., a finite
\widehat{r}
corresponds to a finite
\widehat{\varepsilon}<1
.
We may write explicitly the subscripts to avoid potential confusions.

FIG. 23:
Sketch for two types of dependence of certain physical quantities on energy density.
Figures taken from Ref.
Cai et al. (2023)
.

FIG. 24:
(Color Online). Radial dependence of
\widehat{\varepsilon}
and
\widehat{P}/{\mathrm{X}}
to
\widehat{r}\approx 0.20
(about one-fifth of the reduced radius
\widehat{R}
), here
0.1\lesssim{\mathrm{X}}\lesssim 0.374
as a typical range for NSs is adopted.
Figure taken from Ref.
Cai and Li (2024)
.

The expansion coefficients of
\widehat{P}
given in Eq. (
2.23
) and
\widehat{\varepsilon}
given in Eq. (
2.22
) over
\widehat{r}
are heuristic as they contain fundamentally the intrinsic information on the NS structure starting from the center.
For example, the coefficients
a_{2}=b_{2}/s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}
,
b_{2}
of Eq. (
2.30
) and
b_{4}
of Eq. (
2.31
) are all independent of the matter EOS while the coefficient
a_{4}

has certain randomness (owing to the uncertainties of the EOS).
In the appendix of Ref.
Cai and Li (2024)
, we give an example on how the coefficient
a_{4}
is related to the dense matter EOS and its maximum size is found to be around 1 based on our current best knowledge about nuclear EOS. Therefore, at places with small
\widehat{r}
(i.e., near the NS center) where higher-order coefficients have weak impact, the resulted EOS is expected to be nearly model-independent, and so has the predictive power.
Considering typical NSs with
\widehat{R}\approx 1
, see an order-of-magnitude estimate of Eq. (
2.26
), the form of the energy density
\widehat{\varepsilon}\approx 1+a_{2}\widehat{r}^{2}+a_{4}\widehat{r}^{4}+\cdots
indicates the magnitude of
a_{2}
and
a_{4}
, etc., is
\sim\mathcal{O}(1)
.
See also the discussion/estimate given in (
4.58
).
Consequently, the contribution from the term
a_{4}\widehat{r}^{4}
to
\widehat{\varepsilon}
, e.g., for
\widehat{r}\approx 0.20
(one-fifth of the reduced radius
\widehat{R}
), is
\lesssim 0.4\%
(compared with 1) using
-2\lesssim a_{4}\lesssim 2
(which is a rather large magnitude for
a_{4}
compared to the best estimate).
Although
a_{4}
has tiny impact on the
\widehat{\varepsilon}
, it may become relevant for the peaked behavior in
s^{2}
, and this is the issue of SECTION
5
.
Similarly, the
b_{4}
-term contributes
\lesssim 0.5\%
to
\widehat{P}/{\mathrm{X}}
for
\widehat{r}\approx 0.20
and
{\mathrm{X}}\gtrsim 0.1
.
In FIG.
24
, we show the radial dependence of
\widehat{\varepsilon}
and
\widehat{P}/{\mathrm{X}}
from the NS center to
\widehat{r}\approx 0.20
.
Naturally, the
b_{6}
- and
a_{6}
-terms have even smaller contributions to
\widehat{P}/{\mathrm{X}}
and
\widehat{\varepsilon}
, respectively.

FIG. 25:
(Color Online). Left panel: core EOS of NSs by expanding
\widehat{P}
and
\widehat{\varepsilon}
over
\widehat{r}
to order
\widehat{r}^{4}
where
0.1\lesssim{\mathrm{X}}\lesssim 0.374
and
-2\lesssim a_{4}\lesssim 2
(plum band).
The EOS incorporating the pQCD effects at densities
\gtrsim 40\rho_{\mathrm{sat}}
is shown by the dashed-dotted cyan band
Gorda et al. (2023)
.
An instance with
{\mathrm{X}}\approx 0.24
and
a_{4}\approx-1
(dashed magenta) and a few empirical EOSs are shown (solid grey).
An increasing of
s^{2}
indicates a peak occurring there.
Figure taken from Ref.
Cai and Li (2024)
. Right panel: same as the left panel but adopting
0.25\lesssim\mathrm{X}\lesssim 0.35
, the continuous crossover EOS from Ref.
Baym et al. (2019)
(their set-B) is shown by the orange line.

We can work out the central EOS, e.g., at the order
\mu^{3}{\mathrm{X}}^{3}

Cai and Li (2024)
,

\displaystyle\widehat{P}/{\mathrm{X}}=P/P_{\mathrm{c}}\approx

\displaystyle 1+\frac{4}{3}\mu+\frac{16}{15}\mu^{2}+\frac{4}{15}\mu^{3}+\left(\frac{4}{3}-\frac{4}{5}\mu-\frac{268}{135}\mu^{2}\right)\mu{\mathrm{X}}

\displaystyle+\left[2+\left(\frac{28}{3}-\frac{256a_{4}}{3}\right)\mu+\left(\frac{370}{27}-\frac{30208}{315}a_{4}\right)\mu^{2}\right]\mu{\mathrm{X}}^{2}

\displaystyle+\left[4+\left(\frac{1280a_{4}}{3}-\frac{262}{15}\right)\mu+\left(\frac{68608}{105}a_{4}+\frac{2048}{3}a_{6}-\frac{1496}{27}\right)\mu^{2}\right]\mu{\mathrm{X}}^{3}+\mathcal{O}\left({\mathrm{X}}^{4},\mu^{4}\right),

(4.59)

here the expansions of
b_{2}
,
b_{4}
and
b_{6}
of Eqs. (
2.30
), (
2.31
) and (
2.32
) over
{\mathrm{X}}
are also executed.
As an order-of-magnitude estimate, we have
|\mu^{3}{\mathrm{X}}^{3}|\sim 10^{-5}
for
{\mathrm{X}}\sim 0.2
and
\mu\sim-0.1
, implying
a_{6}
has small effect on the core EOS near
\widehat{r}=0
, namely
|2048a_{6}\mu^{3}{\mathrm{X}}^{3}/3|\lesssim 1\%
by considering
a_{6}\sim\mathcal{O}(1)
.
In the left panel of FIG.
25
, we show the core EOS
\widehat{P}
-
\widehat{\varepsilon}
near
\widehat{r}=0
by considering the expansion of
\widehat{P}
and
\widehat{\varepsilon}
over
\widehat{r}
to order
\widehat{r}^{4}
using
0.1\lesssim{\mathrm{X}}\lesssim 0.374
and
-2\lesssim a_{4}\lesssim 2
, an example with
{\mathrm{X}}\approx 0.24
and
a_{4}\approx-1
is given.
Notice that the plum band of the left panel of FIG.
25
originates mainly from the band for
{\mathrm{X}}
, and the “cone” would be shrunk if
{\mathrm{X}}
is further refined by other approaches, as shown in the right panel where a different range of
\mathrm{X}
about
0.25\lesssim\mathrm{X}\lesssim 0.35
is adopted.
The ripples on the curve of
\widehat{P}
-
\widehat{\varepsilon}
characterize the variation of
s^{2}
, as exemplified by the light-blue instance in which case there is an increasing of
s^{2}
, i.e., a possible peak occurring there.
The dotted black line represents configurations with
s^{2}>1
(which violate the causality principle), while the black dashed line (marked by
P={\mathrm{X}}\varepsilon
) is the boundary given by
s^{2}/{\mathrm{X}}=1
, which corresponds to the conformal limit since

\gamma_{\mathrm{c}}\equiv\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}\ln P}{\mathrm{d}\ln\varepsilon}\right|_{\mathrm{center}}=s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}/{\mathrm{X}}=1,

(4.60)

see the definition for
\gamma
given in Eq. (
1.4
).
A few empirical EOSs (solid grey lines) are also shown for comparisons, see Ref.
Cai and Li (2024)
for detailed description on these EOSs.
A recent constraint on the NS EOS incorporating the pQCD effects
Gorda et al. (2023)
is shown by the dot-dashed cyan band (
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}\approx 1000\,\mathrm{MeV}/\mathrm{fm}^{3}
and
P_{\mathrm{c}}\approx 250\mbox{-}400\,\mathrm{MeV}\mathrm{fm}^{3}
are taken).
Because the pQCD theory predicts an approximate conformal symmetry at very high densities (
\gtrsim 40\rho_{\mathrm{sat}})
, the upper boundary of its constraining band is close to the conformal prediction
P={\mathrm{X}}\varepsilon
.

The core EOS shown by the plum band is directly inferred from the gravity (curved geometry of compact NSs) encapsulated in the TOV equations without any presumption about it, unlike in the traditional approach.
Indeed, the EOSs from the two approaches match with each other as demonstrated by the hatched tan band in both panels of FIG.
25
.
Particularly, we have
0.925\lesssim\widehat{\varepsilon}\lesssim 0.945
(shown by two grey solid rectangles) for
\widehat{P}/{\mathrm{X}}\approx 0.9
by combining these two types of EOS.
Moreover, we have
s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}\neq 0
according to Eq. (
4.17
), therefore
a sharp phase transition (PT) near the center or equivalently a plateau on the
P
-
\varepsilon
curve occurring there is basically excluded.
However, a continuous crossover signaled by a smooth reduction of
s^{2}
in NS cores (or equivalently a peaked behavior at some places near the center), see SECTION
5
; and PTs occurring far from the centers are not excluded.
Moreover, the polytropic index
\gamma_{\mathrm{c}}=s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}/{\mathrm{X}}
should not approach 1 due to the nonlinear feature of
s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}
shown in Eq. (
4.17
). In fact, we have
\gamma_{\mathrm{c}}\geq 4/3\approx 1.33
,
implying that the matter in NS cores can hardly be conformal. This finding is very different from the predictions in Refs.
Annala et al. (2023)
;
Gorda et al. (2023)
.
In addition, our prediction on the core EOS (plum band in FIG.
25
) is consistent with several empirical EOSs (grey lines) having a continuous crossover near the centers, see Ref.
Cai and Li (2024)
for details on the EOSs.
Interestingly, the EOSs of Ref.
Alford et al. (2005)
and of Ref.
Kapusta and Welle (2021)
fall out of the tan band (which are above the upper plum boundary in the left panel). The reason is that in these two models the quark matter EOS with approximate conformal symmetry (nearly constant
s^{2}
) is used at high densities (but still within the NS region), they would be closer to the conformal boundary in FIG.
25
.
On the other hand, in a recent study with an improved hadron-quark crossover EOS
Baym et al. (2019)
interpolated smoothly at about
5\rho_{\mathrm{sat}}
, the predicted overall EOS is found to be consistent with our plum band but has certain deviation from the dashed-dotted cyan band, see the right panel of FIG.
25
.
In FIG.
26
, we show the EOS of this crossover model as well as the SSS as a function of density. Notice that the
s^{2}
in the quark matter phase is not a constant (which is the case in a conformal region).

Our discussions thus far indicate that the pQCD effects (with an approximate conformal symmetry) at extremely high densities may have little relevance for the dense NS matter EOS since it is fundamentally inconsistent with the TOV predictions on the EOS. Also, this finding does not mean that there should be no appearance of quark matter in the cores of NSs.

FIG. 26:
(Color Online). Left panel: the crossover EOS interpolated smoothly from the hadron phase to quark matter phase with a transition density about
5\rho_{\mathrm{sat}}
. Right panel: the SSS as a function of density, notice that
s^{2}
is not a constant at
\rho\gtrsim 5\rho_{\mathrm{sat}}
.
Figures taken from Ref.
Baym et al. (2019)
.

Based on Eq. (
4.59
), we may obtain the SSS for finite
\mu
straightforwardly as
s^{2}/{\mathrm{X}}=\mathrm{d}(\widehat{P}/{\mathrm{X}})/\mathrm{d}\mu
, e.g., by neglecting the finite-
{\mathrm{X}}
corrections (this is the Newtonian limit). We have
{\mathrm{X}}^{-1}\mathrm{d}s^{2}/\mathrm{d}\mu=\mathrm{d}^{2}(\widehat{P}/{\mathrm{X}})/\mathrm{d}\mu^{2}=32/15+8\mu/5>0
for
-1\leq\mu\leq 0
, i.e., the
s^{2}
is a monotonically increasing function of
\widehat{\varepsilon}
, as expected.
We point out that one could directly work out the dependence of
s^{2}
on
\phi=P/\varepsilon
.
Starting from
\widehat{P}/{\mathrm{X}}\approx 1+4\mu/3+16\mu^{2}/15+(4/3-4\mu/5)\mu{\mathrm{X}}

to order
\mu^{2}{\mathrm{X}}
(see Eq. (
4.59
). For example for simplicity, we obtain

\displaystyle\phi\approx

\displaystyle\frac{{\mathrm{X}}}{1+\mu}\left[1+\frac{4\mu}{3}+\frac{16\mu^{2}}{15}+\left(\frac{4}{3}-\frac{4\mu}{5}\right)\mu{\mathrm{X}}\right]\approx{\mathrm{X}}\left[1+\frac{\mu}{3}\left(4{\mathrm{X}}+1\right)+\frac{\mu^{2}}{15}\left(11-32{\mathrm{X}}\right)\right],

(4.61)

which gives inversely that

\mu\approx\frac{3}{4{\mathrm{X}}+1}\left(\frac{\phi}{{\mathrm{X}}}-1\right)+\frac{9}{5}\frac{32{\mathrm{X}}-11}{4{\mathrm{X}}+1}\left(\frac{\phi}{{\mathrm{X}}}-1\right)^{2}.

(4.62)

Putting this
\mu=\mu(\phi)
back into
s^{2}/{\mathrm{X}}\approx 4/3+32\mu/15+4\mu^{2}/5+(4/3-8\mu/5-268\mu^{2}/45){\mathrm{X}}
via Eq. (
4.59
) gives the
s^{2}
profile near the NS center to order
(\phi/\phi_{\mathrm{c}}-1)^{2}
as (with
\phi/\phi_{\mathrm{c}}\lesssim 1
),

\displaystyle s^{2}/\phi_{\mathrm{c}}\approx

\displaystyle\frac{4}{3}+\frac{32}{5}\left(1-\frac{19}{4}{\mathrm{X}}\right)\left(\frac{\phi}{\phi_{\mathrm{c}}}-1\right)-\frac{876}{25}\left(1-\frac{3439}{219}{\mathrm{X}}\right)\left(\frac{\phi}{\phi_{\mathrm{c}}}-1\right)^{2},~~\phi_{\mathrm{c}}\equiv\mathrm{X}.

(4.63)

We then find
[\phi_{\mathrm{c}}^{-1}\mathrm{d}s^{2}/\mathrm{d}\phi]_{\phi=\phi_{\mathrm{c}}}=(32/5)(1-19{\mathrm{X}}/4)
, which may either be positive (Newtonian limit
{\mathrm{X}}\to 0
) or negative, depending on the magnitude of
{\mathrm{X}}
.
Moreover, since
s^{2}(\phi)
as a function of
\phi
of Eq. (
4.63
) is non-monotonic, implying
s^{2}
and
\phi
are two different attributes of NSs.
In fact, all the descriptions, namely the
\widehat{r}
-dependence, the
\mu
-dependence and the
\phi
-dependence, are equivalent on the peaked profile of
s^{2}
. In this review, we may
adopt these representations interchangeably.
We discuss in more details on the peaked
s^{2}
in SECTION
5
.

4.5 Central EOSs of superdense matter in canonical and massive NSs

In the above subsections of this section, we have drawn attention to the EOS and related issues for NSs at the TOV configuration. These analyses are mort relevant for massive NSs. Generally, for other NSs similar investigates are necessary. For this purpose, we extend the mass, radius and compactness scalings to stable NSs along the M-R curve and extract useful information on several NS properties.

In this and the following two subsections, we use a meta-model for generating randomly NS EOSs in a broad parameter space that can mimic diverse model predictions consistent will existing constraints from terrestrial experiments and astrophysical observations as well as general physics principles
Cai and Li (2024)
;
Zhang and Li (2021)
;
Zhang et al. (2018)
;
Zhang and Li (2019)
;
Xie and Li (2020)
.
It is based on a so-called minimum model of NSs consisting of neutrons, protons, electrons and muons (npe
\mu
matter) at
\beta
-equilibrium. Its most basic input is the EOS of isospin-asymmetric nucleonic matter in the form of energy per nucleon
E(\rho,\delta)=E_{0}(\rho)+E_{\mathrm{sym}}(\rho)\delta^{2}
,
where the EOS of SNM
E_{0}(\rho)
and the nuclear symmetry energy
E_{\mathrm{sym}}(\rho)
are expanded around the saturation density
\rho_{0}
according to Eq. (
3.1
) and Eq. (
3.2
), respectively.

The total energy density is

\varepsilon(\rho,\delta)=\left[E(\rho,\delta)+M_{\mathrm{N}}\right]\rho+\varepsilon_{\ell}(\rho,\delta),

(4.64)

where
M_{\mathrm{N}}\approx 939\,\mathrm{MeV}
and
\varepsilon_{\ell}(\rho,\delta)
is the energy density of leptons from an ideal Fermi gas model
Oppenheimer and Volkoff (1939)
. The pressure
P(\rho,\delta)
is
P(\rho,\delta)=\rho^{2}\mathrm{d}[\varepsilon(\rho,\delta)/\rho]/\mathrm{d}\rho
. The density profile of isospin asymmetry
\delta(\rho)
is obtained by solving the
\beta
-equilibrium condition

\mu_{\mathrm{n}}-\mu_{\mathrm{p}}=\mu_{\mathrm{e}}\approx\mu_{\mu}\approx 4\delta E_{\mathrm{sym}}(\rho),

(4.65)

and the charge neutrality requirement
\rho_{\mathrm{p}}=\rho_{\mathrm{e}}+\rho_{\mu}
, here the chemical potential
\mu_{i}
for a particle
i
is calculated from the energy density via

\mu_{i}=\partial\varepsilon(\rho,\delta)/\partial\rho_{i}.
With the
\delta(\rho)
calculated consistently using the inputs given above, both the pressure
P
and energy density
\varepsilon(\rho)=\varepsilon(\rho,\delta(\rho))
become barotropic, i.e., depend on the density
\rho
only. The EOS in the form of
P(\varepsilon)
can then be used in solving the TOV equations.
The core-crust transition density
\rho_{\mathrm{cc}}
is determined self-consistently by the thermodynamic method
Iida and Sato (1997)
;
Xu et al. (2009)
. In the inner crust with densities between
\rho_{\mathrm{cc}}
and
\rho_{\mathrm{out}}\approx 2.46\times 10^{-4}\,\mathrm{fm}^{-3}
corresponding to the neutron dripline we adopt the parametrized EOS
P=\alpha+\beta\varepsilon^{4/3}

Iida and Sato (1997)
; and for
\rho<\rho_{\mathrm{out}}
we adopt the Baym–Pethick–Sutherland (BPS) and the Feynman–Metropolis–Teller (FMT) EOSs
Baym et al. (1971)
.
In order to make a verification of the scalings studied in a broad EOS parameter space accommodating predictions of diverse classes of models, we select the saturation density
\rho_{0}
to be
0.15\,\mathrm{fm}^{-3}\lesssim\rho_{0}\lesssim 0.17\,\mathrm{fm}^{-3}
, the binding energy in the range of
-17\,\mathrm{MeV}\lesssim B\lesssim-15\,\mathrm{MeV}
, the incompressibility for symmetric matter in
210\,\mathrm{MeV}\lesssim K_{0}\lesssim 250\,\mathrm{MeV}
and the skewness in
-400\,\mathrm{MeV}\lesssim J_{0}\lesssim 0\,\mathrm{MeV}
. For the symmetry energy, we adopt
28\,\mathrm{MeV}\lesssim S\lesssim 36\,\mathrm{MeV}
for its magnitude at
\rho_{0}
,
30\,\mathrm{MeV}\lesssim L\lesssim 90\,\mathrm{MeV}
for its slope,
-300\,\mathrm{MeV}\lesssim K_{\mathrm{sym}}\lesssim 0\,\mathrm{MeV}
for its curvature as well as
200\,\mathrm{MeV}\lesssim J_{\mathrm{sym}}\lesssim 1000\,\mathrm{MeV}

Zhang and Li (2021)
;
Zhang et al. (2018)
;
Zhang and Li (2019)
;
Xie and Li (2020)
for its skewness.
The EOS parameters generated randomly within the specified uncertainty ranges above are consistent with terrestrial experimental and contemporary astrophysical constraints.

FIG. 27:
(Color Online). Samples of the M-R curves generated by meta-model NS EOSs.
Figure taken from Ref.
Cai and Li (2024)
.

FIG.
27
gives 215 samples of the randomly generated M-R relation with its local derivative
\mathrm{d}M_{\mathrm{NS}}/\mathrm{d}R
switching broadly between negative and positive values and also covering mass regions (“vertical shape of M-R”) where
\mathrm{d}M_{\mathrm{NS}}/\mathrm{d}R=\infty
as well as cases where two NSs have the same radius. As discussed in detail in Ref.
Li et al. (2024)
, similarly diverse predictions for the
M(R)
curve have been made using different parametric meta-models, phenomenological models and/or microscopic theories. A changing sign of
\mathrm{d}M_{\mathrm{NS}}/\mathrm{d}R
in a large range of mass or radius is often associated with the onset of new degrees of freedom or phases (e.g., strange/quark stars with hyperons or quark deconfinement), while a consistently positive one is normally for nucleonic or hadronic NSs, see, e.g., (1) the 40435
M(R)
curves from using a meta-model by parameterizing the high-density EOS with piecewise polytropes
Ferreira and Providência (2024)
, (2) many samples of
M(R)
curves from using hybrid models coupling various hadronic EOSs through a first-order phase transition to quark matter EOSs characterized by different speeds of sound in studying the possible formation of twin stars
Han and Prakash (2020)
, and (3) many samples of
M(R)
curves with EOSs encapsulating quarkyonic matter in NSs
Zhao and Lattimer (2020)
.
The
10^{5}
EOSs we generated produce diverse
M(R)
curves with features similar to those predicted by the EOSs mentioned above. While we can not guarantee that our meta-model EOSs cover every single NS EOS that has ever been proposed, we are confident that they are diverse enough to mimic most if not all existing NS EOSs in the literature.

FIG. 28:
(Color Online). Left panel: the
\xi
-
\Pi_{\mathrm{c}}
correlation using meta-model EOSs consistent with observational/experimental constraints. The compactnesses for PSR J0740+6620
Riley et al. (2021)
, PSR J0030+0451
Riley et al. (2019)
and the NS in the X-ray burster GS 1826-24
Zhou et al. (2023)
are shown. The function
\mathrm{X}(\Pi_{\mathrm{c}})
is plotted by the dashed orange curve and the compactness for a typical canonical NS
Brandes et al. (2023)
;
Richter and Li (2023)
(with
R\approx 12_{-1}^{+1}\,\mathrm{km}
) by the hatched pink band.
Right panel: same as panel (a) but for the correlation
M_{\mathrm{NS}}
-
\Gamma_{\mathrm{c}}
, the lavender and pink bands in panel (a) and (b) represents their 68% confidence intervals (CIs).
Figures taken from Ref.
Cai and Li (2024)
.

FIG. 29:
(Color Online). The same as FIG.
28
but for the
R
-
\nu_{\mathrm{c}}
correlation using the meta-model EOSs.

To test the mass, radius and compact scalings of Eqs. (
4.7
), (
4.6
) and (
4.8
), we randomly generate
10^{5}
meta-model EOSs
Cai and Li (2024)
.
To explore the whole EOS parameter space currently allowed and present our results clearly, we select randomly one point on each mass-radius (M-R) curve from a given EOS (as shown in FIG.
27
) within the mass range of
1.2M_{\odot}\lesssim M_{\mathrm{NS}}\lesssim 2.2M_{\odot}
. The resulting scalings are shown in FIG.
28
and FIG.
29
; here the panel (a) of FIG.
28
shows the compactness-
\Pi_{\mathrm{c}}
scaling while the panel (b) is the mass-
\Gamma_{\mathrm{c}}
scaling. In each panel, for clarity only 500 representative samples are shown while
10^{5}
EOSs are used in calculating the scaling coefficients and their error bands.
The standard error (ste) and the coefficient of determination (the r-value) actually start converging quickly using about 300 samples.
In particular, the ste for the compactness-
\Pi_{\mathrm{c}}
(mass-
\Gamma_{\mathrm{c}}
, radius-
\nu_{\mathrm{c}}
) regression is about 0.03 (0.002
M_{\odot}
, 0.3 km) and the r-value for the
\xi
-
\Pi_{\mathrm{c}}
and
M_{\mathrm{NS}}
-
\Gamma_{\mathrm{c}}
regressions is about 0.97 while that for the
R
-
\nu_{\mathrm{c}}
is about 0.71
Cai and Li (2024)
.
The regression and its 68% confidence interval (CI) are shown in the figures. Quantitatively, we have

\displaystyle\xi\approx

\displaystyle A_{\xi}\Pi_{\mathrm{c}}+B_{\xi}\approx 2.31_{-0.03}^{+0.03}\Pi_{\mathrm{c}}-0.032_{-0.003}^{+0.003},

(4.66)

\displaystyle M_{\mathrm{NS}}/M_{\odot}\approx

\displaystyle A_{\mathrm{M}}+B_{\mathrm{M}}\approx 1242_{-15}^{+15}\left(\frac{\Gamma_{\mathrm{c}}}{\mathrm{fm}^{3/2}/\mathrm{MeV}^{1/2}}\right)-0.08_{-0.02}^{+0.02},

(4.67)

\displaystyle R/\mathrm{km}\approx

\displaystyle A_{\mathrm{R}}\nu_{\mathrm{c}}+B_{\mathrm{R}}\approx 572_{-25}^{+25}\left(\frac{\nu_{\mathrm{c}}}{\mathrm{fm}^{3/2}/\mathrm{MeV}^{1/2}}\right)+4.22_{-0.35}^{+0.35},

(4.68)

here
\Gamma_{\mathrm{c}}
,
\nu_{\mathrm{c}}
and
\Pi_{\mathrm{c}}
(which is dimensionless) are defined in Eqs. (
4.7
), (
4.6
) and (
4.8
), respectively.
We notice that dividing
R
by
M_{\mathrm{NS}}
in calculating the compactness
\xi
largely diminishes the relatively large uncertainty in the radius scaling, in particular the prediction
\xi=2\Pi_{\mathrm{c}}
of Eq. (
4.8
) (indicated by the blue dash-dotted line) is quite close to the numerical result.
Moreover, the ste of
R
about 0.3 km is much smaller than that from the current NS observations.
Since
\mathrm{X}
is limited to
\mathrm{X}\lesssim 0.374
, see Eq. (
4.18
), by causality applied in GR for strong-field gravity
Cai et al. (2023)
, we have from the scaling (
4.66
) that,

\boxed{\xi\lesssim 0.264_{-0.005}^{+0.005}\equiv\xi_{\mathrm{GR}},~~\mbox{for general stable NSs along M-R curve.}}

(4.69)

Obviously, this general limit for
\xi
holding for all stellar models is consistent with (and smaller than) the one given by (
6.32
) which is an upper limit for the NS compactness.
In the panel (a) of FIG.
28
, we show the compactnesses for PSR J0740+6620 and PSR J0030+0451 via NICER’s simultaneous mass-radius observation, namely
M_{\mathrm{NS}}/M_{\odot}\approx 2.08_{-0.07}^{+0.07}
and
R/\mathrm{km}\approx 12.39_{-0.98}^{+1.30}
(at 95% CI) for the former
Riley et al. (2021)
and
M_{\mathrm{NS}}/M_{\odot}\approx 1.34_{-0.16}^{+0.15}
and
R/\mathrm{km}\approx 12.71_{-1.19}^{+1.14}
(at 68% CI) for the latter
Riley et al. (2019)
, both indicated by the superscript “a”.
Shown also are the compactness
\xi\approx 0.183\sim 0.259
for the NS in GS 1826-24 directly from its surface gravitational red-shift measurement
Zhou et al. (2023)
and the
\xi
for a canonical NS with
R\approx 12_{-1}^{+1}\,\mathrm{km}

Brandes et al. (2023)
;
Richter and Li (2023)
. For a given
\xi
, one can directly obtain the
\Pi_{\mathrm{c}}
from their scaling and the
\mathrm{X}
via the function
\mathrm{X}(\Pi_{\mathrm{c}})
(orange dashed curve) defined in Eq. (
4.8
).
Similarly, the mass/radius bands for PSR J0740+6620
Fonseca et al. (2021)
and PSR J0030+0451
Riley et al. (2019)
are shown in the panel (b) of FIG.
28
and FIG.
29
, respectively.

It is necessary to point out here that as we include more terms in the expansions of
\widehat{\varepsilon}
,
\widehat{P}
and
\widehat{M}
, the relevant scalings should be modified accordingly.
For example, the NS mass scaling may read as

M_{\mathrm{NS}}\sim\frac{1}{\sqrt{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}}}\left(\frac{\mathrm{X}}{1+3\mathrm{X}^{2}+4\mathrm{X}}\right)^{3/2}\cdot\left(1+\kappa_{1}\mathrm{X}+\kappa_{2}\mathrm{X}^{2}+\cdots\right),

(4.70)

with the coefficients of high-order
\mathrm{X}
terms,
\kappa_{1}
,
\kappa_{2},\cdots
determined by the dimensionless TOV equations themselves, or fitting predictions of realistic NS EOSs within the traditional approach. By investigating the scaled TOV equations alone, we found in Ref.
Cai and Li (2024)
that the next-to-leading order correction for the mass is
\kappa_{1}=18/25
and the radius scaling still takes the present form of (
4.6
).
Consequently, at the next-to-leading order the NS compactness scales as:

\xi\sim\frac{\mathrm{X}}{1+3\mathrm{X}^{2}+4\mathrm{X}}\cdot\left(1+\frac{18}{25}\mathrm{X}\right).

(4.71)

Since the first correction term
18\mathrm{X}/25
is generally small especially since
\mathrm{X}\lesssim 0.374
(compared with 1) limited by causality, both the scalings (with or without the first correction) for
M_{\mathrm{NS}}
(and
\xi
) work well for realistic NSs. Therefore, the NS properties (central pressure, central energy density and the maximum compactness) extracted/inferred are very similar and qualitatively equivalent.
See Ref.
Cai and Li (2024)
for some illustrations and quantitative comparisons of the results from the two scaling schemes with or without the first correction
18\mathrm{X}/25
. In this review, we use the scalings of (
4.7
), (
4.6
) and (
4.8
) and focus on the information thus extracted. Nevertheless, we note that the high-order corrections might have appreciable effects on some properties and/or correlations. They may thus need to be considered for some analyses especially as the precision of astrophysical observations further improve. Fortunately, the formalism we presented here allows people to systematically incorporate them when it is necessary for various physics purposes.

FIG. 30:
(Color Online). Mass and radius scalings in the
M_{\mathrm{NS}}^{\max}/M_{\odot}
-analysis (using the NS masses and radii at the TOV configuration) and the
M_{\mathrm{NS}}/M_{\odot}
-analysis (considering the general stable NSs).

Next, we compare the mass and radius scalings for two cases: (1) the one based on NSs at the TOV configuration (denoted as the
M_{\mathrm{NS}}^{\max}/M_{\odot}
-analysis) and (2) the other using the general stable NSs along the M-R curve (denoted as the
M_{\mathrm{NS}}/M_{\odot}
-analysis).
The mass-
\Gamma_{\mathrm{c}}
and radius-
\nu_{\mathrm{c}}
scalings for the two cases are shown in FIG.
30
. As one expects, for a given NS mass depending on whether it is at the TOV configuration or not, the
\Gamma_{\mathrm{c}}
factor extracted from the two analyses are different, as shown in the left panel. Specifically, the one from the
M_{\mathrm{NS}}^{\max}/M_{\odot}
-analysis is smaller than that from analyzing the
M_{\mathrm{NS}}/M_{\odot}
-scaling.
Considering the factor
\Pi_{\mathrm{c}}
appearing in
\Gamma_{\mathrm{c}}=\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}^{-1/2}\Pi_{\mathrm{c}}^{3/2}
is a slow-varying function of
\mathrm{X}
, this means the central energy density
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}
extracted from the
M_{\mathrm{NS}}^{\max}/M_{\odot}
-analysis is necessarily larger than that from the
M_{\mathrm{NS}}/M_{\odot}
-analysis.
Similar phenomenon holds for the radius scalings above (on the right side of) the intersection point with
R_{\mathrm{inter}}\approx R\approx R_{\max}\approx 8.50\pm 0.96\,\mathrm{km}
(tomato diamond), as shown in the right panel of FIG.
30
.
However, below the intersection point (on its left side), we have
\nu_{\mathrm{c}}^{[\mathrm{TOV}]}>\nu_{\mathrm{c}}^{[\mathrm{G}]}
(with “G” abbreviating for generally stable NSs) for a given radius. This would be impossible for some critical radius
R_{\mathrm{crit}}<R_{\mathrm{inter}}
except
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}^{[\mathrm{TOV}]}<\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}^{[\mathrm{G}]}
. The latter condition is unphysical as the central energy density for the critically stable NS at TOV configuration should always be larger than that for a generally stable NS sharing the same radius. This then means that there exists a lower bound on NS radius (similar to the existence of an upper bound
M_{\mathrm{TOV}}
for NS mass).

-analysis.
