Central EOS for four NSs: PSR J0740+6620
Riley et al. (2021)
, PSR J0030+0451
Riley et al. (2019)
, PSR J0437-4715
Choudhury et al. (2024)
and a canonical NS, here
\mathrm{X}
is dimensionless and both
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}
and
P_{\mathrm{c}}
are measured in
\mathrm{MeV}/\mathrm{fm}^{3}
.

Once the mass or radius is known/observed for a NS, we can use the mass or radius scaling to determine the central EOS. This was already done using the
M_{\mathrm{NS}}^{\max}/M_{\odot}
-analysis for PSR J0740+6620 in Subsection
4.2
, see FIG.
17
.
We can also do this similarly by adopting the
M_{\mathrm{NS}}/M_{\odot}
-analysis assuming all the NSs are stable along the M-R curve (without limiting them to be at the TOV configuration), the result for the central EOS for PSR J0740+6620 is shown in the left panel of FIG.
31
, here the light-blue solid curve (associated with a tan band and indicated by “G (
M_{\mathrm{NS}}
)” and “G (
R
)”) and the light-blue dashed curve (associated with a light-blue band and indicated by “TOV (
M_{\mathrm{NS}}
)” and “TOV (
R
)”) are the predictions on the central EOS using the mass and radius scalings, respectively.
For comparisons, we also plot the central EOSs using the mass and radius scalings in the
M_{\mathrm{NS}}^{\max}/M_{\odot}
-analysis, by the solid magenta curve (associated with lavender hatched band) and the dashed magenta curve (associated with pink hatched band), respectively.
In both analyses, i.e., the
M_{\mathrm{NS}}^{\max}/M_{\odot}
-analysis and the
M_{\mathrm{NS}}/M_{\odot}
-analysis, the uncertainties from the radius-scaling on the central EOS are much larger than those from the mass scaling, comparing it with the left panel of FIG.
16
.
Moreover, if both the mass and radius are known/observed, we can then determine both
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}
and
P_{\mathrm{c}}
simultaneously. The results with error bars are shown by the shallow grey squares in FIG.
31
and the numerical values are given in TAB.
3
.
We see that the
P_{\mathrm{c}}
and
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}
for PSR J0030+0451 (in the
M_{\mathrm{NS}}/M_{\odot}
-analysis) are consistent with those obtained in Ref.
Brandes et al. (2023)
using a Bayesian inference method.
Another feature is that the inferred values for
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}
and
P_{\mathrm{c}}
in the
M_{\mathrm{NS}}/M_{\odot}
-analysis is smaller than those in the
M_{\mathrm{NS}}^{\max}/M_{\odot}
-analysis; as we discussed in the previous paragraphs regarding FIG.
30
.
The right panel of FIG.
31
shows the results similarly obtained for a canonical NS.
We may point out that for light NSs such as a canonical one, it is more appropriate to infer the central EOSs using the
M_{\mathrm{NS}}/M_{\odot}
-analysis since they are generally far from the TOV configurations predicted by using various EOSs and are far below the maximum NS mass observed so far. On the hand, for massive NSs, e.g. PSR J0740+6620 or the second component of GW190814 with a mass of about 2.59
M_{\odot}
, an inference using the
M_{\mathrm{NS}}^{\max}/M_{\odot}
-analysis is preferred.
In TAB.
3
, the central EOSs for PSR J0030+0451
Riley et al. (2019)
and PSR J0437-4715
Choudhury et al. (2024)
are also given.

Since the radius scaling in the general
M_{\mathrm{NS}}/M_{\odot}
-analysis has quite a non-perfect regression feature (as indicated by FIG.
29
), the prediction on
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}
and
P_{\mathrm{c}}
has much larger uncertainties.
In order to improve the prediction on
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}
and
P_{\mathrm{c}}
, we may use the combination of the mass scaling and the compactness scaling, since both are very strong and model-independent (see FIG.
28
).
In particular, we can obtain
\mathrm{X}
straightforwardly from
\xi=A_{\xi}\Pi_{\mathrm{c}}+B_{\xi}
since now
\xi
is available, after having
\mathrm{X}
, we then calculate the central energy density by

\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}=\left(\frac{\xi-B_{\xi}}{A_{\xi}}\right)^{3}\left(\frac{A_{\mathrm{M}}}{M_{\mathrm{NS}}/M_{\odot}-B_{\mathrm{M}}}\right)^{2}=\underbrace{\left(\frac{\mathrm{X}}{1+3\mathrm{X}^{2}+4\mathrm{X}}\right)^{3}}_{\mbox{upper bounded}}\left(\frac{A_{\mathrm{M}}}{M_{\mathrm{NS}}/M_{\odot}-B_{\mathrm{M}}}\right)^{2},

(4.72)

and consequently the central pressure
P_{\mathrm{c}}=\mathrm{X}\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}
.
The resulted values for
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}
and
P_{\mathrm{c}}
are shown in FIG.
31
by the orange diamonds and also in TAB.
3
in the last four rows.
We can see that the prediction is very close to the value using the mass and radius scalings, but has a much smaller errorbar.

4.6 Physics behind the slopes of NS mass-radius curves and their approximately “vertical” shapes

The factor
\Psi
defined in Eq. (
4.16
) is an important quantity characterizing the generally stable NSs with masses
M_{\mathrm{NS}}
.
Using the meta-model EOSs, one can similarly investigate quantitatively such dependence; the result is shown in FIG.
32
.
Specifically, we have

\Psi\approx a_{\Psi}\left(\frac{M_{\mathrm{NS}}}{M_{\odot}}\right)+b_{\Psi}\approx-1.62_{-0.13}^{+0.13}\left(\frac{M_{\mathrm{NS}}}{M_{\odot}}\right)+5.12_{-0.22}^{+0.22}.

(4.73)

The
M_{\mathrm{NS}}/M_{\odot}
-dependence of the
\Psi
-factor is expected to work better at low NS masses than high masses since for NSs with large
M_{\mathrm{NS}}/M_{\odot}
, they may depend on the (unknown value of)
M_{\mathrm{TOV}}
.
For example, for a model (theory) with
M_{\mathrm{TOV}}\approx 2.3M_{\odot}
, the
\Psi
-factor for a NS with mass
\lesssim 2.3M_{\odot}
is vanishingly small.
For a canonical NS, for example, we have

\Psi\approx 2.85\pm 0.29
.

FIG. 32:
(Color Online). The same as FIG.
29
but for the
\Psi
dependence on NS mass
M_{\mathrm{NS}}
.

FIG. 33:
(Color Online). Inferred NS M-R relations using contemporary astrophysical constraints.
Figures taken from Ref.
Jiang et al. (2023)
(left panel) and from Ref.
Fujimoto et al. (2024)
(right panel).

The
\Psi
-
M_{\mathrm{NS}}
scaling of FIG.
32
implies that the
M_{\mathrm{NS}}
increases with the central energy density
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}
according to an approximately power low (by treating
\Psi\approx\mathrm{const.}
):

\boxed{M_{\mathrm{NS}}/M_{\odot}\sim\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}^{\Psi/2}.}

(4.74)

Therefore, mathematically the case with
\Psi>2
implies a super-linear growth while that with
\Psi<2
indicates a sub-linear growth.
The critical NS mass occurring at
\Psi_{0}=2
is
M^{0}_{\mathrm{NS}}/M_{\odot}\approx 1.93\pm 0.21
.
For canonical NSs, the mass growth rate is about:

\mbox{at }M_{\mathrm{NS}}\approx 1.4M_{\odot}:~~M_{\mathrm{NS}}/M_{\odot}\sim\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}^{1.43\pm 0.14},

(4.75)

while the growth rate of their radii is given by Eq. (
4.20
):

\mbox{at }M_{\mathrm{NS}}\approx 1.4M_{\odot}:~~R\sim\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}^{0.14\pm 0.05}.

(4.76)

According to Eq. (
4.19
),
\mathrm{d}R/\mathrm{d}\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}=\mathrm{d}R/\mathrm{d}M_{\mathrm{NS}}\cdot\mathrm{d}M_{\mathrm{NS}}/\mathrm{d}\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}
, so
\mathrm{d}R/\mathrm{d}M_{\mathrm{NS}}=\mathrm{d}R/\mathrm{d}\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}\cdot(\mathrm{d}M_{\mathrm{NS}}/\mathrm{d}\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}})^{-1}
, or

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}R}{\mathrm{d}M_{\mathrm{NS}}}=\mathrm{const.}\times\left(1-\frac{2}{\Psi}\right)\cdot\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}^{-3^{-1}(1+\Psi)}=\mathrm{const.}\times\left(1-\frac{2}{\Psi}\right)\cdot\left(\frac{M_{\mathrm{NS}}}{M_{\odot}}\right)^{-(2/3)(1+\Psi^{-1})},

(4.77)

where the second relation follows from
M_{\mathrm{NS}}/M_{\odot}\sim\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}^{\Psi/2}
.
We then find for NSs with
\Psi\approx\Psi_{0}=2
or equivalently
M^{0}_{\mathrm{NS}}/M_{\odot}\approx 1.93\pm 0.21
, the dependence of
R
on
M_{\mathrm{NS}}
is weak, i.e.,
\mathrm{d}R/\mathrm{d}M_{\mathrm{NS}}\approx 0
(similarly
\mathrm{d}R/\mathrm{d}\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}\sim\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}^{-0.01\pm 0.04}
using Eq. (
4.20
)), or
\mathrm{d}M_{\mathrm{NS}}/\mathrm{d}R\to+\infty
for
M_{\mathrm{NS}}\to M_{\mathrm{NS}}^{0}
from below and
\mathrm{d}M_{\mathrm{NS}}/\mathrm{d}R\to-\infty
for
M_{\mathrm{NS}}\to M_{\mathrm{NS}}^{0}
from above.
This explains the empirical “vertical” shape seen in NS M-R sequences in both observations and many calculations, e.g., see the right panel
Fujimoto et al. (2024)
of FIG.
33
or meta-model predictions shown in FIG.
27
.
In addition, as
\Psi>2
for light NSs, Eq. (
4.77
) implies
\mathrm{d}R/\mathrm{d}M_{\mathrm{NS}}>0
; this means the “vertical” shape is slightly slanted from the lower-left corner to the upper-right corner, see a typical inference on the M-R curve shown in the left panel of FIG.
33
.
In fact, the compilation of NS observational data shown in FIG.
5
also indicates such empirical fact. In short, our analyses above provide for the first time an EOS-model independent explanation for a well-known phenomenon in NS physics from analyzing the scaled TOV equations.

Combined with Eq. (
4.73
), we can obtain the NS mass dependence factor
(1-2\Psi^{-1})^{-1}(M_{\mathrm{NS}}/M_{\odot})^{(2/3)(1+\Psi^{-1})}
in its derivative
\mathrm{d}M_{\mathrm{NS}}/\mathrm{d}R
with respect to
R
according to Eq. (
4.77
), see FIG.
34
. Our analysis implies that the
\mathrm{d}M_{\mathrm{NS}}/\mathrm{d}R
probably changes signs along the full M-R curve, see related discussions on this issue in Refs.
Han and Prakash (2020)
;
Zhao and Lattimer (2020)
;
Ferreira and Providência (2024)
;
Li et al. (2024)
.
Similarly, using Eq. (
4.20
), we have:

\boxed{{\mathrm{d}R}/{\mathrm{d}M_{\mathrm{NS}}}\sim\left(1-2\Psi^{-1}\right)\cdot R^{-2(1+\Psi^{-1})/(1-2\Psi^{-1})},}

(4.78)

or,

\boxed{{\mathrm{d}R}/{\mathrm{d}M_{\mathrm{NS}}}\approx 3^{-1}\xi^{-1}\left(1-2\Psi^{-1}\right),}

(4.79)

considering
\xi=M_{\mathrm{NS}}/R
.
Therefore, the
\mathrm{d}M_{\mathrm{NS}}/\mathrm{d}R\approx 3\xi(1-2\Psi^{-1})^{-1}
may either be positive or negative although
\xi=M_{\mathrm{NS}}/R
is always positive. This feature is similar to the relation between
\mathrm{d}P/\mathrm{d}\varepsilon=s^{2}
(non-monotonic) and
P/\varepsilon=\phi
(monotonic).
In particular, for canonical NSs with
R\approx 12\pm 1\,\mathrm{km}
, we have
\mathrm{d}M_{\mathrm{NS}}/\mathrm{d}R\approx 1.73\pm 0.44
using
\Psi\approx 2.85\pm 0.29
obtained previously.

FIG. 34:
(Color Online). Dependence of
(1-2\Psi^{-1})^{-1}(M_{\mathrm{NS}}/M_{\odot})^{(2/3)(1+\Psi^{-1})}\sim\mathrm{d}M_{\mathrm{NS}}/\mathrm{d}R
on NS mass using Eq. (
4.73
).

The factor
\Psi
is also very relevant for estimating the central SSS of generally stable NSs via Eq. (
4.15
), we give in Subsection
5.6
the central SSS of a canonical NS and compare it with existing results in the literature.

4.7 A counter-intuitive feature of NS mass scaling

In this and the next subsections, we use our scalings as a tool to explore why and how a maximum NS mass emerges naturally in GR. The mass scaling of Eq. (
4.67
) has a very important (though counter-intuitive) feature: a heavier NS has a lower upper limit for the central energy density. It is a representation or reflection of the self-gravitating nature of compact stars.
Our scaling of (
4.67
) reveals such novel feature naturally.
According to Eq. (
4.72
) where
\Pi_{\mathrm{c}}=\mathrm{X}/(1+3\mathrm{X}^{2}+4\mathrm{X})
is upper bounded to about
0.002
at
\mathrm{X}\approx 0.374
, we have then

\boxed{\mathrm{Y}\equiv\frac{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}}{\varepsilon_{0}}\lesssim\frac{21.71}{(M_{\mathrm{NS}}/M_{\odot}+0.08)^{2}}\equiv\mathrm{Y}_{+}\sim M_{\mathrm{NS}}^{-2},~~\mbox{under }\mathrm{X}\lesssim 0.374\leftrightarrow\Delta_{\mathrm{c}}\gtrsim\Delta_{\mathrm{GR}}\approx-0.041,}

(4.80)

here
\varepsilon_{0}\approx 150\,\mathrm{MeV}/\mathrm{fm}^{3}
is the energy density at nuclear saturation density.
For example, if one takes
\mathrm{Y}\lesssim 2.3
for
M_{\mathrm{NS}}/M_{\odot}\approx 3
, does it mean a
3M_{\odot}
NS has a central density smaller than about
2.3\rho_{0}
? This looks unreasonable.
Therefore, on the contrary, the mass-
\Gamma_{\mathrm{c}}
scaling indicates that there exists an upper bound for NS masses.
The factor
\Pi_{\mathrm{c}}
in Eq. (
4.72
) is crucial for upper bounding
\mathrm{Y}
. If we neglect the GR correction term “
3\mathrm{X}^{2}+4\mathrm{X}
”, then
\Pi_{\mathrm{c}}^{\mathrm{N},3}=\mathrm{X}^{3}\approx 0.42
(here “N” is for Newtonian limit and
\mathrm{X}
is upper limited as
\mathrm{X}\leq 3/4
). Consequently,
\Pi_{\mathrm{c}}^{\mathrm{N},3}/\Pi_{\mathrm{c}}^{3}\approx 200
.
In fact, the inverse scaling between
\mathrm{Y}
and NS mass is not new, e.g., Ref.
Lattimer and Prakash (2010)
constrained the upper limit of
\mathrm{Y}
according to the following relation
Lattimer (2012)

\mathrm{Y}\lesssim 51\left({M_{\mathrm{NS}}}/{M_{\odot}}\right)^{-2},

(4.81)

see Eq. (
6.43
).
In such case, assuming the existence of a
3M_{\odot}
NS makes the
\mathrm{Y}
upper bounded as
\mathrm{Y}\lesssim 5.7
, which looks quite reasonable.
However, the
M_{\mathrm{TOV}}
being smaller than
3M_{\odot}
is the current community consensus.
On the other hand, (
4.81
) induces a weak restriction on
\mathrm{Y}
as
\mathrm{Y}\lesssim 9.6
for
M_{\mathrm{NS}}^{\max}/M_{\odot}\approx 2.3
.
Our expression (
4.80
) gives a tight bound on
\mathrm{Y}
considering the upper limit of NS masses.
The upper limit on
\mathrm{Y}
is equivalent to a lower limit (denoted by
R_{-}
) on the radius
R
, since from our compactness scaling we have

\boxed{R/\mathrm{km}=\frac{1.477M_{\mathrm{NS}}/M_{\odot}}{A_{\xi}\Pi_{\mathrm{c}}+B_{\xi}}\gtrsim 5.58M_{\mathrm{NS}}/M_{\odot},}

(4.82)

using the upper bounded nature of
\Pi_{\mathrm{c}}
.
Therefore we establish that

\mathrm{Y}_{+}\leftrightarrow R_{-},

(4.83)

this is shown in FIG.
35
.

FIG. 35:
(Color Online). NS mass scaling implies that for a given mass
M_{\mathrm{NS}}
there exists an upper limit
\mathrm{Y}_{+}
for the central energy density, roughly scaling as
\mathrm{Y}_{+}\sim M_{\mathrm{NS}}^{-2}
, so massive NSs allow a lower upper limit for
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}
.
On the other hand, the
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}
increases with
M_{\mathrm{NS}}
due to the stability condition
\mathrm{d}M_{\mathrm{NS}}/\mathrm{d}\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}>0
, indicating a lower limit
\mathrm{Y}_{-}
exists for a given
M_{\mathrm{NS}}
.
Generally, one has
\mathrm{Y}_{-}<\mathrm{Y}_{+}
; and the difference
\mathrm{Y}_{+}-\mathrm{Y}_{-}
decreases with
M_{\mathrm{NS}}
.
If the NS mass
M_{\mathrm{NS}}
exceeds some critical value, then the relation between
\mathrm{Y}_{-}
and
\mathrm{Y}_{+}
would be inverted, implying the existence of a maximum NS mass.

On the other hand, for stable NSs, the derivative of NS mass with respect to the central (energy) density is positive
Hartle (1978)
;
Shapiro and Teukolsky (1983)
,

\boxed{\mathrm{d}M_{\mathrm{NS}}/\mathrm{d}\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}>0\leftrightarrow\mathrm{d}\mathrm{Y}/\mathrm{d}(M_{\mathrm{NS}}/M_{\odot})>0,~~\mbox{for stable NSs along the M-R curve},}

(4.84)

see for example FIG.
36
for a recent inference on such relation using contemporary astrophysical observations.
This means there exists a lower limit
\mathrm{Y}_{-}
on the central energy density for a given NS mass, e.g., we have
\mathrm{Y}_{-}\approx 3.5
from FIG.
36
for a
2M_{\odot}
NS like PSR J0740+6620.
For a given
M_{\mathrm{NS}}
, our mass and compactness scalings enable us to write out explicitly that,

\boxed{R/\mathrm{km}=\frac{\Sigma M_{\mathrm{NS}}/M_{\odot}}{\displaystyle A_{\xi}\left(\frac{M_{\mathrm{NS}}/M_{\odot}-B_{\mathrm{M}}}{A_{\mathrm{M}}}\sqrt{\mathrm{Y}\varepsilon_{0}}\right)^{2/3}+B_{\xi}}\approx\frac{1.477M_{\mathrm{NS}}/M_{\odot}}{0.106(M_{\mathrm{NS}}/M_{\odot}+0.08)^{2/3}\mathrm{Y}^{1/3}-0.032},}

(4.85)

here
\Sigma\equiv M_{\odot}/\mathrm{km}\approx 1.477
.
Thus, a lower limit
\mathrm{Y}_{-}
corresponds to an upper limit
R_{+}
for the radius, i.e.,

\mathrm{Y}_{-}\leftrightarrow R_{+},

(4.86)

we also indicate this relation in FIG.
35
.

FIG. 36:
(Color Online). The central density as a function of NS mass, where the magenta (red)
contours in the left (right) panel show the 50% and 90% confidence level for the mass-central density posterior for PSR J0740+6620 (for the maximum-mass NS). Figure taken from Ref.
Legred et al. (2021)
.

FIG. 37:
(Color Online). An illustration on using
\mathrm{Y}\lesssim\mathrm{Y}_{+}
of Eq. (
4.80
) to estimate some upper limit of
M_{\mathrm{NS}}
; here three
\mathrm{Y}_{-}
’s are used (grey lines for
\mathrm{Y}_{-}=3
, 4 and 5).
The effects of upper bound on
\mathrm{X}
(either
\mathrm{X}\lesssim 0.374
or
\mathrm{X}\lesssim 1/3
) are also shown.
The light-tan band shows the effective region for allowed NS masses in the case of
\mathrm{Y}_{-}\approx 4
and
\mathrm{X}\lesssim 0.374
.

Considering all of the above in this subsection, we come to the following concluding points: (a) there should physically be an overlapped region for
\mathrm{Y}
as
\mathrm{Y}_{-}\lesssim\mathrm{Y}\lesssim\mathrm{Y}+
, and if
M_{\mathrm{NS}}/M_{\odot}
exceeds some critical value no such overlapped region exists, this critical NS mass is an indication for the existence of
M_{\mathrm{TOV}}
; and (b) for NSs with masses smaller than the above critical value, relations (
4.83
) and (
4.86
) together set an effective region for the radius.
For example, in FIG.
37
we illustrate how to use
\mathrm{Y}\lesssim\mathrm{Y}_{+}
of Eq. (
4.80
) to estimate some upper limits of
M_{\mathrm{NS}}
adopting three
\mathrm{Y}_{-}
’s (shown by grey lines); e.g., adopting
\mathrm{Y}_{-}\approx 4
gives
M_{\mathrm{NS}}/M_{\odot}\lesssim 2.25
while using
\mathrm{Y}_{-}\approx 5
we obtain
M_{\mathrm{NS}}/M_{\odot}\lesssim 2.00
.
It is necessary to point out that these estimates are direct consequences of the self-gravitating nature of NSs considered in GR and the upper limit for
\mathrm{X}
as
\mathrm{X}\lesssim 0.374
as well as the upper limit for
\Pi_{\mathrm{c}}
correspondingly, as implied in Eq. (
4.72
).
If one requires
\mathrm{X}\lesssim 1/3
as predicted by pQCD at very high densities
Kurkela et al. (2010)
;
Bjorken (1983)
, then Eq. (
4.80
) should be replaced by
\mathrm{Y}_{+}\approx 20.09/(M_{\mathrm{NS}}/M_{\odot}+0.08)^{2}
.
In this case,
\mathrm{Y}_{-}\approx 4
gives
M_{\mathrm{NS}}/M_{\odot}\lesssim 2.16
, since a smaller upper bound on
\mathrm{X}
implies the NS is less compact
Cai and Li (2024)
.
In the next subsection (Subsection
4.8
), we may use all the mass, radius and compactness scalings explicitly to estimate the maximum mass of stable NSs.

Moreover, the above scheme for restricting the radius becomes more effective for massive NSs than for light ones, since for the latter the distance between
\mathrm{Y}_{-}(\leftrightarrow R_{+})
and
\mathrm{Y}_{+}(\leftrightarrow R_{-})
are quite large.
For example, for a canonical NS,
\mathrm{Y}_{+}\approx 9.9
using relation (
4.80
) while the lower limit is around
\mathrm{Y}_{-}\approx 2.5
(see FIG.
36
).
Therefore (
4.82
) and (
4.85
) together give us
R_{-}\approx 7.8\,\mathrm{km}\lesssim R\lesssim R_{+}\approx 13.4\,\mathrm{km}
, which is not quite useful.
On the other hand, the lower limit
\mathrm{Y}_{-}
for a
2.1M_{\odot}
NS (such as PSR J0740+6620
Fonseca et al. (2021)
) from FIG.
36
is around about
\mathrm{Y}_{-}\approx 3.7
while the upper limit
\mathrm{Y}_{+}
from (
4.80
) is about
\mathrm{Y}_{+}\approx 4.6
, so in this case one can effectively restrict the
\mathrm{Y}
and similarly the radius
R
. In particular, we have
11.7\,\mathrm{km}\lesssim R\lesssim 12.7\,\mathrm{km}
, which is quite close to the observed radius
Miller et al. (2021)
;
Riley et al. (2021)
;
Dittmann et al. (2024)
;
Salmi et al. (2022)
;
Salmi et al. (2024)
.
Similarly, considering PSR J2215+5135 with its mass about
2.15\,M_{\odot}
, we may obtain
12.0\,\mathrm{km}\lesssim R\lesssim 12.8\,\mathrm{km}
by adopting a similar lower limit
\mathrm{Y}_{-}\approx 3.7
.
However, the estimate on the lower limit
\mathrm{Y}_{-}
in this scheme itself is non-trivial.

FIG. 38:
(Color Online). Upper panel: influence of a finite
\Psi\neq 0
on the lower limit for
R
(compare the two bands); in each band the solid line is for
s^{2}\leq 1
and the dashed line is for
s^{2}\lesssim 0.781

Hippert et al. (2024)
; the inset plots the upper bound for
\xi
as a function of
M_{\mathrm{NS}}/M_{\odot}
(notice the solid black limit is given by Eq. (
4.69
)); two symbols on the upper band are the radii for
M_{\mathrm{NS}}/M_{\odot}\approx 2
.
Lower panel: the impact of a lower upper limit for
s^{2}
as
s^{2}\lesssim 0.781

Hippert et al. (2024)
on the NS M-R curve. Figure taken from Ref.
Hippert et al. (2024)
.

In obtaining (
4.82
) the
\mathrm{X}\lesssim 0.374
(with
\Psi=0
) is used, i.e.,
\xi\approx A_{\xi}\Pi_{\mathrm{c}}+B_{\xi}\lesssim 0.264
(of Eq. (
4.69
)) in the denominator is obtained at
\mathrm{X}\lesssim 0.374
. A finite
\Psi
of Eq. (
4.16
) can be used to improve the upper limit for
\xi
as a function of
M_{\mathrm{NS}}/M_{\odot}
since now
\mathrm{X}
is upper bounded to a lower value compared with
0.374
, and consequently the lower limit for
R
for a given
M_{\mathrm{NS}}/M_{\odot}
.
The result is shown in the upper panel of FIG.
38
, here the two bands (pink and light-tan) are obtained with
\Psi=0
and
\Psi\neq 0
(of Eq. (
4.16
)), respectively.
In each band, the solid (dashed) line is for
s^{2}\leq 1
(
s^{2}\lesssim 0.781
); here the upper bound on
s^{2}
as
s^{2}\lesssim 0.781
was given recently by considering nuclear matter transport constraints
Hippert et al. (2024)
.
A smaller upper bound on
s^{2}
corresponds to a softer EOS (and a smaller compactness as indicated in the inset), which may reduce the maximum NS mass (lower panel of FIG.
38
) and also generates a relatively larger
R
for a given
M_{\mathrm{NS}}
.
For example, for a NS with
M_{\mathrm{NS}}/M_{\odot}\approx 2
, the set with
\Psi\neq 0
and
s^{2}\lesssim 0.781
constrains
R\gtrsim 13.2\,\mathrm{km}
while that with
\Psi\neq 0
and
s^{2}\leq 1
gives
R\gtrsim 12.4\,\mathrm{km}
, inducing an effect about 0.8 km (see the two symbols on the upper band).
Future high-precision NSs radius measurements may help determine whether the
s^{2}
should be upper bounded to a lower value compared with 1.
As a comparison, it is seen that the smaller upper bound on
s^{2}
as
s^{2}\lesssim 0.781
almost has no impact on the radii of NSs with masses around
2M_{\odot}

Hippert et al. (2024)
as shown in the lower panel of FIG.
38
; this is different from our analysis.
Notice in their analysis
Hippert et al. (2024)
, a constant SSS model near NS centers is adopted.
In addition, in our calculations with
\Psi=0
(TOV configuration), the upper bound for
\xi
is independent of
M_{\mathrm{NS}}/M_{\odot}
(see the two thin lines in the inset).
For reference, we write out
\mathrm{X}
as a function
s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}
:

\displaystyle\mathrm{X}(s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2},\Psi)\approx

\displaystyle\frac{3s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}}{4+\Psi}\left[1-\frac{12(1+\Psi)}{(4+\Psi)^{2}}s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}+\frac{18(1+\Psi)(4+13\Psi)}{(4+\Psi)^{4}}s_{\mathrm{c}}^{4}+\cdots\right]

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle\frac{3s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}}{4}\left(1-\frac{3}{4}s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}+\frac{9}{32}s_{\mathrm{c}}^{4}+\cdots\right),

(4.87)

where the second relation follows from
\Psi=0
.
A similar derivation for
\mathcal{M}(s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2},\Psi)
defined in Eq. (
4.21
) was given previously.
Similarly, the function
\Pi_{\mathrm{c}}
of Eq. (
4.8
) becomes

\displaystyle\Pi_{\mathrm{c}}(s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2},\Psi)\approx

\displaystyle\frac{3s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}}{4+\Psi}\left[1-\frac{12(5+2\Psi)}{(4+\Psi)^{2}}s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}+\frac{9(344+298\Psi+71\Psi^{2})}{(4+\Psi)^{4}}s_{\mathrm{c}}^{4}+\cdots\right]

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle\frac{3s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}}{4}\left(1-\frac{15}{4}s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}+\frac{387}{32}s_{\mathrm{c}}^{4}+\cdots\right).

(4.88)

Reducing on the upper limit for
s^{2}
(and therefore on
s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}
) naturally leads to the reduction on
\mathrm{X}
or
\Pi_{\mathrm{c}}
, so the compactness
\xi\sim\Pi_{\mathrm{c}}
is also reduced; the NSs then become less compact (larger
R
for a given
M_{\mathrm{NS}}
).

To this end, we point out that in these analyses the radius scaling itself dose not come into play (either (
4.80
) or (
4.82
)), although it contains extra information that the mass and compactness scalings do not have. It is encapsulated in the fitting coefficient
B_{\mathrm{R}}
, its effects will be studied in the next subsection.

4.8 Maximum masses of generally and critically stable NSs along M-R curves

In the previous subsections, we used together two of the three (mass, radius and the compactness) scalings to evaluate individually the
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}
and
P_{\mathrm{c}}
or the compactness scaling alone to infer the
\mathrm{X}
.
At first glance, it seems that only two of the three scalings are independent. However, from the viewpoint of the numerical fitting of the scalings, using the three scalings as a whole could provide more information.
It could be understood as follows: Since
B_{\mathrm{M}}\approx-0.08
(see Eq. (
4.67
)) is near zero, when we investigate the compactness scaling via
\xi=M_{\mathrm{NS}}/R
the corresponding intercept
B_{\xi}\approx-0.032
(see Eq. (
4.66
)) is also found to be near zero (as it should be). It means that effect of the two coefficients
A_{\mathrm{R}}
and
B_{\mathrm{R}}
(see Eq. (
4.68
)) in the radius scaling is partially transferred into the coefficient
A_{\xi}
in the compactness scaling, or, equivalently the effect of
B_{\mathrm{R}}
is lost when considering the compactness scaling.
Breaking the degeneracy in these combinations may provide us extra information on some relevant quantities.

How to understand that
M_{\mathrm{NS}}
is upper bounded?
In this subsection, we use the full information contained in the mass, radius and compactness scalings, especially
A_{\mathrm{R}}
and
B_{\mathrm{R}}
, to estimate the maximum NS mass allowed. As discussed briefly in the last subsection, NSs unavoidably have a maximum mass due to two competing factors: the stability condition (
4.84
) requires
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}
increase with
M_{\mathrm{NS}}
while the self-gravitating property of NSs encapsulated in the mass scaling (see Eq. (
4.80
)) requires (the upper bound for)
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}
be inversely proportional to
M_{\mathrm{NS}}
roughly as
M_{\mathrm{NS}}^{-2}
, see the sketch given in FIG.
35
.
The counter-intuitive feature of the mass scaling can be traced back to the bound structure of
\Pi_{\mathrm{c}}^{3}
in Eq. (
4.72
) which is an intrinsic property of GR. The overlapped region satisfying both conditions on
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}
eventually shrinks, even to disappear at a critical NS mass (the maximum NS mass allowed)
\gtrsim 2.2M_{\odot}
(see FIG.
36
).

FIG. 39:
(Color Online). The lower limit of NS radius
R
linearly increases with
M_{\mathrm{NS}}/M_{\odot}
according to Eq. (
4.89
), while the radius
R
is upper bounded as shown by Eq. (
4.91
); these two facts on
R
imply that there exists a maximum NS mass allowed.

How can we estimate an upper limit for
M_{\mathrm{NS}}
?
Firstly, we obtain the following condition by combining the mass and radius scalings of Eqs. (
4.67
) and (
4.68
),

\boxed{R/\mathrm{km}\gtrsim 3.59M_{\mathrm{NS}}/M_{\odot}+4.51,}

(4.89)

this is similar to the Eq. (
4.82
) of
R/\mathrm{km}\gtrsim 5.58M_{\mathrm{NS}}/M_{\odot}
obtained via the compactness scaling of Eq. (
4.66
) alone.
Therefore, the lower limit of
R
is linearly proportional to NS mass
M_{\mathrm{NS}}/M_{\odot}
.
Next, combining all the three scalings of mass, radius and compactness enable us to write out,

R/\mathrm{km}=\frac{(A_{\mathrm{M}}B_{\mathrm{R}}\Pi_{\mathrm{c}}-A_{\mathrm{R}}B_{\mathrm{M}})\Sigma}{(A_{\mathrm{M}}\Sigma-A_{\xi}A_{\mathrm{R}})\Pi_{\mathrm{c}}-A_{\mathrm{R}}B_{\xi}},

(4.90)

here
\Sigma=M_{\odot}/\mathrm{km}
.
This expression for
R
implies that it is upper bounded.
Specifically, since
|A_{\mathrm{R}}B_{\mathrm{M}}|\ll|A_{\mathrm{M}}B_{\mathrm{R}}\Pi_{\mathrm{c}}|
and
|A_{\mathrm{R}}B_{\xi}|\ll|(A_{\mathrm{M}}\Sigma-A_{\xi}A_{\mathrm{R}})\Pi_{\mathrm{c}}|
, we could approximate (
4.90
) without loosing its main features:

\displaystyle\frac{R\Sigma^{-1}}{\mathrm{km}}\approx

\displaystyle\underbrace{\frac{A_{\mathrm{M}}B_{\mathrm{R}}}{A_{\mathrm{M}}\Sigma-A_{\xi}A_{\mathrm{R}}}}_{\mbox{no $\Pi_{\mathrm{c}}$ factor}}\times\Bigg[1+\underbrace{\overbrace{\left(\frac{A_{\mathrm{R}}B_{\xi}}{A_{\mathrm{M}}\Sigma-A_{\xi}A_{\mathrm{R}}}-\frac{A_{\mathrm{R}}B_{\mathrm{M}}}{A_{\mathrm{M}}B_{\mathrm{R}}}\right)}^{\mbox{negative:$-0.027\pm 0.007$}}\frac{1}{\Pi_{\mathrm{c}}}}_{\mbox{upper bounded}}+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{\Pi_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}}\right)\Bigg].

(4.91)

Since

\left(\frac{A_{\mathrm{R}}B_{\xi}}{A_{\mathrm{M}}\Sigma-A_{\xi}A_{\mathrm{R}}}-\frac{A_{\mathrm{R}}B_{\mathrm{M}}}{A_{\mathrm{M}}B_{\mathrm{R}}}\right)<0,

(4.92)

and
\Pi_{\mathrm{c}}^{-1}
is lower bounded at
\mathrm{X}\approx 0.374
, the
R\Sigma^{-1}/\mathrm{km}
is therefore upper bounded according to (
4.91
).
Neglecting the correction in the square bracket of (
4.91
), we have approximately the following upper bound

\boxed{\frac{R\Sigma^{-1}}{\mathrm{km}}\approx\frac{A_{\mathrm{M}}B_{\mathrm{R}}}{A_{\mathrm{M}}\Sigma-A_{\xi}A_{\mathrm{R}}}.}

(4.93)

These two facts — the lower limit of
R
linearly increases with
M_{\mathrm{NS}}/M_{\odot}
according to the Eq. (
4.89
) while
R
is upper bounded by the Eq. (
4.91
) — together imply that
M_{\mathrm{NS}}/M_{\odot}
is upper bounded, i.e., the
M_{\mathrm{NS}}
could not increase without a limit,
see FIG.
39
.
The Eq. (
4.91
) also explains the empirical finding that the radii of NSs with
M_{\mathrm{NS}}/M_{\odot}\approx 1.8\pm 0.4
share similar values, i.e., the “vertical” shape on the M-R curve (see FIG.
33
, Eq. (
4.77
) and the discussions around it earlier).
Similarly, we have

\boxed{\left(\frac{M_{\mathrm{NS}}}{M_{\odot}}\right)=\frac{(A_{\xi}\Pi_{\mathrm{c}}+B_{\xi})(A_{\mathrm{M}}B_{\mathrm{R}}\Pi_{\mathrm{c}}-A_{\mathrm{R}}B_{\mathrm{M}})}{(A_{\mathrm{M}}\Sigma-A_{\xi}A_{\mathrm{R}})\Pi_{\mathrm{c}}-A_{\mathrm{R}}B_{\xi}}\approx\frac{A_{\xi}A_{\mathrm{M}}B_{\mathrm{R}}}{A_{\mathrm{M}}\Sigma-A_{\xi}A_{\mathrm{R}}}\Pi_{\mathrm{c}}\approx\left(\frac{R\Sigma^{-1}}{\mathrm{km}}\right)A_{\xi}\Pi_{\mathrm{c}},}

(4.94)

here the second approximation follows under
B_{\mathrm{M}}\approx B_{\xi}\approx 0
,
|A_{\mathrm{R}}B_{\mathrm{M}}|\ll|A_{\mathrm{M}}B_{\mathrm{R}}\Pi_{\mathrm{c}}|
as well as
|A_{\mathrm{R}}B_{\xi}|\ll|(A_{\mathrm{M}}\Sigma-A_{\xi}A_{\mathrm{R}})\Pi_{\mathrm{c}}|
.
We see from Eq. (
4.93
) or Eq. (
4.94
) that the coefficients
A_{\mathrm{R}}
and
B_{\mathrm{R}}
(of Eq. (
4.68
)) appear independently in the NS radius or mass expression. Therefore, the
B_{\mathrm{R}}
is very relevant for estimating
R
or the upper limit of
M_{\mathrm{NS}}
(since the factor
\Pi_{\mathrm{c}}
is upper bounded).
Using the full expression of Eq. (
4.94
) for NS mass and Eq. (
4.90
) for the radius, we finally obtain the following two limits for NS masses and radii

\boxed{M_{\mathrm{NS}}/M_{\odot}\lesssim 2.26\pm 0.28,~~R/\mathrm{km}\lesssim 12.62\pm 1.51.}

(4.95)

In the left panel of FIG.
40
, we show the
\mathrm{X}
-dependence of
M_{\mathrm{NS}}/M_{\odot}
of Eq. (
4.94
) by the solid orange curve, here the solid magenta pentagon is the termination point at
\mathrm{X}\approx 0.374
.
Similarly, we show in the right panel of FIG.
40
the
\mathrm{X}
-dependence of the upper bound for
R
of Eq. (
4.90
) by the plum solid curve.

FIG. 40:
(Color Online). Maximum masses for generally stable NSs and critically stable NSs (TOV configurations). The insert of the left panel plots the
\mathrm{X}
-dependence of
\Pi_{\mathrm{c}}
from which we can clearly see that
\Pi_{\mathrm{c}}
is upper bounded at
\mathrm{X}\approx 0.374
.

As an independent check of our method, we use the 104 EOSs (phenomenological/microscopic) of Ref.
Cai et al. (2023)
with
M_{\mathrm{NS}}^{\max}\equiv M_{\mathrm{TOV}}
and
R_{\max}\equiv R_{\mathrm{TOV}}
to determine/estimate the maximum mass configuration of NSs; the mass and radius scalings together with the regression coefficients are given in Eqs. (
4.31
) and (
4.30
), respectively.
Similarly, we can also do the compactness scaling using the same EOS samples, and the result is

\xi_{\max}\equiv\xi_{\mathrm{TOV}}\approx A_{\xi}^{\max}\Pi_{\mathrm{c}}+B_{\xi}^{\max}\approx 2.59\Pi_{\mathrm{c}}-0.05.

(4.96)

Then using the similar formulas as Eq. (
4.94
) and Eq. (
4.90
) for NSs at the TOV configuration, we have
M_{\mathrm{TOV}}/M_{\odot}\approx 2.28
and
R_{\mathrm{TOV}}/\mathrm{km}\approx 11.91
under
\mathrm{X}\lesssim 0.374
.
Since the number of EOSs used in Ref.
Cai et al. (2023)
is far less than
10^{5}
used in our meta-modeling
Cai and Li (2024)
,
the errorbars on the
M_{\mathrm{TOV}}
and
R_{\mathrm{TOV}}
are consequently large.
If we adopt the similar errorbar level as we estimate for the stable NS masses as in Eq. (
4.95
), we may have

\boxed{M_{\mathrm{TOV}}/M_{\odot}\approx 2.28\pm 0.28,~~R_{\mathrm{TOV}}/\mathrm{km}\approx 11.91\pm 1.51.}

(4.97)

The regression (
4.96
) implies that an upper limit about
0.283
exists for
\xi_{\mathrm{TOV}}
, i.e.,
\xi_{\mathrm{TOV}}\lesssim 0.283
. The latter is highly consistent with the estimate (
6.32
). This is not surprising since we used the same input information on the EOSs. For comparisons, added in the left panel of FIG.
40
is the
\mathrm{X}
-dependence of
M_{\mathrm{TOV}}
thus obtained by the dashed orange curve, and in the right panel the radius
R_{\mathrm{TOV}}
by the dashed plum curve.
We see that the estimate
M_{\mathrm{TOV}}/M_{\odot}\lesssim 2.28
(for the critically stable NSs) of (
4.97
) is consistent with
M_{\mathrm{NS}}/M_{\odot}\lesssim 2.26
(for general stable NSs) of (
4.95
), as one expects since the latter should be smaller than the former.
In addition, it is interesting to notice that the
M_{\mathrm{TOV}}
and
R_{\mathrm{TOV}}
we estimated are quite consistent with the results from a very recent study
Fan et al. (2024)
which predicted that
M_{\mathrm{TOV}}/M_{\odot}\approx 2.25_{-0.07}^{+0.08}
and
R_{\mathrm{TOV}}/\mathrm{km}\approx 11.90_{-0.60}^{+0.63}
using two approaches: (1) modeling the mass distribution function of 136 NSs observed with a sharp cut-off at
2.28M_{\odot}
, and (2) Bayesian inference of the EOS from the currently available multi-messenger data of NSs considering constraints from the
CEFT at low densities
Essick et al. (2021)
and the pQCD at very high densities. Incidentally, the NS mass cut-off they used is exactly the
M_{\mathrm{TOV}}
we estimated above.

FIG. 41:
(Color Online). A partial list of the estimated NS maximum-mass
M_{\mathrm{TOV}}
of non-rotating NSs since 1974 after the seminal work of Rhoades and Ruffini
Rhoades and Ruffini (1974)
.
See the text for details on these constraints.

Estimating accurately the value of
M_{\mathrm{TOV}}
has been an important and challenging task in NS physics since the seminal work of Rhoades and Ruffini in 1974
Rhoades and Ruffini (1974)
when they predicted that
M_{\mathrm{TOV}}\lesssim 3.2M_{\odot}
. Since then a lot of works have been devoted to carrying out this task using various theories, models and data. Summarized in FIG.
41
is a (partial) list of the available estimates of
M_{\mathrm{TOV}}
. In particular, besides Ref.
Rhoades and Ruffini (1974)
, Ref.
Kalogera and Baym (1996)
also obtained a secure upper bound
M_{\mathrm{TOV}}\lesssim 2.9M_{\odot}
based on the knowledge of nuclear EOS valid up to about
2\rho_{0}
. Similarly, Ref.
Lawrence et al. (2015)
gave
2.0\lesssim M_{\mathrm{TOV}}/M_{\odot}\lesssim 2.2
considering Gamma-ray bursts constraints.
Since the beginning of the multi-messenger era marked by the detection of gravitational waves from the NS-NS merger in GW170817
Abbott et al. (2017)
;
Abbott et al. (2018)
in 2017 as well as the observations of massive NSs
Antoniadis et al. (2013)
;
Fonseca et al. (2021)
;
Sullivan and Romani (2024)
;
Romani et al. (2022)
, more independent and novel investigations on
M_{\mathrm{TOV}}
emerged. Naturally, the
M_{\mathrm{TOV}}
is required to be at least as large as the mass of the currently observed most massive NS. Specifically, other estimates listed in FIG.
41
are from the following studies: using the signals from GW170817
Abbott et al. (2018)
, the
M_{\mathrm{TOV}}
was predicted to be about
2.01_{-0.04}^{+0.04}\lesssim M_{\mathrm{TOV}}/M_{\odot}\lesssim 2.16_{-0.15}^{+0.17}

Rezzolla et al. (2018)
; similarly Ref.
Margalit and Metzger (2017)
showed it should be
\lesssim 2.17M_{\odot}
by including the electromagnetic information; a consistent prediction of
2.16\lesssim M_{\mathrm{TOV}}/M_{\odot}\lesssim 2.28
through general relativistic magnetohydrodynamics simulations was given in Ref.
Ruiz et al. (2018)
; Ref.
Shibata et al. (2017)
constrained
2.15\lesssim M_{\mathrm{TOV}}/M_{\odot}\lesssim 2.25
considering the absence of optical counterparts associated with relativistic ejecta; Ref.
Zhou et al. (2019)
predicted a quite narrow
M_{\mathrm{TOV}}/M_{\odot}\approx 2.28_{-0.02}^{+0.02}
by constructing nuclear EOSs consistent with low-density microscopic calculations and heavy-ion collision data based on a non-relativistic energy density functional;
Ref.
Jiang et al. (2020)
found
2.04\lesssim M_{\mathrm{TOV}}/M_{\odot}\lesssim 2.4
using the observational data of PSR J0030+0451 and of GW170817 while Ref.
Shao et al. (2020)
predicted
M_{\mathrm{TOV}}/M_{\odot}\approx 2.13_{-0.08}^{+0.09}
by including extra information from GRB 170817A/AT2017gfo; Ref.
Legred et al. (2021)
similarly obtained
M_{\mathrm{TOV}}/M_{\odot}\approx 2.21_{-0.21}^{+0.31}
using a nonparametric EOS model constructed with astrophysical constraints; Ref.
Nathanail et al. (2021)
restricted the maximum mass to about
M_{\mathrm{TOV}}/M_{\odot}\approx 2.210_{-0.123}^{+0.116}
to be consistent with existing astrophysical/nuclear constraints although the GW190814 was studied in their inference;
similarly Ref.
Raaijmakers et al. (2021)
constrained
M_{\mathrm{TOV}}/M_{\odot}\approx 2.23_{-0.25}^{+0.15}
by analyzing the radio mass measurement of
PSR J0740+6620, the joint mass-radius estimate of PSR J0740
+6620, and combining GW170817, GW190425, AT2017gfo,
PSR J0740+6620 as well as PSR J0030+0451; Ref.
Tang et al. (2021)
adopted similar information as in Ref.
Raaijmakers et al. (2021)
and found
M_{\mathrm{TOV}}/M_{\odot}\approx 2.30_{-0.18}^{+0.30}
and Ref.
Han et al. (2023)
predicted
M_{\mathrm{TOV}}/M_{\odot}\approx 2.17_{-0.12}^{+0.15}
; considering the GW190814 led Ref.
Ye and Fishbach (2022)
to predict that
M_{\mathrm{TOV}}/M_{\odot}\gtrsim 2.53
and similarly Ref.
Ai et al. (2023)
found
2.33\lesssim M_{\mathrm{TOV}}/M_{\odot}\lesssim 2.67
at
1\sigma
confidence level; Ref.
Brandes et al. (2023)
found
M_{\mathrm{TOV}}/M_{\odot}\approx 2.31_{-0.17}^{+0.14}
by incorporating the data of PSR J0952-0607; very recently Ref.
Fan et al. (2024)
updated the constraint of Ref.
Han et al. (2023)
to be
M_{\mathrm{TOV}}/M_{\odot}\approx 2.25_{-0.07}^{+0.08}
(with a precision about 3%);
in Ref.
Ferreira and Providência (2024)
the authors constrained the maximum mass to be
\lesssim 2.43M_{\odot}
by restricting the slope
\mathrm{d}M_{\mathrm{NS}}/\mathrm{d}R
to non-negative values in a certain mass range;
Ref.
Musolino et al. (2024)
found
M_{\mathrm{TOV}}/M_{\odot}\approx 2.24_{-0.11}^{+0.07}
by using a quasi-universal maximum-mass ratio between rotating and non-rotating stars;
Ref.
Koehn et al. (2024)
and Ref.
Rutherford et al. (2024)
updated the constraints on
M_{\mathrm{TOV}}
by including data of the newly announced NICER measurement of PSR J0437-4715
Choudhury et al. (2024)
and found
M_{\mathrm{TOV}}/M_{\odot}\approx 2.22_{-0.19}^{+0.38}
and
M_{\mathrm{TOV}}/M_{\odot}\approx 2.15_{-0.16}^{+0.14}
(using a PP model), respectively;
Ref.
Sneppen et al. (2024)
restricted the NS EOS by using a method based on the abundance of helium to constrain the lifetime of NS merger remnants and found that
M_{\mathrm{TOV}}/M_{\odot}\lesssim 2.3
.

Without any surprise, all the constraints on
M_{\mathrm{TOV}}
fall safely within the range
1.97M_{\odot}\lesssim M_{\mathrm{TOV}}\lesssim 2.67M_{\odot}
, set by the lower limit of observed mass of PSR J0348+0432 (about
1.97M_{\odot}
) and the upper limit on the observed mass of the secondary component of GW190814 (about
2.67M_{\odot}
at 68% confidence level).
Returning to our estimate of
M_{\mathrm{TOV}}
in this work, its uncertainty (
\pm 0.28
) is still quite large. In order to refine the prediction on
M_{\mathrm{TOV}}
in our approach, the scalings may be improved by incorporating more terms in the expansions of
\widehat{P}
,
\widehat{\varepsilon}
and
\widehat{M}
over
\widehat{r}
(the current radius scaling is obtained by truncating the pressure as
\widehat{P}(R)\approx\mathrm{X}+b_{2}\widehat{r}^{2}=0
).
These improved/refined scalings should necessarily be verified by existing nuclear EOS models.

5 Strong-field Gravity in GR and Peaked Sound Speed Squared (SSS) in Massive NSs

In this section, we discuss in more details the possible peaked behavior of the SSS (
s^{2}
) in NSs, extending the brief review given in SECTION
2
. Presently, this is quite a hot topic as the fundamental physics underlying the density or radius profile of SSS in NSs, especially whether it has a peak caused by what physics and where its peak is located, is still under debate. We first give in Subsection
5.1
an order-of-magnitude analysis on the
s^{2}
and the possible emergence of a peak in its density profile. Subsection
5.2
is denoted to the role played by
s^{2}
as a bridge linking the superdense NS matter and the strongly curved geometry in NSs. We then discuss analytically in Subsection
5.3
a useful connection between the trace anomaly
\Delta
and
s^{2}
.
The monotonic density profile of
s^{2}
in Newtonian stars is studied in Subsection
5.4
.
Subsection
5.5
is devoted to study the violation of the conformal bound on
s^{2}
for NSs at TOV configuration; the concept of average SSS will also be introduced in this subsection.
The central SSS for generally stable NSs, particularly that for canonical NSs, is investigated in Subsection
5.6
.
Combining these two subsections, we conclude that the conformal bound for
s^{2}
violates for both TOV NSs and generally stable ones. In Subsections
5.7
and
5.8
, we give the first-order and the higher-order approximations of the
\mu
-expansion of
s^{2}
, respectively. The former subsection mainly deals with the issue of whether
s^{2}(\widehat{\varepsilon})
is larger or smaller than its central value
s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}
, while the latter one investigates where the possible peak in the radial profile of
s^{2}
is located.
The peak in the derivative part of a
s^{2}
decomposition is also given in Subsection
5.8
.
Subsection
5.9
is devoted to a unified investigation on how the strong-field gravity may extrude a peak in the
s^{2}
profile.
In Subsection
5.10
, we discuss the connection between the peaked
s^{2}
in NSs and the high-density behavior of nuclear symmetry energy.
Finally, in Subsection
5.11
we briefly discuss the emergence of a peaked
s^{2}
profile in other dense systems, generalizing the results we have obtained for NSs.
As the trace anomaly
\Delta
and SSS are closely connected, a few related issues to
s^{2}
would be given in SECTION
6
after we review the relevant status on
\Delta
.

5.1 Qualitative features of SSS in stellar matter from order-of-magnitude analyses

Because
s^{2}
is dimensionless (adopting units of
c=1
) and
P
and
\varepsilon
have the same dimensions, dimensional consideration implies that
s^{2}
of Eq. (
1.1
) could be written generally as

\boxed{s^{2}\equiv{\mathrm{d}P}/{\mathrm{d}\varepsilon}=\mathrm{d}\widehat{P}/\mathrm{d}\widehat{\varepsilon}=\phi f(\phi),~~\phi=P/\varepsilon,}

(5.1)

with
f(\phi)
a dimensionless function depending on
\phi
only.
A factor
\phi
is separated out to the front in the above expression considering the fact that
s^{2}\to 0
if no matter exists
\phi\to 0
.
The function
f
can be expanded as a power series around
\phi\approx 0
(vacuum state) as

f\approx f_{0}+f_{1}\phi+f_{2}\phi^{2}+\cdots,

(5.2)

with
f_{0}>0
(required by the stability condition
s^{2}\geq 0
).
Obviously, the Eq. (
4.17
) has the form of (
5.1
).
Considering stars as white dwarfs (WDs), one has
P\lesssim 10^{22\mbox{-}23}\,\mathrm{dynes}/\mathrm{cm}^{2}\approx 10^{-(11\mbox{-}10)}\,\mathrm{MeV}/\mathrm{fm}^{3}
and
\varepsilon\lesssim 10^{8\mbox{-}9}\,\mathrm{kg}/\mathrm{m}^{3}\sim 10^{-6}\,\mathrm{MeV}/\mathrm{fm}^{3}
, thus
\phi\lesssim 10^{-(5\mbox{-}4)}
.
The typical radii of WDs are around
R\approx 10^{4}\,\mathrm{km}
, so
\widehat{R}=R/Q\approx 0.05
and
\widehat{R}^{2}\approx 3\times 10^{-3}
.
In fact, the ratio
\phi
of pressure over energy density could be even smaller for main-sequence stars like the sun.
Specifically, the pressure and energy density in the solar core are about
10^{-16}\,\mathrm{MeV}/\mathrm{fm}^{3}
and
10^{-10}\,\mathrm{MeV}/\mathrm{fm}^{3}

Cai and Li (2024)
, respectively, and therefore
\phi\approx 10^{-6}
.
These stars are Newtonian in the sense that GR effects are almost absent.
Similarly, for NS matter around nuclear saturation density
\rho_{0}=\rho_{\mathrm{sat}}\approx 0.16\,\mathrm{fm}^{-3}
, one estimates the pressure as,

P(\rho_{0})\approx P_{0}(\rho_{0})+P_{\mathrm{sym}}(\rho_{0})\delta^{2}\approx 3^{-1}L\rho_{0}\delta^{2}\lesssim 3\,\mathrm{MeV}/\mathrm{fm}^{3},

(5.3)

using
L\approx 60\,\mathrm{MeV}
and the fact
\delta^{2}\lesssim 1
.
Moreover,
P_{0}(\rho_{0})
is the pressure of SNM at
\rho_{0}
, which is zero by definition.
The energy density is similarly estimated as
\varepsilon(\rho_{0})\approx[E_{0}(\rho_{0})+E_{\mathrm{sym}}(\rho_{0})\delta^{2}+M_{\mathrm{N}}]\rho_{0}\approx 150\,\mathrm{MeV}/\mathrm{fm}^{3}
, where
M_{\mathrm{N}}\approx 939\,\mathrm{MeV}
is the static mass of a nucleon, and
E_{0}(\rho_{0})\approx-16\,\mathrm{MeV}
and
E_{\mathrm{sym}}(\rho_{0})\approx 32\,\mathrm{MeV}
are used; therefore
\phi\lesssim 0.02
.
For reference, at
\rho\approx 2\rho_{0}
, we can estimate
P(2\rho_{0})\lesssim 20\,\mathrm{MeV}/\mathrm{fm}^{3}
and
\varepsilon(2\rho_{0})\approx 300\,\mathrm{MeV}/\mathrm{fm}^{3}
, and thus
\phi\lesssim 0.067
by using Eq. (
3.1
) and Eq. (
3.2
) and
S\approx 32\,\mathrm{MeV}
,
L\approx 60\,\mathrm{MeV}
,
K_{\mathrm{sym}}\approx-100\,\mathrm{MeV},J_{\mathrm{sym}}\approx 200\,\mathrm{MeV}
and
K_{0}\approx 230\,\mathrm{MeV}
together with
J_{0}\approx-300\,\mathrm{MeV}
to make the estimate.
In fact, the effect of
J_{\mathrm{sym}}
for estimating
P/\varepsilon
at
2\rho_{0}
is
\lesssim 2\%
.

FIG. 42:
(Color Online). A sketch of the evolution of radial profile of
s^{2}
in NSs: at low densities characterized by small
\phi=P/\varepsilon
(Newtonian limit), the
s^{2}
monotonically decreases from the center to the surface (black line), and as the
\phi
increases approaching the GR case (orange line) a peak eventually emerges near the NS center.
The general expressions for
s^{2}
are also captioned near the curves,
c=1
is adopted. Figure taken from Ref.
Cai and Li (2024)
.

For these systems one can safely neglect the terms
f_{1}\phi+f_{2}\phi^{2}+\cdots
in the expansion of
f
, therefore
s^{2}\approx f_{0}\phi
or equivalently
\widehat{P}=C_{0}\widehat{\varepsilon}^{f_{0}}
is obtained
Shapiro and Teukolsky (1983)
, here
0<C_{0}\ll\mathcal{O}(1)
is an integration constant, indicating
s^{2}
is probably an increasing function of
\widehat{\varepsilon}
. The absence of a peak in
s^{2}
in NSs around
\rho_{\mathrm{sat}}
was confirmed by the CEFT
Tews et al. (2018)
;
Essick et al. (2021)
, e.g., Ref.
Essick et al. (2021)
predicted that
s^{2}
is monotonic around
\rho_{\mathrm{sat}}
and
s^{2}_{\mathrm{sat}}\equiv s^{2}(\rho_{\mathrm{sat}})\approx 0.03\sim\phi
and similarly Ref.
Tews et al. (2018)
found that
0.03\lesssim s^{2}_{\mathrm{sat}}\lesssim 0.05\sim\phi
near
\rho_{\mathrm{sat}}
.
These studies also indicate that

f_{0}\approx s^{2}/\phi\gtrsim 1\sim 2.

(5.4)

In fact, we may argue
f_{0}\approx 2
by using the trace anomaly decomposition formula (see relation (
5.22
)).
On the other hand,
\phi
could be sizable
\gtrsim\mathcal{O}(0.1)
in massive NSs especially in their cores, see TAB.
3
, and possible peaks in
s^{2}
may emerge.
In this sense, massive NSs like PSR J0740+6620
Fonseca et al. (2021)
;
Riley et al. (2021)
;
Miller et al. (2021)
;
Salmi et al. (2022)
, PSR J2215+5135
Sullivan and Romani (2024)
, PSR J1614-2230
Demorest et al. (2010)
and PSR J0348+0432
Antoniadis et al. (2013)
provide us excellent opportunities to study the possible peaked structure in
s^{2}
profiles.
See FIG.
42
for a sketch of the
s^{2}
radial profile evolved with
\phi
.

Consider the next-order correction
f_{1}\phi^{2}
to the SSS, i.e.,
s^{2}\approx f_{0}\phi+f_{1}\phi^{2}
, and perturb the solution
\widehat{P}=C_{0}\widehat{\varepsilon}^{f_{0}}
to
\widehat{P}\approx C_{0}\widehat{\varepsilon}^{f_{0}}[1+\psi(\widehat{\varepsilon})]
with
\psi(\widehat{\varepsilon})
a small correction (since
\phi^{2}\ll 1
is small).
The correction
\psi(\widehat{\varepsilon})
could be obtained:

\psi(\widehat{\varepsilon})\approx C_{1}+C_{0}\frac{f_{1}}{f_{0}-1}\widehat{\varepsilon}^{f_{0}-1},

(5.5)

the integration constant
C_{1}=0
since
\psi(\widehat{\varepsilon})\to 0
if
\widehat{\varepsilon}\to 0
.
After that we obtain the
s^{2}
containing the first-order correction in
\psi(\widehat{\varepsilon})
via
s^{2}\approx f_{0}\phi
and
\phi\approx C_{0}\widehat{\varepsilon}^{f_{0}-1}[1+\psi(\widehat{\varepsilon})]
; and an extreme point
\widehat{\varepsilon}_{\ast}
exists if
f_{1}<0
:

\boxed{\widehat{\varepsilon}_{\ast}\approx\left(-\frac{2C_{0}f_{1}}{f_{0}-1}\right)^{-\frac{1}{f_{0}-1}},~~s^{2}(\widehat{\varepsilon}_{\ast})\approx C_{0}f_{0}\widehat{\varepsilon}_{\ast}^{f_{0}-1}\left(1+\frac{C_{0}f_{1}}{f_{0}-1}\widehat{\varepsilon}_{\ast}^{f_{0}-1}\right)=\frac{f_{0}(1-f_{0})}{4f_{1}},~~f_{0}>1,}

(5.6)

using
[{\mathrm{d}s^{2}}/{\mathrm{d}\widehat{\varepsilon}}]_{\widehat{\varepsilon}_{\ast}}=0
.
The condition
0\leq s^{2}(\widehat{\varepsilon}_{\ast})\leq 1
implies
4^{-1}f_{0}(1-f_{0})-1\lesssim f_{1}\lesssim 4^{-1}f_{0}(1-f_{0})
.
Furthermore, it is a local maximum point since
[\mathrm{d}^{2}s^{2}/\mathrm{d}\widehat{\varepsilon}^{2}]_{\widehat{\varepsilon}_{\ast}}
is negative:

\left[\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}s^{2}}{\mathrm{d}\widehat{\varepsilon}^{2}}\right]_{\widehat{\varepsilon}_{\ast}}\approx C_{0}f_{0}\widehat{\varepsilon}_{\ast}^{f_{0}-3}\left[f_{0}^{2}-3f_{0}+2+4C_{0}\left(f_{0}-\frac{3}{2}\right)f_{1}\widehat{\varepsilon}_{\ast}^{f_{0}-1}\right]=-C_{0}f_{0}\left(f_{0}-1\right)^{2}\left(-\frac{2C_{0}f_{1}}{f_{0}-1}\right)^{-\frac{f_{0}-3}{f_{0}-1}}<0.

(5.7)

Using the range for
f_{1}
limited just above, we have then

-\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{\frac{f_{0}-3}{1-f_{0}}}\left(C_{0}f_{0}\right)^{\frac{2}{f_{0}-1}}(1-f_{0})^{2}\lesssim\left[\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}s^{2}}{\mathrm{d}\widehat{\varepsilon}^{2}}\right]_{\widehat{\varepsilon}_{\ast}}\lesssim-C_{0}f_{0}(1-f_{0})^{2}\left(\frac{1}{2}\frac{1}{f_{0}-1}\right)^{\frac{f_{0}-3}{1-f_{0}}};

(5.8)

and the second-order derivative would be negatively small (
\gtrsim-10^{-2}
), indicating the peak might be wide near
\widehat{\varepsilon}_{\ast}
.
On the other hand, the
s^{2}
is monotonically increasing function of
\widehat{\varepsilon}
if
f_{1}>0
.

5.2 SSS as a bridge connecting superdense matter and strongly curved geometry in and around NSs

Using the dimensionless TOV equations of (
2.3
), the SSS
s^{2}
can be written as

\displaystyle\textsc{GR}:\;s^{2}=\frac{\mathrm{d}\widehat{P}}{\mathrm{d}\widehat{\varepsilon}}=-\frac{\widehat{\varepsilon}\widehat{M}}{\widehat{r}^{2}\mathrm{d}\widehat{\varepsilon}/\mathrm{d}\widehat{r}}\frac{(1+\widehat{P}/\widehat{\varepsilon})(1+\widehat{r}^{3}\widehat{P}/\widehat{M})}{1-2\widehat{M}/\widehat{r}}.

(5.9)

It shows that not only dense NS matter (characterized by
\widehat{P}
,
\widehat{\varepsilon}
as well as their ratio
\widehat{P}/\widehat{\varepsilon}
) but also the strongly curved geometry (characterized by the factor
1-2\widehat{M}/\widehat{r}
) could affect
s^{2}
.
In this sense, the speed of sound acts as a bridge connecting properties of supra-dense matter with curved geometry of compact/massive NSs.
On the other hand, if one starts directly from the Newtonian evolution equation
\mathrm{d}\widehat{P}/\mathrm{d}\widehat{r}=-\widehat{\varepsilon}\widehat{M}/\widehat{r}^{2}
without incorporating the SR and GR effects discussed at the beginning of SECTION
2

Chandrasekhar (2010)
, we have

\textsc{Newtonian}:\;s^{2}=\frac{\mathrm{d}\widehat{P}}{\mathrm{d}\widehat{\varepsilon}}=-\frac{\widehat{\varepsilon}\widehat{M}}{\widehat{r}^{2}\mathrm{d}\widehat{\varepsilon}/\mathrm{d}\widehat{r}}.

(5.10)

Because there is no factor of
\widehat{P}
on the right side, i.e., the pressure (matter effect) could not affect the
s^{2}
explicitly (though
\widehat{\varepsilon}
contains implicitly the effects of
\widehat{P}
).
Moreover, since
1+\widehat{P}/\widehat{\varepsilon}>1
,
1+\widehat{r}^{3}\widehat{P}/\widehat{M}>1
and
0<1-2\widehat{M}/\widehat{r}<1
, we have
(1+\widehat{P}/\widehat{\varepsilon})(1+\widehat{r}^{3}\widehat{P}/\widehat{M})/(1-2\widehat{M}/\widehat{r})>1
, i.e.,
s^{2}
in NSs with GR effects is enhanced compared with its Newtonian limit.
In addition, because
s^{2}\leq 1
due to the principle of causality, it could not always increase (even if matter/geometry effects are strong enough), implying that in certain circumstances
s^{2}
may either increase or decrease with decreasing
\widehat{r}
(or increasing
\widehat{\varepsilon}
) when going into the stellar core.

After this general discussion on the evolutionary behavior of
s^{2}
, we now figure out under which circumstance a possible peak may emerge in
s^{2}
density/radial profile.
By inserting the perturbative expansions of
\widehat{P}
,
\widehat{\varepsilon}
and
\widehat{M}
into Eq. (
5.9
), one can obtain an expression for
s^{2}
similar to Eq. (
4.59
) for the core EOS.
In particular, we have to order
\widehat{r}^{2}
,

\boxed{s^{2}\approx s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}+l_{2}\widehat{r}^{2},~~l_{2}=\frac{2s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}}{b_{2}}\left(b_{4}-s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}a_{4}\right),}

(5.11)

see Eq. (
2.33
) for the
\widehat{r}
-expansion of
s^{2}
.
Because
s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}>0
(see Eq. (
4.17
)) and in order for
s^{2}
to obtain a peak near
\widehat{r}=0
, it is necessary that
l_{2}>0
and therefore
s^{2}(\widehat{r})>s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}
.
Since
b_{2}=-6^{-1}(1+3{\mathrm{X}}^{2}+4{\mathrm{X}})<0
, see Eq. (
2.30
),
the condition
l_{2}>0
is equivalent to
b_{4}-s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}a_{4}<0
,
or
a_{4}>b_{4}/s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}>0
. By using the expression of
b_{4}
given in Eq. (
2.31
) which involves the coefficient
b_{2}
of Eq. (
2.30
), we then obtain an equivalent condition of
l_{2}>0
as,

\displaystyle a_{4}>\frac{1}{12}\frac{1+3{\mathrm{X}}^{2}+4{\mathrm{X}}}{s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}}\left({\mathrm{X}}+\frac{4+9{\mathrm{X}}}{15s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}}\right)\approx\frac{1}{80{\mathrm{X}}^{2}}\left(1+\frac{17}{4}{\mathrm{X}}+\frac{9}{2}{\mathrm{X}}^{2}-\frac{13}{4}{\mathrm{X}}^{3}-\frac{49}{2}{\mathrm{X}}^{4}+\cdots\right).

(5.12)

Besides,
a_{4}
should fulfill some extra general constraints:

(a)

The decreasing of
\widehat{\varepsilon}\approx 1+a_{2}\widehat{r}^{2}+a_{4}\widehat{r}^{4}
with respective to
\widehat{r}
(i.e.,
\mathrm{d}\widehat{\varepsilon}/\mathrm{d}\widehat{r}<0
) requires necessarily
a_{4}<-a_{2}/2\widehat{R}^{2}
.

(b)

The increasing of
\widehat{M}
with respect to
\widehat{r}
(i.e.,
\mathrm{d}\widehat{M}/\mathrm{d}\widehat{r}=\widehat{r}^{2}\widehat{\varepsilon}>0
) gives the criterion
a_{4}\widehat{R}^{4}>-1-a_{2}\widehat{R}^{2}
.

Here
\widehat{R}\sim\mathcal{O}(1)
, and
a_{2}=b_{2}/s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}<0
is fixed at a certain
{\mathrm{X}}
.
Meeting the above three criteria, namely inequality (
5.12
),
a_{4}<-a_{2}/2\widehat{R}^{2}
and
a_{4}\widehat{R}^{4}>-1-a_{2}\widehat{R}^{2}
guarantees a peak in
s^{2}
at some
\widehat{r}\neq 0
with certain ranges of
{\mathrm{X}}
, i.e.,

Necessary and sufficient conditions for the appearance of a peak in
s^{2}
:

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt\frac{1}{12}\frac{1+3{\mathrm{X}}^{2}+4{\mathrm{X}}}{s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}}\left({\mathrm{X}}+\frac{4+9{\mathrm{X}}}{15s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}}\right)\lesssim a_{4}\lesssim\frac{1}{\widehat{R}^{4}}\mbox{ with $\widehat{R}\sim\mathcal{O}(1)$}.

(5.13)

Moreover, the peaked profile of
s^{2}(\widehat{r})
could easily be mapped onto that on
s^{2}(\widehat{\varepsilon})
or
s^{2}(\rho)
.
In addition, the necessity of a positive
a_{4}
can be understood as follows.
Since a positive
b_{4}
(of Eq. (
2.31
)) slows down the decrease of
\widehat{P}
(due to
b_{2}<0
) when going out from the center; for the
s^{2}
to be larger than
s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}
, a positive
a_{4}
is necessary to slow down the decrease of
\widehat{\varepsilon}
(due to
a_{2}<0
) too since approximately

s^{2}\approx{\Delta\widehat{P}}/{\Delta\widehat{\varepsilon}},

(5.14)

obtained by two nearby points on the EOS curve.
In Subsections
5.7
and
5.8
, we carry out detailed calculations of the
s^{2}
profile to verify the qualitative analysis on the peaked behavior of
s^{2}
.
In particular, we find that
l_{2}<0
holds (as in Eq. (
5.23
) and Eq. (
5.26
)) in the Newtonian limit even if
a_{4}
is positive, i.e., no peak would emerge in
s^{2}
profiles for Newtonian stars. We thus conclude that it is the GR effect that extrudes a peak in the
s^{2}
profile.

(Color Online). Left panel: combined region for
a_{4}
(cyan band) for the onset of a peaked profile of
s^{2}
under the inequality (
5.12
) and the criteria (a) and (b), here
\widehat{R}\approx 0.9
is adopted for illustrations.
The vertical dashed line marks
{\mathrm{X}}\approx 0.374
by setting
s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}
of Eq. (
4.17
)
\leq 1
,
l_{2}
appears in
s^{2}\approx s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}+l_{2}\widehat{r}^{2}+\cdots
.
Right panel: same as the left panel but for NS configurations climbing the M-R curve (instead of being at the TOV configuration), here
\Psi\approx 0.88
is adopted. Figures taken from Ref.
Cai and Li (2024)
.

\mathrm{X}
which can generate a peaked
s^{2}
on
\widehat{R}=R/Q
.

s^{2}
as a function of baryon density using nonlinear relativistic mean field models without (left) or with pQCD constraints (right). Figure taken from Ref.
Providência et al. (2024)
.

s^{2}
in cases when a global maximum is present below (left) and above (right)
3\rho_{\mathrm{sat}}=3n_{\mathrm{sat}}
. Figure taken from Ref.
Mroczek et al. (2023)
.

Miao et al. (2024)
.

(Color Online). NS EOS (upper panel) and the SSS
s^{2}
(lower panel) constrained using a non-parametric inference utilizing NS astrophysical observations and pQCD effects. Figure taken from Ref.
Cuceu and Robles (2024)
.

The resulted regions for
a_{4}
are shown in the left panel of FIG.
43
, here the colored arrows indicate whether the corresponding curve is a lower- or an upper-limit.
The criterion
\mathrm{d}\widehat{\varepsilon}/\mathrm{d}\widehat{r}<0
(criterion (a)) in the small-
{\mathrm{X}}
limit becomes
a_{4}\lesssim-16^{-1}\widehat{R}^{-2}{\mathrm{X}}^{-1}\sim\mathcal{O}({\mathrm{X}}^{-1})
while the criterion
l_{2}>0
(inequality (
5.12
)) gives
a_{4}\gtrsim 80^{-1}{\mathrm{X}}^{-2}\sim\mathcal{O}({\mathrm{X}}^{-2})
, implying there would be no overlapped region for
a_{4}
for such small
{\mathrm{X}}
, see the relation (
5.13
).
In fact, the overall condition
a_{4}\lesssim\widehat{R}^{-4}\approx 1
from criteria (a) and (b) certainly becomes incompatible with the magenta bound for
{\mathrm{X}}\lesssim 0.1
as it requires
a_{4}\gtrsim 2
for
{\mathrm{X}}\lesssim 0.1
.
This discrepancy becomes even larger as
{\mathrm{X}}
decreases to even smaller values.
On the other hand, the combined region for
a_{4}
for the onset of a peak in
s^{2}
profile may eventually emerge (shown as the cyan band) with increasing
{\mathrm{X}}
.
The fact that the upper limit of (
5.13
) should be greater than lower limit gives a critical lower
\mathrm{X}
for a given
\widehat{R}
, and the result is shown in FIG.
44
, here
0.15\lesssim\mathrm{X}\lesssim 0.30
is taken as a typical region for
\mathrm{X}
in massive NSs and
0.9\lesssim\widehat{R}\lesssim 1.5
as the typical region for
\widehat{R}
.
A relatively smaller
\widehat{R}
(corresponding to smaller
R
or larger
Q
) is advantageous
for producing a larger region of
\mathrm{X}
for generating a peaked
s^{2}
profile; if
\widehat{R}
is very large (
\approx 1.5
) there would be no such
\mathrm{X}
.

Similarly, for normally stable NSs on the M-R curve, the expression for
b_{2}
and the relation
a_{2}=b_{2}/s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}
do not change. However, the expression for
s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}
is modified from Eq. (
4.17
) to Eq. (
4.15
). Taking
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}\approx 900\,\mathrm{MeV}/\mathrm{fm}^{3}
and
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}\approx 400\,\mathrm{MeV}/\mathrm{fm}^{3}
for a
2M_{\odot}
NS and a canonical NS, respectively, we may find an approximation
\Psi\approx 0.88
for a wide range of central energy densities.
The result is shown in the right panel of FIG.
43
with
\Psi=0.88
. It is seen that the combined region for
a_{4}
is similar to the one shown in the left panel of FIG.
43
for
M_{\mathrm{NS}}^{\max}
.
In this case, one has
{\mathrm{X}}\lesssim 0.31
by requiring
s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}\leq 1
.
In the following, we pay our attention to the
s^{2}
profile for NSs at the TOV configuration.

To this end, we would like to point out that our analysis above is consistent with many model calculations of the
s^{2}
profile. The shared qualitative conclusion is that not any dense matter EOS could generate a peaked
s^{2}
profile.
This is because although
a_{2}
is definitely negative, the coefficient
a_{4}
could be either positive or negative (therefore no peaked structure), i.e., the corresponding
s^{2}
is a monotonic function (of energy density or radial distance).
For example, Ref.
Providência et al. (2024)
studied the
s^{2}
using the nonlinear relativistic mean field models with or without considering the pQCD effects and found that
s^{2}
may not generally have a peaked structure, as shown in the upper panel of FIG.
45
.
The result shown in FIG.
43
is also consistent with a recent study showing that the peaked
s^{2}
is not necessary
Mroczek et al. (2023)
, even after considering several recent observational as well as theoretical constraints, see the lower panel of FIG.
45
, which shows that
s^{2}
is probably monotonic within a wide range of baryon densities.
The monotonicity of
s^{2}
was also found either for NSs or hybrid stars (NSs) using different astrophysical observations. For example, including the supernova remnant HESS J1731-347 data
Doroshenko et al. (2022)
only slightly increases the
s^{2}
at moderate energy densities as shown in FIG.
46
and does not induce a peaked behavior, though the uncertainties on
s^{2}
are quite large.
Very recently in Ref.
Cuceu and Robles (2024)
, the constraints on the NS EOS and SSS using a non-parametric inference utilizing astrophysical observations and pQCD effects found that the density where
s^{2}
peaks is greater than
6\rho_{\mathrm{sat}}
, which may probably exceed the maximum NS densities, as shown in FIG.
47
.
Including the astrophysical data was found to increase the pressure, the
s^{2}
and its peak position, by comparing their yellow and violet bands in FIG.
47
.

5.3 Trace anomaly decomposition of SSS: general features

The trace anomaly decomposition introduced in Ref.
Fujimoto et al. (2022)
is very useful/important to investigate the
s^{2}
using the information on
\Delta

Cai and Li (2024)
.
In this decomposition, we have
Fujimoto et al. (2022)

\boxed{s^{2}=s^{2}_{\mathrm{deriv}}+s^{2}_{\mathrm{non-deriv}}=-\overline{\varepsilon}\frac{\mathrm{d}\Delta}{\mathrm{d}\overline{\varepsilon}}+\frac{1}{3}-\Delta,~~\overline{\varepsilon}\equiv\varepsilon/\varepsilon_{0},}

(5.15)

where the first term is the derivative part and the remaining terms together represent the non-derivative part;
\overline{\varepsilon}
is the dimensionless energy density reduced with any selected energy density scale (in our demonstration we use
\varepsilon_{0}
).
We may understand the behavior (especially the peaked structure) of the
s^{2}
by considering the feature of the derivative term
\Delta^{\prime}\equiv\mathrm{d}\Delta/\mathrm{d}\overline{\varepsilon}
.

FIG. 48:
Sketches of the trace anomaly
\Delta
(upper panel) and its derivative
\mathrm{d}\Delta/\mathrm{d}\overline{\varepsilon}
(lower panel) as functions of energy density.
This shape of
\Delta
corresponds to the magenta curve in FIG.
105
.
Figure taken from Ref.
Cai and Li (2024)
.

Generally, there is a point
\overline{\varepsilon}_{\mathrm{p}}
where
\Delta
decreases fastest; around this point-p we can expand its derivative
\Delta^{\prime}
as

\Delta^{\prime}\approx\Delta_{\mathrm{p}}^{\prime}+2^{-1}\Delta_{\mathrm{p}}^{\prime\prime\prime}\left(\overline{\varepsilon}-\overline{\varepsilon}_{\mathrm{p}}\right)^{2},~~\Delta_{\mathrm{p}}^{\prime}<0,~~\Delta_{\mathrm{p}}^{\prime\prime\prime}>0,

(5.16)

as the first-order derivative of
\Delta^{\prime}
, namely
\Delta^{\prime\prime}
at point-p is zero, see the lower panel of FIG.
48
.
Since
\Delta_{\mathrm{p}}^{\prime\prime\prime}
characterizes the curvature of
\Delta^{\prime}
, its magnitude is small for a shallow-shaped
\Delta^{\prime}
but large for a sharp-shaped one.
Correspondingly, the trace anomaly
\Delta
itself around the point-p can be approximated by integration of
\Delta^{\prime}
over
\overline{\varepsilon}
, i.e.,
\Delta\approx\Delta_{\mathrm{p}}^{\prime}(\overline{\varepsilon}-\overline{\varepsilon}_{\mathrm{p}})+6^{-1}\Delta_{\mathrm{p}}^{\prime\prime\prime}(\overline{\varepsilon}-\overline{\varepsilon}_{\mathrm{p}})^{3}+\Delta_{\mathrm{p}}
.
We could then obtain the
s^{2}
using the formula (
5.15
).
Calculating its derivative
\mathrm{d}s^{2}/\mathrm{d}\overline{\varepsilon}
gives

{\mathrm{d}s^{2}}/{\mathrm{d}\overline{\varepsilon}}=-2\Delta_{\mathrm{p}}^{\prime}+\left(3\overline{\varepsilon}_{\mathrm{p}}\overline{\varepsilon}-2\overline{\varepsilon}^{2}-\overline{\varepsilon}_{\mathrm{p}}^{2}\right)\Delta_{\mathrm{p}}^{\prime\prime\prime}.

(5.17)

We discuss it in the following two cases:

(a)

If the valley of the
\Delta^{\prime}
curve is shallow, i.e.,
\Delta_{\mathrm{p}}^{\prime\prime\prime}
is positively small, we can neglect the second term in (
5.17
), leading to
{\mathrm{d}s^{2}}/{\mathrm{d}\overline{\varepsilon}}\approx-2\Delta_{\mathrm{p}}^{\prime}>0
.
Thus, regardless of the shape of its derivative term
-\overline{\varepsilon}\mathrm{d}\Delta/\mathrm{d}\overline{\varepsilon}
(e.g. it has a peak or equivalently a valley in
\mathrm{d}\Delta/\mathrm{d}\overline{\varepsilon}
), the
s^{2}
is always a monotonically increasing function of
\overline{\varepsilon}
.

(b)

On the other hand, if
|\Delta_{\mathrm{p}}^{\prime}|
is much smaller than
\Delta_{\mathrm{p}}^{\prime\prime\prime}
, i.e., the valley in
\Delta^{\prime}
is sharp, we can treat the first term in (
5.15
) as a perturbation and solve the equation
\mathrm{d}s^{2}/\mathrm{d}\overline{\varepsilon}=0
for its extreme point
\overline{\varepsilon}_{\mathrm{p}}^{\ast}
. The result is

\boxed{\overline{\varepsilon}_{\mathrm{p}}^{\ast}=\frac{3\overline{\varepsilon}_{\mathrm{p}}\Delta_{\mathrm{p}}^{\prime\prime\prime}+\sqrt{\overline{\varepsilon}_{\mathrm{p}}^{2}\Delta_{\mathrm{p}}^{\prime\prime\prime 2}-16\Delta_{\mathrm{p}}^{\prime}\Delta_{\mathrm{p}}^{\prime\prime\prime}}}{4\Delta_{\mathrm{p}}^{\prime\prime\prime}}\approx\overline{\varepsilon}_{\mathrm{p}}\left(1-\frac{2}{\overline{\varepsilon}_{\mathrm{p}}^{2}}\frac{\Delta_{\mathrm{p}}^{\prime}}{\Delta_{\mathrm{p}}^{\prime\prime\prime}}\right)>\overline{\varepsilon}_{\mathrm{p}},~~\mbox{for small }\Delta_{\mathrm{p}}^{\prime}.}

(5.18)

Moreover, we have for the extreme value and second-order derivative of
s^{2}
at
\overline{\varepsilon}_{\mathrm{p}}^{\ast}
as

\displaystyle s^{2}(\overline{\varepsilon}_{\mathrm{p}}^{\ast})\approx

\displaystyle\frac{1}{3}-\Delta_{\mathrm{p}}-\overline{\varepsilon}_{\mathrm{p}}\Delta_{\mathrm{p}}^{\prime},~~\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}s^{2}}{\mathrm{d}\overline{\varepsilon}^{2}}\right|_{\overline{\varepsilon}_{\mathrm{p}}^{\ast}}\approx\Delta_{\mathrm{p}}^{\prime\prime\prime}\overline{\varepsilon}_{\mathrm{p}}\left(\frac{8}{\overline{\varepsilon}_{\mathrm{p}}^{2}}\frac{\Delta_{\mathrm{p}}^{\prime}}{\Delta_{\mathrm{p}}^{\prime\prime\prime}}-1\right)<0.

(5.19)

The negativeness of the second-order derivative shows that it is a maximum point (peak) of
s^{2}
.
The correction in the bracket of (
5.18
) is positive since
\Delta_{\mathrm{p}}^{\prime}
and
\Delta_{\mathrm{p}}^{\prime\prime\prime}
have the opposite signs, this means that the peak in
s^{2}
occurs on the right side of the peak in
-\overline{\varepsilon}\mathrm{d}\Delta/\mathrm{d}\overline{\varepsilon}
.
We can also evaluate separately the decomposition terms
s_{\mathrm{deriv}}^{2}\equiv-\overline{\varepsilon}\mathrm{d}\Delta/\mathrm{d}\overline{\varepsilon}
and
s_{\mathrm{non-deriv}}^{2}\equiv 3^{-1}-\Delta
of
s^{2}
at
\overline{\varepsilon}_{\mathrm{p}}^{\ast}
. The results are

s_{\mathrm{deriv}}^{2}(\overline{\varepsilon}_{\mathrm{p}}^{\ast})\approx-\overline{\varepsilon}_{\mathrm{p}}\Delta_{\mathrm{p}}^{\prime},~~s_{\mathrm{non-deriv}}^{2}(\overline{\varepsilon}_{\mathrm{p}}^{\ast})\approx\frac{1}{3}-\Delta_{\mathrm{p}},\mbox{ and therefore }\frac{s^{2}(\overline{\varepsilon}_{\mathrm{p}}^{\ast})}{s_{\mathrm{deriv}}^{2}(\overline{\varepsilon}_{\mathrm{p}}^{\ast})}=1-\frac{3^{-1}-\Delta_{\mathrm{p}}}{\overline{\varepsilon}_{\mathrm{p}}\Delta_{\mathrm{p}}^{\prime}}>1.

(5.20)

The latter relation means that the height of the peak in
s^{2}
is definitely larger than that in its derivative component
s_{\mathrm{deriv}}^{2}
.

The analysis for the point-q in FIG.
48
where
\Delta^{\prime}
is a maximum is totally parallel. In particular, there exists a valley in
s^{2}
described as:

\boxed{\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}s^{2}}{\mathrm{d}\overline{\varepsilon}^{2}}\right|_{\overline{\varepsilon}_{\mathrm{q}}^{\ast}}\approx\Delta_{\mathrm{q}}^{\prime\prime\prime}\overline{\varepsilon}_{\mathrm{q}}\left(\frac{8}{\overline{\varepsilon}_{\mathrm{q}}^{2}}\frac{\Delta_{\mathrm{q}}^{\prime}}{\Delta_{\mathrm{q}}^{\prime\prime\prime}}-1\right)>0,\mbox{ at }\overline{\varepsilon}_{\mathrm{q}}^{\ast}\approx\overline{\varepsilon}_{\mathrm{q}}\left(1-\frac{2}{\overline{\varepsilon}_{\mathrm{q}}^{2}}\frac{\Delta_{\mathrm{q}}^{\prime}}{\Delta_{\mathrm{q}}^{\prime\prime\prime}}\right)>\overline{\varepsilon}_{\mathrm{q}},}

(5.21)

since now
\Delta_{\mathrm{q}}^{\prime}>0
and
\Delta_{\mathrm{q}}^{\prime\prime\prime}<0
.
This means that the valley in
s^{2}
still appears after that in its derivative part; the new feature is that the valley in the derivative part is definitely negative,
s_{\mathrm{deriv}}^{2}(\overline{\varepsilon}_{\mathrm{q}}^{\ast})\approx-\overline{\varepsilon}_{\mathrm{q}}\Delta_{\mathrm{q}}^{\prime}<0
.
Considering
\mathrm{d}\Delta/\mathrm{d}\overline{\varepsilon}\to 0
for large
\overline{\varepsilon}
, the
s^{2}
finally approaches its asymptotic value determined by pQCD theories
Bjorken (1983)
;
Kurkela et al. (2010)
.
The analyses above provide the physics basis for our “educated guess” in imagining the evolution of trace anomaly
\Delta
and the corresponding
s^{2}
to completely uncharted superdense region in FIG.
105
.

By writing
\Delta(\overline{\varepsilon})\approx 3^{-1}+a\overline{\varepsilon}+b\overline{\varepsilon}^{2}
with
a<0
, we obtain
s^{2}\approx-2a\overline{\varepsilon}-3b\overline{\varepsilon}^{2}
and
\phi=3^{-1}-\Delta(\overline{\varepsilon})\approx-a\overline{\varepsilon}-b\overline{\varepsilon}^{2}
. Putting the
\overline{\varepsilon}(\phi)=-(2b)^{-1}\sqrt{a^{2}-4b\phi}
with
b\leq a^{2}/4\phi
from solving the latter relation into
s^{2}
enables us to write the dependence of
s^{2}
on
\phi
(with
\phi\to 0
or the Newtonian limit) as:

s^{2}(\phi)\approx 3\phi-\frac{a}{2b}\left(a+\sqrt{a^{2}-4b\phi}\right)\approx 2\phi\left(1-\frac{b}{2a^{2}}\phi\right)+\mathcal{O}(\phi^{3}),~\mathrm{and}~~s^{2}/\phi\approx 2\left(1-\frac{b}{2a^{2}}\phi\right).

(5.22)

We then obtain
f_{0}=2
(see relations (
5.2
) and (
5.1
)), and magnitude of the correction
b\phi/2a^{2}\ll 1/8
since
b\ll a^{2}/4\phi
.
Whether
s^{2}(\phi)
may develop a peak depends on the sign/magnitude of the coefficient
b
, e.g., we have
s^{2}(\phi_{\mathrm{pk}})\approx a^{2}/3b
at
\phi_{\mathrm{pk}}\approx 2a^{2}/9b
in the case of
b>0
, so
\gamma_{\mathrm{pk}}=s^{2}(\phi_{\mathrm{pk}})/\phi_{\mathrm{pk}}\approx 3/2
.
It is necessary to point out if the leading-order term in
\Delta(\overline{\varepsilon})-3^{-1}
is not
a\overline{\varepsilon}
but
a\overline{\varepsilon}^{k}
with some constant
k
, then
f_{0}\approx 1+k
.

FIG. 49:
(Color Online). Bayesian model mixing for the reduced pressure (left two panels) and the SSS (right two panels) by combining CEFT and pQCD results via a Gaussian process. Figure taken from Ref.
Semposki et al. (2024)
.

Using what have learned from the above analysis about the trace anomaly and its energy density dependence, we can understand generally why a peak in
s^{2}
may emerge.
This is mostly because
\Delta\to 1/3
at the low-density limit and it reaches some limit
\Delta_{\mathrm{limit}}
at large densities. The latter may be the pQCD value
\Delta_{\mathrm{pQCD}}=0
at extremely high densities or
\Delta_{\mathrm{GR}}\approx-0.041
of Eq. (
6.4
) accessible within realistic NSs.
If
\Delta_{\mathrm{limit}}\approx\Delta_{\mathrm{GR}}
, then a peak definitely emerges in
s^{2}
at densities realizable in NSs. On the other hand, if
\Delta_{\mathrm{limit}}\approx\Delta_{\mathrm{pQCD}}=0
and
\Delta
is always positive then a peak may still emerge in
s^{2}
, but at some larger densities not accessible in realistic NSs.
When does the pQCD constraint become relevant
Zhou (2024)
for NSs? The answer may tell us whether/where the peaked structure in
s^{2}
may occur.
For example, if the pQCD cutoff is set at a relatively lower density about
20\rho_{0}=20\rho_{\mathrm{sat}}
, then by combining the CEFT results at low-densities and the pQCD constraints starting at
20\rho_{\mathrm{sat}}
Ref.
Semposki et al. (2024)
found that a peak emerges in
s^{2}
at about
6.5\rho_{\mathrm{sat}}
using a Bayesian model mixing method
Qiu et al. (2024)
. This emerging density is large but may still be accessible in some NSs, see the panel (b) in FIG.
49
. On the other hand, if the pQCD cutoff density is set to
40\rho_{\mathrm{sat}}
, then the corresponding peak position in
s^{2}
is about
10.5\rho_{\mathrm{sat}}

as shown in panel (d) of FIG.
49
.
This emerging density probably exceeds the maximum density realized in NSs according to many existing calculations, indicating that the
s^{2}
in NSs is monotonic in this case. Similar conclusions also apply for the reduced pressure as indicated in panels (a) and (c) of FIG.
49
.

FIG. 50:
(Color Online). The trace anomaly
\Delta
in the left panel (having a sharp reduction at some
\overline{\varepsilon}
and becoming flat at both small and large
\overline{\varepsilon}
’s) may generate a peaked
s^{2}
; while that in the right panel implies
\mathrm{d}\Delta/\mathrm{d}\overline{\varepsilon}\approx\mathrm{const.}<0
and
s^{2}
may thus increase monotonically with
\varepsilon/\varepsilon_{0}
. The solid curve in each panel represents the probable shape of
\Delta
.
Figure taken from Ref.
Cai and Li (2024)
.
