Two classes of NSs constructed by
\Delta
and
\mathrm{Y}
using scalings of (
4.66
), (
4.67
) and (
4.68
); the
\Delta
for the canonical NSs is around 0.18 while that for massive NSs is about 0.01.

FIG. 51:
(Color Online). Upper panel: two classes of six NS instances constructed in TAB.
4
where there is quick (clear) decreasing of
\Delta
around
\overline{\varepsilon}\approx 3.7
.
Lower panel: since the magnitude of
s^{2}
in massive NSs is probably greater than the pQCD limit 1/3, there would be unavoidably a peak in
s^{2}
as a function of energy density; whether such peak emerges within NS densities rely essentially on the observational data.
The right (left) green band for PSR J0740+6620 corresponds to the case where the peak emerges within (beyond) NS densities.

A more useful approach to infer convincing evidence on whether there exists a peak or not in
s^{2}
is to rely on observational data (masses and radii) themselves if at all possible.
This idea is illustrated in FIG.
50
using two typical shapes of
\Delta
as a function of
\varepsilon/\varepsilon_{0}
.
In the left panel, we expect the
s^{2}
to have a peak somewhere since there is a quick decreasing in
\Delta
and two approximate plateaus at low and high energy densities, respectively. These features together will lead to a peak in
s^{2}
as we just discussed above. While the
\Delta
in the right panel indicates that
\mathrm{d}\Delta/\mathrm{d}\overline{\varepsilon}
is approximately negative and constant, both the derivative part
-\overline{\varepsilon}\mathrm{d}\Delta/\mathrm{d}\overline{\varepsilon}
and the non-derivative term
1/3-\Delta
of
s^{2}
then monotonically increase with
\varepsilon/\varepsilon_{0}
.
Which type of NS mass/radius observations may give such energy density dependence of
\Delta
? Scalings of (
4.66
), (
4.67
) and (
4.68
) provide us the clue: If two classes of NSs, the canonical NSs with radii about 12 km and massive NSs with
M_{\mathrm{NS}}/M_{\odot}\gtrsim 2
and similar radii, are accurately observed with large samples in the future, then we can study whether the
s^{2}
as a function of energy density may have a peaked structure within NS densities.
We construct these two classes of NSs in TAB.
4
, the first three NSs (columns 2 to 4) have masses about 1.4
M_{\odot}
and the last threes have
M_{\mathrm{NS}}/M_{\odot}\gtrsim 2
; all the six NS instances have similar radii about
\gtrsim
12 km.
We illustrate the corresponding
s^{2}
in FIG.
51
. If the
\Delta(\varepsilon)
extracted from observational data takes the left pattern of FIG.
50
, then there would be a peaked structure in
s^{2}
within NS densities reached (e.g., the right green band for PSR J0740+6620). On the other hand, if the
\Delta(\varepsilon)
takes the right pattern of FIG.
50
, then within NS densities reachable the
s^{2}
may monotonically increase (indicated by the gray background in the lower panel of FIG.
51
). As discussed earlier, since
s^{2}\to 1/3
at very high densities and the
s^{2}
in massive NSs is probably greater than 1/3, theoretically there would unavoidably be a peak in
s^{2}
profile. However, this peak is outside the NS densities. We shall use this idea in Subsection
6.4
to explore the (possible) peaked structure in
s^{2}
using available NS data, see FIG.
106
and FIG.
109
. Unfortunately, the currently available NS data could not invariably generate a peaked
s^{2}
profile
Cai and Li (2024)
.

5.4 A reference: SSS in Newtonian stars as a monotonic function of radial distance from their centers

We now work out the specific expression for
s^{2}
for Newtonian stars to be used as a reference.
Going away from the center by expanding the right side of Eq. (
5.10
) considering the perturbative expansions of
\widehat{\varepsilon}
of Eq. (
2.22
) and
\widehat{M}
of Eq. (
2.24
), we obtain the
s^{2}
to order
\widehat{r}^{4}
as
Cai and Li (2024)
,

\displaystyle s^{2}\approx

\displaystyle s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}+l_{2}^{\mathrm{N}}\widehat{r}^{2}+l_{4}^{\mathrm{N}}\widehat{r}^{4}\approx s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}+\left(12a_{4}s_{\mathrm{c}}^{4}-\frac{4}{15}\right)\widehat{r}^{2}+\left(144a_{4}^{2}s_{\mathrm{c}}^{6}+18a_{6}s_{\mathrm{c}}^{4}-\frac{62}{35}a_{4}s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}+\frac{1}{60s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}}\right)\widehat{r}^{4},

(5.23)

where a superscript “N” is added to remind us this is for the Newtonian case.
The Newtonian
s^{2}
radial profile does not depend on
{\mathrm{X}}
explicitly, although
s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}
may implicitly contain factors of
{\mathrm{X}}
.

For Newtonian stars, the reduced radius
\widehat{R}
is generally small; we have estimated in Subsection
5.1
that for WDs
\widehat{R}\approx 0.05
and
\widehat{R}^{2}\approx 3\times 10^{-3}
as well as
\phi=P/\varepsilon\approx 10^{-4}\sim 10^{-5}
.
Therefore it is conservative to use
\phi\lesssim\mathcal{O}(10^{-k})
with
k\gtrsim 4\mbox{-}5
for Newtonian stars.
Moreover, considering
s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}\approx 4{\mathrm{X}}/3\sim{\mathrm{X}}\sim\mathcal{O}(10^{-k})
with
k\gtrsim 4\mbox{-}5
, we have

a_{2}=b_{2}/s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}\approx-1/6s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}\lesssim\mathcal{O}\left(10^{k-1}\right),~~12a_{4}s_{\mathrm{c}}^{4}\lesssim\mathcal{O}\left(10^{2-k}\right),

(5.24)

the latter is obtained via
a_{2}\widehat{R}^{2}\gtrsim a_{4}\widehat{R}^{4}
from the perturbative expansion of
\widehat{\varepsilon}
and therefore
a_{4}\lesssim a_{2}/\widehat{R}^{2}\lesssim\mathcal{O}(10^{k+1})
.
Similarly, we have the order-of-magnitude scalings

144a_{4}^{2}s_{\mathrm{c}}^{6}\sim 18a_{6}s_{\mathrm{c}}^{4}\lesssim\mathcal{O}\left(10^{5-k}\right),~~({62}/{35})a_{4}s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}\lesssim\mathcal{O}\left(10^{1}\right),~~1/60s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}\lesssim\mathcal{O}\left(10^{k-2}\right).

(5.25)

Therefore, the
-4/15
and
1/60s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}
terms denominate over others in Eq. (
5.23
).
Consequently,

\boxed{s^{2}\approx s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}-({4}/{15})\widehat{r}^{2}+({60s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}})^{-1}\widehat{r}^{4}.}

(5.26)

Using this expression, we find
s^{2}
takes its minimum as
s_{\min}^{2}=-{s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}}/{15}
at
\widehat{r}^{2}_{\min}=8s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}

Cai and Li (2024)
.
The vanishing of
s^{2}
gives two special
\widehat{r}^{2}
values, namely
6s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}
and
10s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}
.
Since the stability condition requires
s^{2}\geq 0
, we find
s^{2}
is a decreasing function of
\widehat{r}\leq\widehat{R}
with
\widehat{R}^{2}=6s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}
, see FIG.
52
for the sketch of
s^{2}
in the Newtonian limit.
The decreasing feature of
s^{2}
under Newtonian limit could also be seen from Eq. (
4.59
) since
s^{2}/{\mathrm{X}}\approx 4/3+32\mu/15
for
{\mathrm{X}}\approx 0
, which decreases with decreasing
\widehat{\varepsilon}=\mu+1
(outward from the NS center).

FIG. 52:
(Color Online). Sketch of the Newtonian
s^{2}
when expanding
s^{2}
to order
\widehat{r}^{4}
(black) as in Eq. (
5.26
).
The correction due to a positive
a_{4}
is also shown (light-blue) from Eq. (
5.27
).
Figure taken from Ref.
Cai and Li (2024)
.

The coefficient
l_{4}^{\mathrm{N}}\approx 1/60s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}
in Eq. (
4.59
) originated from
b_{4}
of Eq. (
2.31
) is fundamental for explaining the radial dependence of
s^{2}
in the Newtonian limit.
This is because even when the next high-order term
l_{6}^{\mathrm{N}}\widehat{r}^{6}
is included, the conclusion will not change qualitatively.
Specifically, we have
s^{2}\approx s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}+l_{2}^{\mathrm{N}}\widehat{r}^{2}+l_{4}^{\mathrm{N}}\widehat{r}^{4}+l_{6}^{\mathrm{N}}\widehat{r}^{6}
:

\displaystyle s^{2}\approx

\displaystyle s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}+\left(12a_{4}s_{\mathrm{c}}^{4}-\frac{4}{15}\right)\widehat{r}^{2}+\left(144a_{4}^{2}s_{\mathrm{c}}^{6}+18a_{6}s_{\mathrm{c}}^{4}-\frac{62}{35}a_{4}s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}+\frac{1}{60s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}}\right)\widehat{r}^{4}

\displaystyle+\left[1728a_{4}^{3}s_{\mathrm{c}}^{8}+432a_{4}a_{6}s_{\mathrm{c}}^{6}+\left(24a_{8}-\frac{744}{35}a_{4}^{2}\right)s_{\mathrm{c}}^{4}-\frac{52}{15}a_{6}s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}+\frac{1}{35}a_{4}\right]\widehat{r}^{6}

\displaystyle\approx

\displaystyle s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}-({4}/{15})\widehat{r}^{2}+({60s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}})^{-1}\widehat{r}^{4}+(a_{4}/35)\widehat{r}^{6},

(5.27)

which becomes exact as
s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}\to 0
.
Here the term
a_{4}\widehat{r}^{6}/35
denominates at order
\widehat{r}^{6}
using the same order-of-magnitude estimates given above.
Notice that there are no terms inversely proportional to
s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}
appear in the coefficient
l_{6}^{\mathrm{N}}
(which is different from
l_{4}^{\mathrm{N}}
).
Using the full form of
s^{2}
in Eq. (
5.27
), we obtain:

\displaystyle\widehat{r}_{\min}^{2}\approx

\displaystyle 8s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}\left(1+{285}a_{4}s_{\mathrm{c}}^{4}/7+1416a_{6}s_{\mathrm{c}}^{6}\right),

(5.28)

\displaystyle s_{\min}^{2}\approx

\displaystyle-\frac{s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}}{15}\left(1+{288}a_{4}s_{\mathrm{c}}^{4}/7+9344a_{6}s_{\mathrm{c}}^{6}\right),

(5.29)

\displaystyle\widehat{R}^{2}\approx

\displaystyle 6s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}\left(1+{144}a_{4}s_{\mathrm{c}}^{4}/7+1620a_{6}s_{\mathrm{c}}^{6}\right).

(5.30)

The corrections in the brackets are perturbations (compared with the leading “1”) in the sense that
285a_{4}s_{\mathrm{c}}^{4}/7\lesssim\mathcal{O}(10^{3-k})\ll 1
and
1416a_{6}s_{\mathrm{c}}^{6}\lesssim\mathcal{O}(10^{7-2k})\ll 1
for
k\gtrsim 4\mbox{-}5
, etc.
We show in FIG.
52
the case
a_{4}>0
by the light-blue line, from which we find that the overall shape (especially the monotonicity) does not change
Cai and Li (2024)
.

The feature of
s^{2}
for Newtonian stars near
\widehat{\varepsilon}\approx 1
could be extracted straightforwardly from their structure equations.
Starting directly from Eq. (
5.10
), we obtain,

\displaystyle\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}s^{2}}{\mathrm{d}\widehat{\varepsilon}}\right)_{\mathrm{N}}=

\displaystyle-\frac{3\widehat{\varepsilon}}{\widehat{r}^{3}}\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}\widehat{\varepsilon}}{\mathrm{d}\widehat{r}}\right)^{2}\left(\frac{\widehat{r}^{3}\widehat{\varepsilon}}{3}-\widehat{M}\right)+\frac{\widehat{\varepsilon}\widehat{M}}{\widehat{r}^{2}}\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}\widehat{\varepsilon}}{\mathrm{d}\widehat{r}}\right)^{-3}\left[\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}\widehat{\varepsilon}}{\mathrm{d}\widehat{r}^{2}}-\frac{1}{\widehat{r}}\frac{\mathrm{d}\widehat{\varepsilon}}{\mathrm{d}\widehat{r}}\left(1+\frac{\widehat{r}}{\widehat{\varepsilon}}\frac{\mathrm{d}\widehat{\varepsilon}}{\mathrm{d}\widehat{r}}\right)\right],

(5.31)

where
\widehat{M}=\int\mathrm{d}\widehat{r}\widehat{r}^{2}\widehat{\varepsilon}
.
Since
[{\mathrm{d}}/{\mathrm{d}\widehat{r}}]({\widehat{r}^{3}\widehat{\varepsilon}}/{3}-\widehat{M})=3^{-1}\widehat{r}^{3}{\mathrm{d}\widehat{\varepsilon}}/{\mathrm{d}\widehat{r}}<0
(notice
\mathrm{d}\widehat{M}/\mathrm{d}\widehat{r}=\widehat{r}^{2}\widehat{\varepsilon}
and
\mathrm{d}\widehat{\varepsilon}/\mathrm{d}\widehat{r}<0
),
i.e.,
\widehat{r}^{3}\widehat{\varepsilon}/3-\widehat{M}
decreases with increasing of
\widehat{r}
(starting from 0), therefore we have
\widehat{r}^{3}\widehat{\varepsilon}/3-\widehat{M}<[\widehat{r}^{3}\widehat{\varepsilon}/3-\widehat{M}]_{\widehat{r}=0}=0
. Moreover, the factor in square bracket of Eq. (
5.31
) is negative, one then has
(\mathrm{d}s^{2}/\mathrm{d}\widehat{\varepsilon})_{\mathrm{N}}>0
definitely (near
\widehat{r}=0
), i.e.,
s^{2}
is an increasing function of
\widehat{\varepsilon}
near
\widehat{\varepsilon}\approx 1
. This is equivalent to
l_{2}<0
for Newtonian stars.

5.5 Violation of the perturbative QCD conformal bound on SSS and the average SSS in NSs

In this subsection, we study the SSS in NS cores and the possible violation of the conformal bound on SSS, using our expression for the latter.
The central SSS of a NS at the maximum-mass configuration is given by Eq. (
4.17
), where only the combination
\mathrm{X}
is needed instead of the individual
P_{\mathrm{c}}
and
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}
.
For the
\mathrm{X}\approx 0.24_{-0.07}^{+0.05}
extracted for PSR J0740+6620
Cai et al. (2023)
, one obtains
Cai et al. (2023)
,

\boxed{\mbox{PSR J0740+6620:}~~s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}\approx 0.45_{-0.18}^{+0.14}.}

(5.32)

This value is consistent with constraints on the SSS from other studies.
For example, we shown in the left panel of FIG.
53
the
s^{2}
as a function of the radial distance from the center for general NSs
Ecker and Rezzolla (2022)
, using more than a million randomly generated EOSs satisfying all known theoretical and observational constraints.
In that work, the central SSS for a NS at the TOV configuration is found to be about
s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}\approx 0.3

Ecker and Rezzolla (2022)
.
In addition, Ref.
Ecker and Rezzolla (2022)
also found that the maximum SSS
s_{\max}^{2}
in NSs approaches some fixed value about
s_{\max}^{2}\approx 0.5
. Considering that the maximum enhancement on the central SSS is less than about 10%
Cai et al. (2023)
(see discussions given in the following subsections of this section) if we perturb
s^{2}
to places near the center, we find that the maximum
s_{\max}^{2}
based on (
5.32
) is similarly about 0.5.
In the right panel of FIG.
53
, a similar plot for the SSS as a function of baryon density is shown
Pang et al. (2024)
. It is seen that at densities
\approx(5\pm 1)\rho_{0}
(which is similar as the central density in massive NSs), the
s^{2}
is around 0.3, being consistent with both Ref.
Ecker and Rezzolla (2022)
and our constraint (
5.32
).

FIG. 53:
(Color Online). Left panel: dependence of the SSS in NSs on NS mass and on the radial distance from the center. Figure taken from Ref.
Ecker and Rezzolla (2022)
. Right panel: posterior on the SSS as a function of baryon density with/without incorporating the constraints from AT2017gfo
Abbott et al. (2017)
. Figure taken from Ref.
Pang et al. (2024)
.

FIG. 54:
(Color Online). Dependence of
s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}
on the maximum mass
M_{\mathrm{NS}}^{\max}/M_{\odot}
, the conformal bound (CB) on SSS is indicated by the dashed orange line.
The inset plots the
s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}
as a function of
{\mathrm{X}}
, where the lightblue curve is allowed by conditions of stability and causality (
0\leq s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}\leq 1
).
Figure taken from Ref.
Cai et al. (2023)
.

FIG. 55:
(Color Online). Upper panel: schematic representation of the various sets into which the total sample of EOSs can be decomposed, either when subject to the observational constraints (dark blue) or when not (light blue).
Lower panel: PDFs for the SSS as a function of the energy density, and of the NS mass as a function of the stellar radius; here the
s^{2}
is monotonic and is limited in the range of
0\leq s^{2}\leq 1/3
. Figures taken from Ref.
Altiparmak et al. (2022)
.

The central SSS for
\mathrm{X}\leq 1/3
is bounded as,

\boxed{s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}(\mathrm{X}\leq 1/3)\leq 7/9\approx 0.778,~~\mbox{for NSs at the TOV configuration}.}

(5.33)

However, it does not mean that an URFG has a SSS of
7/9
since
\mathrm{X}=1/3
(which holds only at the center) does not generally indicate
P=\varepsilon/3
(which is a functional relationship for the whole range of
\varepsilon
).
The dependence of
s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}
on
\mathrm{X}
could be straightforwardly transformed into its dependence on
M_{\mathrm{NS}}^{\max}/M_{\odot}
, under some assumptions.
Here, we assume that NSs with masses
\mathrm{Z}_{-0.07}^{+0.07}M_{\odot}
where
\mathrm{Z}=1.3\mbox{$\sim$}2.3
have similar radii as indicated by the NICER observations to extract the
\nu_{\mathrm{c}}
from Eq. (
4.30
).
In particular, NICER found that the radius of PSR J0740+6620 (mass
\approx 2.08_{-0.07}^{+0.07}M_{\odot}
) is about
12.39_{-0.98}^{+1.30}\,\mathrm{km}

Riley et al. (2021)
while that of PSR J0030+0451 (mass
\approx 1.34_{-0.16}^{+0.15}M_{\odot}
) is about
12.71_{-1.19}^{+1.14}\,\mathrm{km}

Riley et al. (2019)
. They are very similar, indicating that the NS radius is less sensitive to the central EOS
Cai et al. (2023)
.
We therefore have
10^{3}\nu_{\mathrm{c}}\approx 11.2\,\mathrm{fm}^{3/2}/\mathrm{MeV}^{1/2}
by using
R_{\max}\approx 12.39_{-0.98}^{+1.30}\,\mathrm{km}

Riley et al. (2021)
.
Perturbatively, we have for the SSS at NS center from expanding Eq. (
4.17
):

\displaystyle s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}

\displaystyle\approx\frac{4}{3}{\mathrm{X}}\left(1+{\mathrm{X}}+\frac{3}{2}{\mathrm{X}}^{2}+3{\mathrm{X}}^{3}\right)+\mathcal{O}\left({\mathrm{X}}^{5}\right)\approx\frac{4}{3}H\left(1+5H+\frac{57}{2}H^{2}+175H^{3}\right)+\mathcal{O}\left(H^{5}\right),

(5.34)

where
H\approx 0.052(M_{\mathrm{NS}}^{\max}/M_{\odot}+0.106)\ll 1
.
Keeping only the leading-order term of (
5.34
) gives the Newtonian prediction
s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}=4{\mathrm{X}}/3
from which one infers
{\mathrm{X}}\lesssim 3/4

Cai and Li (2024)
.
The GR contributions in the TOV equations have an effect about 100% on the upper limit for
{\mathrm{X}}
.
Essentially, the
s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}
increases as
M_{\mathrm{NS}}^{\max}
increases as shown in FIG.
54
.
The results shown there provide us a straightforward way to infer the central SSS
s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}
once the radii/masses are known (measured/observed). It also implies that more compact NSs (characterized by
\xi
or equivalently by
\mathrm{X}
through Eq. (
4.8
)) have larger
s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}
, e.g., NSs of masses
1.5M_{\odot}
and
1.4M_{\odot}
with radii 9.9
\sim
11.2 km
Özel and Freire (2016)
and
11.0_{-0.6}^{+0.9}\,\mathrm{km}

Capano et al. (2020)
have
s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}\approx 0.31_{-0.07}^{+0.07}
and
s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}\approx 0.23_{-0.05}^{+0.03}
, respectively, shown as red hexagons in FIG.
54
.
Assuming a canonical NS at the TOV configuration is unreasonable based on our current knowledge from both astrophysical theories and observations, in order to estimate the central SSS of such NSs the factor
\Psi
should be taken into account appropriately. We study this issue in Subsection
5.6
, see Eq. (
4.15
) and Eq. (
4.16
).
Consequently, although
\mathrm{X}
is smaller for a canonical NS its
\Psi
is greater than zero, and the
s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}
is larger than about
1/3
estimated here; implying that the conformal bound for
s^{2}
is also violated in canonical NSs.

We also show the conformal bound (CB) on the SSS of
s^{2}=1/3

Hohler and Stephanov (2009)
;
Cherman et al. (2009)
in FIG.
54
by the dashed orange line. It is seen that the CB for SSS tends to break down for
M_{\mathrm{NS}}^{\max}/M_{\odot}\gtrsim 1.89
.
This mass threshold is very close to the most probable critical maximum-mass about
M_{\mathrm{NS}}^{\max}/M_{\odot}\approx 1.87
given in Ref.
Bedaque and Steiner (2015)
, above which the CB is likely to break down, see FIG.
10
. Similarly, Ref.
Altiparmak et al. (2022)
predicted that the largest mass of NSs being consistent with the CB is
\lesssim 1.99M_{\odot}
indicated by the lower panel of FIG.
55
. In fact, the number of EOSs passing through all the astrophysical/microscopic physics constraints but with
s^{2}\leq 1/3
is very small as shown in the upper panel of FIG.
55
.
Furthermore, the CB
s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}\leq 1/3
is essentially broken for
{\mathrm{X}}\gtrsim 0.195
from Eq. (
4.17
). This finding is consistent with conclusions from related studies on the same issue, e.g., see Ref.
McLerran and Reddy (2019)
;
Tan et al. (2022)
;
Tan et al. (2022)
;
Altiparmak et al. (2022)
;
Ecker and Rezzolla (2022)
;
Ecker and Rezzolla (2022)
;
Alsing et al. (2018)
;
Tews et al. (2018)
;
Leonhardt et al. (2020)
;
Miao et al. (2021)
, indicating that Eq. (
4.17
) grasps the main features of the SSS. However, we may emphasize that our method adopts no specific model for NS matter EOS and also make no assumptions about the composition of NSs (such as nucleons, hyperons and/or quarks), therefore providing a EOS-model independent way for investigating the
s^{2}
.
Actually, we can deduce from Eq. (
4.17
) that
s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}\to 1/3
occurs earlier than
{\mathrm{X}}\to 1/3
, explaining why the CB for SSS is highly likely to break down in NS cores
Cai et al. (2023)
;
Cai et al. (2023)
.
In addition, since the NS EOSs are often softened considering the exotic components such as hyperons, our formula (
4.17
) indicates that the CB in these NSs with non-nucleonic particles tends to be obeyed
Stone et al. (2021)
;
Otto et al. (2020)
;
Motta et al. (2021)
.

Next, we emphasize that although TOV NSs with lower maximum-mass have
s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}\lesssim 1/3
, it does not mean that these NSs exhibit conformal symmetry (in their cores) since the latter only emerges at extremely high energies where the strong force coupling constant diminishes, leading to the “asymptotic freedom” of quarks and gluons. This symmetry is broken as the energy scale lowers. At the energy scales relevant to NS cores, the presence of massive particles and strong gravitational fields disrupt conformal symmetry. The latter being not exact even in a quark-gluon plasma is generally expected to break down as the system transitions to a hadronic phase including more massive particles. Therefore, the observation of
s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}\lesssim 1/3
in low-mass NSs should not be interpreted as implying that the conformal symmetry is reached there
Cai et al. (2023)
.

FIG. 56:
(Color Online). Relation between the
\mathrm{X}
(also denoted by
\langle c_{\mathrm{s}}^{2}\rangle
) and the compactness
\xi
(C), tidal deformability
\Lambda
, and dimensionless moment of inertia
\overline{I}
using a set of realistic EOSs.
Figure taken from Ref.
Saes et al. (2024)
.

FIG. 57:
(Color Online). Correlation between
\mathrm{X}
(average SSS) and NS compactness using three empirical EOSs with/without rotations. Figure taken from Ref.
Mendes et al. (2024)
.

We can work out the
\mathrm{X}
expressed in terms of the compactness
\xi
perturbatively.
Denoting the in-front coefficient of the scaling between
\xi
and
\Pi_{\mathrm{c}}
as
\tau
, i.e.,
\xi\approx\tau\Pi_{\mathrm{c}}
, Eq. (
4.8
) then gives

\mathrm{X}=-\frac{4\xi-\tau+\sqrt{4\xi^{2}-8\tau\xi+\tau^{2}}}{6\xi}\approx\frac{\xi}{\tau}+4\left(\frac{\xi}{\tau}\right)^{2}+19\left(\frac{\xi}{\tau}\right)^{3}+100\left(\frac{\xi}{\tau}\right)^{4}+\cdots.

(5.35)

The value of
\tau
is about
\tau\approx 2
by the scaling of Eq. (
4.8
) and about
\tau\approx 2.3
by the numerical verification of the scaling between
\xi
and
\mathrm{X}
using meta-model EOSs, see the Subsection
4.5
.
Therefore, the above relation could be approximated as
\mathrm{X}\approx\xi/2+\xi^{2}+19\xi^{3}/8+25\xi^{4}/4+\cdots
from Eq. (
4.8
) or
\mathrm{X}\approx 0.43\xi+0.76\xi^{2}+1.56\xi^{3}+3.56\xi^{4}+\cdots
from the numerical verification;
these relations are also nearly universal.
Recently, Ref.
Saes et al. (2024)
studied the correlation between
\mathrm{X}
(they called it the average SSS and denoted it as
\langle s^{2}\rangle
) and the compactness
\xi
, using both a few realistic EOSs and some simplified approximations like the incompressible fluid, the Tolman VII fluid and the Buchdahl fluid. Interestingly, for the latter three simplified cases, one can work out to order
\xi^{2}
explicitly the following
Saes et al. (2024)

incompressible fluid:

\displaystyle~~\mathrm{X}\approx\frac{\xi}{2}+\xi^{2};

(5.36)

Tolman VII fluid:

\displaystyle~~\mathrm{X}\approx\frac{\xi}{2}+\frac{133\xi^{2}}{120};

(5.37)

Buchdahl fluid:

\displaystyle~~\mathrm{X}\approx\frac{\xi}{2}+\frac{5\xi^{2}}{4}.

(5.38)

The correlation between
\mathrm{X}\equiv\langle c_{\mathrm{s}}^{2}\rangle=\langle s^{2}\rangle

Saes et al. (2024)
and the compactness
C\equiv\xi
is shown in the left panel of FIG.
56
(with an uncertainty less than about 10%). Also shown are the correlations between
\mathrm{X}
and the tidal deformability
\Lambda
(middle) and the dimensionless moment of inertial
\overline{I}
(right). In their work
Saes et al. (2024)
, the average speed of sound squared
\mathrm{X}=\langle c_{\mathrm{s}}^{2}\rangle
has been interpreted as a measure of the mean stiffness of the EOS up to the central energy density of a given NS.
A similar plot on the correlation between
\mathrm{X}
and the NS compactness is shown in FIG.
57
, using three empirical EOSs with/without considering rotations
Mendes et al. (2024)
.

Using our formula for the central SSS of Eq. (
4.15
), we have

s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}\approx\frac{4+\Psi}{3\tau}\xi+\frac{4}{3}\frac{5+2\Psi}{\tau^{2}}\xi^{2}+\frac{38+19\Psi}{\tau^{3}}\xi^{3}+\frac{100}{3}\frac{7+4\Psi}{\tau^{4}}\xi^{4}+\cdots.

(5.39)

We have established that
\mathrm{X}
is also a measure of compactness, i.e.,
\mathrm{X}\sim\xi
, see Eq. (
4.8
). We can reasonably say that the central stiffness
s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}
effectively characterizes the same physics as
\mathrm{X}
(which is the average SSS as first shown in Ref.
Saes et al. (2024)
); and since
\mathrm{X}
and
\xi
are monotonically correlated, we have
at NS centers the correspondence

\boxed{\mbox{compactness }\xi\leftrightarrow\mbox{central pressure over energy density ratio $\mathrm{X}$ (average SSS) }\leftrightarrow\mbox{stiffness $s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}$}.}

(5.40)

This interpretation is consistent with that in Ref.
Saes et al. (2024)
.
However, it does not mean that the stiffness generally characterized by
s^{2}
at places other than the origin (
\widehat{r}=0
) is also effectively equivalent to
\phi=P/\varepsilon
.
This is because the latter is probably a monotonically increasing function of
\widehat{r}
when going toward the NS center, while the
s^{2}(\widehat{r})
may still have sizable probabilities to be larger than
s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}
. Namely,
s^{2}(\widehat{r})
is not monotonic as a function of the radial distance
\widehat{r}
.
Similarly, although
\mathrm{X}
or
s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}
(defined at
\widehat{r}=0
) characterizes the same physics as
\xi
,
\phi
or
s^{2}
(defined at finite
\widehat{r}
) generally can not be used to quantify the compactness.

5.6 Central SSS in canonical NSs from NS compactness scaling

In this subsection, we estimate the central SSS for canonical NSs.
The relevant expression for
s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}
is Eq. (
4.15
), and there are two approaches for determining
\mathrm{X}
: one from the mass and radius scalings and the other using the compactness scaling alone.
In the latter way, the process can be summarized as

\xi\mbox{-scaling}\xrightarrow[\displaystyle\xi\approx A_{\xi}\Pi_{\mathrm{c}}+B_{\xi}]{\displaystyle M_{\mathrm{NS}}/R}\mathrm{X}\xrightarrow[\displaystyle s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}=\mathrm{X}\left(1+\frac{1+\Psi}{3}\frac{1+3\mathrm{X}^{2}+4\mathrm{X}^{2}}{1-3\mathrm{X}^{2}}\right)]{\displaystyle M_{\mathrm{NS}}/M_{\odot}\to\Psi\mbox{ (FIG.\,\ref{fig_k-fac})}}s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}.

(5.41)

Obviously, this approach should give a prediction for
s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}
with small uncertainty, fundamentally because the compactness scaling is much strong than the radius scaling.

In order to use Eq. (
5.41
) to calculate the central SSS in a normal NS, the knowledge of
\Psi
especially its dependence on
M_{\mathrm{NS}}
is desired, this is given in Subsection
4.6
.
Putting
\Psi\approx 2.85\pm 0.29
for canonical NSs into the formula (
5.41
) and considering
\mathrm{X}\approx 0.15\pm 0.02
(see the last row of TAB.
3
) using directly the
\xi
-scaling, we obtain

\boxed{s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}\approx 0.47_{-0.09}^{+0.09},~~\mbox{for a canonical NS with }R\approx 12_{-1}^{+1}\,\mathrm{km}.}

(5.42)

On the other hand, if we use the mass and radius scalings to evaluate the
\mathrm{X}
which is about
\mathrm{X}\approx 0.14_{-0.03}^{+0.03}
(see TAB.
3
), the prediction on the central SSS is about
0.46\pm 0.16
, which has a similar magnitude as (
5.42
) but induces a larger uncertainty.

FIG. 58:
(Color Online). Comparison on the
s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}
for a canonical NS.

Shown in FIG.
58
is a comparison of the central SSS obtained using our approach and some existing ones in the literature
Ecker and Rezzolla (2022)
;
Brandes et al. (2023)
.
It is seen that our results are in excellently agreement with these investigations but have relatively smaller uncertainties.
Moreover, since PSR J0030+0451
Riley et al. (2019)
and PSR J0437-4715
Choudhury et al. (2024)
both have a mass near
1.4M_{\odot}
, they could be treated as canonical NSs. Using the observational NS masses and radii of these two pulsars (consequently one could obtain the compactness straightforwardly), we can evaluate the central SSS similarly; the results are given in TAB.
5
and are plotted in FIG.
58
.
Since the observed radius of PSR J0437-4715 is much smaller than that of PSR J0030+0451, the inferred central SSS is correspondingly much larger, as the matter is deeply compressed in its core.

Our prediction for the central SSS of PSR J0437-4715 and a canonical NS even at the lower limit of the 68% confidence level is greater than the conformal bound of
s^{2}=1/3
, implying that the latter is highly broken in a canonical NS. This conclusion is more reasonable than the one given in FIG.
54
since canonical NSs can hardly be treated as in the TOV configuration regarding obeservational facts.

5.7
1^{\mathrm{st}}
-order expansion of SSS in reduced energy density: is the central SSS greater than its surroundings?

In this and the next subsections, we develop perturbative approximations for
s^{2}
as a function of radial distance
\widehat{r}
from the center (or of the reduced energy density
\mu=\widehat{\varepsilon}-1
).
This subsection is devoted to the issue whether
s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}
is the maximum value, i.e., whether
s^{2}(\widehat{\varepsilon})
could be larger than
s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}
or not.
Then in Subsection
5.8
, we estimate the location of the possible peak in
s^{2}(\widehat{\varepsilon})
.

Using the sum rules of (
2.51
), we can write the coefficient
a_{4}
appearing in
\widehat{\varepsilon}
in terms of the coefficient
d_{k}
,

a_{4}={s_{\mathrm{c}}^{-2}}\left(b_{4}-a_{2}^{2}\sum_{k=1}^{K}2^{-1}{k(k-1)}d_{k}\right),

(5.43)

Here
K
is the effective truncation order of the expansions.
Putting the expression of
a_{4}
of Eq. (
5.43
) into Eq. (
2.33
) leads to the final expression for
s^{2}
as,

\displaystyle s^{2}(\widehat{r})\approx s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}\left[1+({2}/{b_{2}})\left(b_{4}-s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}a_{4}\right)\widehat{r}^{2}\right]+\mathcal{O}\left(\widehat{r}^{4}\right)\approx s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}+2a_{2}D\widehat{r}^{2}+\mathcal{O}(\widehat{r}^{4}),~~D=\sum_{k=1}^{K}2^{-1}{k(k-1)}d_{k}.

(5.44)

By using
\widehat{\varepsilon}(\widehat{r})\approx 1+a_{2}\widehat{r}^{2}
, we can transfer the dependence of
s^{2}
on
\widehat{r}^{2}
to that on
\widehat{\varepsilon}
:

s^{2}(\widehat{\varepsilon})\approx s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}+2D\left(\widehat{\varepsilon}-1\right)=s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}+2D\mu,

(5.45)

where
\mu=\widehat{\varepsilon}-1
, see Eq. (
2.27
).
The approximation (
5.45
) is expected to be valid only near the NS centers; nevertheless, the sign of
D
is sufficient for our purpose to deduce a relative relation between
s^{2}(\widehat{\varepsilon}
) and
s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}
, i.e.,

\boxed{D<0\leftrightarrow D\mu>0\leftrightarrow s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}<s^{2}(\widehat{\varepsilon})\leftrightarrow\mbox{``reduction of $s^{2}$\;toward\;NS\;centers''}.}

(5.46)

In both Eq. (
5.44
) and Eq. (
5.45
) the zeroth-order term is the SSS at center, therefore what we investigate is the possible crossover at zero temperature and densities realized in NSs with densities
\gtrsim 5\rho_{\mathrm{sat}}

Brandes et al. (2023)
;
Fukushima et al. (2020)
;
Fujimoto et al. (2023)
;
Zhang and Li (2023)
, with a peaked behavior in
s^{2}
(next subsection).
Besides the sum rules of (
2.51
), the stability/causality condition for any
\widehat{\varepsilon}
, i.e.,
0\leq s^{2}\leq 1
, put further constraints on the coefficients
\{d_{k}\}
:

\boxed{0\leq d_{1}+2d_{2}\widehat{\varepsilon}+3d_{3}\widehat{\varepsilon}^{2}+\cdots\leq 1,}

(5.47)

which also guarantees the pressure
\widehat{P}
never becomes negative.

For the TOV configuration
M_{\mathrm{NS}}^{\max}
on the NS M-R curve, the inequality
\mathrm{d}^{2}M_{\mathrm{NS}}^{\max}/\mathrm{d}\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}<0
gives a criterion (
4.29
) with
s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}
given by Eq. (
4.17
).
Inequality (
4.29
) implies, e.g., for PSR J0740+6620 that
\mathrm{d}s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}/\mathrm{d}{\mathrm{X}}\lesssim 2.74
using
{\mathrm{X}}\approx 0.24

Cai et al. (2023)
, which means if
{\mathrm{X}}
increases by about 0.1, the increasing of
s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}
should be smaller than 0.274.
Moreover, we obtain using the relation
\mathrm{d}s^{2}/\mathrm{d}\widehat{P}=\mathrm{d}s^{2}/\mathrm{d}\widehat{\varepsilon}\cdot\mathrm{d}\widehat{\varepsilon}/\mathrm{d}\widehat{P}=\mathrm{d}^{2}\widehat{P}/\mathrm{d}\widehat{\varepsilon}^{2}\cdot\mathrm{d}\widehat{\varepsilon}/\mathrm{d}\widehat{P}
an equivalent form of (
4.29
),

\boxed{\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}\widehat{P}}{\mathrm{d}\widehat{\varepsilon}^{2}}\right|_{\widehat{\varepsilon}=\widehat{\varepsilon}_{\mathrm{c}}=1}=\sum_{k=1}^{K}k(k-1)d_{k}=2D<\sigma_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}.}

(5.48)

The coefficients
d_{1}
and
d_{2}
via conditions of (
2.51
) are given respectively by
d_{1}=2{\mathrm{X}}-s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}+\sum_{k=3}^{K}(k-2)d_{k}
, and

\displaystyle d_{2}=-{\mathrm{X}}+s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}-\sum_{k=3}^{K}(k-1)d_{k},

(5.49)

In fact,
d_{1}
should be zero (since
s^{2}=0
for
\widehat{\varepsilon}=0
);
then we can solve for
d_{3}
in terms of other
d_{k}
’s using the previous expression for
d_{1}
:

d_{3}=-2{\mathrm{X}}+s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}-\sum_{k=4}^{K}(k-2)d_{k},

(5.50)

where we split the summation
\sum_{k=3}^{K}(k-2)d_{k}
into
d_{3}+\sum_{k=4}^{K}(k-2)d_{k}
.
Therefore the coefficient
D
becomes:

\displaystyle\boxed{D=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\frac{k(k-1)}{2}d_{k}=d_{2}+3d_{3}+\sum_{k=4}^{K}\frac{k(k-1)}{2}d_{k}=2s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}-3{\mathrm{X}}+\sum_{k=4}^{K}\frac{(k-2)(k-3)}{2}d_{k}.}

(5.51)

The first two terms in
D
are deterministic while the last one has certain randomness (characterizing the uncertainties of the dense matter EOS);
for the special case of
K=3
the coefficient
D
becomes deterministic (see the following discussions).

The probability of
D<0
could be estimated by combining the condition (
5.47
) of
0\leq s^{2}\leq 1
, inequality (
5.48
) together with the general sum rules of (
2.51
), i.e.,

\displaystyle\boxed{\mathrm{prob}(D<0)\approx\frac{\#[0\leq s^{2}\leq 1\;\mathrm{and}\;2D<\sigma_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}\;\mathrm{and}\;D<0]}{\#[0\leq s^{2}\leq 1\;\mathrm{and}\;2D<\sigma_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}]},}

(5.52)

for the TOV configuration
M_{\mathrm{NS}}^{\max}
of the NS M-R curve; here
\#
counts for the number of samples of [
\dots
].
In the following, we uniformly sample the coefficients
d_{k}
for
k\geq 4
within certain ranges fulfilling the requirements
0\leq s^{2}\leq 1
and
2D<\sigma_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}
and count the events of
D<0
to estimate the probability of
D<0
. The value of
d_{2}
could be obtained correspondingly using Eq. (
5.49
) and
d_{3}
via Eq. (
5.50
).
For a certain truncation order
K
, different empirical ranges for the parameters
d_{k}
are adopted to boost the sampling efficiency.

Considering the lower-order expansions with small
K
is beneficial for a general analysis.
The simplest situation is
K=3
, i.e.,
\widehat{P}\approx d_{1}\widehat{\varepsilon}+d_{2}\widehat{\varepsilon}^{2}+d_{3}\widehat{\varepsilon}^{3}\approx d_{2}\widehat{\varepsilon}^{2}+d_{3}\widehat{\varepsilon}^{3}
.
Correspondingly, we shall obtain
d_{2}=-s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}+3{\mathrm{X}}
and
d_{3}=s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}-2{\mathrm{X}}
,
and the condition
D<0
(see Eq. (
5.51
)) is now equivalent to
u_{\mathrm{c}}\equiv 2s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}-3{\mathrm{X}}\approx-{{\mathrm{X}}}/{3}<0
, with the approximation holding for small
{\mathrm{X}}
as we have
s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}\approx 4{\mathrm{X}}/3
.
Considering Eq. (
4.17
), we then obtain
{\mathrm{X}}\lesssim 0.105
in order to make
D<0
.
For
{\mathrm{X}}\gtrsim 0.105
, there is no space for the occurrence of
D<0
, and the transition of the probability for
D<0
from
{\mathrm{X}}\lesssim 0.105
to
{\mathrm{X}}\gtrsim 0.105
is sharp. This means that
s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}
is definitely larger than its surroundings for
{\mathrm{X}}\gtrsim 0.105
.

Due to the importance of
D
for our analysis, we explain/illustrate why the
D
tends to be negative for small
{\mathrm{X}}
using the next-order polynomial with
K=4
.
We now have two relations, namely
d_{2}+d_{3}+d_{4}={\mathrm{X}}
and
2d_{2}+3d_{3}+4d_{4}=s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}
, from which we can solve for
d_{2}
and
d_{3}
to be expressed in terms of
d_{4}
.
The general condition
0\leq s^{2}\leq 1
now reads
0\leq 2d_{2}\widehat{\varepsilon}+3d_{3}\widehat{\varepsilon}^{2}+4d_{4}\widehat{\varepsilon}^{3}\leq 1
.
Putting the expressions of
d_{2}
and
d_{3}
into it gives the upper and lower limit for
d_{4}
, i.e.,
d_{4}^{\mathrm{(l)}}\leq d_{4}\leq d_{4}^{\mathrm{(u)}}
, where

\displaystyle d_{4}^{\mathrm{(l)}}(\widehat{\varepsilon})=

\displaystyle\frac{\widehat{\varepsilon}({\mathrm{X}}-s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2})-{\mathrm{X}}+\widehat{\varepsilon}^{-1}}{4\widehat{\varepsilon}^{2}-3\widehat{\varepsilon}-1},~~d_{4}^{\mathrm{(u)}}(\widehat{\varepsilon})=\frac{\widehat{\varepsilon}({\mathrm{X}}-s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2})-{\mathrm{X}}}{4\widehat{\varepsilon}^{2}-3\widehat{\varepsilon}-1},

(5.53)

the dependence of the two quantities on
\widehat{\varepsilon}
is written explicitly.
Adding the deterministic term
u_{\mathrm{c}}=-3{\mathrm{X}}+2s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}
, we obtain correspondingly
D^{\mathrm{(u/l)}}(\widehat{\varepsilon})=u_{\mathrm{c}}+d_{4}^{\mathrm{(u/l)}}(\widehat{\varepsilon})
(see Eq. (
5.51
)).

FIG. 59:
(Color Online). Explanations on why
D
tends to be negative (positive) for small (large)
{\mathrm{X}}
, using
K=4
.See the text for details.
Figures taken from Ref.
Cai et al. (2023)
.

FIG. 60:
(Color Online). The same as FIG.
59
but adopting the Newtonian limit
s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}\approx 4{\mathrm{X}}/3
(left panel) and conformal limit
s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}\approx{\mathrm{X}}
(right panel). The principle of causality requires then
{\mathrm{X}}\lesssim 3/4
and
{\mathrm{X}}\lesssim 1
for these two situations, respectively.
Symbols and lines have the same meaning as in FIG.
59
. Figures taken from Ref.
Cai et al. (2023)
.

The results are summarized in FIG.
59
.
Here in each panel, the magenta (blue) line is for
D^{(\mathrm{u})}
(
D^{\mathrm{(l)}}
), and the orange line marks the general boundary of
2D<\sigma_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}
(see the estimate algorithm of (
5.52
)).
The positive and negative regions for
D
are shown by the grey and lavender bands, respectively.
Moreover,
D\leq D^{(\mathrm{u})}
indicates that
D
should be smaller than the lowest point
D_{1}
on the magenta line (solid green diamond in panel (c));
D\geq D^{\mathrm{(l)}}
implies that
D
should be larger than the highest point
D_{2}
on the blue line (shallow red circle in panel (c)).
When
{\mathrm{X}}
is small (such as panels (a) and (b)), the magnitude of red circle is larger than that of the greed diamond, indicating that
D
is more probable to be negative than be positive.
As
{\mathrm{X}}
increases one eventually encounters
D_{1}\approx-D_{2}
(as panel (c) shows),
D_{2}\approx 0
and
D_{2}\gtrsim 0
(panel (d)).
The situation
D_{1}\approx-D_{2}
roughly marks the value of
{\mathrm{X}}
corresponding to equal probability of
D>0
and
D<0
, this is about
{\mathrm{X}}\approx 0.3
(see FIG.
61
for simulation results).
When
{\mathrm{X}}
increases even further, the probability of
D<0
(of
D>0
) quickly decreases (increases); this is because the core matter is too stiff to be compressed further for large
{\mathrm{X}}
.
Moreover, if one artificially adopts the Newton approximation for
s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}
as
s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}\approx 4{\mathrm{X}}/3
(panel (a) of FIG.
60
) or the conformal limit
\gamma_{\mathrm{c}}=s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}/{\mathrm{X}}\approx 1\leftrightarrow s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}\approx{\mathrm{X}}
(panel (b) of FIG.
60
), then even a large
{\mathrm{X}}\approx 0.6
may still tend to induce a negative
D
(compared with FIG.
59
).
In particular, the
D
in the conformal limit is very likely negative, since both
D_{1}
and
D_{2}
are negative as indicated by the right panel of FIG.
60
.
This means if one uses a (nearly) conformal EOS as the input, then the
s^{2}(\widehat{\varepsilon})
is probably greater than
s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}
; this strongly depends on the input assumption.
In addition, if
s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}
is nearly a constant (independent of
{\mathrm{X}}
), then the condition
D<\sigma_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}/2
alone tells that
D<0
since
\sigma_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}=\mathrm{d}s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}/\mathrm{d}{\mathrm{X}}=0
(see the inequality (
4.29
)).
In this case if
s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}=1
, then its nearby
s^{2}(\widehat{\varepsilon})
keeps 1, otherwise
s^{2}(\widehat{\varepsilon})>s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}
, i.e., the core is definitely softer than its surroundings.
These results clearly show that the assumption on the input EOS may essentially affect the final inference on the EOS in NS cores, and the nonlinear dependence of
s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}
on
{\mathrm{X}}
(of Eq. (
4.17
)) is fundamental to account for the eventual change on the sign of
D
, and therefore it may influence the possible continuous crossover occurring in cores of NSs.

FIG. 61:
(Color Online). Probability of
D<0
for different
K
; dashed vertical blue line in each panel is for
{\mathrm{X}}\approx 0.24
(PSR J0740+6620) and the black horizontal dashed line is plotted at
\mathrm{prob}(D<0)=50\%
. Figures taken from Ref.
Cai et al. (2023)
.

FIG. 62:
(Color Online). Radial dependence of
s^{2}
with different NS masses. Figure taken from Ref.
Ecker and Rezzolla (2022)
.

The above examples with
K=3
and
K=4
involving the cubic and fourth-order polynomials of
\widehat{P}
over
\widehat{\varepsilon}
are two very special cases. In order to analyze the
probability of
D<0
generally, we study more cases of varying
K
as shown in FIG.
61
(each panel simulates
10^{8}
uniform samples).
It is seen that as the truncation order
K
increases, the probability of
D<0
eventually stabilizes (see panels (a) to (h) of FIG.
61
where
K
varies from 4 to 40).
One finds from the curves that for
{\mathrm{X}}\lesssim 0.3
the probability of
D<0
is larger than about 50%, indicating that the continuous crossover probably occurs near the center.
For PSR J0740+6620 with its mass
\approx 2.08\pm 0.07M_{\odot}

Fonseca et al. (2021)
;
Riley et al. (2021)
;
Miller et al. (2021)
;
Salmi et al. (2022)
;
Dittmann et al. (2024)
;
Salmi et al. (2024)
being close to the theoretically predicted maximum NS mass (see FIG.
41
) as well as
{\mathrm{X}}\approx 0.24
and
s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}\approx 0.45

Cai et al. (2023)
, this probability is found to be larger than about 63%. As
{\mathrm{X}}
increases even further, the probability of
D<0
decreases.
In the limiting case of
s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}\to 1
, this probability is extremely small.

As a temporary summary, a small
\mathrm{X}
is advantageous for generating a negative
D
and therefore a soft core. The physics reason for these behaviors could be traced back to the (deterministic) term
2s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}-3{\mathrm{X}}\approx-{\mathrm{X}}/3
in
D
of Eq. (
5.51
), which is negative for small
{\mathrm{X}}
, though the summation term of Eq. (
5.51
) may be either positive or negative (EOS uncertainties).
It indicates that
\mathrm{X}
is the relevant quantity for the onset of the continuous crossover (
s^{2}(\widehat{\varepsilon})>s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}
) at zero temperature and high densities (in cores of cold NSs)
Brandes et al. (2023)
;
Fukushima et al. (2020)
;
Fujimoto et al. (2023)
;
Zhang and Li (2023)
. It is likely to happen for large (therefore the
{\mathrm{X}}
is relatively small) and massive NSs (so they are near the TOV configuration).
At the Newtonian limit
s_{\mathrm{c}}\approx 4{\mathrm{X}}/3
, since
u_{\mathrm{c}}=-3{\mathrm{X}}+2s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}\approx-{\mathrm{X}}/3
the coefficient
D
tends more likely to be negative (see the left panel of FIG.
60
). In this case, we expect that the probability of
s^{2}(\widehat{\varepsilon})>s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}
is large.
However, as we demonstrated in Subsection
5.4
the
s^{2}
is probably monotonic for Newtonian stars, while the GR effects tend to reduce the probability
s^{2}(\widehat{\varepsilon})>s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}
for small
{\mathrm{X}}
.
The problem arises from the neglecting of
\Psi
of Eq. (
4.16
): For NSs not at TOV configuration (Newtonian stars) on the M-R curve, one has
\mathrm{d}^{2}M_{\mathrm{NS}}/\mathrm{d}\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}<0
and therefore the criterion (
5.52
) holds.
However, the deterministic term
u_{\mathrm{c}}=2s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}-3{\mathrm{X}}
in the coefficient
D
of Eq. (
5.51
) should be modified to,

u_{\mathrm{c}}=2s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}-3{\mathrm{X}}\approx-\frac{1-2\Psi}{3}{\mathrm{X}},~~\Psi>0,

(5.54)

by expanding Eq. (
4.15
) over
{\mathrm{X}}
as
s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}\approx(4+\Psi){\mathrm{X}}/3
(Newtonian limit).
A positive
\Psi
tends to make the correction
D
positive and therefore reduce the probability of
s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}<s^{2}(\widehat{\varepsilon})
(see Eq. (
5.45
)).
For example, a canonical NS has
\Psi\approx 2.85
(see estimate given in Subsection
4.6
) and is less likely to have the crossover in the core than a massive NS (assuming they have similar radii).
The above analysis is consistent with that Ref.
Ecker and Rezzolla (2022)
which predicted that the increasing of
s^{2}
when going out from the NS center is reduced even to disappear as the NS mass decreases from
M_{\mathrm{NS}}^{\max}
to about
1.4M_{\odot}
, as shown in FIG.
62
, indicating a peaked
s^{2}
only exists in massive NSs.
On the other hand, for massive NSs with similar radii of PSR J0740+6620 (thus the
{\mathrm{X}}
’s are also similar), the reduction of the probability for
D<0
due to a (small) positive
\Psi
is expected to be small as they are near the TOV configuration.
We investigate the problem more generally in Subsection
5.9
and find that a sizable
\mathrm{X}
neither being too small (near Newtonian limit) nor too large (near the causality limit
0.374
) is necessary and relevant to generating a peaked
s^{2}
radial profile.

FIG. 63:
(Color Online). Averages of
2a_{2}D/s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}
and
2a_{2}D_{-}/s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}
as functions of
{\mathrm{X}}
(panel (a)) and coefficients
D
and
J
(panel (b)) defined in Eq. (
5.44
) and Eq. (
5.58
), respectively, the vertical dashed line is at
{\mathrm{X}}=0.24
.
Figures taken from Ref.
Cai et al. (2023)
.

According to Eq. (
5.44
), the deviation of SSS from its central value is proportional to
2a_{2}D\widehat{r}^{2}
. To evaluate the relative changing rate of SSS from its central value,
we show in panel (a) of FIG.
63
the
\langle 2a_{2}D/s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}\rangle
defined as

\left\langle{2a_{2}D}/{s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}}\right\rangle=\left({2a_{2}}/{s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}}\right)\sum_{k=\pm}\mathrm{prob}(D_{k})D_{k},

(5.55)

here
D_{k}=D_{\pm}
denotes the value of
D>0
or
D<0
. For a comparison, the average value of
2a_{2}D_{-}/s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}
is also shown (blue dashed line); we then have
\langle 2a_{2}D/s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}\rangle\approx 1.6
for PSR J0740+6620
Fonseca et al. (2021)
;
Riley et al. (2021)
;
Miller et al. (2021)
;
Salmi et al. (2022)
;
Dittmann et al. (2024)
;
Salmi et al. (2024)
with
{\mathrm{X}}\approx 0.24
, i.e.,
s^{2}(\widehat{r})/s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}\approx 1+1.6\widehat{r}^{2}
for small
\widehat{r}
.
Transforming it back using Eq. (
5.45
) gives us
s^{2}(\widehat{\varepsilon})/s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}\approx 1+2(D/s_{\mathrm{s}}^{2})\mu\approx 1-2\mu=3-2\widehat{\varepsilon}
, here
\widehat{\varepsilon}
being very close to 1 is assumed and
D/s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}\approx-1
for PSR J0740+6620 is adopted.
Equivalently, we can rewrite the radial-dependence of the SSS by using Eq. (
4.30
) approximately as (by neglecting the intercept
0.64
),

{s^{2}}/{s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}}\approx 1+\frac{9.4{\mathrm{X}}\langle a_{2}D/s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}\rangle}{1+3{\mathrm{X}}^{2}+4{\mathrm{X}}}\left(\frac{r}{R_{\max}}\right)^{2},

(5.56)

so numerically
s^{2}/s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}\approx 1+0.85(r/R_{\max})^{2}
for PSR J0740+6620.

FIG. 64:
(Color Online). Qualitative sketch of the reduction of
\widehat{P}
,
\widehat{\varepsilon}
,
\widehat{P}/\widehat{\varepsilon}
and
\widehat{\rho}
(these quantities characterize the compactness/denseness of the matter) to order
\widehat{r}^{2}
and the probable increasing of
s^{2}
(characterizing the stiffness of the matter) near NS centers.
Figure taken from Ref.
Cai et al. (2023)
.

We summarize the radial dependence of the relevant quantities in FIG.
64
. According to the perturbative expressions for the energy density
\varepsilon
of Eq. (
2.22
), the pressure
P
of Eq. (
2.23
), their ratio
P/\varepsilon

Cai and Li (2024)
(see also equations of (
4.56
) and (
4.57
)) and the baryon density
\rho
(see Eq. (
4.54
)), they are all decreasing functions of
\widehat{r}
definitely.
While on the other hand, the SSS
s^{2}
has sizable probabilities to be enhanced when going outward from NS centers, characterized by the coefficient
D
, see sketches shown in FIG.
64
.
This demonstrates the fundamental difference between the stiffness (characterized effectively by
s^{2}
) and the denseness (characterized effectively by
\rho
,
P
,
\varepsilon
and
\phi=P/\varepsilon
) or compactness (characterized by
\xi=M_{\mathrm{NS}}/R
). Although they are closely related, see FIG.
111
and relevant discussions around Eq. (
5.40
), the compactness
\mathrm{X}
(the average SSS used in Ref.
Saes et al. (2024)
) is physically equivalent to the stiffness
s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}
only at NS centers, as
s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}
is monotonically varying with
\mathrm{X}
(see Eq. (
4.15
) or Eq. (
4.17
)), so a denser NS (
\xi
or
\mathrm{X}
is larger) has a larger
s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}
.
In another word regarding such attribute, the stiffness at places away from the center (characterized by
s^{2}
instead of
s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}
) is fundamentally different from the compactness. Moreover, the stiffness is basically different from the compactness; an illustrative example is that
s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}
(of Eq. (
4.17
)) approaches
1/3
(stiffness) earlier than
{\mathrm{X}}\to 1/3
(compactness).
Similarly, while
\mathrm{X}
is physically equivalent to
\xi
when describing the compactness, the ratio
\phi
is generally not equivalent to
\xi
although they are closely related. In particular, Eq. (
4.57
) gives
\phi\approx\mathrm{X}+b_{2}\widehat{r}^{2}(1-\gamma_{\mathrm{c}}^{-1})
, we then have
\phi_{(1)}-\phi_{(2)}\approx\mathrm{X}_{(1)}-\mathrm{X}_{(2)}+\widehat{r}^{2}[b_{2}^{(1)}-b_{2}^{(2)}-(b_{2}^{(1)}/\gamma_{\mathrm{c}}^{(1)}-b_{2}^{(2)}/\gamma_{\mathrm{c}}^{(2)})]
for two NSs labeled by (1) and (2), respectively, at
\widehat{r}
.
The relation between
\phi_{(1)}
and
\phi_{(2)}
is not definite even though that between
\mathrm{X}_{(1)}
and
\mathrm{X}_{(2)}
is;
e.g., a NS with larger
\mathrm{X}
may have a smaller
\phi
at a finite
\widehat{r}
, or vice versa.
Therefore, a larger
\xi
corresponds to a larger
\mathrm{X}
, but it is not necessarily equivalent to a larger
\phi
at a finite
\widehat{r}
.
For a given NS,
\phi
monotonically changes with
\mathrm{X}
at a fixed
\widehat{r}
(at least near the center) since
b_{2}(1-\gamma_{\mathrm{c}}^{-1})>0
.

5.8
2^{\mathrm{nd}}
-order expansion of SSS in reduced energy density: where is its peak located?

The case of
D<0
studied in the last subsection implies that there should exist a peak in
s^{2}
somewhere along the path away from the center. Since
\varepsilon
is a decreasing function of
\widehat{r}
, the peak of
s^{2}
is located at an energy density
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{pk}}<\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}
, e.g., Ref.
Takátsy et al. (2023)
predicted that
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{pk}}\approx 565\,\mathrm{MeV}/\mathrm{fm}^{3}
or
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{pk}}/\varepsilon_{0}\approx 3.8
for massive NSs.
Take our over-simplified EOS with truncation order
K=3
as an example, the peak
\widehat{\varepsilon}_{\mathrm{pk}}
of
s^{2}(\widehat{\varepsilon})
can be obtained as
\widehat{\varepsilon}_{\mathrm{pk}}\approx 5/6+{\mathrm{X}}+7{\mathrm{X}}^{2}/2+13{\mathrm{X}}^{3}\gtrsim 5/6
and
s^{2}(\widehat{\varepsilon}_{\mathrm{pk}})\approx 25{\mathrm{X}}/18+5{\mathrm{X}}^{2}/9+17{\mathrm{X}}^{3}/6
(with
{\mathrm{X}}
being small
\lesssim 0.105
) using
s^{2}\approx 2(3{\mathrm{X}}-s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2})\widehat{\varepsilon}+3(s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}-2{\mathrm{X}})\widehat{\varepsilon}^{3}
.
Obviously, the peak of
s^{2}
could not be estimated through Eq. (
5.45
) since it is linear in
\widehat{\varepsilon}
and the higher-order terms, e.g.,
a_{4}
,
b_{4}
and
a_{6}
, etc., are necessary for such estimate.

We now use our perturbative expansions as a tool and include these higher-order contributions to estimate the peak of
s^{2}
(near NS centers) if it exists.
When considering terms including order
b_{6}
-coefficient in the expansion of
\widehat{P}
of Eq. (
2.23
) and the
a_{6}
-term in the expansion of
\widehat{\varepsilon}
of Eq. (
2.22
), we derive the SSS from (
2.33
) that,

\displaystyle s^{2}/s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}\approx

\displaystyle 1+\frac{2}{b_{2}}\left(b_{4}-s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}a_{4}\right)\widehat{r}^{2}+\underbrace{\frac{3}{b_{2}}\left[\left(b_{6}-s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}a_{6}\right)-\frac{4}{3}\frac{a_{4}}{a_{2}}\left(b_{4}-s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}a_{4}\right)\right]\widehat{r}^{4}}_{\mbox{term relevant for estimating the peak}}.

(5.57)

The coefficient
a_{6}
could be similarly eliminated as
a_{4}
: expanding the reduced pressure
\widehat{P}=\sum_{k=1}^{K}d_{k}\widehat{\varepsilon}^{k}
with
\widehat{\varepsilon}=1+\sum_{k=1}^{K}a_{k}\widehat{r}^{k}
and comparing the coefficient of
\widehat{r}^{6}
in the expansion
\widehat{P}={\mathrm{X}}+\sum_{k=1}^{K}b_{k}\widehat{r}^{k}
.
The result is
b_{6}-s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}a_{6}=a_{2}^{3}J+2a_{2}a_{4}D
,
where
J
is a quantity constructed from
d_{k}
’s defined in the expansion
\widehat{P}=\sum_{k=1}^{K}d_{k}\widehat{\varepsilon}^{k}
,

\boxed{J=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\frac{k(k-1)(k-2)}{6}d_{k}=d_{3}+4d_{4}+10d_{5}+\cdots.}

(5.58)

The coefficient
J
has certain randomness like the coefficient
D
(characterizing the uncertainties of the dense matter EOS).
We can similarly understand qualitatively the coefficient
J
by using the polynomial
\widehat{P}
of
\widehat{\varepsilon}
with order 4.
Under this assumption, we have
J=3d_{4}+(s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}-{\mathrm{X}})/3
where
d_{4}^{\mathrm{(l)}}\leq d_{4}\leq d_{4}^{\mathrm{(u)}}
of (
5.53
), and therefore
J^{\mathrm{(l)}}\leq J\leq J^{\mathrm{(u)}}
.
As shown in FIG.
65
, we probably have
J<0
though the allowed region of
J<0
(of
J>0
) eventually shrinks (expands) as
{\mathrm{X}}
increases.
We can rewrite the radial variation of
s^{2}
using
J
as,

s^{2}(\widehat{r})\approx s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}+2a_{2}D\widehat{r}^{2}+\left(3a_{2}^{2}J+2a_{4}D\right)\widehat{r}^{4}.

(5.59)

Taking the derivative
\mathrm{d}s^{2}/\mathrm{d}\widehat{r}
and setting it to zero locates the peak of
s^{2}
to

\boxed{\widehat{r}_{\mathrm{pk}}=\left(-\frac{a_{2}D}{3a_{2}^{2}J+2a_{4}D}\right)^{1/2}.}

(5.60)

Here
a_{2}D>0
, so the expression under square root is positive if
J<-2a_{4}D/3a_{2}^{2}
(with the latter being positive since
a_{4}D<0
).
A further quantitative estimate for
\widehat{r}_{\mathrm{pk}}
depends strongly on the coefficients
a_{4}
,
D
and
J
.

FIG. 65:
(Color Online). The same as FIG.
59
but for the coefficient
J
of Eq. (
5.58
).
Figures taken from Ref.
Cai et al. (2023)
.

The
\widehat{r}
-dependence could be transferred to the
\mu
-dependence.
Inverting
\widehat{\varepsilon}\approx 1+a_{2}\widehat{r}^{2}+a_{4}\widehat{r}^{4}+a_{6}\widehat{r}^{6}
gives us
\widehat{r}^{2}\approx(\mu/a_{2})[1-(a_{4}/a_{2}^{2})\mu+(2a_{4}^{2}/a_{2}^{4}-a_{6}/a_{2}^{3})\mu^{2}]
, where
\mu\equiv\widehat{\varepsilon}-1<0
.
Putting this
\widehat{r}^{2}
into the full expression for
s^{2}(\widehat{r})
to order
\widehat{r}^{4}
of Eq. (
5.59
) enables us to write the
s^{2}(\widehat{\varepsilon})=s^{2}(\mu)
as,

\displaystyle s^{2}(\mu)\approx

\displaystyle s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}+2D\mu+3J\mu^{2}-\frac{2}{a_{2}^{2}}\left({3a_{4}}J+\frac{a_{6}}{a_{2}}D\right)\mu^{3},

(5.61)

which generalizes (
5.45
).
Thus we have to order
\mathcal{O}(\mu^{3})
,

\displaystyle\widehat{P}(\mu)\approx

\displaystyle{\mathrm{X}}+s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}\mu+\left.\frac{1}{2}\frac{\mathrm{d}s^{2}}{\mathrm{d}\mu}\right|_{\mu=0}\mu^{2}+\left.\frac{1}{6}\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}s^{2}}{\mathrm{d}\mu^{2}}\right|_{\mu=0}\mu^{3}\approx{\mathrm{X}}+s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}\mu+D\mu^{2}+J\mu^{3}.

(5.62)

In fact, Eq. (
5.62
) could be obtained straightforwardly via the expansion
\widehat{P}=\sum_{k=1}^{K}d_{k}\widehat{\varepsilon}^{k}
.
However, the coefficient
J
(Eq. (
5.58
)) now like the coefficient
D
(Eq. (
5.51
)) is not totally random, but is constrained through the general requirements
0\leq s^{2}\leq 1
and
\sigma_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}>2D
used in the algorithm of Eq. (
5.52
), i.e., the randomness of
J
and
D
is effectively reduced by the physical constraints.
Therefore, we can estimate
J
in a similar manner as for
D
.
Taking
{\mathrm{X}}\approx 0.24
(for PSR J0740+6620) we obtain an estimate
J\approx-2.7
(see panel (b) of FIG.
63
).
Combining
s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}\approx 0.45
and
D\approx-0.45
(panel (b) of FIG.
63
) for
{\mathrm{X}}\approx 0.24
, the
\widehat{P}(\widehat{\varepsilon})
is found to be an increasing function of
\widehat{\varepsilon}
for
\widehat{\varepsilon}\gtrsim 0.7
. It basically excludes the possibility of having a plateau in
\widehat{P}
at high densities near the center.
Including the last term in Eq. (
5.61
) may slightly shift
\widehat{\varepsilon}\gtrsim 0.7
to a higher value, here
a_{6}
could be estimated via
b_{6}-s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}a_{6}=a_{2}^{3}J+2a_{2}a_{4}D
with the expression for coefficient
b_{6}
given in Eq. (
2.32
).
Moreover, the
D
-term and
J
-term contribute to
s^{2}(\mu)
(and also
\widehat{P}(\mu)
) with different signs (since
\mu<0
) for
{\mathrm{X}}\lesssim 0.3
(panel (b) of FIG.
63
), explaining the existence of a peak in
s^{2}(\mu)
.
One finds the peak of
s^{2}(\mu)
from Eq. (
5.61
) (neglecting the last term) as,

\boxed{\mu_{\mathrm{pk}}\approx-D/3J.}

(5.63)

We may find later that the possible peak in the derivative part of
s^{2}
is shifted to a slightly lower value (see Eq. (
5.66
) of the next subsection), being consistent with our general analysis given in Subsection
5.3
.
Considering that
D/J
is positive and smaller than 1 for
\mathrm{X}\lesssim 0.3
, we may expand Eq. (
5.60
) as:

\widehat{r}_{\mathrm{pk}}\approx\sqrt{-\frac{1}{3a_{2}}\frac{D}{J}}\cdot\left(1-\frac{a_{4}}{3a_{2}^{3/2}}\frac{D}{J}\right),

(5.64)

here
a_{4}/3a_{2}^{3/2}\lesssim\mathcal{O}(1)
.
Therefore
a_{2}\widehat{r}_{\mathrm{pk}}^{2}\approx-D/3J\approx\mu_{\mathrm{pk}}
, as expected.

FIG. 66:
(Color Online). Key quantities for estimating the strength and location of a peak in
s^{2}
profile either for
s^{2}(\widehat{\varepsilon})
or
s^{2}(\widehat{r})
,
the enhancement of
s^{2}
, i.e.,
\Delta s^{2}=s^{2}(\widehat{r}_{\mathrm{pk}})-s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}=-D^{2}/(3J+2a_{4}D/a_{2}^{2})
and its approximation
15\cdot(-D^{2}/3J)=-5D^{2}/J
are shown; the coefficient
D
becomes averagely negative when
{\mathrm{X}}\gtrsim 0.3
(see FIG.
63
); the truncation order of the expansions is
K=8
.
Figure taken from Ref.
Cai et al. (2023)
.

(Color Online). Left panel: radial dependence of
s^{2}
(lower plot) with the constraint
M_{\mathrm{TOV}}/M_{\odot}\gtrsim 2.35
. Figure taken from Ref.
Ecker and Rezzolla (2022)
. Right panel: density dependence of
s^{2}
considering the
restriction
\mathrm{d}M_{\mathrm{NS}}/\mathrm{d}R<0
for all NS mass range (blue) or
\mathrm{d}M_{\mathrm{NS}}/\mathrm{d}R\geq 0
for a certain mass range (red) on the M-R curve.
Figure taken from Ref.
Ferreira and Providência (2024)
.

FIG. 68:
(Color Online). Left panel:
s^{2}
as a function of baryon density (first line) for self-bound quark stars and NSs. Figure taken from Ref.
Cao and Chen (2023)
. Right panel:
s^{2}
inferred by incorporating multi-messenger data of GW170817, PSR J0030+0451, PSR J0740+6620, and state-of-the-art theoretical progresses, including the information from CEFT and pQCD calculations. Figure taken from Ref.
Han et al. (2023)
.

As an illustration, we take
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}\approx 901\,\mathrm{MeV}/\mathrm{fm}^{3}
(which is the central energy density for PSR J0740+6620)
Cai et al. (2023)
and
R\approx 12.39\,\mathrm{km}

Riley et al. (2021)
while let
{\mathrm{X}}
be a free parameter to demonstrate the numerical results. The radius length is obtained as
Q=(4\pi G\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}})^{-1/2}\approx 8.7\,\mathrm{km}
; then
0\leq\widehat{r}Q/R\leq 1
. Shown in FIG.
66
are our estimates for the peak in
s^{2}
for either
s^{2}(\widehat{\varepsilon})
or
s^{2}(\widehat{r})
.
Specifically,
\widehat{\varepsilon}_{\mathrm{pk}}=1+\mu_{\mathrm{pk}}\approx 1-D/3J
using Eq. (
5.63
) and
\widehat{r}_{\mathrm{pk}}
is given by Eq. (
5.60
). We find the peak is very near the NS center, namely
\widehat{\varepsilon}_{\mathrm{pk}}\gtrsim 0.92
and
r_{\mathrm{pk}}/R=\widehat{r}_{\mathrm{pk}}Q/R\lesssim 0.15
.
The enhancement of
s^{2}
, i.e.,
\Delta s^{2}=s^{2}(\widehat{r}_{\mathrm{pk}})-s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}=-D^{2}/(3J+2a_{4}D/a_{2}^{2})
using Eq. (
5.59
) is also shown in FIG.
66
, where
a_{4}\approx 1
is adopted for illustration.
Considering that
|2a_{4}D/a_{2}^{2}|\ll|3J|
(see FIG.
63
), we have approximately
\Delta s^{2}\approx-D^{2}/3J
or directly using Eq. (
5.63
) as
\Delta s^{2}\approx 2D\mu_{\mathrm{pk}}+3J\mu_{\mathrm{pk}}^{2}\approx-D^{2}/3J
, see the cyan dash-dotted line for
15\cdot(-D^{2}/3J)=-5D^{2}/J
.
The difference between
\Delta s^{2}
using Eq. (
5.59
) and Eq. (
5.61
) is that
Eq. (
5.59
) is full to order
\widehat{r}^{4}
while Eq. (
5.61
) is an approximation (even the
\mu^{3}
-term is included).
It is seen that as the
{\mathrm{X}}
increases, the
\widehat{\varepsilon}_{\mathrm{pk}}
eventually approaches 1, implying that the peak
\widehat{\varepsilon}_{\mathrm{pk}}
eventually moves to the center, and it may even disappear if the
{\mathrm{X}}
increases further (causality limit).
Actually for
{\mathrm{X}}\gtrsim 0.3
(see panel (b) of FIG.
63
), the
D
changes from being negative to positive and therefore both terms
2D\mu
and
3J\mu^{2}
in
s^{2}(\mu)
become negative,
i.e.,
s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}
is larger than its surroundings.
This feature is also reflected in
\Delta s^{2}
or
\widehat{r}_{\mathrm{pk}}Q/R
. For example, we have for
{\mathrm{X}}\to 0.374
that
s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}\to 1
(Eq. (
4.17
)) and therefore
\Delta s^{2}\to 0
(no space under this limit for
s^{2}
to be enhanced when going outward from the center).
In our illustration, we find
(\Delta s^{2})_{\max}\approx 0.038
occurring at about
{\mathrm{X}}\approx 0.16
, therefore
(\Delta s^{2})_{\max}/s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}\approx 14\%
using
s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}\approx 0.26
.
Similarly, we have
\Delta s^{2}/s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}\approx 5\%
for
{\mathrm{X}}\approx 0.24
.
The enhancement of
s^{2}
due to the peak is generally smaller than about 20%; and it is consistent with predictions of Refs.
Brandes et al. (2023)
;
Cao and Chen (2023)
;
Ferreira and Providência (2024)
.
These conclusions and numerical values would be verified in Subsection
5.9
using a slightly different but equivalently analysis.

FIG. 69:
(Color Online). Upper panel: the SSS in NSs using machine learning algorithms under different inference schemes.
Figure taken from Ref.
Fujimoto et al. (2024)
.
Lower panel: a similar prediction on
s^{2}
using deep neural network algorithms. Figure taken from Ref.
Zhou et al. (2024)
.

We compare our estimates on the peak position and the ones existing in the literature: our
r_{\mathrm{pk}}/R\lesssim 0.15
is different from the one given by Ref.
Ecker and Rezzolla (2022)
, which found that the peak of
s^{2}
for TOV NSs under the constraint
M_{\mathrm{NS}}^{\max}\gtrsim 2.35M_{\odot}
is at about 2/3 of its radius, i.e.,
r_{\mathrm{pk}}/R\approx 2/3
, see the left panel of FIG.
67
(see also FIG.
62
).
Recently, Ref.
Ferreira and Providência (2024)
found that the peak position in
s^{2}
is at about (4-5)
\rho_{\mathrm{sat}}
considering the constraint
\mathrm{d}M_{\mathrm{NS}}/\mathrm{d}R<0
for all NS mass range (blue band in the right panel of FIG.
67
), being much closer to NS centers than the previous predictions of the peak position at about
3\rho_{\mathrm{sat}}

Altiparmak et al. (2022)
;
Ecker and Rezzolla (2022)
.
Similarly, Ref.
Cao and Chen (2023)
found
\rho_{\mathrm{pk}}/\rho_{\mathrm{c}}\approx 0.98
via
\rho_{\mathrm{c}}\approx 0.56\,\mathrm{fm}^{-3}
as well as
\rho_{\mathrm{pk}}\approx 0.55\,\mathrm{fm}^{-3}
for NSs with masses
\gtrsim 2M_{\odot}
, thus
\widehat{\varepsilon}_{\mathrm{pk}}\lesssim 0.98
using the approximation (
4.55
); the result is shown in the left panel of FIG.
68
.
By incorporating multi-messenger data of GW170817, PSR J0030+0451, PSR J0740+6620, and state-of-the-art theoretical progresses including the information from CEFT and pQCD calculations, the
s^{2}
profile is predicted and the peak is found to be located at
\rho\lesssim 5\rho_{\mathrm{sat}}

Han et al. (2023)
, shown in the right panel of FIG.
68
.
A Bayesian analysis
Brandes et al. (2023)
utilizing the CEFT and astrophysical constraints especially the PSR J052-0607 predicted the peak in
s^{2}
is wide and very close to the center.
In the upper panel of FIG.
69
,
the energy density dependence of
s^{2}
using machine learning algorithms is shown adopting different inference schemes, one finds that
\widehat{\varepsilon}_{\mathrm{pk}}\gtrsim 4
.
A similar investigation on
s^{2}
using deep neural network algorithms also find the peak in
s^{2}
(if existing) is at about (4-5)
\varepsilon_{0}

Zhou et al. (2024)
, see the lower panel of FIG.
69
(blue bands).
These comparisons suggest that accurately determining the peak position in
s^{2}
will require further efforts, particularly using precise astrophysical data, such as observations of NS masses/radii, alongside advanced inference algorithms.

Our above qualitative and quantitative analyses/estimates on the peak of
s^{2}
as well as the qualitative predictions for the
\widehat{P}(\widehat{\varepsilon})=\widehat{P}(\mu)
are expected to be (nearly) model independent. This is because we rely only on (without using extra assumptions) the general conditions/requirements to make the prediction, these include the inequality
0\leq s^{2}\leq 1
of (
5.47
), the inequality (
5.48
), the sum rules of (
2.51
) together with formula (
4.17
) and the bounds on the high-order coefficients such as
a_{4}
(see Subsection
4.3
) and
a_{6}
, etc.
It is necessary to emphasize that due to its perturbative nature, however, the above analysis on the
\widehat{\varepsilon}
-dependence of
s^{2}
(or the signature of the continuous crossover) is only effective and qualitatively reasonable at finite distances
\widehat{r}
being very close to NS centers (
\widehat{r}=0
).
Whether there exist sharp PTs at some large distances away from the centers (or at certain intermediate energy density
\varepsilon
) could not be excluded immediately. This issue should be in principle analyzed by including more higher-order terms in the expansions, and the results may have certain model dependence.
Furthermore,
\widehat{P}(\mu)<{\mathrm{X}}
is definite for
\mu\approx 0^{-}
considering Eq. (
5.62
) which is fundamentally different from
s^{2}(\mu)
of Eq. (
5.61
), since
D
still has sizable probabilities to be positive and thus
s^{2}(\mu)<s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}
, see the sketch given in FIG.
64
.

We use the perturbative expansion of
P/\varepsilon
and Eq. (
5.61
) to explain the existence of a peak in the derivative part
-t
of the SSS
Fujimoto et al. (2022)
, according to the trace anomaly decomposition.
Dividing Eq. (
5.62
) by
\mu=\widehat{\varepsilon}-1
gives the ratio
P/\varepsilon
to order
\mu^{2}
as
P/\varepsilon\approx{\mathrm{X}}-t_{\mathrm{c}}\mu+(D+t_{\mathrm{c}})\mu^{2}
where
t_{\mathrm{c}}={\mathrm{X}}-s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}
.
Consequently, the derivative part
-t
of
s^{2}
is obtained as to order
\mu^{2}
:

-t(\widehat{\varepsilon})=-t(\mu)\approx-t_{\mathrm{c}}+\left(2D+t_{\mathrm{c}}\right)\mu+\left(3J-D-t_{\mathrm{c}}\right)\mu^{2},

(5.65)

from which the peak could be found,

\mu_{\mathrm{pk}}^{(-t)}=\widehat{\varepsilon}_{\mathrm{pk}}^{(-t)}-1=\frac{1}{2}\frac{2D+t_{\mathrm{c}}}{D+t_{\mathrm{c}}-3J}.

(5.66)

Taking for example
{\mathrm{X}}\approx 0.16\mbox{$\sim$}0.30
(being relevant for PSR J0740+6620), this peak is shown to be located at about
-\mu_{\mathrm{pk}}^{(-t)}\approx 10\%\mbox{$\sim$}5\%
(see discussions given after Eq. (
5.62
) for the estimate on
D
and
J
).

On the other hand, using the expansion
\phi=P/\varepsilon=s_{\mathrm{non-deriv}}^{2}
just given here, we deduce that
t_{\mathrm{c}}/2(D+t_{\mathrm{c}})>0
, i.e., there would be no peak in the non-derivative part of
s^{2}
, or this part is an increasing function of
\varepsilon

Fujimoto et al. (2022)
.
The value of
-t
at
\mu_{\mathrm{pk}}^{(-t)}
is,

-t_{\mathrm{pk}}\equiv-t(\mu_{\mathrm{pk}}^{(-t)})=\frac{4D^{2}+12Jt_{\mathrm{c}}-3t_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}}{4D-12J+4t_{\mathrm{c}}}.

(5.67)

Then, we have
-t_{\mathrm{pk}}\approx 0.15\mbox{$\sim$}0.35
and the non-derivative part (of
s^{2}
), namely
\phi=P/\varepsilon\approx 0.14\mbox{$\sim$}0.28
for
{\mathrm{X}}\approx 0.16\mbox{$\sim$}0.30
. Moreover, comparing
\mu_{\mathrm{pk}}^{(-t)}
of Eq. (
5.66
) and
\mu_{\mathrm{pk}}=-D/3J
of
s^{2}(\mu)
(see Eq. (
5.63
)) leads us to

\boxed{\mu_{\mathrm{pk}}-\mu_{\mathrm{pk}}^{(-t)}=\frac{2}{9}\left(\frac{D}{J}\right)^{2}\cdot\frac{1+t_{\mathrm{c}}/D+3Jt_{\mathrm{c}}/2D^{2}}{1-D/3J-t_{\mathrm{c}}/3J}>0.}

(5.68)

This means the location of the peak in
s^{2}
(i.e.,
\mu_{\mathrm{pk}}
) occurs at a higher energy density than that of the peak in
-t
(i.e.,
\mu_{\mathrm{pk}}^{(-t)}
), see our general discussions given in Subsection
5.3
. Equivalently, the peak in
s^{2}
is closer to the NS center than the peak in
-t
.
When higher order terms of
\mu
are included in the expansions, the specific location of
\mu_{\mathrm{pk}}
and
\mu_{\mathrm{pk}}^{(-t)}
may vary, however the relation
\mu_{\mathrm{pk}}-\mu_{\mathrm{pk}}^{(-t)}>0
would not be changed.

5.9 Strong-field gravity extruding a peak in SSS profile: analytical and numerical demonstrations

In a few subsections above, we studied the sign of
s^{2}(\widehat{\varepsilon})-s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}
and the possible peak in
s^{2}
profile by simulating the coefficients
D
and
J
. By doing so, several useful properties of the dense matter in NS cores have been revealed, e.g., how the coefficient
D
affects the appearance of a peaked structure in
s^{2}
; how the coefficients
D
and
J
, both characterizing the dense matter EOS, are related with each other, etc.
These aspects more or less involve the treatments of the dense matter EOS via the
D
and
J
.
In order to investigate the physical origin of a peaked
s^{2}
more apparently and with the least EOS-model dependence, we may start from the dimensionless TOV equations straightforwardly and make as little use as possible of the dense matter EOS. The Eq. (
5.57
) for SSS to order
\widehat{r}^{4}
provides a useful starting point for this purpose. In the following, we summarize how we did this originally in Ref.
Cai and Li (2024)
.

Compared with the Newtonian case discussed in Subsection
5.4
, the strong GR effects bring two modifications to
s^{2}
: (a)
{\mathrm{X}}
can be sizable
\gtrsim\mathcal{O}(0.1)
in NSs; and (b) the expression for
s^{2}
is changed using the GR version (of Eq. (
5.9
)).
Parallel to Eq. (
5.31
), we obtain
\mathrm{d}s^{2}/\mathrm{d}\widehat{\varepsilon}
with GR as
Cai and Li (2024)
,

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}s^{2}}{\mathrm{d}\widehat{\varepsilon}}=

\displaystyle\frac{Y}{1-2\widehat{M}/\widehat{r}}\left\{\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}s^{2}}{\mathrm{d}\widehat{\varepsilon}}\right)_{\mathrm{N}}-\frac{\widehat{\varepsilon}\widehat{M}}{1-2\widehat{M}/\widehat{r}}\frac{2}{\widehat{r}^{4}}\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}\widehat{r}}{\mathrm{d}\widehat{\varepsilon}}\right)^{2}\left(\widehat{r}^{3}\widehat{\varepsilon}-\widehat{M}\right)\right.

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt\left.-\frac{\widehat{\varepsilon}}{\widehat{M}}\frac{\mathrm{d}\widehat{r}}{\mathrm{d}\widehat{\varepsilon}}\left(1+\frac{\widehat{r}^{3}\widehat{P}}{\widehat{M}}\right)^{-1}\left[\widehat{r}\widehat{M}s^{2}+\widehat{P}\frac{\mathrm{d}\widehat{r}}{\mathrm{d}\widehat{\varepsilon}}\left(3\widehat{M}-\widehat{r}^{3}\widehat{\varepsilon}\right)\right]-\frac{\widehat{M}}{\widehat{r}^{2}}\frac{\mathrm{d}\widehat{r}}{\mathrm{d}\widehat{\varepsilon}}\left(1+\frac{\widehat{P}}{\widehat{\varepsilon}}\right)^{-1}\left(s^{2}-\frac{\widehat{P}}{\widehat{\varepsilon}}\right)\right\},

(5.69)

where
Y=(1+\widehat{P}/\widehat{\varepsilon})(1+\widehat{r}^{3}\widehat{P}/\widehat{M})
(not to be confused with
\mathrm{Y}\equiv\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}/\varepsilon_{0}
introduced earlier for the reduced central energy density) and
(\mathrm{d}s^{2}/\mathrm{d}\widehat{\varepsilon})_{\mathrm{N}}>0
is given by Eq. (
5.31
).
In Eq. (
5.69
), besides
3\widehat{M}-\widehat{r}^{3}\widehat{\varepsilon}>0
(proved after Eq. (
5.31
)) and
s^{2}-\widehat{P}/\widehat{\varepsilon}=s^{2}-P/\varepsilon=s^{2}-\phi>0
is the derivative part of
s^{2}

Fujimoto et al. (2022)
, we also have (for small
\widehat{r}
),

\widehat{r}^{3}\widehat{\varepsilon}-\widehat{M}\approx\frac{2\widehat{r}^{3}}{3}\left(1+\frac{6}{5}a_{2}\widehat{r}^{2}\right)>0.

(5.70)

This means each term in Eq. (
5.69
) contributes to
\mathrm{d}s^{2}/\mathrm{d}\widehat{\varepsilon}
with definite sign.

The two terms in Eq. (
5.69
) contributing negatively to the derivative
\mathrm{d}s^{2}/\mathrm{d}\widehat{\varepsilon}
can be combined as,

\displaystyle-\frac{Y}{1-2\widehat{M}/\widehat{r}}\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}\widehat{r}}{\mathrm{d}\widehat{\varepsilon}}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\left[\frac{\widehat{\varepsilon}\widehat{M}}{1-2\widehat{M}/\widehat{r}}\frac{2}{\widehat{r}^{4}}\left(\widehat{r}^{3}\widehat{\varepsilon}-\widehat{M}\right)+\frac{\widehat{P}\widehat{\varepsilon}}{\widehat{M}}\left(1+\frac{\widehat{r}^{3}\widehat{P}}{\widehat{M}}\right)^{-1}\left(3\widehat{M}-\widehat{r}^{3}\widehat{\varepsilon}\right)\right]<0.

(5.71)

The first term in (
5.71
) originated from “
-2\widehat{M}/\widehat{r}
” in Eq. (
5.9
) survives even when
\widehat{P}=0
is considered (so it is a geometric correction); while the second term is the geometry-matter coupling (from “
\widehat{r}^{3}\widehat{P}/\widehat{M}
” in Eq. (
5.9
)), which disappears if
\widehat{P}\approx 0
is taken.
This demonstrates that GR geometrical effects, the matter-geometry couplings can both effectively modify the
\widehat{\varepsilon}
-dependence (or equivalently the
\widehat{r}
-dependence) of
s^{2}
compared with its Newtonian counterpart.
Specifically, they all tend to make the
s^{2}
decrease with increasing
\widehat{\varepsilon}
(i.e.,
\mathrm{d}s^{2}/\mathrm{d}\widehat{\varepsilon}<0
).
On the other hand,
s^{2}>0
itself contributes positively to the derivative
\mathrm{d}s^{2}/\mathrm{d}\widehat{\varepsilon}
in Eq. (
5.69
), namely

\displaystyle-\frac{Y}{1-2\widehat{M}/\widehat{r}}\left[\frac{\widehat{\varepsilon}}{\widehat{M}}\frac{\mathrm{d}\widehat{r}}{\mathrm{d}\widehat{\varepsilon}}\left(1+\frac{\widehat{r}^{3}\widehat{P}}{\widehat{M}}\right)^{-1}\widehat{r}\widehat{M}s^{2}+\frac{\widehat{M}}{\widehat{r}^{2}}\frac{\mathrm{d}\widehat{r}}{\mathrm{d}\widehat{\varepsilon}}\left(1+\frac{\widehat{P}}{\widehat{\varepsilon}}\right)^{-1}s^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{\widehat{\varepsilon}\widehat{r}s^{2}}{1-2\widehat{M}/\widehat{r}}\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}\widehat{r}}{\mathrm{d}\widehat{\varepsilon}}\right)\left(1+\frac{2\widehat{P}}{\widehat{\varepsilon}}+\frac{\widehat{M}}{\widehat{\varepsilon}\widehat{r}^{3}}\right)>0.

(5.72)

This means
s^{2}
itself tends to remove the peak, i.e., it tends to make
\mathrm{d}s^{2}/\mathrm{d}\widehat{\varepsilon}>0
near
\widehat{r}=0
.
Only the first term in the curry bracket of Eq. (
5.69
), namely
(\mathrm{d}s^{2}/\mathrm{d}\widehat{\varepsilon})_{\mathrm{N}}
survives in the Newtonian limit, and all the other terms (proportional to
(\mathrm{d}\widehat{r}/\mathrm{d}\widehat{\varepsilon})^{2}
and
\mathrm{d}\widehat{r}/\mathrm{d}\widehat{\varepsilon}
) disappear.
Therefore, the correction (
5.72
) also disappears in
\mathrm{d}s^{2}/\mathrm{d}\widehat{\varepsilon}
for Newtonian stars.
The final sign of
\mathrm{d}s^{2}/\mathrm{d}\widehat{\varepsilon}
is the result of a balance/competition between Eqs. (
5.71
) and (
5.72
). It may depend on the EOS model, and therefore not every dense matter EOS could induce a peaked
s^{2}
profile, as expected.

A formula for
s^{2}\approx s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}+l_{2}\widehat{r}^{2}+l_{4}\widehat{r}^{4}
similar to Eq. (
4.59
) for the reduced pressure could be obtained for the GR case directly from (
5.57
) by expanding terms to linear order of
{\mathrm{X}}
(the
s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}
is kept without being expanded over
{\mathrm{X}}
),

\displaystyle s^{2}\approx

\displaystyle s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}+\left[\left(12a_{4}s_{\mathrm{c}}^{4}-\frac{4}{15}\right)-\left(48a_{4}s_{\mathrm{c}}^{4}+s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}+\frac{3}{5}\right){\mathrm{X}}\right]\widehat{r}^{2}

\displaystyle+\left[\left(144a_{4}^{2}s_{\mathrm{c}}^{6}+18a_{6}s_{\mathrm{c}}^{4}-\frac{62}{35}a_{4}s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}+\frac{1}{60s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}}+\frac{1}{12}s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}-\frac{1}{18}\right)\right.

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt+\left.\left(\frac{1}{15s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}}+\frac{1}{15}-12a_{4}s_{\mathrm{c}}^{4}-72a_{6}s_{\mathrm{c}}^{4}-1152a_{4}^{2}s_{\mathrm{c}}^{6}+\frac{116}{35}a_{4}s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}\right){\mathrm{X}}\right]\widehat{r}^{4}.

(5.73)

Expanding
s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}
of Eq. (
4.17
) over
{\mathrm{X}}
as
s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}\approx 4{\mathrm{X}}/3+4{\mathrm{X}}^{2}/3+\cdots
further approximates (
5.73
) as,

\displaystyle s^{2}\approx

\displaystyle\frac{4}{3}{\mathrm{X}}+\frac{4}{3}{\mathrm{X}}^{2}+\left[-\frac{4}{15}-\frac{3}{5}{\mathrm{X}}+\left(\frac{64a_{4}}{3}-\frac{4}{3}\right){\mathrm{X}}^{2}\right]\widehat{r}^{2}

\displaystyle+\left[\frac{1}{80{\mathrm{X}}}-\frac{13}{720}+\left(\frac{229}{1440}-\frac{248a_{4}}{105}\right){\mathrm{X}}+\left(\frac{157}{360}+\frac{72a_{4}}{35}+32a_{6}\right){\mathrm{X}}^{2}\right]\widehat{r}^{4}.

(5.74)
