.

We find that Eq. (
5.73
) with GR is very different from Eq. (
5.23
) for Newtonian stars even if
{\mathrm{X}}\approx 0
is taken (this is due to the geometrical correction “
-2\widehat{M}/\widehat{r}
”), e.g., the terms “
s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}/12
” and “
-1/18
” at order
\widehat{r}^{4}{\mathrm{X}}^{0}=\widehat{r}^{4}
are new compared with Eq. (
5.23
).
Similarly, new terms may emerge at other orders of
{\mathrm{X}}
.
Considering Eq. (
5.74
), e.g., we find
a_{4}
starts to operate at order
{\mathrm{X}}^{2}
(in the form of
64a_{4}{\mathrm{X}}^{2}/3
).
Then for finite
{\mathrm{X}}\sim\mathcal{O}(0.1)
, the coefficient
l_{2}
may take positive values.
For example, taking
a_{4}\approx 1
(
a_{4}\approx 0.5
) and requiring
l_{2}>0
leads to
{\mathrm{X}}\gtrsim 0.15
(
{\mathrm{X}}\gtrsim 0.23
), which are typical values of
{\mathrm{X}}
in massive NSs.
Thus, a sizable
{\mathrm{X}}
is necessary for inducing a peaked
s^{2}
profile:

\boxed{\mbox{a peaked $s^{2}$: }l_{2}>0\mathrm{\;needs\;}a_{4}>0\mathrm{\;as\;well\;as\;}{\mathrm{X}}\gtrsim\mathcal{O}(0.1).}

(5.75)

However,
a_{4}>0
in the Newtonian limit could still not generally make
l_{2}>0
(see Subsection
5.4
and especially Eq. (
5.27
)), we thus see clearly that
{\mathrm{X}}\gtrsim\mathcal{O}(0.1)
is fundamental. The left panel of FIG.
70
gives an illustration of the
{\mathrm{X}}
-dependence of
l_{2}
(adopting four different
a_{4}
’s), where
0.15\lesssim{\mathrm{X}}\lesssim 0.30
is marked as the range for
{\mathrm{X}}
in typical massive NSs.
The grey line near each colored curve is for the approximated
l_{2}\approx-4/15-3{\mathrm{X}}/5+(64a_{4}/3-4/3){\mathrm{X}}^{2}
. Obviously,
l_{2}^{\mathrm{N}}=-4/15
(see Eq. (
5.26
)).
Once the coefficient
l_{2}
becomes positive, there would unavoidably be a peak in
s^{2}
profile at some finite distance
\widehat{r}<\widehat{R}
regardless of the higher-order coefficients (since near the surface the
s^{2}\to 0
).
One can demonstrate that the coefficient
a_{6}
needs to be negative if
l_{4}<0
is required.
The resulted condition for
a_{6}
could be obtained from Eq. (
5.57
), namely
l_{4}<0
,
or equivalently,

a_{6}<\frac{b_{6}}{s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}}+\frac{4}{3}\frac{a_{4}}{b_{2}}\left(s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}a_{4}-b_{4}\right)\equiv a_{6}^{\mathrm{(up)}},

(5.76)

where all the expressions (for
b_{2},b_{4}
and
b_{6}
as well as
s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}
) are available.
The
a_{4}
-dependence of
a_{6}^{\mathrm{(up)}}
is shown in the right panel of FIG.
70
, from which
we find that
a_{6}<0
and notice that both
a_{4}
and
a_{6}
are
\sim\mathcal{O}(1)
, e.g.,
for
{\mathrm{X}}\approx 0.2
and
a_{4}\approx 1
, we then have
a_{6}\lesssim-1
.
If the coefficient
l_{4}
is also positive, then an analysis of the even higher-order terms becomes necessary, e.g., the coefficient
l_{6}
, etc.
Nonetheless, the peak will emerge at some finite
\widehat{r}
.
In this review, we focus on
l_{2}>0
and
l_{4}<0
, and point out the extension if the
l_{6}
-term is included when necessary.

(Color Online). Same as FIG.

but for the fraction
\mathrm{x}({\mathrm{X}})
for the enhancement
\Delta s^{2}
.
Figure taken from Ref.
Cai and Li (2024)
.

Consequently, we find the position of the peak using
s^{2}\approx s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}+l_{2}\widehat{r}^{2}+l_{4}\widehat{r}^{4}

Cai and Li (2024)
:

\widehat{r}_{\mathrm{pk}}=\sqrt{-l_{2}/2l_{4}}.

(5.77)

For each
{\mathrm{X}}
, we randomly sample
a_{4}\sim\mathrm{Unif}[0,2]
and
a_{6}\sim\mathrm{Unif}[-2,2]
by
m
times, and count the number
m_{\mathrm{pk}}
of samples where there is a peak in the
s^{2}
profile while satisfying the causality condition
s_{\max}^{2}\leq 1
, here
Cai and Li (2024)

s_{\max}^{2}\equiv s^{2}(\widehat{r}_{\mathrm{pk}})=s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}-l_{2}^{2}/4l_{4}.

(5.78)

The probability is estimated as
m_{\mathrm{pk}}/m
, see the left panel of FIG.
71
for the simulated results.
For a relatively small
{\mathrm{X}}\approx 0.1
, the probability is also small since the
{\mathrm{X}}
is not large enough to make
l_{2}
positive (even if
a_{4}
is positive). The probability eventually increases with increasing
{\mathrm{X}}
, but it becomes small again when the
{\mathrm{X}}
is close to the causality limit about 0.374. This is because the matter is too stiff to be further compressed by gravity, i.e., there is no space for
s^{2}(\widehat{r})>s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}
since
s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}
itself is
\to 1

Cai et al. (2023)
.
Combining the above information with what we learned from FIG.
43
, we find that the probability is relatively larger (compared with its surroundings) for
0.2\lesssim{\mathrm{X}}\lesssim 0.35
, where the strong gravitational force effectively bends down the
s^{2}
.
Interestingly,
{\mathrm{X}}\approx 0.24_{-0.07}^{+0.05}

Cai et al. (2023)
for PSR J0740+6620 extracted directly from its observed mass and radius
Fonseca et al. (2021)
;
Miller et al. (2021)
;
Riley et al. (2021)
;
Salmi et al. (2022)
;
Dittmann et al. (2024)
;
Salmi et al. (2024)
is quite close to this region, indicating massive NSs with radii
\approx 12\mbox{-}14\,\mathrm{km}
are excellent objects to study the peaked
s^{2}
profile.
The resulted
a_{4}
and
a_{6}
for
{\mathrm{X}}\approx 0.24
are found respectively to be about
a_{4}\approx 0.71\pm 0.03
and
a_{6}\approx-1.37\pm 0.19
, both are
\sim\mathcal{O}(1)
.
We show in FIG.
73
the radial dependence of
s^{2}
,
\widehat{\varepsilon}
and
\mathrm{d}\widehat{\varepsilon}/\mathrm{d}\widehat{r}
for PSR J0740+6620 in the case that there exists a peak in
s^{2}
, using these constrained
a_{4}
and
a_{6}
;
the peak in
s^{2}
is weak and wide and the plots are effective at small
\widehat{r}\lesssim 0.5
.
See the left panel of FIG.
72
for the general
{\mathrm{X}}
-dependence of the coefficients
a_{4}
,
a_{6}
,
l_{2}
and
l_{4}
, where the coefficient
l_{4}
(compared with
a_{4},a_{6}
and
l_{2}
) may take some large values (for large
{\mathrm{X}}
).
Furthermore, the coefficient
l_{2}
starting from about zero at
{\mathrm{X}}\approx 0.1
implies that for even smaller
{\mathrm{X}}
values there would be no peaked feature in the
s^{2}
profile.
The right panel of FIG.
72
shows the dependence of
l_{4}
on
a_{4}
and that of
l_{2}
on
a_{2}
(inset), from which we find that
l_{2}
positively correlates with
a_{2}
and
l_{4}
positively correlates with
a_{4}
.
Moreover, while
a_{2}
,
a_{4}
,
l_{2}
are
\sim\mathcal{O}(1)
the coefficient
l_{4}
may take some large values (especially for large
{\mathrm{X}}
).

is the peak position
\widehat{r}_{\mathrm{pk}}

for a wide range of
{\mathrm{X}}
.
Since the reduced radius
\widehat{R}=R/Q\approx 1
, this means the peak locates at about
0.35\sim 0.4
times the radius from the center.
Moreover, we show in the left panel of FIG.

the (average) enhancement of
s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}

5.78
))
Cai and Li (2024)
,

\boxed{\Delta s^{2}\equiv s_{\max}^{2}/s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}-1=-l_{2}^{2}/4l_{4}s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2},}

(5.79)

which is generally small about
\lesssim 5\%
;
and this is consistent with FIG.
66
.
FIG.
74
shows the radial profiles of
s^{2}
adopting nine different
{\mathrm{X}}
values specified in the plots.
The
s^{2}
is probably a monotonically decreasing function of
\widehat{r}
for small
{\mathrm{X}}\lesssim 0.1
(panel (a) of FIG.
74
), the peak eventually emerges/develops as
{\mathrm{X}}
increases to about
{\mathrm{X}}\approx 0.30
(panels (b) to (e)) and then tends to disappear for even large
{\mathrm{X}}\gtrsim 0.35
(panels (f)-(i)).
We may also find that at the two limiting sides of
{\mathrm{X}}
(i.e., small
{\mathrm{X}}\lesssim\mathcal{O}(0.1)
and
{\mathrm{X}}\gtrsim 0.35
) the fraction
\mathrm{x}({\mathrm{X}})
of large enhancement
\Delta s^{2}(\gtrsim 15\%)
is extremely small, and most of the fraction is accumulated at
\Delta s^{2}\lesssim 5\%
, as shown in FIG.
75
.

If the contribution
l_{6}\widehat{r}^{6}
(which involves the coefficient
a_{8}
) is included as
s^{2}\approx s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}+l_{2}\widehat{r}^{2}+l_{4}\widehat{r}^{4}+l_{6}\widehat{r}^{6}
,
then the equation determining the peak position
\widehat{r}_{\mathrm{pk}}
becomes (
l_{2}>0
),

\boxed{l_{2}+2l_{4}\widehat{r}^{2}+3l_{6}\widehat{r}^{4}=0.}

(5.80)

The expression for
l_{6}
could be obtained straightforwardly like that for
l_{4}
(see Eq. (
5.57
)),

l_{6}=\frac{4}{b_{2}}\left[\left(b_{8}-s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}a_{8}\right)-\frac{3}{2}\frac{a_{4}}{a_{2}}\left(b_{6}-s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}a_{6}\right)-\left(\frac{3}{2}\frac{a_{6}}{a_{2}}-2\left(\frac{a_{4}}{a_{2}}\right)^{2}\right)\left(b_{4}-s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}a_{4}\right)\right],

(5.81)

where the expression for
b_{8}
is

\displaystyle b_{8}=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{648}\left(1+3\mathrm{X}^{2}+4\mathrm{X}\right)\left(1-3\mathrm{X}-\frac{27}{2}\mathrm{X}^{3}\right)-\left(\frac{19}{1620}+\frac{\mathrm{X}}{54}+\frac{\mathrm{X}^{2}}{90}+\frac{7\mathrm{X}^{3}}{120}\right)a_{2}

\displaystyle-\left(\frac{4}{225}+\frac{\mathrm{X}}{150}\right)a_{2}^{2}-\left(\frac{11}{756}-\frac{\mathrm{X}}{252}-\frac{\mathrm{X}^{2}}{12}\right)a_{4}-\frac{3a_{2}a_{4}}{70}-\left(\frac{1}{18}+\frac{5\mathrm{X}}{36}\right)a_{6},

(5.82)

see relevant expressions of (
2.30
), (
2.31
) and (
2.32
) for coefficients
b_{2}
,
b_{4}
and
b_{6}
.
The magnitude of
b_{8}
is even smaller than that of
b_{2}
,
b_{4}
or
b_{6}
(see FIG.
4
).
Using Eq. (
5.80
), we immediately obtain the peak position
\widehat{r}_{\mathrm{pk}}
as
\widehat{r}_{\mathrm{pk}}^{2}=[-l_{4}+(l_{4}^{2}-3l_{2}l_{6})^{1/2}]/3l_{6}
if
l_{4}>0
and
\widehat{r}_{\mathrm{pk}}^{2}=[-l_{4}-(l_{4}^{2}-3l_{2}l_{6})^{1/2}]/3l_{6}
for
l_{4}<0
(both satisfy the conditions
l_{6}\leq l_{4}^{2}/3l_{2}
and
\widehat{r}_{\mathrm{pk}}^{2}>0
).
By sampling
a_{8}\sim\mathrm{Unif}[-2,2]
, one can similarly analyze the peaked behavior of
s^{2}
, and the results are shown by the dashed grey lines in FIG.
74
.
It is seen that the correction
l_{6}\widehat{r}^{6}
slightly increases the enhancement
\Delta s^{2}
while has almost no effect on the location of the peak
\widehat{r}_{\mathrm{pk}}
.
Moreover, using
\widehat{\varepsilon}\approx 1+a_{2}\widehat{r}^{2}+a_{4}\widehat{r}^{4}+a_{6}\widehat{r}^{6}
, one finds that
85\%\lesssim\widehat{\varepsilon}_{\mathrm{pk}}\lesssim 95\%
for
0.15\lesssim{\mathrm{X}}\lesssim 0.35
.
Furthermore, we show in FIG.
76
the
s^{2}/s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}
as a function of
\widehat{\varepsilon}
.
The peak location
\widehat{\varepsilon}_{\mathrm{pk}}
in energy density is consistent with our previous studies, i.e.,
\widehat{\rho}\equiv\rho/\rho_{\mathrm{c}}\approx\widehat{\varepsilon}-\mu(1+4\mu/3){\mathrm{X}}(1-{\mathrm{X}})
, see Eq. (
4.55
), from which we inferred that
\rho_{\mathrm{pk}}/\rho_{\mathrm{c}}\approx\widehat{\varepsilon}_{\mathrm{pk}}\lesssim 95\%

Cai et al. (2023)
; and this is also consistent with findings in a few recent studies by others
Mroczek et al. (2023)
;
Cao and Chen (2023)
;
Ferreira and Providência (2024)
and consistent with findings of FIG.
66
.
Finally, we show in FIG.
77
the
\widehat{r}
-dependence of
\widehat{\varepsilon}(\widehat{r})\approx 1+a_{2}\widehat{r}^{2}+a_{4}\widehat{r}^{4}+a_{6}\widehat{r}^{6}
, where the uncertainty is small for
\widehat{r}\approx 0
and it is consistent with FIG.
24
, see also the results of FIG.
73
.

Cai et al. (2023)
.

For normal stable NSs on the M-R curve, we could analyze the probability for the
s^{2}
profile to have a peak near
\widehat{r}=0
as done for the
M_{\mathrm{NS}}^{\max}
configuration; the result is shown in the right panel of FIG.
71
.
We find that the probability is relatively large for
0.17\lesssim{\mathrm{X}}\lesssim 0.25
(compared with its low-
{\mathrm{X}}
surroundings), while on the other hand the location of the peak
\widehat{r}_{\mathrm{pk}}
(lavender band) and the enhancement
\Delta s^{2}
(tan band) are very similar to those of the left panel of FIG.
71
.
Combining the results of the right panels of FIG.
43
and FIG.
71
and
considering
{\mathrm{X}}\lesssim 0.15

Brandes et al. (2023)
for a canonical NS (see TAB.
6
), we see that the probability of occurrence of a peak in
s^{2}
for such NSs is probably very low.
These features are consistent with the findings of Ref.
Ecker and Rezzolla (2022)
and FIG.
62
, which predicted that the
s^{2}
in canonical NSs is a monotonically decreasing function of
\widehat{r}
.

FIG. 79:
(Color Online). Sketch of the
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}
-dependence of NS mass
M_{\mathrm{NS}}
(left panel) and the energy density
\varepsilon
-, the density
\rho
- or the
\phi
-dependence of
s^{2}
(right panel). Figures taken from Ref.
Cai and Li (2024)
.

Now we have a coherent picture for generating a potential peak in
s^{2}
.
Consider a NS with mass
M_{\mathrm{NS}}^{\max}=2M_{\odot}
fixed but its radius
R
could vary.
The central EOS
{P}_{\mathrm{c}}
-
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}
is determined by the correlation (
4.33
), see the light-blue line shown in the left panel of FIG.
78
.
The
{\mathrm{X}}
eventually increases with decreasing radius
R
(e.g., from 17 km to 12 km) since the NS becomes more compact, accompanied with the emergence, enhancement and disappearing of the peak in
s^{2}
.
The resulted
{\mathrm{X}}
’s are also shown in FIG.
78
, e.g., for
R\approx 13\,\mathrm{km}
we have
{\mathrm{X}}\approx 0.20
, using the correlation (
4.30
).
So there may exist a peak in
s^{2}
near
\widehat{r}=0
when
R
is relatively small (e.g., 12 km) and the peak becomes enhanced when
R
increases a little further (e.g., to 13 km or 15 km).
If
R
is very small, e.g.,
R\lesssim 11\,\mathrm{km}
, then the
\mathrm{X}
approaches the causality limit 0.374, in this case the peak disappears.
In essence, the profile of
s^{2}
is analogous to the M-R curve where the GR leads to a maximum-mass point beyond which the NSs are unstable against collapsing into BHs, sketched in the left panel of FIG.
79
.
The strong-gravitational force changes
\phi=P/\varepsilon
from being small
\lesssim 10^{-5}
in Newtonian star to being sizable
\gtrsim\mathcal{O}(0.1)
and extrudes a peak in the
s^{2}
profile, as sketched in the right panel of FIG.
79
(and numerically demonstrated in FIG.
74
).
If
R
is extra-ordinarily large (e.g., 17 km and correspondingly
{\mathrm{X}}\approx 0.12
from Eqs. (
4.31
) and (
4.30
)), the peak in
s^{2}
may reduce and even disappear as illustrated in the left panel of FIG.
78
.

FIG. 80:
(Color Online). An illustration of the relative probability of seeing a peaked behavior of
s^{2}
profile near NS centers.
There tends to be no peaks in
s^{2}
for light NSs like the canonical ones involved in GW170817 (left); the peak eventually emerges for massive NSs with radii about 12-13 km such as PSR J0740+6620 (middle) and then disappears for more massive and very compact NSs with
R\lesssim 11\,\mathrm{km}
(right).

The above discussions highlight the key role played by the central pressure
{\mathrm{X}}
in determining the relative probabilities of seeing a peaked structure of
s^{2}
in NSs with different masses and/or radii, as sketched in FIG.
80
. There is little chance to see a peak in the density/radius profile of
s^{2}
for light NSs like the canonical ones involved in GW170817 (left) because of the low
{\mathrm{X}}
reached. The peak eventually emerges for massive NSs with radii about 12-13 km such as PSR J0740+6620 (middle) and then disappears for more massive and very compact NSs with
R\lesssim 11\,\mathrm{km}
(right).
Our main conclusion here is also consistent with the finding of a recent analysis
Ecker and Rezzolla (2022)
;
Ecker and Rezzolla (2022)
using as input in solving the TOV equations in the traditional approach the EOS from
nuclear theory and pQCD and imposing observational
constraints. In fact, their predictions imply that
{\mathrm{X}}
is relevant for determining the structure of
s^{2}
. Nevertheless,
we emphasize that not every EOS can induce a peaked
s^{2}
profile, just similar to the fact that not every EOS could induce a maximum mass for NSs below the causality limit, e.g., the URFG predicts a linear M-R relation
M_{\mathrm{NS}}=3R/14

LIGHTMAN et al. (1975)
.

Compact massive NSs (such as PSR J0740+6620 with its
{\mathrm{X}}\approx 0.24
) provide excellent opportunities for studying the density or radius profile of
s^{2}
. Our studies also predict that more massive and very compact NSs (with radii
\lesssim 11\,\mathrm{km}
) may not have a peaked
s^{2}
as we illustrated in FIG.
80
. Therefore, pulsars like PSR J0740+6620 are presently the best objects for exploring the possible peaked structure of
s^{2}
density or radius profile.
Looking forward, we expect the masses and radii of massive NSs as well as properties of post-merger remnants of NSs from future high-frequency GW observations as well as high-precision radius measurements using both X-rays and GWs will be very useful for inferring features of the
s^{2}
profile especially near
\widehat{r}=0
.

FIG. 81:
(Color Online). Left panel: density dependence of
\Delta
inferred under the constraint
\mathrm{d}M_{\mathrm{NS}}/\mathrm{d}R<0
for all NS masses (blue) or
\mathrm{d}M_{\mathrm{NS}}/\mathrm{d}R\geq 0
for a certain mass range (red); inference in the bottom figure with astrophysical constraints. Figure taken from Ref.
Ferreira and Providência (2024)
.
Right panel: two types of M-R curves classified by using the derivative
\mathrm{d}M_{\mathrm{NS}}/\mathrm{d}R
for NS masses between about
1\,M_{\odot}
and
M_{\mathrm{TOV}}
to help understand the behavior of the trace anomaly in the left panel as well as the maximum baryon densities shown in the right panel of FIG.
67
.
Figure taken from Ref.
Cai and Li (2024)
.

Bearing in mind that the strong gravity in GR plays an important/relevant role in extruding a peaked
s^{2}
profile, our analysis above based on solving the dimensionless TOV equations without using any input nuclear EOS does not rely on the pQCD constraint at all. In this regard, besides the reviews given in SECTION
2
(summarized in FIG.
11
) and other places in this section, it is interesting to note that very recently in Ref.
Ferreira and Providência (2024)
the authors classified their 40435
M(R)
curves into three groups with the
\mathrm{d}M_{\mathrm{NS}}/\mathrm{d}R
negative, infinity and positive for NS masses between about 1.2
M_{\odot}
and
M_{\mathrm{TOV}}
. Their EOSs are generated in a way similar to our meta-model with the high-density part of their EOSs parameterized with piecewise polytropes. They then explored how the global and/or local
\mathrm{d}M_{\mathrm{NS}}/\mathrm{d}R
slopes at 1.4
M_{\odot}
and two heavier mass points can inform us about
M_{{\mathrm{TOV}}}
and the corresponding radius
R_{{\mathrm{TOV}}}
. In turn, observational constraints on the latter are expected to constrain the sign of
\mathrm{d}M_{\mathrm{NS}}/\mathrm{d}R
globally or locally and thus the underlying dense matter EOS. In particular, they analyzed the SSS as a function of density with/without considering the constraint
\mathrm{d}M_{\mathrm{NS}}/\mathrm{d}R<0
at certain mass range on the NS M-R curves. The latter condition may effectively affect both the position and strength of the peak in
s^{2}
, as shown in the right panel of FIG.
67
where the blue band is for
\mathrm{d}M_{\mathrm{NS}}/\mathrm{d}R<0
while the red band for
\mathrm{d}M_{\mathrm{NS}}/\mathrm{d}R\gtrsim 0
.
The
\mathrm{d}M_{\mathrm{NS}}/\mathrm{d}R<0
case has larger central densities probably because the matter is compressed more efficiently by gravity, see the right panel of FIG.
81
for a sketch to illustrate why the set with
\mathrm{d}M_{\mathrm{NS}}/\mathrm{d}R<0
may induce a larger central baryon density. A larger maximum baryon density (corresponding to a more compact NS) essentially generates a deeper
\Delta
, see the left panel of FIG.
81
. Since the investigation of Ref.
Ferreira and Providência (2024)
does not rely on specific EOS models, it thus provides an interesting viewpoint on
s^{2}
directly from the general NS M-R curves.

5.10 Effects of high-density nuclear symmetry energy on the density profile of SSS in NSs

In the study of Ref.
Zhang and Li (2023)
, it was found that by changing the high-density behavior of nuclear symmetry energy within the minimum model of NSs consisting of nucleons, electrons and muons at
\beta
-equilibrium the density profile of
s^{2}
naturally shows a peak.

As illustrated in the left panels of FIG.
82
, the high-density behavior of nuclear symmetry energy can be varied by modifying its curvature
K_{\mathrm{sym}}
and/or skewness

J_{\mathrm{sym}}
(see Eq. (
3.2
)) within their current uncertain ranges while fixing all the others at their currently known most probably empirical values. Due to the
\beta
-equilibrium condition and the
E_{\mathrm{sym}}(\rho)\delta^{2}
term in the energy per nucleon in neutron-rich nucleonic matter, it is well known that there exists the so-called isospin fractionation phenomenon, namely wherever
E_{\mathrm{sym}}(\rho)
is low, the isospin asymmetry
\delta
there would be high or vice versa to minimize the total energy and keep the isospin equilibrium. More specifically,
considering two nearby regions with local densities
\rho_{1}
and
\rho_{2}
, respectively, the chemical equilibrium condition requires
E_{\mathrm{s}ym}(\rho_{1})\delta(\rho_{1})=E_{\mathrm{s}ym}(\rho_{2})\delta(\rho_{2})
. Thus, for a given
E_{\mathrm{sym}}(\rho)
function, the local isospin asymmetries at different densities will adjust themselves according to the relative symmetry energies in those regions. This is well illustrated in the upper right panel of
of FIG.
82
by varying the
J_{\mathrm{sym}}
between
-200
and +800 MeV.
It is seen that the super-stiff
E_{\mathrm{sym}}(\rho)
with
J_{\mathrm{sym}}
=800 MeV leads to a vanishing
\delta
(SNM) at high densities. While the super-soft
E_{\mathrm{sym}}(\rho)
with negative
J_{\mathrm{sym}}
leads to
\delta=1
(pure neutron matter) at high densities. Most importantly, in both cases the product of
E_{\mathrm{sym}}(\rho)
and
\delta
at high densities reach zero as required by the
\beta
-equilibrium and charge neutrality conditions. The density profile of the isospin asymmetry
\delta
in NSs can essentially influence the appearance of the peak in
s^{2}
, as shown in the lower-right panel of FIG.
82
. It is clearly seen that regardless of the high-density behavior of
E_{\mathrm{sym}}(\rho)
(super-soft or super-stiff), the equilibrium speed of sound
C^{2}_{s}(\rho)
in NSs at
\beta
-equilibrium always show a peak with its position depending on the high-density behavior of nuclear symmetry energy characterized by mostly
K_{\mathrm{sym}}
and
J_{\mathrm{sym}}
.

FIG. 82:
(Color Online). Left panel: High-density behaviors of nuclear symmetry energy by changing its curvature
K_{\mathrm{sym}}
(upper left) and skewness
J_{\mathrm{sym}}
(lower left) within their current uncertain ranges while fixing all the other parameters at their currently known most probable empirical values. Right panel: effects of varying the skewness

J_{\mathrm{sym}}
between
-200
and
+800
MeV
of nuclear symmetry energy on (1) (upper right): the density profile of the isospin asymmetry
\delta
in NSs at
\beta
-equilibrium and (2) (lower right) the appearance of a peak in the density profile of
s^{2}
in NSs. Figures taken from Ref.
Zhang and Li (2023)
.

Thus, within the simplest NS model consisting of nucleons, electrons and muons only without introducing the degree of freedom of quarks could account for a peaked structure in the density profile of
s^{2}
.
We can understand this phenomenon by working out the expression for
a_{4}
in expanding the reduced energy density
\widehat{\varepsilon}

Cai and Li (2024)
.
By introducing the reduced densities
\widehat{\rho}=\rho/\rho_{\mathrm{c}}
and
\widehat{\rho}_{0}=\rho_{0}/\rho_{\mathrm{c}}=\widehat{\rho}_{\mathrm{sat}}
with
\rho_{\mathrm{c}}
being the central density in NSs, we have
\widehat{\rho}\approx 1+\beta_{1}\widehat{r}^{2}+(\beta_{2}a_{4}-\beta_{3})\widehat{r}^{4}
, which defines the coefficients
\beta_{1}
,
\beta_{2}
and
\beta_{3}
from Eq. (
4.54
).
Based on these relations, we can express the energy density
\varepsilon=[E(\rho)+M_{\mathrm{N}}]\rho
, or its reduced form as
\widehat{\varepsilon}={\varepsilon}/{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}}=[E(\rho)+M_{\mathrm{N}}]({\rho_{\mathrm{c}}}/{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}})\widehat{\rho}
.
Putting the
\widehat{\rho}
into
\chi=(\rho-\rho_{0})/3\rho_{0}
and further putting
\chi
back into
E_{0}(\rho)
as well as
E_{\mathrm{sym}}(\rho)
gives the
\widehat{\varepsilon}
as a function of
\widehat{r}^{2}

Cai and Li (2024)
:

\displaystyle\widehat{\varepsilon}\approx

\displaystyle\left({\rho_{\mathrm{c}}}/{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}}\right)\left[M_{\mathrm{N}}+E_{0}(\rho_{0})+2^{-1}K_{0}\chi^{2}+6^{-1}J_{0}\chi^{3}+\left(S+L\chi+2^{-1}K_{\mathrm{sym}}\chi^{2}+6^{-1}J_{\mathrm{sym}}\chi^{3}\right)\delta^{2}\right].

(5.83)

For our purpose here, we need the expression of the
\widehat{r}^{4}
-term in Eq. (
5.83
) which itself involves
a_{4}
due to the expression for
\widehat{\rho}
.
Equaling it with
a_{4}\widehat{r}^{4}
gives a self-consistent equation for determining
a_{4}
(both sides of which involve
a_{4}
).

FIG. 83:
(Color Online). Dependence of
a_{4}
on the empirical EOS parameters
K_{0}
,
J_{0}
,
S
,
L
,
K_{\mathrm{sym}}
and
J_{\mathrm{sym}}
according to Eq. (
5.84
), see text for the details on the choice/determination of the parameters. Figures taken from Ref.
Cai and Li (2024)
.

The expression for
a_{4}
is quite complicated and since the magnitude of
\overline{M}_{\mathrm{N}}\equiv M_{\mathrm{N}}+E_{0}(\rho_{0})\approx 923\,\mathrm{MeV}
(using
M_{\mathrm{N}}\approx 939\,\mathrm{MeV}
and
E_{0}(\rho_{0})\approx-16\,\mathrm{MeV}
) is generally larger than
K_{0}\approx 230\,\mathrm{MeV}
,
J_{0}\approx-300\,\mathrm{MeV}
,
S\approx 30\,\mathrm{MeV}
,
L\approx 60\,\mathrm{MeV}
,
K_{\mathrm{sym}}\approx-100\,\mathrm{MeV}
and
J_{\mathrm{sym}}\approx 300\,\mathrm{MeV}

Li et al. (2021)
, one can introduce small quantities
K_{0}/\overline{M}_{\mathrm{N}}
,
J_{0}/\overline{M}_{\mathrm{N}}
,
S/\overline{M}_{\mathrm{N}}
,
L/\overline{M}_{\mathrm{N}}
,
K_{\mathrm{sym}}/\overline{M}_{\mathrm{N}}
and
J_{\mathrm{sym}}/\overline{M}_{\mathrm{N}}
to expand
a_{4}
to dig out the main features of its dependence on the empirical EOS characteristics
Cai and Li (2024)
:

\displaystyle a_{4}\approx

\displaystyle-\frac{\beta_{3}}{54(\beta_{3}\beta_{2}-1)^{2}\widehat{\rho}_{0}^{3}}\left\{+2\left(\left(\beta_{3}\beta_{2}-1\right)\beta_{1}^{2}+\frac{2\beta_{2}\beta_{3}}{3}\right)\left[\left(\frac{J_{0}}{\overline{M}_{\mathrm{N}}}\right)+\left(\frac{J_{\mathrm{sym}}}{\overline{M}_{\mathrm{N}}}\right)\delta^{2}\right]\right.

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt-3\left(\left(\beta_{3}\beta_{2}-1\right)\beta_{1}^{2}+\beta_{2}\beta_{3}\right)\left[\left(\frac{J_{0}}{\overline{M}_{\mathrm{N}}}\right)-3\left(\frac{K_{0}}{\overline{M}_{\mathrm{N}}}\right)+\left(\left(\frac{J_{\mathrm{sym}}}{\overline{M}_{\mathrm{N}}}\right)-3\left(\frac{K_{\mathrm{sym}}}{\overline{M}_{\mathrm{N}}}\right)\right)\delta^{2}\right]\widehat{\rho}_{0}

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt+\left(\left(\beta_{3}\beta_{2}-1\right)\beta_{1}^{2}+2\beta_{2}\beta_{3}\right)\left[\left(\frac{J_{0}}{\overline{M}_{\mathrm{N}}}\right)-6\left(\frac{K_{0}}{\overline{M}_{\mathrm{N}}}\right)+\left(\left(\frac{J_{\mathrm{sym}}}{\overline{M}_{\mathrm{N}}}\right)-6\left(\frac{K_{\mathrm{sym}}}{\overline{M}_{\mathrm{N}}}\right)+18\left(\frac{L}{\overline{M}_{\mathrm{N}}}\right)\right)\delta^{2}\right]\widehat{\rho}_{0}^{2}

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt-54\beta_{2}\beta_{3}\left[\left(\beta_{3}\beta_{2}-1\right)+\frac{1}{162}\left(\frac{J_{0}}{\overline{M}_{\mathrm{N}}}\right)-\frac{1}{18}\left(\frac{K_{0}}{\overline{M}_{\mathrm{N}}}\right)\right.

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt\left.\left.+\left(\frac{1}{162}\left(\frac{J_{\mathrm{sym}}}{\overline{M}_{\mathrm{N}}}\right)-\frac{1}{18}\left(\frac{K_{\mathrm{sym}}}{\overline{M}_{\mathrm{N}}}\right)+\frac{1}{3}\left(\frac{L}{\overline{M}_{\mathrm{N}}}\right)-\left(\frac{S}{\overline{M}_{\mathrm{N}}}\right)\right)\delta^{2}\right]\widehat{\rho}_{0}^{3}\right\},

(5.84)

here
\beta_{3}=\overline{M}_{\mathrm{N}}\rho_{\mathrm{c}}/\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}
.
For a given set of nuclear EOS parameters
(K_{0}
,
J_{0}
,
S
,
L
,
K_{\mathrm{sym}}
,
J_{\mathrm{sym}}
) and a central energy density
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}
(or a central density
\rho_{\mathrm{c}}
),
the NS mass as well as its radius could be obtained.

A main feature of Eq. (
5.84
) is that
J_{0}
and
J_{\mathrm{sym}}
are the leading-order contributions to
a_{4}
, since they appear at
\widehat{\rho}_{0}^{0}
order in the curly brackets. Other characteristics such as
K_{0}
,
L
,
K_{\mathrm{sym}}
, etc., appear at higher-orders of
\widehat{\rho}_{0}
. As an example, taking
\rho_{\mathrm{c}}\approx 5\rho_{0}\approx 0.8\,\mathrm{fm}^{-3}
(equivalently
\widehat{\rho}_{0}=\rho_{0}/\rho_{\mathrm{c}}\approx 1/5
) and
K_{0}\approx 230\,\mathrm{MeV}
,
J_{0}\approx-300\,\mathrm{MeV}
,
S\approx 30\,\mathrm{MeV}
,
L\approx 60\,\mathrm{MeV}
,
K_{\mathrm{sym}}\approx-100\,\mathrm{MeV}
together with
J_{\mathrm{sym}}\approx 300\,\mathrm{MeV}

Li et al. (2021)
, then
\delta\approx 0.6
is obtained at
\rho_{\mathrm{c}}
, the central energy density
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}
and the central pressure
P_{\mathrm{c}}
are obtained as
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}\approx 863\,\mathrm{MeV}/\mathrm{fm}^{3}
and
P_{\mathrm{c}}\approx 182\,\mathrm{MeV}/\mathrm{fm}^{3}
, respectively. Consequently,
\beta_{3}=\overline{M}_{\mathrm{N}}\rho_{\mathrm{c}}/\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}\approx 0.86
,
{\mathrm{X}}\approx 0.21
and
\beta_{1}\approx-0.74
,
\beta_{2}\approx 0.86
and
\beta_{3}\approx 0.10
.
In FIG.
83
, we show the dependence of
a_{4}
on the nuclear EOS parameters
K_{0}
,
J_{0}
,
S
,
L
,
K_{\mathrm{sym}}
and
J_{\mathrm{sym}}
according to Eq. (
5.84
), within their currently known uncertainty ranges.
Besides
a_{4}\sim\mathcal{O}(1)
, we find
a_{4}
can take either positive or negative values, depending on the empirical parameters characterizing the high-density EOS.
For example,
a_{4}
can be positive with the increasing skewness parameter
J_{0}
of SNM or the skewness parameter
J_{\mathrm{sym}}
of the symmetry energy, e.g.,
J_{\mathrm{sym}}\gtrsim 560\,\mathrm{MeV}
is needed for
a_{4}\gtrsim 0
(while fixing other parameters). It is seen that a more positive
J_{\mathrm{sym}}
or
J_{0}
makes
a_{4}
more positive, tending to generate the peak in
s^{2}
at a smaller density
Zhang and Li (2023)
.

FIG. 84:
(Color Online). Similar to FIG.
82
but demonstrating effects of the slope parameter
L
on SSS. Figure taken from Ref.
Wang et al. (2024)
.

In a very recent study, Ref.
Wang et al. (2024)
explored the SSS as a function of number density by varying the slope
L
of nuclear symmetry energy (left panel of FIG.
84
). They found that for
L\lesssim 35\,\mathrm{MeV}
, a peak emerges in
s^{2}
at densities accessible in some normal NSs, as indicated in the right panel of FIG.
84
.
However, in their work
Wang et al. (2024)
not only
L
varies with different interaction sets but also
K_{\mathrm{sym}}
and
J_{\mathrm{sym}}
are different. Thus, it is not quite clear which are really the deterministic quantities for the peaked
s^{2}
. Using our analysis of FIG.
83
, the effects of
S\equiv E_{\mathrm{sym}}(\rho_{0})
and
L
on
a_{4}
are small. Therefore, these low-order parameters may not be the important quantities for generating a peaked
s^{2}
density profile. Nevertheless, we notice that the
J_{\mathrm{sym}}
’s in their interaction sets with smaller
L
’s are relatively larger than those corresponding to larger
L
’s
Wang et al. (2024)
, indicating that
J_{\mathrm{sym}}
may be the hidden agent causing the peak in the density profile of
s^{2}
.

FIG. 85:
(Color Online). The SSS obtained by using an effective theory implemented with a conformal compensator (nonlinear realization of scale symmetry) regarded as the source of the lightest scalar meson. Figure taken from Ref.
Zhang et al. (2024)
.

Finally, it is also interesting to note that a peaked
s^{2}
without retrospect to phase transition or crossover from hadron to exotic configuration was recently found by using an effective theory implemented with a conformal compensator (nonlinear realization of scale symmetry) regarded as the source of the lightest scalar meson
Zhang et al. (2024)
, see FIG.
85
.
In their work, the peaked position was found to be located at about
(1\mbox{-}2.5)\rho_{0}
, and the result may be related to the issue on how the hidden scale symmetry manifests in nuclear medium from the unitarity limit in dilute matter to dilaton limit in compact star matter.

5.11 SSS in supradense matter from lattice QCD or other QCD-based effective theories

As we discussed in the last few subsections, the strong-field gravity in GR realized in NSs plays an essential role in generating a peaked structure in
s^{2}
at densities accessible in NSs.
On the other hand as we mentioned earlier, properties of supra-dense matter have also been studied extensively from QCD-based theories. Our knowledge on superdense matter confined by strong-field gravity in NSs from the two directions have to match. It is thus useful to review briefly the SSS
s^{2}
obtained for QCD matter simulated on a lattice or from other QCD-based effective theories. Because of our limited knowledge on this topic, we have no intention to review comprehensively all the relevant works along this line. We refer the interested readers to Refs.
Brandt et al. (2023)
and references therein for more detailed discussions.
In the following, we give a few examples that have been very useful for us to explore and understand the density profiles of
s^{2}
in NSs.
When necessary and possible, we make connections between the predictions of these QCD-based theories with what our perturbative analyses of the scaled TOV equation have taught us in the previous sections.

FIG. 86:
(Color Online). Left panel: relation between the isospin density
n_{\mathrm{I}}
(reduced by
m_{\pi}^{3}
) and the isospin chemical potential
\mu_{\mathrm{I}}
(reduced by
m_{\pi}
) for a
N_{\mathrm{f}}=(2+1)
-flavor QCD matter using the space-time lattice of sizes
(24^{3}\times 32)\,\mathrm{fm}^{4}
(with lattice spacing about
a\approx 0.22\,\mathrm{fm}
) and
(32^{3}\times 48)\,\mathrm{fm}^{4}
(
a\approx 0.15\,\mathrm{fm}
), respectively.
Right panel: same as that in the left panel but for the
s^{2}
as a function of
\mu_{\mathrm{I}}/m_{\pi}
.
A physical mass about 140 MeV for pions is used.
Figures taken from Ref.
Brandt et al. (2023)
.

FIG. 87:
(Color Online). Same as FIG.
86
but for the dimensionless trace anomaly
\Delta
(left panel) and the polytropic index
\gamma=s^{2}/\phi
with
\phi=P/\varepsilon
.
Figures taken from Ref.
Brandt et al. (2023)

FIG. 88:
(Color Online). Speed of sound and the dimensionless trace anomaly obtained from Ref.
Abbott et al. (2023)
, where two lattices
(14.4^{3}\times 8.8)\,\mathrm{fm}^{4}
and
(15.8^{3}\times 11.6)\,\mathrm{fm}^{4}
are adopted; the pion mass is set as 170 MeV.
Figures taken from Ref.
Abbott et al. (2023)
.

Firstly, in Ref.
Brandt et al. (2023)
, the lattice QCD simulations were performed by employing
N_{\mathrm{f}}=2+1
flavors of dynamical staggered quarks at physical masses and using different lattice spacings.
Their results are shown in FIG.
86
.
The left panel of FIG.
86
shows the relation between isospin density
n_{\mathrm{I}}
(in unit of
m_{\pi}^{3}
) and the isospin chemical potential
\mu_{\mathrm{I}}
(in unit of
m_{\pi}
), here the isospin density
n_{\mathrm{I}}
is obtained via
n_{\mathrm{I}}=(T/V)\partial\ln\mathcal{Z}/\partial\mu_{\mathrm{I}}
with
\mathcal{Z}
the partition function in the grand ensemble; the full
\mu_{\mathrm{I}}
-dependence of the EOS could then be obtained.
Shown in the right panel of FIG.
86
is the isospin chemical potential dependence of the SSS
s^{2}
; it increases at small
\mu_{\mathrm{I}}\approx 0.5m_{\pi}
eventually to exceed the conformal bound 1/3 and develops a peak at
\mu_{\mathrm{I}}\approx 0.77m_{\pi}\approx 108\,\mathrm{MeV}
using
m_{\pi}\approx 140\,\mathrm{MeV}
.
The peak value of
s^{2}
is about
s_{\max}^{2}\approx 0.57
for
a\approx 0.15\,\mathrm{fm}
.
The dimensionless trace anomaly
\Delta=1/3-\phi=1/3-P/\varepsilon
and the polytropic index
\gamma=s^{2}/\phi
are shown similarly in FIG.
87
. The
\Delta
at the peak position of
s^{2}
is about 0.07 and is likely to be negative for even larger
\mu_{\mathrm{I}}
.
We notice that
\Delta\approx 0.07
implies that the ratio of pressure to energy density
\phi\approx 0.26
is large, while the corresponding energy density of the system is about
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{pk}}\approx 30\,\mathrm{MeV}/\mathrm{fm}^{3}
that is far smaller than those in NS cores and is even smaller than
\varepsilon_{0}\approx 150\,\mathrm{MeV}/\mathrm{fm}^{3}
.
This means although the energy density is small, the large ratio
\phi
implies that the matter is highly compressed (due to the lattice spacing set). In this sense, the emergence of a peaked
s^{2}
is consistent with our analysis, i.e., the “artificially strong gravity” between quarks on the lattice effectively extrudes the peak in
s^{2}
density profile.
Similarly, the
\gamma
parameter reaches its maximum about 2.4 at
\mu_{\mathrm{I}}\approx 0.7m_{\pi}
and approaches eventually to the conformal limit 1 at even larger isospin chemical potentials.

FIG. 89:
(Color Online). The chemical potential dependence of
s^{2}
obtained from Refs.
Cotter et al. (2013)
;
Abbott et al. (2023)
;
Brandt et al. (2023)
, here the results of Ref.
Brandt et al. (2023)
and of Ref.
Cotter et al. (2013)
are stretched along the horizontal axis to be comparable with that of Ref.
Abbott et al. (2023)
.

In a recent study, Ref.
Abbott et al. (2023)
adopted two lattices of sizes
14.4^{3}\times 8.8\,\mathrm{fm}^{4}
and
15.8^{3}\times 11.6\,\mathrm{fm}^{4}
to simulate the QCD matter with a pion mass about 170 MeV.
An exceeding of the
s^{2}
over 1/3 was also verified as shown in the upper panel of FIG.
88
. In particular,
s^{2}
exceeds 1/3 for
1.5\lesssim\mu_{\mathrm{I}}/m_{\pi}\lesssim 14
and takes the maximum value about 0.6 at
\mu_{\mathrm{I}}/m_{\pi}\approx 2
. This result is consistent with that in Ref.
Brandt et al. (2023)
but extends to a wider range of isospin densities (or isospin chemical potentials).
The isospin chemical potential was implemented using the grand canonical partition function in Ref.
Brandt et al. (2023)
and so the systematic uncertainties are different from those of Ref.
Abbott et al. (2023)
.
Studies of Ref.
Abbott et al. (2023)
found that the asymptotic agreement wit the pQCD constraints requires very large values of the isospin chemical potential about
\mu_{\mathrm{I}}\gtrsim 2\,\mathrm{GeV}\approx 12m_{\pi}
.
A lower bound
\gtrsim-0.2
on the trace anomaly was observed as shown in the lower panel of FIG.
88
. This finding is consistent with the result of our analysis based on the TOV equations that
\Delta
is likely to be negative in cores of massive NSs.

Summarized in FIG.
89
are the predictions on the SSS (
s^{2}
) from Refs.
Abbott et al. (2023)
;
Brandt et al. (2023)
, and Ref.
Cotter et al. (2013)
using a two-color QCD matter (cyan band). We find from the cyan band that a peak emerges in
s^{2}
at about
\mu/m_{\pi}\approx 0.8
with its value also exceeding the conformal bound, here
m_{\pi}\approx 700\,\mathrm{MeV}
was adopted and the plot is taken from Ref.
Hippert et al. (2024)
using the data of Ref.
Cotter et al. (2013)
.
Moreover, a prediction on
s^{2}
(tan solid line) in an isospin-dependent QCD is given by using a quark-meson model
Chiba and Kojo (2024)
. This model is renormalizable and eliminates high density artifacts in models with the ultraviolet cutoffs, here
m_{\pi}\approx 140\,\mathrm{MeV}
is used.

FIG. 90:
(Color Online). Indication of the emergence of a peak in
s^{2}
at densities
\lesssim 10\rho_{0}
by combining the prediction from an effective field theory constrained by QCD renormalization-group (RG) flows (blue band) and that by a functional-RG (fRG) analysis (pink band)
Leonhardt et al. (2020)
together with the low-density CEFT.
Figures taken from Ref.
Braun and Schallmo (2022)
.

FIG. 91:
(Color Online). The EOS and
s^{2}
of cold and dense QCD matter obtained in a resummed perturbation theory based on hard thermal/dense loops. Figures taken from Ref.
Fujimoto and Fukushima (2022)
.

FIG. 92:
(Color Online). The SSS obtained in a two-flavor color-superconducting quark matter at next-to-leading order, using different gap parameters.
Figure taken from Ref.
Geißel et al. (2024)
.

FIG. 93:
(Color Online). Speed of sound as well as
\phi=P/\varepsilon
for QCD matter at finite temperature with
N_{\mathrm{f}}=2+1
, here
I=\varepsilon-3P
is the dimensional trace anomaly.
Figures taken from Ref.
Borsányi et al. (2010)
.

FIG. 94:
(Color Online). Left pane: sketch of the relation between
\overline{\varepsilon}_{\ell}^{\ast}
(valley position in
s^{2}
) and
\overline{\varepsilon}_{\ell}
(peak position in
\Delta
).
Right panel: function
\mathrm{H}(k)
defined in Eq. (
5.87
) by considering the trace anomaly
\Delta(\overline{\varepsilon})\approx\Delta_{\ell}+2^{-1}\Delta_{\ell}^{\prime\prime}(\overline{\varepsilon}-\overline{\varepsilon}_{\ell})^{2}+6^{-1}\Delta_{\ell}^{\prime\prime\prime}(\overline{\varepsilon}-\overline{\varepsilon}_{\ell})^{3}
where
\overline{\varepsilon}_{\ell}
is the peak position in
\Delta
so
\Delta_{\ell}^{\prime\prime}
is negative.

FIG. 95:
(Color Online). Similar as FIG.
93
using a highly improved staggered quark action. Figures taken from Ref.
Bazavov et al. (2014)
.

In Ref.
Braun and Schallmo (2022)
, a peak occurring at about
\rho/\rho_{0}\lesssim 10
in
s^{2}
is implied by combining the prediction on the SSS profile from a functional renormalization group (fRG) analysis
Leonhardt et al. (2020)
combined with the Fierz-complete setting, the low-density CEFT and an effective field theory constructed by the QCD RG flows
Braun and Schallmo (2022)
. The predicted
s^{2}
is shown in FIG.
90
.
Notice that such large densities
\approx 10\rho_{0}
is probably inaccessible even in very massive NSs. Also notice that in this investigation the pQCD constraints (being effective at densities
\approx 40\rho_{0}
) are not included.
The conclusions of Ref.
Braun and Schallmo (2022)
imply that QCD effects (either perturbatively at high densities or effectively by RG flows) may not be the origin of a peaked structure in
s^{2}
in NSs, partially supporting our finding that the strong-field gravity in GR is responsible for it.
Moreover, it also implies that the
s^{2}
may approach the conformal bound from above (which is different from the pQCD prediction), this was verified recently by a resummed perturbation theory using hard thermal/dense loops
Fujimoto and Fukushima (2022)
, as shown in FIG.
91
.
Other ingredients may also affect the pattern how
s^{2}
approaches the conformal limit. For example, different gap parameters in a two-flavor color-superconducting quark matter (calculated at next-to-leading order) may effectively affect such pattern
Geißel et al. (2024)
, namely
s^{2}
increases with the gap parameter
\Delta_{\ast}
at a given density, as shown in FIG.
92
, see also Refs.
Fujimoto (2024)
;
Fukushima and Minato (2024)
.

It is also interesting to note that the speed of sound and trace anomaly have been studied for QCD matter at finite temperature.
For example, the
s^{2}
,
\phi=P/\varepsilon
and the dimensional trace anomaly
I=\varepsilon-3P
are calculated on a lattice using
N_{\mathrm{f}}=2+1
staggered flavors and one-link stout improvement
Borsányi et al. (2010)
; the results are shown in FIG.
93
.
From the lower panel one can see that there exists a peak in
I/T^{4}
; this peak may correspondingly induce a peak in
\Delta
as a function of energy density
\varepsilon
.

Using a similar method of Subsection
5.3
(see FIG.
48
), we can analyze and understand the influence of the above results (shown in FIG.
93
) on the possible valley structure of
s^{2}
. Denote the peak in
\Delta
as
\Delta_{{\ell}}
, we have (
\Delta_{\ell}^{\prime}\equiv\Delta^{\prime}(\overline{\varepsilon}_{\ell})=0
)

\Delta(\overline{\varepsilon})\approx\Delta_{{\ell}}+\frac{1}{2}\Delta_{{\ell}}^{\prime\prime}\left(\overline{\varepsilon}-\overline{\varepsilon}_{{\ell}}\right)^{2},~~\Delta_{{\ell}}\equiv\Delta(\overline{\varepsilon}_{\ell})>0,~~\Delta_{{\ell}}^{\prime\prime}\equiv\Delta^{\prime\prime}(\overline{\varepsilon}_{\ell})<0,

(5.85)

since “
\ell
” is the local maximum point on
\Delta
, see the left panel of FIG.
94
.
Using the decomposition formula (
5.15
), we than write out the SSS as
s^{2}\approx-\Delta_{{\ell}}^{\prime\prime}\overline{\varepsilon}(\overline{\varepsilon}-\overline{\varepsilon}_{{\ell}})+3^{-1}-\Delta_{{\ell}}-2^{-1}\Delta_{{\ell}}^{\prime\prime}(\overline{\varepsilon}-\overline{\varepsilon}_{{\ell}})^{2}
; taking derivative of this
s^{2}
with respective to
\overline{\varepsilon}
and making it be zero gives the position of the local minimum:

\overline{\varepsilon}_{\ell}^{\ast}\approx{2\overline{\varepsilon}_{{\ell}}}/{3}\leftrightarrow\overline{\varepsilon}_{\ell}^{\ast}/\overline{\varepsilon}_{\ell}\approx 2/3.

(5.86)

This means that the valley in
s^{2}
occurs on the left side of that in
\Delta
. Moreover, the second-order derivative at
\overline{\varepsilon}_{\ell}^{\ast}
is
[\mathrm{d}^{2}s^{2}/\mathrm{d}\overline{\varepsilon}^{2}]_{\overline{\varepsilon}_{\ell}^{\ast}}\approx-3\Delta_{{\ell}}^{\prime\prime}>0
, i.e., it is really a local minimum point.

Ref.
Bazavov et al. (2014)
found the valley (crossover region) in
s^{2}
is at about
T\approx 154\,\mathrm{MeV}
(right panel of FIG.
95
), corresponding to
\overline{\varepsilon}_{\ell}^{\ast}\approx 2
; while that in
I
is at about
T\approx 190\,\mathrm{MeV}
(lower panel of FIG.
93
or the left panel of FIG.
95
), corresponding to
\overline{\varepsilon}_{\ell}\approx 9
.
Therefore
\overline{\varepsilon}_{\ell}^{\ast}/\overline{\varepsilon}_{\ell}\approx 2/9
, which has sizable deviation from the estimate (
5.86
), indicating we need to add next-order terms to the trace anomaly
\Delta(\overline{\varepsilon})
.
Using similar steps, we found that

\boxed{\mathrm{H}(k)\equiv\left(\frac{\overline{\varepsilon}_{\ell}^{\ast}}{\overline{\varepsilon}_{\ell}}\right)=\frac{3}{4}\left(1-\frac{1}{k}\right)+\frac{\sqrt{k^{2}-2k+9}}{4k},}

(5.87)

by including
6^{-1}\Delta_{\ell}^{\prime\prime\prime}(\overline{\varepsilon}-\overline{\varepsilon}_{\ell})^{3}
in
\Delta(\overline{\varepsilon})
;
the
k\equiv{\overline{\varepsilon}_{\ell}\Delta_{\ell}^{\prime\prime\prime}}/{\Delta_{\ell}^{\prime\prime}}
in function
\mathrm{H}(k)
could takes all real values. From the above relation, we obtain inversely that,

\boxed{k\equiv\frac{\overline{\varepsilon}_{\ell}\Delta_{\ell}^{\prime\prime\prime}}{\Delta_{\ell}^{\prime\prime}}=\frac{\mathrm{d}\ln\Delta_{\ell}^{\prime\prime}}{\mathrm{d}\ln\overline{\varepsilon}_{\ell}}=\left[2-3\left(\frac{\overline{\varepsilon}_{\ell}^{\ast}}{\overline{\varepsilon}_{\ell}}\right)\right]\left[1-3\left(\frac{\overline{\varepsilon}_{\ell}^{\ast}}{\overline{\varepsilon}_{\ell}}\right)+2\left(\frac{\overline{\varepsilon}_{\ell}^{\ast}}{\overline{\varepsilon}_{\ell}}\right)^{2}\right]^{-1},}

(5.88)

here
\mathrm{d}\ln\Delta_{\ell}^{\prime\prime}/\mathrm{d}\ln\overline{\varepsilon}_{\ell}\equiv[\mathrm{d}\ln\Delta_{\ell}^{\prime\prime}(\overline{\varepsilon})/\mathrm{d}\ln\overline{\varepsilon}]_{\overline{\varepsilon}=\overline{\varepsilon}_{\ell}}
.
The second-order derivative of
s^{2}
at
\overline{\varepsilon}_{\ell}^{\ast}
is

\boxed{\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}s^{2}}{\mathrm{d}\overline{\varepsilon}^{2}}\right|_{\overline{\varepsilon}=\overline{\varepsilon}_{\ell}^{\ast}}=-\Delta_{\ell}^{\prime\prime}\sqrt{k^{2}-2k+9}>0,~~\mbox{since}~~\Delta_{\ell}^{\prime\prime}<0,}

(5.89)

i.e., it is a local minimum point in
s^{2}
.
For
k=0
,
[\mathrm{d}^{2}s^{2}/\mathrm{d}\overline{\varepsilon}^{2}]_{\overline{\varepsilon}_{\ell}^{\ast}}
reduces to
-3\Delta_{\ell}^{\prime\prime}>0
as given above.

The ratio
\overline{\varepsilon}_{\ell}^{\ast}/\overline{\varepsilon}_{\ell}
of (
5.87
) monotonically increases with
k
and takes the minimum value of 1/2 when
k\to-\infty
, see the right panel of FIG.
94
. Therefore, in order to make
\overline{\varepsilon}_{\ell}^{\ast}/\overline{\varepsilon}_{\ell}
be close to about
2/9
, a large negative
k
is advantage. This means
k<0
or
\Delta_{\ell}^{\prime\prime\prime}>0
(a positive skewness of
\Delta
at point “
\ell
”) and in the mean while
k^{-1}
is small. Consequently, we have

\overline{\varepsilon}_{\ell}^{\ast}/\overline{\varepsilon}_{\ell}\approx 2^{-1}\left(1-k^{-1}-2k^{-2}-2k^{-3}+2k^{-4}+10k^{-5}\right);~~\mbox{for large and negative }k.

(5.90)

We also give the expansions for
\overline{\varepsilon}_{\ell}^{\ast}/\overline{\varepsilon}_{\ell}
under other two limits as:

\displaystyle\overline{\varepsilon}_{\ell}^{\ast}/\overline{\varepsilon}_{\ell}\approx

\displaystyle 1-\frac{1}{k}+\frac{1}{k^{2}}+\frac{1}{k^{3}}-\frac{1}{k^{4}}-\frac{5}{k^{5}},~~\mbox{for large and positive }k;

(5.91)

\displaystyle\overline{\varepsilon}_{\ell}^{\ast}/\overline{\varepsilon}_{\ell}\approx

\displaystyle\frac{2}{3}\left(1+\frac{1}{18}k+\frac{1}{162}k^{2}-\frac{1}{1458}k^{3}-\frac{5}{13122}k^{4}-\frac{1}{39366}k^{5}\right),~~\mbox{for small }k\approx 0.

(5.92)

Generally, we have from (
5.87
) that,

\boxed{\frac{\overline{\varepsilon}_{\ell}^{\ast}\leftrightarrow\mbox{valley position in SSS $s^{2}$}}{\overline{\varepsilon}_{\ell}\leftrightarrow\mbox{peak position in trace anomaly $\Delta$}}:~~\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}1/2\leq\overline{\varepsilon}_{\ell}^{\ast}/\overline{\varepsilon}_{\ell}\leq 2/3&~~~~\Delta_{\ell}^{\prime\prime\prime}\geq 0;\\
2/3\leq\overline{\varepsilon}_{\ell}^{\ast}/\overline{\varepsilon}_{\ell}\leq 1&~~~~\Delta_{\ell}^{\prime\prime\prime}\leq 0,\end{array}\right.}

(5.93)

see the right panel of FIG.
94
for the
k
-dependence of the function
\mathrm{H}(k)
.

The
s^{2}
at
\overline{\varepsilon}_{\ell}^{\ast}
is

\boxed{s^{2}(\overline{\varepsilon}_{\ell}^{\ast})=\frac{1}{3}-\Delta_{\ell}-\frac{1}{96}\frac{\Delta_{\ell}^{\prime\prime}\overline{\varepsilon}_{\ell}^{2}}{k^{2}}\left(3+k-\sqrt{k^{2}-2k+9}\right)^{2}\left(6-k+\sqrt{k^{2}-2k+9}\right),~~k=\frac{\overline{\varepsilon}_{\ell}\Delta_{\ell}^{\prime\prime\prime}}{\Delta_{\ell}^{\prime\prime}}.}

(5.94)

Therefore for large and negative
k
, it reduces to

s^{2}(\overline{\varepsilon}_{\ell}^{\ast})\approx\frac{1}{3}-\Delta_{\ell}+\overline{\varepsilon}_{\ell}^{2}\Delta_{\ell}^{\prime\prime}\left(\frac{k}{12}-\frac{1}{8}\right)=\frac{1}{3}-\Delta_{\ell}+\frac{\overline{\varepsilon}_{\ell}^{3}\Delta_{\ell}^{\prime\prime\prime}}{12}\left(1-\frac{3}{2k}\right)\approx\frac{1}{3}-\Delta_{\ell}+\frac{\overline{\varepsilon}_{\ell}^{3}\Delta_{\ell}^{\prime\prime\prime}}{12},~~\mbox{large negative }k,

(5.95)

so
s^{2}(\overline{\varepsilon}_{\ell}^{\ast})\geq 0
implies
\Delta_{\ell}\lesssim 3^{-1}+12^{-1}\overline{\varepsilon}_{\ell}^{3}\Delta_{\ell}^{\prime\prime\prime}
.
While for large and positive
k
,

s^{2}(\overline{\varepsilon}_{\ell}^{\ast})\approx\frac{1}{3}-\Delta_{\ell}-\frac{1}{96}\frac{\Delta_{\ell}^{\prime\prime}\overline{\varepsilon}_{\ell}^{2}}{k^{2}}\left(80-\frac{96}{k}\right)\approx\frac{1}{3}-\Delta_{\ell}-\frac{5\Delta_{\ell}^{\prime\prime}}{6}\left(\frac{\Delta_{\ell}^{\prime\prime}}{\Delta_{\ell}^{\prime\prime\prime}}\right)^{2},~~\mbox{large positive }k,

(5.96)

so the non-negativeness of
s^{2}
implies
\Delta_{\ell}\lesssim 3^{-1}-5\Delta_{\ell}^{\prime\prime,3}/6\Delta_{\ell}^{\prime\prime\prime,2}
.
On other hand, for small
k
, we have

s^{2}(\overline{\varepsilon}_{\ell}^{\ast})\approx\frac{1}{3}-\Delta_{\ell}-\left(\frac{1}{6}-\frac{5k}{81}\right)\overline{\varepsilon}_{\ell}^{2}\Delta_{\ell}^{\prime\prime}\approx\frac{1}{3}-\Delta_{\ell}-\frac{\overline{\varepsilon}_{\ell}^{2}\Delta_{\ell}^{\prime\prime}}{6},~~k\approx 0,

(5.97)

and now
s^{2}(\overline{\varepsilon}_{\ell}^{\ast})\geq 0
gives
\Delta_{\ell}\lesssim 3^{-1}-6^{-1}\overline{\varepsilon}_{\ell}^{2}\Delta_{\ell}^{\prime\prime}
.

Similarly, if we include the fourth-order correction of
\Delta_{\ell}^{\prime\prime\prime\prime}
(from
24^{-1}\Delta_{\ell}^{\prime\prime\prime\prime}(\overline{\varepsilon}-\overline{\varepsilon}_{\ell})^{4}
) and treat
\Delta_{\ell}^{\prime\prime\prime\prime}
as small, the result is given by,

\boxed{\overline{\varepsilon}_{\ell}^{\ast}/\overline{\varepsilon}_{\ell}\approx\mathrm{H}(k)\left(1+\frac{\overline{\varepsilon}_{\ell}^{2}\Delta_{\ell}^{\prime\prime\prime\prime}}{6\Delta_{\ell}^{\prime\prime}}\frac{(2-5\mathrm{H}(k))(1-\mathrm{H}(k))^{2}}{\mathrm{H}(k)\sqrt{k^{2}-2k+9}}\right),}

(5.98)

where the function
\mathrm{H}(k)
is defined in (
5.87
).
The overall factor involving
k
in the bracket is negative, so a negative
\Delta_{\ell}^{\prime\prime\prime\prime}
(since
\Delta_{\ell}^{\prime\prime}<0
) may help reduce the value of
\overline{\varepsilon}_{\ell}^{\ast}/\overline{\varepsilon}_{\ell}
. In fact, considering large negative
k
as discussed in the above, we may obtain,

\displaystyle\overline{\varepsilon}_{\ell}^{\ast}/\overline{\varepsilon}_{\ell}\approx

\displaystyle\mathrm{H}(k)\left[1+\frac{\overline{\varepsilon}_{\ell}^{2}\Delta_{\ell}^{\prime\prime\prime\prime}}{24\Delta_{\ell}^{\prime\prime}}\frac{1}{k}\left(1-\frac{1}{k}-\frac{20}{k^{2}}+\cdots\right)\right]

\displaystyle\approx

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\left[1-\frac{1}{k}\left(1-\frac{\overline{\varepsilon}_{\ell}^{2}\Delta_{\ell}^{\prime\prime\prime\prime}}{24\Delta_{\ell}^{\prime\prime}}\right)-\frac{2}{k^{2}}\left(1+\frac{\overline{\varepsilon}_{\ell}^{2}\Delta_{\ell}^{\prime\prime\prime\prime}}{24\Delta_{\ell}^{\prime\prime}}\right)-\frac{2}{k^{3}}\left(1+\frac{7\overline{\varepsilon}_{\ell}^{2}\Delta_{\ell}^{\prime\prime\prime\prime}}{16\Delta_{\ell}^{\prime\prime}}\right)+\cdots\right]

\displaystyle\approx

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\left[1-\frac{1}{k}-\frac{2}{k^{2}}-\frac{2}{k^{3}}+\frac{\overline{\varepsilon}_{\ell}^{2}\Delta_{\ell}^{\prime\prime\prime\prime}}{24\Delta_{\ell}^{\prime\prime}}\frac{1}{k}\left(1-\frac{2}{k}-\frac{21}{k^{2}}\right)+\cdots\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\left[1-\frac{1}{k}-\frac{2}{k^{2}}-\frac{2}{k^{3}}+\frac{1}{24}\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}\ln\Delta_{\ell}^{\prime\prime\prime}}{\mathrm{d}\ln\overline{\varepsilon}_{\ell}}\right)\left(1-\frac{2}{k}-\frac{21}{k^{2}}\right)+\cdots\right].

(5.99)

Similarly, for large and positive
k
, we have

\displaystyle\overline{\varepsilon}_{\ell}^{\ast}/\overline{\varepsilon}_{\ell}\approx

\displaystyle 1-\frac{1}{k}+\frac{1}{k^{2}}+\frac{1}{k^{3}}-\frac{\overline{\varepsilon}_{\ell}^{2}\Delta_{\ell}^{\prime\prime\prime\prime}}{2\Delta_{\ell}^{\prime\prime}}\frac{1}{k^{3}}\left(1-\frac{8}{3k}-\frac{8}{3k^{2}}\right)+\cdots

\displaystyle\approx

\displaystyle 1-\frac{1}{k}+\frac{1}{k^{2}}+\frac{1}{k^{3}}-\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}\ln\Delta_{\ell}^{\prime\prime\prime}}{\mathrm{d}\ln\overline{\varepsilon}_{\ell}}\right)\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}\ln\Delta_{\ell}^{\prime\prime}}{\mathrm{d}\ln\overline{\varepsilon}_{\ell}}\right)^{-2}\left(1-\frac{8}{3k}-\frac{8}{3k^{2}}\right)+\cdots,

(5.100)

while for small
k
,

\overline{\varepsilon}_{\ell}^{\ast}/\overline{\varepsilon}_{\ell}\approx\frac{2}{3}\left[1+\frac{1}{18}k+\frac{1}{162}k^{2}-\frac{1}{1458}k^{3}-\frac{1}{81}\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}\ln\Delta_{\ell}^{\prime\prime\prime}}{\mathrm{d}\ln\overline{\varepsilon}_{\ell}}\right)\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}\ln\Delta_{\ell}^{\prime\prime}}{\mathrm{d}\ln\overline{\varepsilon}_{\ell}}\right)\left(1+\frac{1}{36}k-\frac{2}{27}k^{2}-\frac{4}{243}k^{3}\right)\right].

(5.101)

FIG. 96:
(Color Online). The EOS for finite-temperature QCD matter obtained in Ref.
Bazavov et al. (2014)
(hatched pink band) and that from Ref.
Borsányi et al. (2021)
at three different baryon chemical potential
\mu_{\mathrm{B}}
(shown by solid symbols).
The hatch plum band is the central EOS of NSs at TOV configuration with
M_{\mathrm{NS}}^{\max}\approx 1.7_{-0.2}^{+0.2}M_{\odot}
.

FIG. 97:
(Color Online). Comparison between the
s^{2}
(as a function of energy density) obtained from the finite-temperature lattice QCD simulations
Borsányi et al. (2010)
;
Bazavov et al. (2014)
and the SSS at NS centers (varying with the central energy density); a small NS mass about
1.3_{-0.2}^{+0.2}M_{\odot}
is necessary to make the comparison be harmonic.

FIG. 98:
(Color Online). The speed of sound squared as a function of the effective
temperature
T_{\mathrm{eff}}
with the CMS data point obtained from ultra-central PbPb collision data at
\sqrt{s_{\mathrm{NN}}}\approx 5.02\,\mathrm{TeV}
. Figure taken from Ref.
Collaboration (2024)
.

We show in FIG.
96
the EOS for QCD matter at finite temperature obtained from Ref.
Bazavov et al. (2014)
(hatched pink band and FIG.
95
) and that from Ref.
Borsányi et al. (2021)
at three different baryon chemical potential
\mu_{\mathrm{B}}
(shown by solid symbols);
the central EOS of NSs at the TOV configuration with
M_{\mathrm{NS}}^{\max}\approx 1.7_{-0.2}^{+0.2}M_{\odot}
is also shown by the hatched plum band.
The comparison in FIG.
96
provides us an alternative on understanding why the conformal bound on
s^{2}=1/3
is not violated in such lattice QCD simulations. This is because the analogous NS EOS (though not perfectly consistent with the lattice-simulated EOS) is not stiff enough to reach the boundary of
s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}=1/3
(indicated by the light-green band). It also implies that the dimensionless trace anomaly
\Delta
is positive in the simulations as a
1.7M_{\odot}
NS at the TOV configuration could even hardly reach the boundary of
\Delta_{\mathrm{c}}\leq 0
(shown by the light-grey band).
We can similarly understand the realization of
s^{2}<1/3
in these finite-temperature lattice QCD simulations
Borsányi et al. (2010)
;
Borsányi et al. (2021)
;
Bazavov et al. (2014)
by directly comparing the SSS (as a function of energy density) obtained with the one predicted for NSs; the result is shown in FIG.
97
. It is seen that a relatively small NS mass about
1.3_{-0.2}^{+0.2}M_{\odot}
at the TOV configuration is necessary/relevant to make the SSS’s obtained from the two sides be harmonic with each other. This means that the EOS of QCD matter at finite temperature from these lattice simulations is similar as that in the core of a light NS; whose
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}
can hardly exceed
1\,\mathrm{GeV}/\mathrm{fm}^{3}
, thus
s^{2}<1/3
.

Finally, it is necessary to mention that measuring the speed of sound in hot and dense matter created during ultra-relativistic heavy-ion collision has been an ongoing endeavor. Some interesting results have been obtained from several experiments. For example, using the CMS/LHC data obtained from ultra-central lead-lead collisions at
\sqrt{s_{\mathrm{NN}}}\approx 5.02\,\mathrm{TeV}
; the SSS in the hot and dense quark-gluon matter is found to be about
0.241\pm 0.018
with an effective medium temperature about
T_{\mathrm{eff}}\approx 219\pm 8\,\mathrm{MeV}

Collaboration (2024)
, see FIG.
98
for the temperature dependence of the
s^{2}
.
These results are quite consistent with an earlier measurement which revealed that
s^{2}\approx 0.24\pm 0.04
at
T_{\mathrm{eff}}\approx 222\pm 9\,\mathrm{MeV}

Gardim et al. (2020)
.
We shall not discuss more on this issue as our primary motivation here is to note an important alternative approach (besides studying NSs) for understanding why both the conformal bound
s^{2}<1/3
and positiveness of trace anomaly
\Delta>0
are fulfilled in the lattice QCD simulations
Borsányi et al. (2010)
;
Borsányi et al. (2021)
;
Bazavov et al. (2014)
.

6 Gravitational Bound on Trace Anomaly and Related Issues

In this section, we study the gravitational bound on the trace anomaly
\Delta
and some related issues.
In Subsection
6.1
we briefly discuss the upper (lower) bound on the ratio
\phi=P/\varepsilon
(thus the corresponding dimensionless trace anomaly
\Delta\equiv 1/3-\phi
) in NS cores. This topic was recently reviewed by us in Ref.
Cai and Li (2024)
. For completeness, here we recall the main points. Subsection
6.2
is devoted to the question whether the core matter in NSs could be conformal based on some empirical characteristics. Then in Subsection
6.3
, we show that the compactness scaling straightforwardly probe the trace anomaly
\Delta
in a model-independent manner.
As a direct consequence, we then study in Subsection
6.4
whether the currently available NS data (mass and radius) could invariably generate a peaked SSS profile
using the trace anomaly decomposition.
Since
\mathrm{X}
is closely related to the NS compactness
\xi
(as shown in Eq. (
4.8
)), the upper bound on
\mathrm{X}
may naturally induce a relevant bound on
\xi
and consequently impact the causality boundary.
A causality boundary for stable NSs could basically be described as

\boxed{\mbox{causality boundary: }R/\mathrm{km}\gtrsim\mbox{some minimum value for a given }M_{\mathrm{NS}}.}

(6.1)

The simplest (though unrealistic) boundary is the Schwarzshild relation
R_{\mathrm{Sch}}=2M
derived from the BH event.
Consequently, it leads to the boundary
R_{\mathrm{Sch}}/\mathrm{km}\gtrsim 2.95M_{\mathrm{NS}}/M_{\odot}
.
However, such boundary has little relevance for our research purpose here since BHs are not NSs.
More efficient and tight causality boundaries exist, based on simple stellar models or using empirical considerations.
We briefly discuss the existing causality boundaries for NSs in Subsection
6.5
, and then give an upper bound for the compactness in Subsection
6.6
using our novel scalings, see Eq. (
6.32
).
Then in Subsection
6.7
, we give a new causality boundary using our mass and radius scalings obtained in SECTION
4
for NSs at the TOV configuration. We study in Subsection
6.8
the ultimate energy density and pressure allowed in realistic NSs.
Finally, we discuss the implications of the conjecture of a positive
\Delta
on the peaked structure of
s^{2}
in Subsection
6.9
.

6.1 Lower bound on trace anomaly due to strong-field gravity in GR

We have shown earlier with Eq.(
4.18
) and Eq. (
4.17
) that the
\mathrm{X}=P_{\mathrm{c}}/\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}
is upper bounded as
\mathrm{X}\lesssim 0.374
. This result is obtained under the specific condition that it gives the upper limit for
\phi=P/\varepsilon
at the centers of NSs at the TOV configuration.
By generalizing this special situation, we can show that
Cai et al. (2023)
;
Cai and Li (2024)

\boxed{\mbox{near/at the centers of generally stable NSs along the M-R curve:}~~\phi=P/\varepsilon=\widehat{P}/\widehat{\varepsilon}\leq\mathrm{X}\lesssim 0.374.}

(6.2)

An upper bound on
\phi
sizably deviating from 1 implies that the EOS of core NS matter is significantly nonlinear. As we discussed earlier, this is because
s^{2}\leq 1
due to causality is equivalent to
\phi\equiv P/\varepsilon\leq 1
only for a linear EOS of the form
P=\zeta\varepsilon
with
\zeta
being some constant.
It is well known that the strong-field gravity in GR is fundamentally nonlinear, the EOS of NS matter especially in its core is thus also nonlinear. Therefore, the causality condition
s^{2}\leq 1
is expected to be appreciably different from
\phi\leq 1
, and it may also effectively render the upper bound for
\phi
to be smaller than 1.

The EOS of nuclear matter may be strongly nonlinear depending on both the internal interactions and the external environment/constraint of the system. This means that
\phi\leq 1
is necessary but not sufficient to ensure supra-dense matter in all NSs always stay casual. For example, the EOS of noninteracting degenerate Fermions (e.g., electrons) can be written in the polytropic form
P=K\varepsilon^{\beta}

(
Shapiro and Teukolsky, 1983
)
where
\beta=5/3
for non-relativistic and
\beta=4/3
for extremely relativistic electrons; consequently
\phi\leq\beta^{-1}<1
.
Similarly, a long time ago Zel’dovich considered the EOS of an isolated ultra-dense system of baryons interacting through a vector field
(
Zel’dovich, 1961
)
. In this case,
P=\varepsilon\sim\rho^{2}
, here
\rho
is the baryon number density; therefore
P/\varepsilon\leq 1
.
The EOS of dense nuclear matter where nucleon interactions are dominated by the
\omega
-meson exchange in the Walecka model
(
Walecka, 1974
)
is an example of this type, namely
Cai and Li (2022)

P^{(\omega)}\approx\varepsilon^{(\omega)}\approx\frac{1}{2}g_{\omega}^{2}\omega^{2}\approx\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{g_{\omega}}{m_{\omega}}\right)^{2}\rho^{2},

(6.3)

here
m_{\omega}
is the mass of
\omega
-meson and
g_{\omega}
is the
\omega
-nucleon coupling constant.
More generally, however, going beyond the vector field, the baryon density dependence of either
P(\rho)
or
\varepsilon(\rho)
could be very complicated and nontrivial; and the resulting EOS
P(\varepsilon)
could also be significantly nonlinear.

The upper bound for
\phi
is a fundamental quantity encapsulating essentially the strong-field properties of gravity in GR. Its accurate determination can help improve our understanding about the nature of gravity
Hoyle (2003)
.
An upper bound on
\phi
substantially different from 1 then vividly characterizes how GR affects the superdense matter existing in NSs.
Indeed, there are fundamental physics issues regarding both the strong-field gravity and supra-dense matter as well as their couplings. For example, what is gravity? This has been one of the most fundamental questions unanswered yet. Massive NSs provide a natural laboratory to test possible answers to this question. It is well known that a gravity-matter duality exists in theories describing NS properties, see, e.g., Refs.
Psaltis (2008)
;
Shao (2019)
for reviews. The duality reflects a deep connection between microscopic physics of elementary particles in supra-dense matter and the nature of super-powerful gravity in and around massive NSs.
Developing a unified theory for the matter-gravity system in compact objects has been a long standing goal of cosmology. They both have to be fully understood to finally unravel mysteries associated with the most compact visible objects in the Universe.
Interestingly, some NS observational properties have been shown to have the potential to help break the gravity-matter duality, see, e.g., Refs.
DeDeo and Psaltis (2003)
;
Wen et al. (2009)
;
Lin et al. (2014)
;
He et al. (2015)
;
Yang et al. (2020)
.

FIG. 99:
(Color Online). Trace anomaly
\Delta
as a function of energy density
\varepsilon/\varepsilon_{0}
, here the
\Delta
in NSs tends to be negative although the pQCD prediction on it approaches zero,
\varepsilon_{0}\approx 150\,\mathrm{MeV}/\mathrm{fm}^{3}
is the energy density at nuclear saturation density.
Figure taken from Ref.
Fujimoto et al. (2022)
.

Back to the upper bound (
6.2
), we want to emphasize that its validity is limited to small
\widehat{r}
due to the perturbative nature of expanding
\widehat{P}(\widehat{r})
and
\widehat{\varepsilon}(\widehat{r})
. Whether
\phi=P/\varepsilon
could exceed such upper limit at even larger distances away from NS centers depends on the joint analysis of
s^{2}
and
P/\varepsilon
, e.g., by including more higher-order contributions of the expansions
Cai et al. (2023)
. The upper bound
P/\varepsilon\lesssim 0.374
(at least near the NS centers) is an intrinsic property of the TOV equations at the leading order of their expansions. At the next-to-leading order,
the upper bound of
P/\varepsilon
was found to increase by about 2% to
0.381

Cai and Li (2024)
. The corresponding formalism, however, becomes too complicated for our analytical analyses to remain transparent and tractable at this time. In this review, we thus stay within the leading-order expansions. The upper bound of
P/\varepsilon

embodies the strong-field aspects of gravity in GR, especially the strong self-gravitating nature
Cai and Li (2024)
.
In this sense, there is no guarantee
a prior
that this bound is consistent with all microscopic nuclear EOSs (either relativistic or non-relativistic). This is mainly because the latter were conventionally constructed without considering the limit set by the strong-field gravity. The robustness of such gravitational upper bound for
\phi=P/\varepsilon
can be tested only by observable astrophysical quantities/processes under strong-field gravity,
such as NS M-R data, NS-NS mergers and/or NS-BH mergers
(
Baumgarte and Shapiro, 2010
;
Shibata, 2015
;
Baiotti and Rezzolla, 2017
;
Kyutoku et al., 2021
)
. As mentioned earlier, in the NS matter-gravity inseparable system, it is the total action that determines the matter state and NS structure. Thus, to our best knowledge
Cai and Li (2024)
, there is no physics requirement that the EOS of superdense matter created in vacuum from high-energy heavy-ion collisions or other terrestrial laboratory experiments where effects of gravity can be neglected to be the same as that in NSs as nuclear matter in the two situations are in very different environments.
Furthermore, we point out that (
6.2
) is general from analyzing perturbatively analytical solutions of the scaled TOV equations without using any specific nuclear EOS.

Because the TOV equations are results of a hydrodynamical equilibrium of NS matter in the environment of a strong-field gravity from extremizing the total action of the matter-gravity system, features revealed from analyzing the gravitational aspects of the TOV equations must be matched by the nuclear EOS. This requirement can then put strong constraints on the latter. In particular, the upper bound for
\phi
as
\phi\lesssim 0.374
of Eq. (
6.2
) may enable us to limit the density dependence of nuclear EOS relevant for NS modelings, see, e.g. Ref.
Cai and Li (2024)
for an illustrative example. Of course, logically for the same reasons mentioned above, the EOS extracted from NSs can only be compared to that from terrestrial nuclear experiments at the negligible-gravity limit. Thus, in our opinion, the nuclear EOS is environment-dependent and maybe nothing is wrong if the nuclear EOSs from studying massive NSs are very different from those extracted from high-energy heavy-ion collisions experiments on earth. Moreover, the difference may be invaluable for understanding the nature of both strong-field gravity and supra-dense matter.

FIG. 100:
(Color Online). Summary of current constraints on the lower bound of trace anomaly
\Delta
in NSs from different analyses with respect to the pQCD (dot-dashed line) and GR (black solid line) predictions. See Ref.
Cai and Li (2024)
for details. Figure taken from Ref.
Cai and Li (2024)
.

The upper bound on
\phi
of Eq. (
6.2
) induces a lower bound on the dimensionless trace anomaly
Cai and Li (2024)

\boxed{\Delta\geq\Delta_{\mathrm{GR}}\approx-0.04.}

(6.4)

It is very interesting to notice that such GR bound on
\Delta
is very close to the one predicted by perturbative QCD (pQCD) at extremely high densities owning to the realization of approximate conformal symmetry of quark matter
(
Bjorken, 1983
;
Fujimoto et al., 2022
)
, as shown in FIG.
99
using certain NS modelings. A possible negative
\Delta
in NSs was first pointed out in Ref.
Fujimoto et al. (2022)
, since then several studies on this issue have been made. We have summarized the constraints on
\Delta
recently in Ref.
Cai and Li (2024)
and the result is shown again in FIG.
100

Cai and Li (2024)
. We refer the interested readers to Ref.
Cai and Li (2024)
for more details. Finally, we emphasize that the trace anomaly is very useful as its energy density decomposition gives the SSS. We have discussed this issue in Subsection
5.3
and will investigate in Subsection
6.4
whether the current available NS data (mass and radius) can invariably generate a peaked
s^{2}
density or radius profile.

6.2 Can the supradense matter at NS centers be (nearly) conformal?

As we discussed in previous sections that a linear EOS in the form of
\widehat{P}=\zeta\widehat{\varepsilon}
with
\zeta=\mathrm{const.}
is inconsistent with the TOV equations.
In this subsection, we explore further the conformal properties of the NS matter in their cores. A basic quantity to characterize the conformality is the polytropic index defined in Eq. (
1.4
).
The condition of conformality in NSs is expressed as

\boxed{\Delta\to 1/3\mbox{~~and~~}s^{2}\to 1/3,~~\mbox{or equivalently }\gamma\to 1.}

(6.5)

For example, Ref.
Annala et al. (2023)
claimed that for a
2M_{\odot}
NS the polytropic index
\gamma
approximately equals to 2 while that for a NS at the TOV configuration is very close to 1. The latter means that NSs at the TOV configuration contain conformal dense matter in their cores.
However, the
\gamma
parameter in NS centers, actually, can not be unity since
\Delta_{\mathrm{c}}
(or equivalently
\mathrm{X}
) and
s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}
could not approach zero (or 1/3) and 1/3 simultaneously, as shown by our Eq. (
4.17
).
Physically, due to the nonlinear nature of the central EOS in NSs, it is likely that
\gamma\neq 1
(actually
\gamma=1
if and only if
P\propto\varepsilon
).
In particular, it is straightforward to find that
4/3\leq\gamma_{\mathrm{c}}\lesssim 2.67
using our formula (
4.17
). For example, considering
{\mathrm{X}}\approx 0.24_{-0.07}^{+0.05}
for PSR J0740+6620
Cai et al. (2023)
leads to
\gamma_{\mathrm{c}}\approx 1.86_{-0.21}^{+0.22}
.
Moreover, for
\Delta_{\mathrm{c}}=0
the
\gamma_{\mathrm{c}}
index is
7/3\approx 2.33
.
These values are summarized in TAB.
6
for comparisons.

{\mathrm{X}}
are shown (last four columns),

is the logarithmic
derivative of
\Delta_{\mathrm{c}}
. Table taken from Ref.
Cai et al. (2023)
with slight modifications.

FIG. 101:
(Color Online). Relation between
\gamma_{\mathrm{c}}
and
\xi_{\max}
connected by Eq. (
6.6
); the three vertical gray lines mark the critical compactness for
\gamma_{\mathrm{c}}\approx 1.75
, giving the corresponding maximum NS mass (under a given radius).

We can also establish the relation between
\gamma_{\mathrm{c}}
and the NS compactness
\xi_{\max}
for NSs at TOV configuration, the result is

\boxed{\gamma_{\mathrm{c}}=\frac{2}{3}\frac{(6\Lambda-1)\sqrt{1-8\Lambda+4\Lambda^{2}}+1-10\Lambda+6\Lambda^{2}}{(4\Lambda-1)\sqrt{1-8\Lambda+4\Lambda^{2}}+1-8\Lambda+4\Lambda^{2}}\approx\frac{4}{3}\left(1+\Lambda+\frac{11}{2}\Lambda^{2}+34\Lambda^{3}\right)+\cdots,}

(6.6)

where,

\Lambda\approx\frac{105}{173}\frac{\xi_{\max}/\Sigma+0.106/(R_{\max}/\mathrm{km})}{1-0.64/(R_{\max}/\mathrm{km})}\lesssim\frac{105}{173}\frac{\xi_{\max}}{\Sigma},

(6.7)

with
\Sigma=M_{\odot}/\mathrm{km}\approx 1.477
.
The relation (
6.6
) connects the polytropic index directly with the NS compactness.
For example, we have
\gamma_{\mathrm{c}}\approx 1.88
for
\xi_{\max}\approx 0.25
and
R_{\max}\approx 12.5\,\mathrm{km}
; and similarly in order that
\gamma_{\mathrm{c}}\lesssim 1.75
one needs
\xi_{\max}\lesssim 0.231
(
\xi_{\max}\lesssim 0.233
) or equivalently
M_{\mathrm{NS}}^{\max}\lesssim 1.88M_{\odot}
(
M_{\mathrm{NS}}^{\max}\lesssim 2.05M_{\odot}
) using
R_{\max}\approx 12\,\mathrm{km}
(
R_{\max}\approx 13\,\mathrm{km}
).
See FIG.
101
for the relation between
\gamma_{\mathrm{c}}
and
\xi_{\max}
.
Including the first-order correction in (
6.6
), we have
\gamma_{\mathrm{c}}\approx 4/3+0.55\xi_{\max}
, which is close to
4/3+89M_{\mathrm{NS}}/105R
obtained under the assumption that both
\varepsilon
and
\rho
are constants in the interior of the stars
Shapiro and Teukolsky (1983)
. Notice the
\gamma
defined in Ref.
Shapiro and Teukolsky (1983)
is
(s^{2}/\phi)(1+\phi)
so it is slightly larger than
s^{2}/\phi
.
For NSs not at the TOV configuration, we shall use Eq. (
4.15
), and then

\gamma_{\mathrm{c}}=1+\frac{1+\Psi}{3}\frac{1+3\mathrm{X}^{2}+4\mathrm{X}}{1-3\mathrm{X}^{2}}\geq\frac{4+\Psi}{3}\geq 4/3.

(6.8)

This analysis is model-independent, reflecting the intrinsic property encapsulated in the TOV equations. Therefore, the TOV equations alone imply that the
\gamma
could not be 1 in NS centers.
In particular, we have
\gamma_{\mathrm{c}}\approx 1+(1+\Psi)/3
for small
\mathrm{X}
(Newtonian limit). It is consistent with the relation between the polytropic index
n
(appearing in
P\sim\varepsilon^{1+1/n}
) and
\Psi
we have established in the relation (
2.49
).

FIG. 102:
(Color Online). The measure
\Theta\equiv d_{\mathrm{c}}
as a function of baryon density, here
\Delta^{\prime}=s^{2}(1/\gamma-1)=\phi-s^{2}
. Figures taken from Ref.
Annala et al. (2023)
and Ref.
Takátsy et al. (2023)
, respectively.

In order to relax the conformality condition of (
6.5
), Ref.
Annala et al. (2023)
furthermore defined a quantity

\boxed{\Theta\equiv\sqrt{\Delta^{2}+(P/\varepsilon-s^{2})^{2}}=\sqrt{\left(1/3-\phi\right)^{2}+\left(\phi-s^{2}\right)^{2}},}

(6.9)

to measure the conformality and found it should be
\lesssim 0.65
for all NSs. The empirical criterion
\Theta\lesssim 0.2
(besides
\gamma\lesssim 1.75
in conventional analysis) was then adopted
Annala et al. (2023)
to identify the nearly-conformality at a given density, see the left panel of FIG.
102
.
The right panel of FIG.
102
shows a similar prediction for the
\Theta_{\mathrm{c}}
as a function of baryon density using NICER’s measurements and the secondary component of GW190814
Abbott et al. (2020)
. The predicted value for
\Theta_{\mathrm{c}}
is slightly smaller than 0.2 at the TOV configuration.
Based on our formula (
4.17
), we find that
0.19\lesssim\Theta_{\mathrm{c}}\lesssim 0.63
with the minimum (maximum) value obtained at
{\mathrm{X}}\approx 0.18
(
{\mathrm{X}}\approx 0.374
) and
\Theta_{\mathrm{c}}\approx 0.22_{-0.03}^{+0.09}
for
{\mathrm{X}}\approx 0.24_{-0.07}^{+0.05}
(PSR J0740+6620).
We summarize in TAB.
6
these values at four reference
{\mathrm{X}}
(0.18, 0.24,
1/3
and 0.374), where
t_{\mathrm{c}}\equiv\mathrm{d}\Delta_{\mathrm{c}}/\mathrm{d}\ln\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}={\mathrm{X}}-s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}
as the logarithmic
derivative of
\Delta_{\mathrm{c}}

Annala et al. (2023)
(with respect to
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}
) is also given. We have
t_{\mathrm{c}}\to 0
if
\gamma_{\mathrm{c}}\to 1
(the so-called conformal limit of matter), therefore
-t_{\mathrm{c}}
characterizes the deviation from the conformal limit. In Ref.
Fujimoto et al. (2022)
,
-t=s^{2}-P/\varepsilon
and
1/3-\Delta=P/\varepsilon
are decomposed as the derivative and non-derivative parts of
s^{2}
, respectively, see more detailed discussions in Subsection
5.3
.
Equivalently, one has
\Theta=[{\Delta^{2}+t^{2}}]^{1/2}
.
See FIG.
103
for an example where
M_{\mathrm{NS}}^{\max}/M_{\odot}=2
is adopted for the illustration (using Eq. (
4.31
) to solve for
{\mathrm{X}}
and
s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}
).
According to Eq. (
4.17
), we find both the non-derivative and derivative parts of
s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}
are increasing functions of
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}
, and take their maximum values at
{\mathrm{X}}\approx 0.374
.
The solid diamonds in the left panel of FIG.
103
characterize the boundary
{\mathrm{X}}\lesssim 0.374
, i.e., the curves above
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}\gtrsim 1.42\,\mathrm{GeV}/\mathrm{fm}^{3}
(for
M_{\mathrm{NS}}^{\max}/M_{\odot}=2
) violate the causality condition
s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}\leq 1
, see Subsection
6.8
and FIG.
117
.
For other values of
M_{\mathrm{NS}}^{\max}
, the shapes of the curves in FIG.
103
are similar.

(Color Online). Left panel: decomposition of
s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}
into its non-derivative part (
\mathrm{X}
) and derivative part (
s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}-{\mathrm{X}}

.

(left of the lavender band).

\Theta_{\mathrm{c}}
on the polytropic index

for the maximum-mass configuration

, where in the lightblue region two criteria
\Theta_{\mathrm{c}}\lesssim 0.2
and
\gamma_{\mathrm{c}}\lesssim 1.75
hold simultaneously.
