Annala et al. (2023)
on
\gamma
and
\Theta
are shown by the green solid lines associated with arrows. The inset amplifies the lightblue region. Figures taken from Ref.
Cai et al. (2023)
.

In TAB.
6
, the column with
{\mathrm{X}}\approx 0.18
is also shown as the measure
\Theta_{\mathrm{c}}
takes its minimum at this
{\mathrm{X}}
.
Interestingly, if one requires
\Theta_{\mathrm{c}}\approx 0.2
, then two solutions
{\mathrm{X}}\approx 0.21
and
{\mathrm{X}}\approx 0.16
should be obtained, the corresponding
\gamma_{\mathrm{c}}
is found to be about 1.75 and 1.62, respectively.
We have approximately

\Theta_{\mathrm{c}}\approx 1/3-\eta+2\eta^{2}/3+13\eta^{3}/6+41\eta^{4}/8+\cdots,

(6.10)

where
0.5\gtrsim\eta\equiv 1-4/3\gamma_{\mathrm{c}}\geq 0
acts as a small-expansion quantity (since
2.67\gtrsim\gamma_{\mathrm{c}}\geq 4/3
).
We notice that
{\mathrm{X}}\approx 0.189

Cai et al. (2023)
and correspondingly
\Theta_{\mathrm{c}}\approx 0.195
and
\gamma_{\mathrm{c}}\approx 1.70
are obtained if the radius about
13.7\,\mathrm{km}
was adopted for PSR J0740+6620
Miller et al. (2021)
.
These numerical values indicate that empirically the near-conformality may be possible in the core of very massive NSs.
However, the likelihood for realizing conformality near the centers is small if the criteria
\Theta_{\mathrm{c}}\lesssim 0.2
and
\gamma_{\mathrm{c}}\lesssim 1.75

Annala et al. (2023)
were adopted simultaneously, as shown in the right panel of FIG.
103
, where the constraints
\gamma\lesssim 1.79
and
\Theta\lesssim 0.24
from Ref.
Annala et al. (2023)
are shown (green solid lines associated with arrows) for comparison.
In a very recent study, Ref.
Marquez et al. (2024)
found that
\Theta_{\mathrm{c}}
is greater than 0.2 for a hadronic matter using an extended NJL model.
Furthermore, the right panel of FIG.
103
supports the conclusion that the criterion
\Theta_{\mathrm{c}}\lesssim 0.2
is more restrictive than
\gamma_{\mathrm{c}}\lesssim 1.75

Annala et al. (2023)
.

The inference on
\Theta_{\mathrm{c}}
is largely affected by the pQCD effects. For example, in Ref.
Musolino et al. (2024)
the constraint for
\Theta_{\mathrm{c}}
changes from 0.138 to 0.161 (with an enhancement about 17%) if the pQCD effects are removed.
Moreover, considering a canonical NS with
R\approx 12_{-1}^{+1}\,\mathrm{km}
, we have in Subsection
5.6
that
s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}\approx 0.47\pm 0.09
,
\mathrm{X}\approx 0.15\pm 0.02
,
\Psi\approx 2.85\pm 0.29
, therefore

\boxed{\Theta_{\mathrm{c}}\approx 0.38\pm 0.05,~~\mbox{for a canonical NS with }R\approx 12_{-1}^{+1}\,\mathrm{km},}

(6.11)

where
(3^{-1}-\mathrm{X})^{2}
contributes less than
(s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}-\mathrm{X})^{2}
to
\Theta_{\mathrm{c}}
.
We can also obtain the polytropic index about
\gamma_{\mathrm{c}}\approx 3.26\pm 0.23
.
The prediction on
\Theta_{\mathrm{c}}
for a canonical NS is consistent with those from Ref.
Annala et al. (2023)
and Ref.
Takátsy et al. (2023)
, so the dense matter in cores of canonical NSs could not be conformal according to the empirical criterion
\Theta_{\mathrm{c}}\lesssim 0.2

Annala et al. (2023)
.
If we consider the canonical pulsar PSR J0437-4715
Choudhury et al. (2024)
with a small radius
R\approx 11.36_{-0.63}^{+0.95}\,\mathrm{km}
, the
\Theta_{\mathrm{c}}\approx 0.21\pm 0.01
could be obtained.
For NSs at the causality limit (without considering their masses and radii individually), i.e.,
s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}\to 1
and
\mathrm{X}\to 0.374
, TAB.
6
gives
\Theta_{\mathrm{c}}\approx 0.63
(much larger than the empirical criterion). Such NSs are the densest since they could not be compressed even further. In this sense, the densest matter in NSs could hardly be conformal, this may be fundamentally different from the compressed QCD matter at very high baryon densities.

Using the notations of Subsection
5.8
, we investigate how the polytropic index and the empirical conformality measure
\Theta
of Eq. (
6.9
) behave at finite
\widehat{r}

Cai et al. (2023)
, extending the above discussions.
In particular, we can work out using the
\widehat{r}
-dependence of the relevant quantities that:

\displaystyle\gamma/\gamma_{\mathrm{c}}\approx

\displaystyle 1+\frac{b_{2}}{s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}}\left(1+\frac{2D}{s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}}-\frac{s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}}{{\mathrm{X}}}\right)\widehat{r}^{2}\approx 1-\frac{3D}{16{\mathrm{X}}^{2}}\widehat{r}^{2},

(6.12)

\displaystyle\Theta/\Theta_{\mathrm{c}}\approx

\displaystyle 1+\frac{b_{2}}{s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}}\frac{3t_{\mathrm{c}}(1+3s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}-6{\mathrm{X}}-6D)}{1+9s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}-6{\mathrm{X}}(1+3s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2})+18{\mathrm{X}}^{2}}\widehat{r}^{2}\approx 1+\frac{1-6D}{8}\widehat{r}^{2},

(6.13)

where the coefficients in front of
\widehat{r}^{2}
in
\gamma/\gamma_{\mathrm{c}}
and
\Theta/\Theta_{\mathrm{c}}
are both positive, the second approximation for each quantity keeps only the leading-order term in
{\mathrm{X}}
.
Numerically, we then have
\Theta/\Theta_{\mathrm{c}}\approx 1+3.8\widehat{r}^{2}
and
\gamma/\gamma_{\mathrm{c}}\approx 1+2.3\widehat{r}^{2}
for
{\mathrm{X}}\approx 0.24
using
D\approx-0.45
(panel (b) of FIG.
63
).
For small
{\mathrm{X}}\to 0
, Eq. (
6.13
) gives approximately
\Theta/\Theta_{\mathrm{c}}\approx 1+\widehat{r}^{2}/8
.
Similarly, the
\mu
-dependence of
\gamma
and
\Theta
could be obtained,

\displaystyle\gamma/\gamma_{\mathrm{c}}\approx

\displaystyle 1+\left(\frac{t_{\mathrm{c}}}{{\mathrm{X}}}+\frac{2D}{s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}}\right)\mu\approx 1+\frac{3D}{2{\mathrm{X}}}\mu,

(6.14)

\displaystyle\Theta/\Theta_{\mathrm{c}}\approx

\displaystyle 1+\frac{3t_{\mathrm{c}}+9s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}(t_{\mathrm{c}}+2D)-18{\mathrm{X}}(t_{\mathrm{c}}+D)}{1+9s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}-6{\mathrm{X}}(1+3s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2})+18{\mathrm{X}}^{2}}\mu\approx 1+(6D-1){\mathrm{X}}\mu.

(6.15)

The coefficients in front of
\mu
in Eqs. (
6.14
) and (
6.15
) are both negative, e.g., we have
\gamma/\gamma_{\mathrm{c}}\approx 1-2.9\mu
and
\Theta/\Theta_{\mathrm{c}}\approx 1-4.8\mu
for
{\mathrm{X}}\approx 0.24
.
Therefore, Eq. (
6.12
)-Eq. (
6.15
) together generalize the conclusion of the right panel of FIG.
103
: If the dense matter at centers of massive NSs was not conformal, it is likely its nearby surroundings are also not conformal
Cai et al. (2023)
.

Combining the leading-order terms of Eq. (
6.14
) and Eq. (
6.15
) or those of Eq. (
6.12
) and Eq. (
6.13
) leads us to the correlation between the
\gamma
and
\Theta
parameters as,

\boxed{\frac{\Delta\Theta}{\Delta\gamma}\approx\left(4-\frac{2D}{3}\right){\mathrm{X}}^{2}\approx\left(4-\frac{2D}{3}\right)\mathrm{X}^{2},}

(6.16)

which holds near NS centers. It tells us that the two positive quantities
\Delta\Theta\equiv\Theta/\Theta_{\mathrm{c}}-1
and
\Delta\gamma\equiv\gamma/\gamma_{\mathrm{c}}-1
are positively correlated since (
4-2D/3>0
according to panel (b) of FIG.
63
).

6.3 Unraveling the central trace anomaly from compactness scaling
nearly independent of EOS models

The strong linear correlation between
\xi
and
\Pi_{\mathrm{c}}
indicated by Eq. (
4.8
) and numerically verified in Eq. (
4.66
) using the meta-model EOSs enables us to read off the
\mathrm{X}
straightforwardly from the
\xi
obtained either using NS mass-radius observation or the measurement on red-shift
z
(see Eq. (
6.33
) for the formula). Therefore, the central trace anomaly
\Delta_{\mathrm{c}}=1/3-\mathrm{X}
can be obtained straightforwardly from the observational data. Since the ratio
\phi=P/\varepsilon
increases towards NS centers and reaches its maximum value
\mathrm{X}
there, the trace anomaly takes its minimum
\Delta_{\mathrm{c}}
there.
A lower bound for trace anomaly of Eq. (
6.4
) from the GR limit
\mathrm{X}\lesssim 0.374

Cai et al. (2023)
is basically equivalently to the limit (
4.69
) for the compactness. All the currently known NSs are safely consistent with inequality (
4.69
);
any future observations violating (
4.69
) may imply a deeper trace anomaly than the
\Delta_{\mathrm{GR}}
realized in NSs.

is quite accurate so the errorbar on its central trace anomaly is relatively small;
the NS in X-ray burster GS 1826-24
Zhou et al. (2023)
, a canonical NS with radius
R\approx 12_{-1}^{+1}\,\mathrm{km}

Brandes et al. (2023)
;
Richter and Li (2023)
; and three central trace anomalies for PSR J2215+5135
Sullivan and Romani (2024)
.
See FIG.
5
for the related observational data.
Though there is currently no observational constraint on the radius of PSR J2215+5135, we can use the
\xi_{\mathrm{GR}}
or
\Delta_{\mathrm{GR}}
to limit its radius to
R\gtrsim 12\,\mathrm{km}
. Here three typical radii, namely 12 km, 13 km and 14 km are adopted for an illustration.
The error bar of
\Delta_{\mathrm{c}}
is mainly due to the uncertainty of
\xi
itself as the correlation
\xi
-
\Pi_{\mathrm{c}}
is strong and model independent.
For instance, the error bar of
\Delta_{\mathrm{c}}
for a canonical NS with
R\approx 12_{-1}^{+1}\,\mathrm{km}
is apparently smaller than that for the two NSs in GW 170817
Abbott et al. (2018)
as the latter have larger mass uncertainties although they share similar radii (compare the red solid pentagon and dark-violet solid circles).
Besides the
\xi
-
\Pi_{\mathrm{c}}
scaling, the mass-
\Gamma_{\mathrm{c}}
scaling of Eq. (
4.7
) and the specific form of Eq. (
4.67
) further gives individually the values of
P_{\mathrm{c}}
and
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}
if both the
M_{\mathrm{NS}}
and
R
(or one of them together with the compactness) are observationally known.
In order to obtain
\mathrm{X}
and
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}
for the NS in GS 1826-24 (only its compactness is known), we adopt three typical radii (12 km, 13 km and 14 km), the same as that for PSR J2215+5135, see the supplementary material of Ref.
Cai and Li (2024)
.
These 17 NS instances are also displayed in panel (b) of FIG.
104
. They are enclosed roughly by the dotted black ellipse (treated as the effective region of NS data).
The values for
\mathrm{X}
and
\mathrm{Y}
for these NS instances are given in TAB.
7
.

For comparisons, also shown in the right panel of FIG.
104
are the trace anomalies obtained/constrained by a few contemporary state-of-the-art NS EOS modelings
using different input data and/or inference algorithms. These include the NS EOS inference
Gorda et al. (2023)
incorporating the pQCD impact (dashed orange band), a Bayesian inference of NS EOS
Al-Mamun et al. (2021)
combining the electromagnetic and gravitational-wave signals (plum solid band), the interpolation
Fujimoto et al. (2022)
between low-density chiral effective field theories
Essick et al. (2021)
(CEFT) and high-density pQCD constraints
Bjorken (1983)
(between the dash-dotted light-blue lines), a minimal parametrization
Fujimoto et al. (2022)
between
\Delta
and
\varepsilon/\varepsilon_{0}
(grey dotted line) accounting for NS data and the NS EOS
Fujimoto et al. (2022)
inferred via machine learning algorithms (lavender band).
Our results of (
\mathrm{Y}\equiv\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}/\varepsilon_{0}
,
\Delta_{\mathrm{c}}=1/3-\mathrm{X})
for the 17 NS instances put stringent constraints on the theoretical NS EOSs.
As shown in panel (b) of FIG.
104
, apparently the
\Delta
’s from some NS EOS modelings have sizable tensions with the limits set by the observational data based on our scaling analyses, especially for massive NSs. It implies that some ingredients in NS EOS inferences may need to be revised. For example, the NS EOS model incorporating pQCD effect
Gorda et al. (2023)
can well explain the
(\mathrm{Y},\Delta_{\mathrm{c}})
’s of PSR J0030+0451, GW 190425 and GS 1826-24 (with the radii
R\approx 12\,\mathrm{km}
and 13 km, the rightest two green diamonds). However, it could hardly account for the results for PSR J0740+6620 for all three radii
Riley et al. (2021)
;
Miller et al. (2021)
;
Salmi et al. (2022)
, the two NSs in GW 170817
Abbott et al. (2018)
as well as those for PSR J2215+5135
Sullivan and Romani (2024)
(for all three
R
’s).
In particular, it has apparent deviation from the data of the newly announced PSR J0437-4715
Choudhury et al. (2024)
. Because the latter has a relatively smaller radius, its
\mathrm{Y}\approx 3.76
is larger. Similarly, the NS EOS modeling with both the electromagnetic and gravitational-wave signals included
Al-Mamun et al. (2021)
can effectively explain the data of GW 170817, the canonical NS and PSR J0437-4715. However, it has certain tensions with our results based on observations for PSR J0030+451 and PSR J0740+6620, GW 190425 and the redback spider pulsar PSR J2215+5135. On the other hand, the interpolation
Fujimoto et al. (2022)
between low-density CEFT
Essick et al. (2021)
and high-density pQCD
Bjorken (1983)
predicts a quite large
\Delta
compared with what we extracted from PSR J0740+6620, the NS in GS 1826-24 and PSR J2215+5135 observations; however, it is consistent with
\Delta_{\mathrm{c}}
of PSR J0437-4715.
More interestingly, although GW 190425 executes weaker limits on NS radii
Abbott et al. (2020)
, it effectively puts useful constraints on the trace anomaly
\Delta
.

Compared with the predictions of existing NS EOS modelings, our analysis shows that the actual
\Delta
is deeper.
Physically, this is because the
\mathrm{Y}=\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}/\varepsilon_{0}
allowed is actually smaller for heavier NSs, fundamentally due to the
M_{\mathrm{NS}}
-
\Gamma_{\mathrm{c}}
correlation (which we discussed in more details in Subsection
4.5
around FIG.
35
), see the three chocolate triangles for PSR 2215+5135
Sullivan and Romani (2024)
, here
\mathrm{Y}\lesssim 4.37
(from the mass scaling) and
R\gtrsim 12\,\mathrm{km}
(via
\Delta\gtrsim\Delta_{\mathrm{GR}}
of (
6.4
) or (
4.69
)) are obtained.
Similarly, if we use the even heavier black widow pulsar PSR J0952-0607
Romani et al. (2022)
with
2.35M_{\odot}
as the input data, its radius
R
should be
\gtrsim 13.1\,\mathrm{km}
via
\Delta\gtrsim\Delta_{\mathrm{GR}}
; and therefore
\mathrm{Y}\lesssim 3.68
.
These examples clearly illustrate that including data from massive NSs in the analysis necessarily pulls down the
\Delta
-curve, even if the radius constraints are weak.
Moreover, assuming different radii for GS 1826-24 horizontally shifts the green diamond. Nevertheless, the overall shape of
\Delta(\varepsilon/\varepsilon_{0})
remains almost unchanged due to the strong constraints set by PSR J0030+0451 and PSR J0740+6620.
The extracted
\mathrm{X}
and therefore
\Delta_{\mathrm{c}}
from either mass-radius observations or the red-shift measurement are very useful in analyzing the SSS in NSs as we shall demonstrate in Subsection
6.4
.

FIG. 105:
(Color Online). Sketch of the patterns for
\Delta=1/3-P/\varepsilon
in NSs. The
\Delta
is well constrained around the fiducial density
\varepsilon_{0}\approx 150\,\mathrm{MeV}/\mathrm{fm}^{3}
by low-energy nuclear theories and is predicted to vanish due to conformality of the matter at
\varepsilon\gtrsim 50\varepsilon_{0}
(or equivalently
\rho\gtrsim 40\rho_{0}
) using pQCD theories.
Massive and compact NSs provide a unique opportunity to probe the negativeness of
\Delta
in certain energy density regions, where the GR bound on
\Delta
with
\varepsilon
being around (
4\mbox{$\sim$}8)\varepsilon_{0}
is expected to be more relevant than the pQCD bound (
\varepsilon\gtrsim 50\varepsilon_{0}
) in these NSs. Figure taken from Ref.
Cai et al. (2023)
.

Another interesting feature of the right panel of FIG.
104
is that
\Delta
is probably positive considering all NS data analyzed. This feature may have some important consequences. Sketched in FIG.
105

Cai et al. (2023)
are two possible evolution curves of the trace anomaly
\Delta
. Their behaviors at intermediate-high energy densities regions are mostly based on educated guess with the wish of reaching eventually the pQCD prediction of
\Delta=0
at extremely large energy densities
\varepsilon\gtrsim 50\varepsilon_{0}\approx 7.5\,\mathrm{GeV}/\mathrm{fm}^{3}

Fujimoto et al. (2022)
;
Kurkela et al. (2010)
or equivalently
\rho\gtrsim 40\rho_{0}
.
Unfortunately, the latter is far larger than the energy or baryon density reachable in NSs based on our current knowledge. Thus, the pQCD prediction is possibly relevant but not fundamental for explaining the observed
\phi=P/\varepsilon\gtrsim 1/3
in massive NSs (either using microscopic or phenomenological models).
On the other hand, we have demonstrated that a GR bound on
{\mathrm{X}}=P_{\mathrm{c}}/\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}
and
P/\varepsilon
(near NS centers) naturally emerges, leading to the lower limit
\Delta_{\mathrm{GR}}\approx-0.04
, when dissecting perturbatively the TOV equations without using any specific EOS model
Cai et al. (2023)
. Thus, it is logical to say that the GR bound on
\Delta
with the
\varepsilon
being roughly around (4
\sim
8
)\varepsilon_{0}
is likely more relevant/fundamental than the pQCD prediction at
\varepsilon\gtrsim 50\varepsilon_{0}
for nuclear EOS in NSs, although the latter may influence the extraction of
\Delta
in some models
Musolino et al. (2024)
. It is also necessary to point out that understanding whether these two specific bounds on
\Delta
(
\Delta\gtrsim\Delta_{\mathrm{GR}}\approx-0.04
or
\Delta_{\mathrm{pQCD}}=0
being very close to each other) are inner-related may deepen our understanding on the connection between GR and the microscopic theories of elementary particles.
Our above discussions and findings are probably not out of bounds as ultimately properties of compact objects are determined by the Hamilton’s principle using the total action of the whole system including gravity, matter (including nuclear matter, dark matter and energy) and their couplings
Committee on the Physics of the Universe (2003)
. Some of these issues were discussed in Subsection
5.11
.

As a negative
\Delta
is unlikely to be observed in ordinary NSs (e.g., NSs with masses
\sim 1.7M_{\odot}
or canonical NSs) as indicated by
FIG.
104
, the real evolution of
\Delta
is probably more like the green curve in FIG.
105
at intermediate energy densities. An (unconventional) exception may come from light but very compact NSs, e.g., a
1.7M_{\odot}
NS with a radius about 9.3 km is expected to have its
\Delta_{\mathrm{c}}\approx-0.02
, since
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}\approx 1.86\,\mathrm{GeV}/\mathrm{fm}^{3}
together with
P_{\mathrm{c}}\approx 654\,\mathrm{MeV}/\mathrm{fm}^{3}
should be obtained via the mass and radius scalings of (
4.31
) and (
4.30
) and so
\mathrm{X}\approx 0.351
.
On the other hand, massive and compact NSs (masses
\gtrsim 2M_{\odot}
) have the most relevance to observe a negative
\Delta
(as indicated by the magenta curve in FIG.
105
), but how the negative
\Delta
evolves to the pQCD bound is up for imagination about properties of extraordinarily superdense matter
Cai et al. (2023)
.
Interestingly, both the green and magenta curves for the imagined
\Delta
evolutions are closely connected with the density-dependence of the SSS, see Subsection
5.3
for a detailed discussion.

6.4 Can the currently available NS observational data invariably generate a peaked SSS density profile?

In Subsection
6.3
and FIG.
104
we established the strong correlation between NS compactness
\xi
and the factor
\Pi_{\mathrm{c}}=\mathrm{X}/(1+3\mathrm{X}^{2}+4\mathrm{X})
; therefore either mass-radius observation or red-shift measurement can directly probe the
\mathrm{X}
via this
\xi
-
\mathrm{X}
scaling without using any nuclear EOS model.
Consequently, the (central) trace anomaly could be evaluated largely model-independently
Cai and Li (2024)
. Using the formula (
5.15
), the SSS profile and its decomposition can then be studied mostly based on the astrophysical observations alone.

FIG. 106:
(Color Online). The trace anomaly
\Delta
,
s^{2}
and its decomposition via Eq. (
5.15
) using two effective parametrizations for
\Delta
in panel (a) and (b), respectively.
The 17 NS instances of panel (b) of FIG.
104
are plotted with the tan band for its 68% CI regression in each panel, here
\overline{\varepsilon}=\varepsilon/\varepsilon_{0}
.
The grey dotted line represents the minimal parametrization of Ref.
Fujimoto et al. (2022)
.
Figures taken from Ref.
Cai and Li (2024)
.

In panel (a) of FIG.
106
, we show a parametrization of trace anomaly
\Delta
:

\Delta\approx\frac{1}{3}\left(1-ft\overline{\varepsilon}^{a}\right)\exp\left(-t\overline{\varepsilon}^{a}\right),

(6.17)

as a function of

\overline{\varepsilon}=\varepsilon/\varepsilon_{0}

to effectively account for the 17 NS instances (including the data of the newly announced PSR J0437-4715
Choudhury et al. (2024)
by the navy diamond in the right panel of FIG.
104
), valid for
2.5\lesssim\overline{\varepsilon}\lesssim 4.5
(data available). Here
a\approx 2.24
and
t\approx 0.03
are two parameters, and
f\equiv\mathrm{L}^{-1}_{W}(-3^{-1}/\mathrm{e}\Delta_{\mathrm{GR}})\approx 0.9539
with
\mathrm{L}_{W}(x)
being the Lambert-
W
function defined as the solution of the equation
w\exp(w)=x
.
This parametrization has the following properties: (i)
\Delta\to 0
for
\overline{\varepsilon}\to\infty

Bjorken (1983)
, and (ii)
\Delta\to 1/3
as
\overline{\varepsilon}\to 0
; it also generates a minimum
\Delta_{\min}=\Delta_{\mathrm{GR}}\approx-0.041
at
\overline{\varepsilon}_{\mathrm{GR}}=[(f+1)/ft]^{1/a}\approx 6.5
by construction (through the factor
f
).
Numerically, we find
\Delta
in panel (a) drops most quickly at roughly about
\overline{\varepsilon}\approx 3.5
from being
\gtrsim 0.1
to
\lesssim 0.1
.
This feature actually strongly connects with the possible peaked behavior of the squared sound speed
Fujimoto et al. (2022)
.
The expected peak emerges at
\overline{\varepsilon}_{\mathrm{pk}}\approx 4.3
or
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{pk}}\approx 645\,\mathrm{MeV}/\mathrm{fm}^{3}
with
s^{2}\approx 0.62
; this is because
\Delta\to\Delta_{\mathrm{GR}}
somewhere and thus a peak emerges at
\overline{\varepsilon}\approx 3.7
in the derivative term “
-\overline{\varepsilon}\mathrm{d}\Delta/\mathrm{d}\overline{\varepsilon}
”
Fujimoto et al. (2022)
.
So the peak position in
s^{2}
is larger than that in
s_{\mathrm{deriv}}^{2}
, see the analytical demonstration given in Subsection
5.3
and Eq. (
5.18
).
More interestingly, the GR bound
\Delta_{\min}=\Delta_{\mathrm{GR}}<0
induces a valley in
s^{2}
at
\overline{\varepsilon}_{\mathrm{vl}}\approx 8.4
with
s^{2}\approx 0.25
, since
\Delta\to 0>\Delta_{\mathrm{GR}}
as
\varepsilon/\varepsilon_{0}\to\infty
due to a valley appearing at about
\overline{\varepsilon}\approx 8.2
in the derivative part; however this density may exceed the one allowed in realistic NSs.
In this case, the pQCD limit for
\Delta
is the origin for the emergence of a valley in
s^{2}
instead of a peak.
We summarize the fitting properties of SSS at the peak and valley positions as well as its decomposition terms in TAB.
8
, under the parametrization (
6.17
).

6.17
); here
s^{2}_{\mathrm{pk}}\equiv s^{2}(\overline{\varepsilon}_{\mathrm{pk}})
,
s^{2}_{\mathrm{vl}}\equiv s^{2}(\overline{\varepsilon}_{\mathrm{vl}})
, etc.,
\overline{\varepsilon}=\varepsilon/\varepsilon_{0}
with
\overline{\varepsilon}_{0}\approx 150\,\mathrm{MeV}/\mathrm{fm}^{3}
.

FIG. 107:
(Color Online). The SSS obtained in a three-flavor nonlocal NJL model of quark matter with the scalar attractive, vector repulsive and diquark pairing interaction channels. Figure taken from Ref.
Ivanytskyi (2024)
.

What we learned above from parametrizing
\Delta
with Eq. (
6.17
) is consistent with a recent study on SSS by using a three-flavor nonlocal NJL model of quark matter with the scalar attractive, vector repulsive and diquark pairing interaction channels
Ivanytskyi (2024)
, see FIG.
107
.
It also shows that the valley position
\overline{\varepsilon}_{\mathrm{vl}}
in
s^{2}
is slightly larger than that (
\overline{\varepsilon}_{\mathrm{vl,deriv}}
) in
s^{2}_{\mathrm{deriv}}
. This is consistent with the analytical analysis in Subsection
5.3
, see Eq. (
5.21
).
In addition, whether including the newly announced PSR J04317-4715
Choudhury et al. (2024)
with both mass and radius observations available under the parametrization (
6.17
)
may essentially impact qualitatively both the position and magnitude of the peak (valley), as shown in TAB.
9
.

Influence of the mass and radius observational data of PSR J0437-4715
Choudhury et al. (2024)
on the

and related quantities using the parametrization (
6.17
).

FIG. 108:
(Color Online). Decomposition of
s^{2}
into the derivative and non-derivative parts as in Eq. (
5.15
). The peak in
s^{2}
is attributed to the peak appearing in the derivative part in the work of Ref.
Fujimoto et al. (2022)
.
Figure taken from Ref.
Fujimoto et al. (2022)
.

FIG. 109:
(Color Online). Within the effective data region about
2.5\lesssim\overline{\varepsilon}\lesssim 4.5
, parametrization (
6.17
) and parametrization (
6.18
) for
\Delta
behaves very similarly; here p1 and p2 denote parametrizations
\Delta\approx 3^{-1}(1-ft\overline{\varepsilon}^{a})\exp(-t\overline{\varepsilon}^{a})

and
3^{-1}\exp(-k_{1}\overline{\varepsilon}^{2})-k_{2}\overline{\varepsilon}
, respectively.
Figure taken from Ref.
Cai and Li (2024)
.

On the other hand, if we adopt another effective parametrization for
\Delta
considering only the constraint
\lim_{\overline{\varepsilon}\to 0}\Delta=3^{-1}
(without its large
\overline{\varepsilon}
-limit) but can describe the same NS data approximately equally well as the previous one as shown in panel (b) of FIG.
106
, e.g.,

\Delta\approx\frac{1}{3}\exp\left(-k_{1}\overline{\varepsilon}^{2}\right)-k_{2}\overline{\varepsilon},

(6.18)

with two parameters
k_{1}\approx 0.037
and
k_{2}\approx 0.032
, the resulting
s^{2}
just monotonically increases with
\overline{\varepsilon}
in spite of the fact that a broad peak exists in the derivative term “
-\overline{\varepsilon}\mathrm{d}\Delta/\mathrm{d}\overline{\varepsilon}
”.
Specifically, we find the peak in the derivative part
-\overline{\varepsilon}\mathrm{d}\Delta/\mathrm{d}\overline{\varepsilon}
occurs at
\overline{\varepsilon}\approx 6.2
with the full
s^{2}\approx 0.88
.
This is different from the analysis given in Ref.
Fujimoto et al. (2022)
which indicates that a peak in the derivative part may induce a peaked behavior of the full
s^{2}
, as shown in FIG.
108
; see also Ref.
Carlomagno et al. (2024)
for the emergence of the peaked structure in the derivative part of
s^{2}
using a non-local NJL model.
Within the effective data region in energy density, parametrizations (
6.17
) and (
6.18
) behave very similarly and they are approximately equally accurate, see FIG.
109
.
It demonstrates that a peaked shape of
s^{2}
is an implication considering some well-founded theoretical limits but not practically inevitable, and it is not a direct consequence of the currently available NS data themselves.

As more observational data of mass, radius or red-shift becomes available (e.g., see the sketch in FIG.
50
), our approach based on NS compactness and mass scalings can better constrain the trace anomaly
\Delta
in a wide range of energy densities, locate precisely the possible peak positions of
s^{2}
relying on NS data alone without using other physical inputs
Zhou (2024)
, and restrict the NS EOS to a much narrower band.

6.5 Brief summary of the existing causality boundaries for NS mass-radius relations

Besides the Schwarzschild boundary, a spherical star composed of uniform incompressible fluids is a special and simplest case
Buchdahl (1959)
.
For such model,
\varepsilon(r)=\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}=\mathrm{const.}
, the TOV equations could be solved analytically to give

\mathrm{X}=\frac{P_{\mathrm{c}}}{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}}=\frac{1-\sqrt{1-2\xi}}{3\sqrt{1-2\xi}-1}\approx\frac{\xi}{2}\left(1+2\xi+\frac{17}{4}\xi^{2}+\frac{37}{4}\xi^{3}+\cdots\right),~~\xi=M_{\mathrm{NS}}/R.

(6.19)

To avoid the singularity in the denominator of (
6.19
), one needs
R>9M_{\mathrm{NS}}/4
, giving the Buchdahl causality boundary,

\boxed{\mbox{Buchdahl causality boundary:}~~R_{\mathrm{Buch}}/\mathrm{km}\gtrsim 3.32M_{\mathrm{NS}}/M_{\odot}.}

(6.20)

In addition, if we put the energy dominant condition (or equivalently the causality condition) on (
6.19
), namely
\mathrm{X}\leq 1
, then (
6.19
) gives
R>8M_{\mathrm{NS}}/3
.
Therefore the Buchdahl limit with energy dominant condition (EDC) is

\boxed{\mbox{Buchdahl causality boundary with EDC:}~~R_{\mathrm{Buch}}^{\mathrm{EDC}}/\mathrm{km}\gtrsim 3.94M_{\mathrm{NS}}/M_{\odot}.}

(6.21)

Denoting the upper limit for
\mathrm{X}
generally as
\mathrm{X}_{+}
, i.e.,
\mathrm{X}\leq\mathrm{X}_{+}
, (
6.19
) gives then

Buchdahl causality boundary with
\mathrm{X}_{+}
:

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055ptR_{\mathrm{Buch}}^{(+)}\gtrsim\frac{(1+3\mathrm{X}_{+})^{2}}{2\mathrm{X}_{+}(1+2\mathrm{X}_{+})}M_{\mathrm{NS}}\leftrightarrow\frac{R_{\mathrm{Buch}}^{(+)}}{\mathrm{km}}\gtrsim 1.477\frac{(1+3\mathrm{X}_{+})^{2}}{2\mathrm{X}_{+}(1+2\mathrm{X}_{+})}\left(\frac{M_{\mathrm{NS}}}{M_{\odot}}\right),

(6.22)

here the superscript “+” in the radius reminds us that this is obtained under the condition
\mathrm{X}\leq\mathrm{X}_{+}
.
Using our upper limit
\mathrm{X}_{+}\approx 0.374
(of Eq. (
4.18
)) extracted directly from the TOV equations, we then have

\boxed{\mbox{Buchdahl causality boundary with $\mathrm{X}_{+}\approx 0.374$:~~}R_{\mathrm{Buch}}^{\mathrm{X}_{+}\approx 0.374}/\mathrm{km}\gtrsim 5.01M_{\mathrm{NS}}/M_{\odot}.}

(6.23)

Later, Buchdahl considered an improved EOS
Buchdahl (1967)
by generalizing the constant
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}
assumption to
\varepsilon(P)=12\sqrt{P_{\ast}P}-5P
with
P_{\ast}
a constant. In this case (the Buchdahl fluid), one can work out the following boundary
Buchdahl (1967)
;
Saes et al. (2024)

\boxed{\mathrm{X}=\frac{\xi}{2-5\xi}\approx\frac{\xi}{2}\left(1+\frac{5}{2}\xi+\frac{25}{4}\xi^{2}+\frac{125}{8}\xi^{3}+\cdots\right),~~\mbox{or}~~\frac{R_{\mathrm{BF}}^{(+)}}{\mathrm{km}}\gtrsim 1.477\left(\frac{5}{2}+\frac{1}{2\mathrm{X}_{+}}\right)\left(\frac{M_{\mathrm{NS}}}{M_{\odot}}\right).}

(6.24)

Paralleling to inequalities of (
6.20
), (
6.21
) and (
6.23
), we should similarly obtain

\boxed{R_{\mathrm{BF}}/\mathrm{km}\gtrsim 3.69M_{\mathrm{NS}}/M_{\odot};~~R_{\mathrm{BF}}^{\mathrm{EDC}}/\mathrm{km}\gtrsim 4.43M_{\mathrm{NS}}/M_{\odot};~~R_{\mathrm{BF}}^{\mathrm{X}_{+}\approx 0.374}/\mathrm{km}\gtrsim 5.67M_{\mathrm{NS}}/M_{\odot};}

(6.25)

by taking
\mathrm{X}_{+}\to\infty
(avoiding the singularity in
\mathrm{X}
),
\mathrm{X}_{+}=1
(energy dominant condition) and
\mathrm{X}_{+}\approx 0.374
(upper limit predicted by TOV equations) in (
6.24
), respectively;
here “BF” is abbreviated for “Buchdahl fluid”.

FIG. 110:
(Color Online). Same as FIG.
5
but with a few causality boundaries (solid lightgrey lines) added: Schwarzschild relation (black hole constraint)
R=2M_{\mathrm{NS}}
; the Buchdahl limit
R=9M_{\mathrm{NS}}/4
, the Buchdahl limit with dominant energy condition
R=8M_{\mathrm{NS}}/3
and the condition
\mathrm{X}\lesssim 0.374
as
R\approx 3.44M_{\mathrm{NS}}
; the Buchdahl fluid
R=5M_{\mathrm{NS}}/2
as well as that with the dominant energy condition
R=7M_{\mathrm{NS}}/2
and the condition
\mathrm{X}\lesssim 0.374
as
R\approx 3.83M_{\mathrm{NS}}
; and
R\approx 2.83M_{\mathrm{NS}}
from Ref.
Lindblom (1984)
,
R\approx 3.05M_{\mathrm{NS}}
from Ref.
Lattimer et al. (1990)
and
R\approx 2.94M_{\mathrm{NS}}
from Ref.
Glendenning (1992)
; the
R=4M_{\mathrm{NS}}
from using an “ultra-stiff” EOS
P=\varepsilon
(or
\phi=1
) and
R=14M_{\mathrm{NS}}/3
using an URFG EOS.

Ref.
Lindblom (1984)
found that the red-shift
z
has a maximum value about
0.863
, which inversely gives the causality boundary as
Lindblom (1984)
;
Zhang and Li (2019)

\boxed{\mbox{Lindblom:}~~R/\mathrm{km}\gtrsim 4.18M_{\mathrm{NS}}/M_{\odot}.}

(6.26)

This is perhaps one of the two most frequently used boundaries in the NS literature.
The other one was given in Ref.
Lattimer et al. (1990)
by further improving the NS EOS modeling
Lattimer et al. (1990)
;
Zhang and Li (2019)
using the prescription
s^{2}\to 1
above a fiducial energy density,

\boxed{\mbox{Lattimer et al. (LPMY):}~~R/\mathrm{km}\gtrsim 4.51M_{\mathrm{NS}}/M_{\odot}.}

(6.27)

In addition, Ref.
Glendenning (1992)
constrained the boundary to be

\boxed{\mbox{Glendenning:}~~R/\mathrm{km}\gtrsim 4.34M_{\mathrm{NS}}/M_{\odot},}

(6.28)

by considering first-order phase transitions with more than one conserved charge in NSs.
In Ref.
Link et al. (1999)
, the boundary is established by restricting the fractional moment of inertia of NS crust to
\Delta I/I\gtrsim 1.4\%
,

\boxed{\mbox{Link et al.:}~~R/\mathrm{km}\gtrsim 3.6+3.9M_{\mathrm{NS}}/M_{\odot}.}

(6.29)

Stellar models that are compatible with the lower bound on
\Delta I
must fall below this line.
Furthermore, the causality boundaries predicted by the linear EOS
P=\zeta\varepsilon
with
\zeta=\mathrm{const.}
are
R/\mathrm{km}\gtrsim 5.91M_{\mathrm{NS}}/M_{\odot}
for an “ultra-stiff” EOS with
\phi=1
and

R/\mathrm{km}\gtrsim 6.89M_{\mathrm{NS}}/M_{\odot}
for an URFG with
\phi=1/3
; see Eq. (
2.9
) and (
2.10
).
For comparisons and ease of further discussions, we summarize the above causality boundaries on the M-R plot with several NS observational data in FIG.
110
(see also FIG.
5
).

6.6 Upper bound for the compactness of NSs at TOV configuration

In this subsection, we study the upper limit for NS compactness, which is closely related to the lower (upper) bound on
\Delta
(on
\phi
).
For the maximum-mass configuration on the NS M-R curve, one obtains the upper bound for
\xi_{\max}={M_{\mathrm{NS}}^{\max}}/{R_{\max}}
by neglecting the intercepts
0.106
and
0.64
in Eq. (
4.31
) and Eq. (
4.30
) respectively, as

\xi_{\max}\equiv\frac{M_{\mathrm{NS}}^{\max}}{R_{\max}}=\frac{M_{\mathrm{NS}}^{\max}/M_{\odot}}{R_{\max}/\mathrm{km}}\left(\frac{M_{\odot}}{\mathrm{km}}\right)<\frac{1.73\times 10^{3}\Gamma_{\mathrm{c}}}{1.05\times 10^{3}\nu_{\mathrm{c}}}\left(\frac{M_{\odot}}{\mathrm{km}}\right)\approx\frac{2.44{\mathrm{X}}}{1+3{\mathrm{X}}^{2}+4{\mathrm{X}}}\equiv\xi_{\max}^{\mathrm{(up)}}.

(6.30)

Therefore,
\xi_{\max}\lesssim 0.313_{-0.01}^{+0.01}
by taking
{\mathrm{X}}\approx 0.374
, which is about
30\%
smaller than the Buchdahl limit
4/9\approx 0.444
.
In FIG.
111
, we plot the compactness parameter
\xi_{\max}
of Eq. (
6.30
) as a function of the central SSS
s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}
which depends on the
{\mathrm{X}}
via Eq. (
4.17
), where the compactness for PSR J0740-6620 about
0.217\mbox{$\sim$}0.279
(with the central value about 0.248) is shown by the lightblue hatched band (and the purple shallow circle) directly from its observational mass
\approx 2.08_{-0.07}^{+0.07}M_{\odot}

Fonseca et al. (2021)
and radius
\approx 12.39_{-0.98}^{+1.30}\,\mathrm{km}

Riley et al. (2021)
.
The compactness about
0.163_{-0.026}^{+0.019}
for PSR J0030+0451
Miller et al. (2019)
is shown by the light-green diamond (the SSS is obtained from its mass and radius using Eqs. (
4.31
), (
4.30
) and (
4.17
)).
A similar result for NS EXO 1745-248
Özel and Freire (2016)
is shown by the magenta hexagon.

FIG. 111:
(Color Online). Compactness parameter
\xi_{\max}
for the maximum-mass configuration as a function of central SSS
s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}
. Figure taken from Ref.
Cai et al. (2023)
with a correction made on the compactness of the NS in GS 1826-24.

We notice that neglecting the intercepts
0.106
and
0.64
in Eq. (
4.31
) and Eq. (
4.30
) slightly overestimates the compactness. Actually, by rewriting the form of
\xi_{\max}=M_{\mathrm{NS}}^{\max}/R_{\max}
, we can obtain

\xi_{\max}\lesssim\Pi_{\mathrm{c}}(0.374)\left(\frac{M_{\odot}}{\mathrm{km}}\right)\frac{A_{\mathrm{M}}^{\max}}{A_{\mathrm{R}}^{\max}}\left[1+\frac{\mathrm{km}}{R_{\max}}\left(\frac{B_{\mathrm{M}}^{\max}A_{\mathrm{R}}^{\max}}{A_{\mathrm{M}}\Pi_{\mathrm{c}}(0.374)}-B_{\mathrm{R}}^{\max}\right)\right]\approx 0.313_{-0.01}^{+0.01}\cdot\left(1-\frac{1.14_{-0.3}^{+0.3}\,\mathrm{km}}{R_{\max}}\right),

(6.31)

where
A_{\mathrm{M}}^{\max}
,
B_{\mathrm{M}}^{\max}
,
A_{\mathrm{R}}^{\max}
and
B_{\mathrm{R}}^{\max}
are defined in Eqs. (
4.31
) and (
4.30
), and
1.14_{-0.3}^{+0.3}/R_{\max}\ll 1
could be treated as a correction to Eq. (
6.30
). Considering a typical NS radius
R_{\max}\approx 12.5\pm 1\,\mathrm{km}
(with a reduction about 10% on
\xi_{\max}
), it then leads to

\boxed{\xi_{\max}\equiv\xi_{\mathrm{TOV}}\lesssim 0.283_{-0.014}^{+0.014},~~\mbox{for NSs at the TOV configuration.}}

(6.32)

shown by the grey dash-dotted line in FIG.
111
.
In this sense, the
\xi_{\max}\lesssim 0.313
from Eq. (
6.30
) provides an upper limit for the NS compactness.
An early constraint on the compactness
\xi
about
\xi\lesssim 0.353

Lindblom (1984)
is indicated by the dashed red line (with a row of arrows).
Furthermore, the constraint on NS compactness can also be obtained from the gravitational red-shift
z
defined by

1+z=\left(1-\frac{2M_{\mathrm{NS}}}{R_{\mathrm{GR}}}\right)^{-1}=\left(1-\frac{2\xi}{\sqrt{1+z}}\right)^{-1},~~R_{\mathrm{GR}}=\sqrt{1+z}R,~~\xi=M_{\mathrm{NS}}/R.

(6.33)

Interestingly, a new constraint on
z
was derived very recently from comparing X-ray burst model simulations with observational data for GS 1826-24 using newly measured atomic masses around the rp-process waiting-point nucleus
{}^{64}\mathrm{Ge}

Zhou et al. (2023)
. The resulting NS compactness is about
0.183\mbox{$\sim$}0.259
with the most probable value 0.221 at 95% confidence level (shown by the tan band). Its impact on both the EOS of supra-dense matter and the nature of GW190814’s second component was studied very recently in Ref.
Xie et al. (2024)
.
It was found that the EOS of high-density symmetric nuclear matter has to be softened appreciably while the symmetry energy at supersaturation densities stiffened compared to our prior knowledge.

In a recent study
Annala et al. (2022)
, the universal relation between non-rotating and maximally rigidly rotating (Kepler) NS compactnesses was explored under the conditions
M_{\mathrm{NS}}^{\max}\gtrsim 2M_{\odot}
and the dimensionless tidal deformability of GW170817 being smaller than 720, see FIG.
112
.
They found that the compactness is upper limited as
\lesssim 0.33

Annala et al. (2022)
.
Compared to all of the above constrains available on NS compactness, ours is probably the most stringent one so far.

FIG. 112:
(Color Online). Universal relation between non-rotating and maximally rigidly rotating (Kepler) NS compactnesses under the restriction conditions
M_{\mathrm{NS}}^{\max}\gtrsim 2M_{\odot}
and the dimensionless tidal deformability of GW170817 being smaller than 720. Figure taken from Ref.
Annala et al. (2022)
.

We notice by passing that the compactness parameter is closely related to some bulk properties of NSs such as the moment of inertial
I
and/or the NS binding energy
E_{\mathrm{b}}

Lattimer and Prakash (2001)
;
Lattimer and Prakash (2004)
;
Lattimer and Prakash (2007)
;
Xu et al. (2009)
;
Bejger and Haensel (2003)
;
Steiner et al. (2016)
;
Lattimer and Schutz (2005)
;
Breu and Rezzolla (2016)
. Therefore, the constraint on
\xi
may naturally lead to some constraints on these quantities. For example, via the empirical expression for the moment of inertial as
I/M_{\mathrm{NS}}R^{2}\approx 0.237(1+2.84\xi^{2}+18.9\xi^{4})
given by Ref.
Lattimer and Schutz (2005)
, one may obtain that
I/M_{\mathrm{NS}}R^{2}\lesssim 0.49
using
\xi\lesssim 0.313
or
I/M_{\mathrm{NS}}R^{2}\lesssim 0.46
using
\xi\lesssim 0.283
.
Similarly, Ref.
Lattimer and Prakash (2001)
approximated the
E_{\mathrm{b}}
as
E_{\mathrm{b}}/M_{\mathrm{NS}}\approx 0.6\xi/(1-\xi/2)
, and therefore
E_{\mathrm{b}}/M_{\mathrm{NS}}\lesssim 0.22
and
E_{\mathrm{b}}/M_{\mathrm{NS}}\lesssim 0.20
using
\xi\lesssim 0.313
and
\xi\lesssim 0.283
, respectively.
In addition, the crustal fraction of the NS moment of inertia is approximately given by
Lattimer and Prakash (2001)
;
Xu et al. (2009)
,

\frac{\Delta I}{I}\approx\frac{28\pi P_{\mathrm{t}}R^{3}}{3M_{\mathrm{NS}}}\frac{1.67\xi-0.6\xi^{2}}{\xi}\left[1+\frac{2P_{\mathrm{t}}(1+5\xi-14\xi^{2})}{\rho_{\mathrm{t}}M_{\mathrm{N}}\xi^{2}}\right]^{-1},

(6.34)

where
\rho_{\mathrm{t}}
and
P_{\mathrm{t}}
are the core-crust transition density and the pressure there, respectively.
We will not discuss these quantities further in this review since they could be treated as direct corollaries of the constraints on
\xi
.

6.7 Can a massive NS have a radius smaller than about 10 km?

The mass scaling of Eq. (
4.31
) and radius scaling of Eq. (
4.30
) together enable us to obtain a causality boundary, since they describe NSs at the TOV configuration.
Since the factor
(1+3{\mathrm{X}}^{2}+4{\mathrm{X}})/{\mathrm{X}}
takes its minimum
\approx 7.8
at
{\mathrm{X}}\approx 0.374
, we have from Eqs. (
4.31
) and (
4.30
) or equivalently Eq. (
4.32
) that
Cai et al. (2023)
,

\boxed{\mbox{intrinsic analysis from scaled TOV equations (this work):}~~{R_{\max}}/{\mathrm{km}}\gtrsim 4.73{M_{\mathrm{NS}}^{\max}}/{M_{\odot}}+1.14.}

(6.35)

Abbott et al. (2017)
;

and GW190425 (cyan solid contour)

are also shown.

as in the inequality (

).
The causality boundary for NS M-R curve was recently localized using an analysis of trace anomaly
Fujimoto et al. (2022)
via the condition
\Delta\geq 0
. It can be written as (blue dashed line in FIG.

)
Fujimoto et al. (2022)

.

Ofengeim et al. (2023)
,

\boxed{\mbox{Ofengeim et al.:}~~R_{\max}/\mathrm{km}\gtrsim 3.75M_{\mathrm{NS}}^{\max}/M_{\odot}+2.27,}

(6.37)

see the right panel of FIG.
114
.

We can see from FIG.
113
that even an “ultra-stiff” EOS
P=\varepsilon
may introduce some tension with the observational data of NSs, by comparing the upper grey dashed line (marked by “
P=\varepsilon
”) and the red solid contour for PSR J0740+6620 (at a 68% confidence level).
This is because the dense matter in NS cores (especially near the centers) could not be described by the linear EOS
P=\phi\varepsilon
; consequently the SSS is nontrivial and nonlinear depending on the
\mathrm{X}
.
Furthermore, compared with the observational data, the URFG EOS (with
P=\varepsilon/3
) leads to quite an inconsistent causality boundary (shown as the lower grey dashed line marked by “
P=\varepsilon/3
”), see Eq. (
2.10
).

FIG. 115:
(Color Online). Constraints on the radius of a
1.6M_{\odot}
NS (left) and that of a NS at the TOV configurations using the threshold mass for prompt black-hole formation from the absence of strong helium features in the kilonova spectrum and the signals from GW 170817;
viable regions are obtained considering multiple constraints. For more details see Ref.
Sneppen et al. (2024)
where this figures is taken from.

Interestingly, our result (
6.35
) puts a more stringent constraint on the M-R curve than the frequently-used boundaries of Ref.
Lindblom (1984)
and Ref.
Lattimer et al. (1990)
.
More importantly, it is very consistent with the observational data which basically excludes several celebrated empirical dense matter EOSs.
In particular, the observational boundary of M-R data for PSR J0740+6620
Fonseca et al. (2021)
;
Riley et al. (2021)
;
Miller et al. (2021)
;
Salmi et al. (2022)
and those from the events GW170817
Abbott et al. (2018)
and GW190425
Abbott et al. (2020)
are highly consistent with our relation (
6.35
).
Therefore, it is important to check the new causality boundary (
6.35
) as more observational data on NS masses/radii or GW events become available (see similar discussions given in Refs.
Fujimoto et al. (2022)
;
Ofengeim et al. (2023)
and the plots in FIG.
114
).
We would like to emphasize that the causality boundary (
6.35
) is a direct and intrinsic consequence of the TOV equations, without relying on any input EOS models. Therefore, it should not necessarily be consistent with microscopic nuclear EOSs;
and its correctness could only be checked by observational NS data similar as the upper bound for
\mathrm{X}
about 0.374. Inversely, incorporating the boundary (
6.35
) may further help us construct more reliable nuclear EOSs.
As an illustration of applying the new causal boundary on the M-R curve, we give a lower limit for the radius of the black widow PSR J0952-0607 as

R_{\max}\gtrsim 12.25\,\mathrm{km},\mbox{ for PSR J0952-0607}.

(6.38)

On the other hand, for a canonical NS, its radius is bounded from below to about 7.77 km (shown as the cyan solid diamond in FIG.
113
).
For massive NSs, we have

\boxed{R_{\max}\gtrsim 10.6\,\mathrm{km},~~\mbox{for}~~M_{\mathrm{NS}}^{\max}/M_{\odot}\gtrsim 2,}

(6.39)

this means massive NSs can hardly have radii smaller than about 10 km.
On the opposite side, if the radii of NSs were known (constrained), e.g.,
R_{\max}\approx 12.39\,\mathrm{km}
(for PSR J0740+6620
Riley et al. (2021)
from NICER), using the causal limit
{\mathrm{X}}\lesssim 0.374
gives an upper limit
M_{\mathrm{NS}}^{\max}\lesssim 2.38M_{\odot}
. Similarly if
R_{\max}\approx 11.41\,\mathrm{km}
or
13.69\,\mathrm{km}
(the lower/upper constraints for the radius of PSR J0740+6620
Riley et al. (2021)
) is adopted, one has
M_{\mathrm{NS}}^{\max}\lesssim 2.17M_{\odot}
or
M_{\mathrm{NS}}^{\max}\lesssim 2.65M_{\odot}
, respectively.
These features were discussed around FIG.
35
. Interestingly, in a very recent study
Sneppen et al. (2024)
, the radius
R_{1.6}
of a
1.6M_{\odot}
NS and
R_{\max}
of a NS at the TOV configuration are constrained by using the astrophysical helium spectrum combined with the GW170817 signals. They found that
R_{1.6}\approx 11.4\,\mathrm{km}
for
M_{\mathrm{TOV}}/M_{\odot}\approx 2.3
(shown in the left panel of FIG.
115
). Moreover,
R_{\max}\gtrsim 10.2\,\mathrm{km}
is found if
M_{\mathrm{TOV}}/M_{\odot}\approx 2
(right panel of FIG.
115
). Their finding is very close to our limit on
R_{\max}
given previously.
For
M_{\mathrm{TOV}}/M_{\odot}\approx 2.3
, the
R_{\max}
is found to fall within a very narrow region around 10 km, indicating that for even larger
M_{\mathrm{TOV}}/M_{\odot}\gtrsim 2.3
no parameter space for
R_{\max}
exists
Sneppen et al. (2024)
.
The decreasing upper limit (based on helium spectrum) on radius (for both
R_{1.6}
and
R_{\max}
) with
M_{\mathrm{TOV}}
from Ref.
Sneppen et al. (2024)
contrasts with predictions of many EOS models, where large radii are typically accompanied by large values of
M_{\mathrm{TOV}}
. This finding may put more stringent constraints on modeling NS EOS.

FIG. 116:
(Color Online). A partial list of the upper limits for the NS compactness, see the text for details.

One feature of FIG.
110
is that the causality boundaries given by the uniform incompressible fluid model (Buchdahl limit)
Buchdahl (1959)
and the Buchdahl fluid
Buchdahl (1967)
combined with the upper limit on
\mathrm{X}
revealed by our analysis about 0.374 (inequality (
4.18
)) are quite consistent with (
6.35
) as well as the inequalities (
6.36
) and (
6.37
).
This can be more clearly demonstrated using the compactness limits. Using the uniform incompressible fluid model with
\mathrm{X}\lesssim 0.374
, the compactness is upper bounded as
\xi\lesssim 0.291
since
R\gtrsim 3.44M_{\mathrm{NS}}
and the Buchdahl fluid model gives
\xi\lesssim 0.261
under the same condition
\mathrm{X}\lesssim 0.374
. Our boundary of (
6.35
) then gives
\xi\lesssim 0.283
as shown in Eq. (
6.32
) by adopting
R\approx 12.5\,\mathrm{km}
.
Under the same radius condition, inequalities (
6.36
) and (
6.37
) restrict the compactness as
\xi\lesssim 0.305
and
\xi\lesssim 0.322
, respectively; while (
6.29
) gives
\xi\lesssim 0.270
.
As a further example, an analytical expression for
\mathrm{X}
is available for the Tolman VII fluid, where the energy density profile takes the form of
\varepsilon(r)=\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}[1-(r/R)^{2}]

Tolman (1939)
.
The result is
Saes et al. (2024)

\displaystyle\mathrm{X}=

\displaystyle\frac{2}{15}\left\{\sqrt{\frac{3}{\xi}}\tan\left[\arctan\sqrt{\frac{1}{3}\frac{\xi}{1-2\xi}}+\frac{1}{2}\ln\left(\frac{1}{6}+\sqrt{\frac{1-2\xi}{3\xi}}\right)-\frac{1}{2}\ln\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{3\xi}}-\frac{5}{6}\right)\right]-\frac{5}{2}\right\}

\displaystyle\approx

\displaystyle\frac{\xi}{2}\left(1+\frac{133}{60}\xi+\frac{599}{112}\xi^{2}+\frac{17915}{1344}\xi^{3}+\cdots\right).

(6.40)

Requiring
\mathrm{X}\lesssim 0.374
gives
\xi\lesssim 0.268
.

We summarize these results in FIG.
116
. It is seen that the constraints on the upper limit for
\xi
are consistent.
Although the uniform incompressible fluid (Buchdahl limit), the Buchdahl fluid and the Tolman VII fluid are simplified models (on the dense matter EOS), the expressions for
\mathrm{X}
in these models in Eqs. (
6.19
), (
6.24
) and (
6.40
) contain important information especially near the NS centers, therefore they could give reasonable prediction on the
\xi
.
Moreover, the leading-order terms in Eqs. (
6.19
), (
6.24
) and (
6.40
) for
\mathrm{X}
are all
\xi/2
while the higher-order terms in
\xi
differ among them.

6.8 Ultimate energy density allowed in generally stable NSs

Actually, the correlation
M_{\mathrm{NS}}^{\max}
-
\Gamma_{\mathrm{c}}
alone is already useful for inferring the ultimate (maximum) energy density
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{ult}}
as well as the ultimate pressure
P_{\mathrm{ult}}
allowed in NSs before they collapse into BHs.
By rewriting Eq. (
4.31
) and considering the casual limit
\mathrm{X}\lesssim 0.374
, we obtain

\boxed{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}\leq\varepsilon_{\mathrm{ult}}\equiv 6.32\left(\frac{M_{\mathrm{NS}}^{\max}}{M_{\odot}}+0.106\right)^{-2}\,\mathrm{GeV}/\mathrm{fm}^{3}.}

(6.41)

Its scaling with mass is
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{ult}}\sim M_{\mathrm{NS}}^{\max,-2}\cdot[1+\cdots]
, see Eq. (
4.80
) for a similar relation holding for generally stable NSs away from the TOV configuration.
Since (
6.41
) is based on the mass scaling for NSs at the TOV configuration, this gives the absolute upper limit for the energy density (the meaning of “ultimate”).
Using the constraint
{\mathrm{X}}\lesssim 0.374
once again gives,

\boxed{P_{\mathrm{c}}\leq P_{\mathrm{ult}}\equiv 2.36\left(\frac{M_{\mathrm{NS}}^{\max}}{M_{\odot}}+0.106\right)^{-2}\,\mathrm{GeV}/\mathrm{fm}^{3}.}

(6.42)

We note that the correlation between
R_{\max}
and
\nu_{\mathrm{c}}
of Eq. (
4.30
) is not used in establishing these limits.

FIG. 117:
(Color Online). The ultimate energy density

(inset) allowed in NS cores as functions of the maximum-mass
M_{\mathrm{NS}}^{\max}

Lattimer and Prakash (2005)
.

Based on Eq. (
6.41
) for
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{ult}}
and Eq. (

P_{\mathrm{ult}}
, we find that the existence of a
2.08M_{\odot}
(

\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}\lesssim 1.32\,\mathrm{GeV}/\mathrm{fm}^{3}
(
1.47\,\mathrm{GeV}/\mathrm{fm}^{3}
) and
P_{\mathrm{c}}\lesssim 494\,\mathrm{MeV}/\mathrm{fm}^{3}
(

It is necessary to point out that the upper limit
P_{\mathrm{c}}\lesssim 548\,\mathrm{MeV}/\mathrm{fm}^{3}
obtained from assuming
M_{\mathrm{NS}}^{\max}\gtrsim 1.97M_{\odot}

Demorest et al. (2010)
is quite consistent with
P_{\max}\lesssim 570\,\mathrm{MeV}/\mathrm{fm}^{3}
shown by the cyan diamond with error bars in the inset of FIG.

obtained from a recent inference in Ref.
Capano et al. (2020)
.

M_{\mathrm{NS}}^{\max}

are shown by the magenta lines (together with the grey band). Also shown are the predictions on
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{ult}}
and
P_{\mathrm{ult}}
(black dashed lines with the lavender band) by Lattimer and Prakash in Ref.

,

\boxed{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{ult}}\approx 7.62\left(\frac{M_{\odot}}{M_{\mathrm{NS}}^{\max}}\right)^{2}\,\mathrm{GeV}/\mathrm{fm}^{3},~~P_{\mathrm{ult}}\approx 5.12\left(\frac{M_{\odot}}{M_{\mathrm{NS}}^{\max}}\right)^{2}\,\mathrm{GeV}/\mathrm{fm}^{3},}

(6.43)

;

.
Considering
M_{\mathrm{NS}}^{\max}/M_{\odot}\approx 2.08
, the above relations (
6.43
) lead to the estimates
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{ult}}\lesssim 1.76\,\mathrm{GeV}/\mathrm{fm}^{3}
and
P_{\mathrm{ult}}\lesssim 1.18\,\mathrm{GeV}/\mathrm{fm}^{3}
, which are about 33% and 139% larger than those from Eq. (
6.41
) and Eq. (
6.42
), respectively. These differences are easy to understand since Refs.
Lattimer and Prakash (2005)
;
Lattimer and Prakash (2010)
used simplified EOS models like the Tolman VII fluid and the Buchdahl fluid. Their limits on the ultimate energy density (as well as the pressure) are thus not tight enough, compared with our largely EOS model independent predictions of Eq. (
6.41
) and Eq. (
6.42
)
as we have analytically demonstrated and numerically verified
Cai et al. (2023)
.
In addition, we find that the constraint
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}\lesssim 1.41\,\mathrm{GeV}/\mathrm{fm}^{3}
(by using
M_{\mathrm{NS}}^{\max}/M_{\odot}\approx 2.01

Antoniadis et al. (2013)
in Eq. (
6.41
)) is close to
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}\lesssim 1.18_{-0.17}^{+0.17}\,\mathrm{GeV}/\mathrm{fm}^{3}
from recent ab-initio QCD calculations
Gorda et al. (2023)
, which also assumed the NS masses are greater than
2.01M_{\odot}
as shown by the blue hexagon in FIG.
117
. Similarly, we have the limit
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}\lesssim 1.42\,\mathrm{GeV}/\mathrm{fm}^{3}
(using
M_{\mathrm{NS}}^{\max}/M_{\odot}\approx 2
in Eq. (
6.41
)), which is close to the limit
1.46\,\mathrm{GeV}/\mathrm{fm}^{3}
of Ref.
Altiparmak et al. (2022)
where the algorithm rejects masses
\leq 2M_{\odot}
(red circle).
Furthermore, the maximum central energy density
\lesssim 1.16_{-0.10}^{+0.11}\,\mathrm{GeV}/\mathrm{fm}^{3}
from Ref.
Marczenko et al. (2023)
is also shown using the green diamond, in which the maximum mass
M_{\mathrm{NS}}^{\max}
is greater than about 2.2
M_{\odot}
.
Using our estimate of Eq. (
6.41
), for such NSs with
M_{\mathrm{NS}}^{\max}\approx 2.2M_{\odot}
the central energy density is found to be about
1.19\,\mathrm{GeV}/\mathrm{fm}^{3}
.

M_{\odot}

\varepsilon_{\mathrm{ult}}
and pressure

1.05\,\mathrm{GeV}/\mathrm{fm}^{3}
and
392\,\mathrm{MeV}/\mathrm{fm}^{3}
(lightblue bands in FIG.
