), respectively.
The analyses of very massive NSs are important for extracting the trace anomaly especially its high-density limit as demonstrated in FIG.
104
. We show in FIG.
119
the central trace anomaly
\Delta_{\mathrm{c}}=1/3-{\mathrm{X}}
as a function of
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}
for different values of
M_{\mathrm{NS}}^{\max}
(varying from 1.7
M_{\odot}
to
2.4M_{\odot}
) using the mass scaling of Eq. (
4.30
). The parametrization for
\Delta
suggested by Ref.
Fujimoto et al. (2022)
is also shown (green dotted line), which is positive-definite by construction.
In addition, two model predictions for
\Delta
are represented by lavender
Fujimoto et al. (2022)
and cyan dashed
Gorda et al. (2023)
bands using machine-learning algorithms and ab-initio QCD calculations, respectively.
For relatively light NSs, the condition
\Delta_{\mathrm{c}}\geq 0
generally holds since the central energy density
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}
is relatively low, e.g., for
M_{\mathrm{NS}}^{\max}/M_{\odot}=1.7
the condition
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}\lesssim 1.9\,\mathrm{GeV}/\mathrm{fm}^{3}
is safely satisfied. On the other hand, the
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}
may exceed the value set by
\Delta_{\mathrm{c}}\approx 0
as
M_{\mathrm{NS}}^{\max}
increases; so FIG.
119
explains why the criterion
\Delta\geq 0
tends to break down for massive NSs with increasing
M_{\mathrm{NS}}^{\max}

Ecker and Rezzolla (2022)
;
Musolino et al. (2024)
;
Pang et al. (2024)
;
Cao and Chen (2023)
;
Takátsy et al. (2023)
;
Annala et al. (2023)
;
Marczenko et al. (2024)
;
Brandes et al. (2023)
, instead of for light NSs.
In this sense, in order to observe a pattern of
\Delta
on energy density indicated by the magenta line in FIG.
105
, observations of massive and compact NSs are relevant/necessary.

6.9 Implications of the conjecture of a positive trace anomaly on the peaked structure of SSS

Finally, we discuss the implications of a positive
\Delta
on the possible peaked behavior of
s^{2}

Marczenko (2024)
.
The relation between
\Delta
and the peak position in
s^{2}
was recently analyzed by using the average speed of sound
Marczenko (2024)
:

\langle s^{2}(\varepsilon)\rangle\equiv\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int_{0}^{\varepsilon}\mathrm{d}\varepsilon^{\prime}s^{2}(\varepsilon^{\prime})=\frac{P}{\varepsilon}=\phi.

(6.44)

Under the assumptions (1) that the trace anomaly vanishes at some reference energy density
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{rf}}
(so the average SSS averages to
\langle s^{2}_{\mathrm{rf}}\rangle\equiv\langle s^{2}(\varepsilon_{\mathrm{rf}})\rangle\to 1/3
) and (2)
\Delta\geq 0
at all densities, the SSS may develop a local maximum above 1/3 at an energy density
\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon_{\mathrm{rf}}
.
This can be analyzed as follows.

FIG. 120:
(Color Online).
Upper panel: a sketch of
s^{2}(\varepsilon)
to help understand the peak position in
s^{2}(\varepsilon)
considering trace anomaly constraints; here
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{low}}<\varepsilon_{\mathrm{rf}}<\varepsilon_{\mathrm{high}}
.
Lower panel: the
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{rf}}
-dependence of parameter
\alpha
defined in Ref.
Marczenko (2024)
. Figure taken from Ref.
Marczenko (2024)
, here
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}\leftrightarrow\varepsilon_{\mathrm{rf}}
under our notations.

The first assumption gives

\langle s_{\mathrm{rf}}^{2}\rangle\equiv\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{rf}}}\int_{0}^{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{rf}}}\mathrm{d}\varepsilon s^{2}(\varepsilon)=\frac{1}{3}\leftrightarrow\int_{0}^{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{low}}}\mathrm{d}\varepsilon s^{2}(\varepsilon)+\int_{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{low}}}^{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{rf}}}\mathrm{d}\varepsilon s^{2}(\varepsilon)=\frac{1}{3}\varepsilon_{\mathrm{low}}+\frac{1}{3}\left(\varepsilon_{\mathrm{rf}}-\varepsilon_{\mathrm{low}}\right).

(6.45)

Applying the second assumption
\Delta\geq 0
to the density region of
0\leq\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon_{\mathrm{low}}
, we have

\langle s_{\mathrm{low}}^{2}\rangle\equiv\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{low}}}\int_{0}^{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{low}}}\mathrm{d}\varepsilon s^{2}(\varepsilon)\leq\frac{1}{3}\leftrightarrow\int_{0}^{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{low}}}\mathrm{d}\varepsilon s^{2}(\varepsilon)\leq\frac{1}{3}\varepsilon_{\mathrm{low}}.

(6.46)

Combining these two results leads to the following average SSS (
\alpha
) over
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{low}}\leq\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon_{\mathrm{rf}}

\boxed{\alpha\equiv\langle s^{2}(\varepsilon_{\mathrm{low}}\to\varepsilon_{\mathrm{rf}})\rangle\equiv\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{rf}}-\varepsilon_{\mathrm{low}}}\int_{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{low}}}^{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{rf}}}\mathrm{d}\varepsilon s^{2}(\varepsilon)\geq\frac{1}{3}.}

(6.47)

This relation shows that the average SSS in the region
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{low}}\leq\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon_{\mathrm{rf}}
is equal to or greater than 1/3.
Assume that the EOS at low densities
\varepsilon\lesssim\varepsilon_{\mathrm{low}}
is well known (e.g., from CEFT), one can further obtain
\langle s^{2}_{\mathrm{low}}\rangle<1/3
; then

\alpha=\frac{1}{3}+\frac{3^{-1}-\langle s_{\mathrm{low}}^{2}\rangle}{1-\varepsilon_{\mathrm{low}}/\varepsilon_{\mathrm{rf}}}\frac{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{low}}}{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{rf}}}>\frac{1}{3}.

(6.48)

Consequently, there must be some density smaller than
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{rf}}
at which the SSS is greater than 1/3.
Using the parameter
\alpha
, we can rewrite the average SSS
\langle s_{\mathrm{rf}}^{2}\rangle
as
\langle s_{\mathrm{rf}}^{2}\rangle=\langle s_{\mathrm{low}}^{2}\rangle(\varepsilon_{\mathrm{low}}/\varepsilon_{\mathrm{rf}})+\alpha(1-\varepsilon_{\mathrm{low}}/\varepsilon_{\mathrm{rf}})
.

Next, applying the assumption
\Delta\geq 0
to the even larger density region
0\leq\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon_{\mathrm{high}}
, we may obtain

\displaystyle\langle s_{\mathrm{high}}^{2}\rangle\equiv\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{high}}}\int_{0}^{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{high}}}\mathrm{d}\varepsilon s^{2}(\varepsilon)\leq\frac{1}{3}

\displaystyle\leftrightarrow

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{high}}}\left[\int_{0}^{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{low}}}\mathrm{d}\varepsilon s^{2}(\varepsilon)+\int_{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{low}}}^{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{rf}}}\mathrm{d}\varepsilon s^{2}(\varepsilon)+\int_{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{rf}}}^{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{high}}}\mathrm{d}\varepsilon s^{2}(\varepsilon)\right]\leq\frac{1}{3}

\displaystyle\leftrightarrow

\displaystyle\int_{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{rf}}}^{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{high}}}\mathrm{d}\varepsilon s^{2}(\varepsilon)<\frac{1}{3}\varepsilon_{\mathrm{low}}-\int_{0}^{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{low}}}\mathrm{d}\varepsilon s^{2}(\varepsilon)+\frac{1}{3}\left(\varepsilon_{\mathrm{high}}-\varepsilon_{\mathrm{rf}}\right),~~\mbox{using}~~\int_{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{low}}}^{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{rf}}}\mathrm{d}\varepsilon s^{2}(\varepsilon)>\frac{1}{3}\left(\varepsilon_{\mathrm{rf}}-\varepsilon_{\mathrm{low}}\right)

\displaystyle\leftrightarrow

\displaystyle\langle s^{2}(\varepsilon_{\mathrm{rf}}\to\varepsilon_{\mathrm{high}})\rangle\equiv\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{high}}-\varepsilon_{\mathrm{rf}}}\int_{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{rf}}}^{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{high}}}\mathrm{d}\varepsilon s^{2}(\varepsilon)<\frac{1}{3}+\frac{3^{-1}\varepsilon_{\mathrm{low}}-\int_{0}^{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{low}}}\mathrm{d}\varepsilon s^{2}(\varepsilon)}{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{high}}-\varepsilon_{\mathrm{rf}}}.

(6.49)

Taking
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{high}}\to\infty
gives

\boxed{\langle s^{2}(\varepsilon_{\mathrm{rf}}\to\varepsilon_{\mathrm{high}})\rangle<\frac{1}{3},}

(6.50)

this means the average SSS over density
\varepsilon\geq\varepsilon_{\mathrm{rf}}
is smaller than 1/3.
Equivalently, there must exist some density region over which the SSS is smaller than 1/3.
If the SSS approaches 1/3 at extremely high density from above then the pattern for
s^{2}
takes the form of the black line in the upper panel of FIG.
120
; and it may have the light-blue pattern if
s^{2}
approaches 1/3 from below.
In case (a) or (b) in the upper panel of FIG.
120
, where
s_{\mathrm{rf}}^{2}<1/3
or
s_{\mathrm{rf}}^{2}>1/3
, the peak in
s^{2}
emerges somewhere
\leq\varepsilon_{\mathrm{rf}}
.
In case (c), the peak in
s^{2}
emerges at
\varepsilon\geq\varepsilon_{\mathrm{rf}}
.
In all three cases, the
s^{2}
develops a maximum above 1/3 at energy densities
\leq\varepsilon_{\mathrm{rf}}

Marczenko (2024)
.
Numerically, using the knowledge of the EOS with
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{low}}\approx 2\varepsilon_{0}
and requiring that
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{rf}}

has to reproduce the estimated central energy density of maximally massive NSs
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{TOV}}
about
1\,\mathrm{GeV}/\mathrm{fm}^{3}
, Ref.
Marczenko (2024)
estimated that
\alpha\approx 0.41\mbox{-}0.49
, showing that the SSS exceeds the conformal value.
Taking the basic requirement
\alpha\geq 3^{-1}
and the same CEFT constraint on NS EOS with
\varepsilon\lesssim\varepsilon_{\mathrm{low}}
, Ref.
Marczenko (2024)
also obtained
\langle s^{2}_{\mathrm{rf}}\rangle\gtrsim 0.26
with
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{rf}}\approx 1\,\mathrm{GeV}/\mathrm{fm}^{3}
.
The lower panel of FIG.
120
shows the
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{rf}}
-dependence of the parameter
\alpha
defined and used in Ref.
Marczenko (2024)
; an upper bound on
\alpha
as
\alpha\leq 1
implies that
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{rf}}\gtrsim 0.42\,\mathrm{GeV}/\mathrm{fm}^{3}
.

At this point, we would like to point out that the reference density
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{rf}}
introduced in Ref.
Marczenko (2024)
is a relevant quantity based on which certain/special assumptions are made, e.g.,
\langle s^{2}_{\mathrm{rf}}\rangle\approx\langle s^{2}_{\mathrm{TOV}}\rangle\equiv\langle s^{2}(\varepsilon_{\mathrm{TOV}})\rangle\to 1/3
.
However, the central ratio of pressure over energy density
\phi_{\mathrm{c}}=\mathrm{X}=\langle s^{2}_{\mathrm{c}}\rangle
for NSs at the TOV configuration may either be smaller or larger than 1/3. It may thus partially reduce the predicting power of the approach.
For example, by letting generally
\langle s^{2}_{\mathrm{low}}\rangle\lesssim\mathrm{X}_{\mathrm{low}}
,
\langle s^{2}_{\mathrm{rf}}\rangle\approx\langle s^{2}_{\mathrm{TOV}}\rangle\lesssim\mathrm{X}_{\mathrm{rf}}
and
\Delta\gtrsim\Delta_{\mathrm{GR}}=3^{-1}-\mathrm{X}_{+}
, where
\mathrm{X}_{\mathrm{low}}
is smaller than
\mathrm{X}_{\mathrm{rf}}
and
\mathrm{X}_{+}\approx 0.374
, we may similarly obtain

\langle s^{2}(\varepsilon_{\mathrm{low}}\to\varepsilon_{\mathrm{rf}})\rangle\gtrsim\mathrm{X}_{\mathrm{rf}}+\frac{\mathrm{X}_{\mathrm{rf}}-\mathrm{X}_{\mathrm{low}}}{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{rf}}/\varepsilon_{\mathrm{low}}-1}\approx\frac{(\varepsilon_{\mathrm{rf}}/\varepsilon_{\mathrm{low}})\mathrm{X}_{\mathrm{rf}}-\mathrm{X}_{\mathrm{low}}}{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{rf}}/\varepsilon_{\mathrm{low}}-1}.

(6.51)

In addition, we have
\langle s^{2}(\varepsilon_{\mathrm{rf}}\to\varepsilon_{\mathrm{high}})\rangle\lesssim\mathrm{X}_{+}
by taking
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{high}}\to\infty
.
Adopting artificially
\mathrm{X}_{\mathrm{rf}}\approx\mathrm{X}_{\mathrm{low}}\approx\mathrm{X}_{+}\approx 1/3
recovers the analysis in Ref.
Marczenko (2024)
.
The peak position analysis in such case depends on the ratio
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{rf}}/\varepsilon_{\mathrm{low}}
and the three
\mathrm{X}
’s, which is more involved.

In a recent work of Ref.
Marczenko et al. (2024)
, a peaked
s^{2}
in NSs was attributed to the vanishing of the curvature
\beta
of energy per particle. This can be analyzed as follows.
Using the thermodynamic relation
\mathrm{d}\varepsilon=\mu\mathrm{d}\rho
and
P=\rho^{2}\mathrm{d}(\varepsilon/\rho)/\mathrm{d}\rho
with
\mu
the baryon chemical potential and
\varepsilon/\rho
the energy per particle, we rewrite the SSS as:

\displaystyle s^{2}=

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}P}{\mathrm{d}\varepsilon}=\frac{1}{\mu}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\rho}\left(\rho^{2}\frac{\mathrm{d}(\varepsilon/\rho)}{\mathrm{d}\rho}\right)=\frac{2\rho}{\mu}\frac{\mathrm{d}(\varepsilon/\rho)}{\mathrm{d}\rho}+\frac{\rho^{2}}{\mu}\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}(\varepsilon/\rho)}{\mathrm{d}\rho^{2}},

(6.52)

the first term is the slope of the energy per particle and the second term is the curvature. We denote them as
\ell_{\mathrm{sp}}
and
\beta
, respectively.
The slope could be further expressed as
Marczenko et al. (2024)

\displaystyle\ell_{\mathrm{sp}}=

\displaystyle\frac{2\rho}{\mu}\frac{\mathrm{d}(\varepsilon/\rho)}{\mathrm{d}\rho}=2\left(1-\frac{\varepsilon}{\rho}\frac{\mathrm{d}\rho}{\mathrm{d}\varepsilon}\right)=2\left(1-\frac{\varepsilon}{\rho}\frac{\rho}{P+\varepsilon}\right)=\frac{2P}{P+\varepsilon}=\frac{2}{1+\phi^{-1}}=2\frac{\Delta-1/3}{\Delta-4/3},

(6.53)

where the relation
P=\rho\mathrm{d}\varepsilon/\mathrm{d}\rho-\varepsilon=\rho\mu-\varepsilon
is used.
Consequently, we have

\beta=s^{2}-\ell_{\mathrm{sp}}=-\varepsilon{\mathrm{d}\Delta}/{\mathrm{d}\varepsilon}+({3}^{-1}-\Delta)\left[1+{2}/({\Delta-4/3})\right].

(6.54)

Since
\phi
is a monotonically increasing function of
\varepsilon
,
\ell_{\mathrm{sp}}
also increases with
\varepsilon
.
Consequently, if
s^{2}
has a peak somewhere, it should be attributed to the curvature term
\beta
.
Moreover,
\beta(\varepsilon)\approx 2\Delta^{\prime 2}(0)\varepsilon^{2}+2\Delta^{\prime 3}(0)\varepsilon^{3}
increases with
\varepsilon
, where
\Delta^{\prime}(0)<0
is the first-order derivative of
\Delta(\varepsilon)
with respect to
\varepsilon
at
\varepsilon=0
, i.e.,
\Delta(\varepsilon)\approx 3^{-1}+\Delta^{\prime}(0)\varepsilon
.
So
\beta(\varepsilon)\geq 0
for small
\varepsilon
and it develops a local maximum
\varepsilon_{\beta}^{\ast}
and then a zero point
\varepsilon_{\beta}
, here
\varepsilon_{\beta}>\varepsilon_{\beta}^{\ast}
.
See the left panel of FIG.
121
for the
\varepsilon
-dependence of such curvature function.
The curvature
\beta
of energy per particle is closely related to the incompressibility coefficient
K_{\mathrm{NM}}
in nuclear matter
Ivanytskyi and Blaschke (2022)

K_{\mathrm{NM}}(\rho)\equiv 9\rho^{2}\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}(\varepsilon/\rho)}{\mathrm{d}\rho^{2}}=9\mu\left(s^{2}-\frac{2P}{P+\varepsilon}\right)=9\mu\left(s^{2}-\frac{2}{1+\phi^{-1}}\right),~~\phi=P/\varepsilon,

(6.55)

consequently
\beta=K_{\mathrm{NM}}/9\mu
.
If
K_{\mathrm{NM}}(\rho)
is defined as
9\mathrm{d}P/\mathrm{d}\rho
, then the relation becomes
\beta=K_{\mathrm{NM}}/9\mu-2/(1+\phi^{-1})
.
Connecting the
\beta
with the nuclear matter incompressibility coefficient provides a useful perspective to investigate the conformal properties of NS matter
Ivanytskyi and Blaschke (2022)
.

FIG. 121:
Two types of the
\varepsilon
-dependence of the curvature
\beta(\varepsilon)
: the
\beta
function increases with
\varepsilon
at small
\varepsilon
, then it reaches the maximum point
\varepsilon_{\beta}^{\ast}
and the zero point
\varepsilon_{\beta}
at which
\beta(\varepsilon_{\beta})=0
(left), or it monotonically increases with
\varepsilon
(right).

Expanding the curvature
\beta
around the zero point
\varepsilon_{\beta}
as

\beta(\varepsilon)\approx\beta^{\prime}\left(\varepsilon-\varepsilon_{\beta}\right)+\frac{1}{2}\beta^{\prime\prime}\left(\varepsilon-\varepsilon_{\beta}\right)^{2},~~\beta^{\prime}\equiv\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}\beta}{\mathrm{d}\varepsilon}\right|_{\varepsilon=\varepsilon_{\beta}}<0,~~\beta^{\prime\prime}\equiv\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}\beta}{\mathrm{d}\varepsilon^{2}}\right|_{\varepsilon=\varepsilon_{\beta}},

(6.56)

from which we obtain
\varepsilon_{\beta}^{\ast}-\varepsilon_{\beta}=-\beta^{\prime}/\beta^{\prime\prime}
; therefore
\beta^{\prime\prime}<0
since
\varepsilon_{\beta}^{\ast}-\varepsilon_{\beta}<0
. Notice that if we include
6^{-1}\beta^{\prime\prime\prime}(\varepsilon-\varepsilon_{\beta})^{3}
in the expansion of
\beta(\varepsilon)
, then
\beta^{\prime\prime}
could be positive as long as
\beta^{\prime\prime\prime}>0
since now
\varepsilon_{\beta}^{\ast}-\varepsilon_{\beta}=-[\beta^{\prime\prime}+\sqrt{\beta^{\prime\prime 2}-2\beta^{\prime}\beta^{\prime\prime\prime}}]/\beta^{\prime\prime\prime}<0
; for our purpose the expansion (
6.56
) to order
\beta^{\prime\prime}
is enough.
Similarly, we expand the trace anomaly
\Delta
around
\varepsilon_{\beta}
as:

\Delta(\varepsilon)\approx\Delta_{\beta}+\Delta^{\prime}_{\beta}\left(\varepsilon-\varepsilon_{\beta}\right)+2^{-1}\Delta_{\beta}^{\prime\prime}\left(\varepsilon-\varepsilon_{\beta}\right)^{2}.

(6.57)

Putting it into
\beta(\varepsilon)
of (
6.54
) and keeping terms to order
(\varepsilon-\varepsilon_{\beta})^{2}
gives

\displaystyle\beta(\varepsilon)\approx

\displaystyle-\varepsilon_{\beta}\Delta_{\beta}^{\prime}-\frac{1}{3}\frac{2-3\Delta_{\beta}-9\Delta_{\beta}^{2}}{4-3\Delta_{\beta}}

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{(4-3\Delta_{\beta})^{2}}\left[2\left(7-24\Delta_{\beta}+9\Delta_{\beta}^{2}\right)\Delta_{\beta}^{\prime}+\left(16-24\Delta_{\beta}+9\Delta_{\beta}^{2}\right)\varepsilon_{\beta}\Delta_{\beta}^{\prime\prime}\right]\left(\varepsilon-\varepsilon_{\beta}\right)

\displaystyle+\frac{3}{2}\frac{1}{(4-3\Delta_{\beta})^{3}}\left[36\Delta_{\beta}^{\prime 2}-\left(40-126\Delta_{\beta}+108\Delta_{\beta}^{2}-27\Delta_{\beta}^{3}\right)\Delta_{\beta}^{\prime\prime}\right]\left(\varepsilon-\varepsilon_{\beta}\right)^{2}.

(6.58)

The vanishing of the first line (there is no constant term in
\beta(\varepsilon)
according to (
6.56
)) leads to the expression for
\Delta_{\beta}^{\prime}
:

\Delta_{\beta}^{\prime}=-\frac{1}{3\varepsilon_{\beta}}\frac{(2+3\Delta_{\beta})(1-3\Delta_{\beta})}{4-3\Delta_{\beta}},

(6.59)

which is semi-negative definite for
-2/3\leq\Delta_{\beta}\leq 1/3
.
Equaling the coefficient of the second line of (
6.58
) with
\beta^{\prime}
and putting
\Delta_{\beta}^{\prime}
into it gives the expression for
\Delta_{\beta}^{\prime\prime}
:

\Delta_{\beta}^{\prime\prime}=-\frac{\beta^{\prime}}{\varepsilon_{\beta}}+\frac{2}{3\varepsilon_{\beta}^{2}}\frac{(2+3\Delta_{\beta})(7-3\Delta_{\beta})(1-3\Delta_{\beta})^{2}}{(4-3\Delta_{\beta})^{3}},

(6.60)

which is semi-positive definite for
-2/3\leq\Delta_{\beta}\leq 1/3
.
Now, we have (
\delta\varepsilon\equiv\varepsilon-\varepsilon_{\beta}
):

\boxed{\Delta(\varepsilon)\approx\Delta_{\beta}-\frac{\delta\varepsilon}{3\varepsilon_{\beta}}\frac{(2+3\Delta_{\beta})(1-3\Delta_{\beta})}{4-3\Delta_{\beta}}-\frac{\delta\varepsilon^{2}}{2\varepsilon_{\beta}^{2}}\left[\varepsilon_{\beta}\beta^{\prime}-\frac{2}{3}\frac{(2+3\Delta_{\beta})(7-3\Delta_{\beta})(1-3\Delta_{\beta})^{2}}{(4-3\Delta_{\beta})^{3}}\right].}

(6.61)

Consequently, a local minimum develops at
\varepsilon_{\Delta}>\varepsilon_{\beta}
for
\Delta(\varepsilon)
:

\varepsilon_{\Delta}/\varepsilon_{\beta}=1-\frac{\Delta_{\beta}^{\prime}}{\Delta_{\beta}^{\prime\prime}\varepsilon_{\beta}}=\frac{3(2+3\Delta_{\beta})(1-3\Delta_{\beta})(10-24\Delta_{\beta}+9\Delta_{\beta}^{2})-3\varepsilon_{\beta}\beta^{\prime}(4-3\Delta_{\beta})^{3}}{2(2+3\Delta_{\beta})(7-3\Delta_{\beta})(1-3\Delta_{\beta})^{2}-3\varepsilon_{\beta}\beta^{\prime}(4-3\Delta_{\beta})^{3}},

(6.62)

with the minimum
\Delta_{\min}\equiv\Delta(\varepsilon_{\Delta})
given by:

\Delta_{\min}=\Delta_{\beta}-\frac{1}{2}\frac{\Delta_{\beta}^{\prime 2}}{\Delta_{\beta}^{\prime\prime}}=-\frac{(2+3\Delta_{\beta})(1-3\Delta_{\beta})^{2}(8-78\Delta_{\beta}+27\Delta_{\beta}^{2})+18\varepsilon_{\beta}\beta^{\prime}\Delta_{\beta}(4-3\Delta_{\beta})^{3}}{12(2+3\Delta_{\beta})(7-3\Delta_{\beta})(1-3\Delta_{\beta})^{2}-18\varepsilon_{\beta}\beta^{\prime}(4-3\Delta_{\beta})^{3}}.

(6.63)

For
\Delta_{\beta}\approx 0
, the above relations become

\Delta(\varepsilon)\approx-\frac{1}{6}\frac{\delta\varepsilon}{\varepsilon_{\beta}}+\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{7}{48}-\varepsilon_{\beta}\beta^{\prime}\right)\frac{\delta\varepsilon^{2}}{\varepsilon_{\beta}^{2}},~~\varepsilon_{\Delta}/\varepsilon_{\beta}\approx\frac{15-48\varepsilon_{\beta}\beta^{\prime}}{7-48\varepsilon_{\beta}\beta^{\prime}},~~\Delta_{\min}\approx\frac{2}{3}\frac{1}{48\varepsilon_{\beta}\beta^{\prime}-7}.

(6.64)

A small negative
\Delta_{\min}\gtrsim-2/21\approx-0.1
(corresponding to
\varepsilon_{\beta}\beta^{\prime}\approx 0
) and
\varepsilon_{\Delta}/\varepsilon_{\beta}\gtrsim 1
would be obtained, considering that
0\lesssim-\varepsilon_{\beta}\beta^{\prime}\approx\mathcal{O}(1)
; e.g., we have
\varepsilon_{\Delta}/\varepsilon_{\beta}\approx 1.26
and
\Delta_{\min}\approx-0.02
by taking
\varepsilon_{\beta}\beta^{\prime}\approx-0.5
.

FIG. 122:
(Color Online). Left panel: the curvature
\beta

and several other related quantities obtained under a specific parametrization for
\Delta(\varepsilon)
.
Right panel: the energy density dependence of the curvature
\beta
obtained using constraints from astrophysical observations, nuclear-many calculations and from pQCD at high densities. Figures taken from Ref.
Marczenko et al. (2024)
.

After obtaining the expressions for
\Delta_{\beta}^{\prime}
and
\Delta_{\beta}^{\prime\prime}
, the third line of (
6.58
) gives an equation for determining
\Delta_{\beta}
(in terms of
\beta^{\prime}
and
\beta^{\prime\prime}
), which is a complicated function of
\Delta_{\beta}
; the approximation for
\Delta_{\beta}
is:

\Delta_{\beta}\approx\frac{2}{3}\frac{29-240\varepsilon_{\beta}\beta^{\prime}+128\varepsilon_{\beta}^{2}\beta^{\prime\prime}}{159-744\varepsilon_{\beta}\beta^{\prime}+320\varepsilon_{\beta}^{2}\beta^{\prime\prime}},

(6.65)

then
\Delta_{\beta}\approx 0.16
by taking
\varepsilon_{\beta}\beta^{\prime}\approx\varepsilon_{\beta}^{2}\beta^{\prime\prime}\approx-1
.
For our purpose, we may not analyze this equation for
\Delta_{\beta}
further and remember that
\Delta_{\beta}
is generally small
\lesssim 1/3
at the zero point
\varepsilon_{\beta}
of
\beta(\varepsilon)
.
Next, by letting
\mathrm{d}s^{2}(\varepsilon)/\mathrm{d}\varepsilon=0
and using the
\Delta(\varepsilon)
of Eq. (
6.57
), we obtain the peak position
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{pk}}
in
s^{2}
as:

\boxed{\frac{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{pk}}}{\varepsilon_{\beta}}\approx\frac{10-32\varepsilon_{\beta}\beta^{\prime}}{7-48\varepsilon_{\beta}\beta^{\prime}}\left[1+12\Delta_{\beta}\cdot\frac{3+8\varepsilon_{\beta}\beta^{\prime}}{(5-16\varepsilon_{\beta}\beta^{\prime})(7-48\varepsilon_{\beta}\beta^{\prime})}\right],}

(6.66)

therefore:

s_{\mathrm{pk}}^{2}\equiv s^{2}(\varepsilon_{\mathrm{pk}})\approx\frac{1}{32}\frac{121-672\varepsilon_{\beta}\beta^{\prime}+256\varepsilon_{\beta}^{2}\beta^{\prime 2}}{7-48\varepsilon_{\beta}\beta^{\prime}}\left[1-\frac{3}{4}\frac{2001-34464\varepsilon_{\beta}\beta^{\prime}+108800\varepsilon_{\beta}^{2}\beta^{\prime 2}}{(7-48\varepsilon_{\beta}\beta^{\prime})(121-672\varepsilon_{\beta}\beta^{\prime}+256\varepsilon_{\beta}^{2}\beta^{\prime 2})}\cdot\Delta_{\beta}\right],

(6.67)

and

\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}s^{2}}{\mathrm{d}\varepsilon^{2}}\right|_{\varepsilon=\varepsilon_{\mathrm{pk}}}\approx-\frac{28-192\varepsilon_{\beta}\beta^{\prime}-75\Delta_{\beta}}{64\varepsilon_{\beta}^{2}}<0.

(6.68)

The negativeness of the second-order derivative of
s^{2}
at
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{pk}}
implies it is a local maximum point (we can see that
28-75\Delta_{\beta}-192\varepsilon_{\beta}\beta^{\prime}>3
since
-2/3\leq\Delta_{\beta}\leq 1/3
and
\varepsilon_{\beta}\beta^{\prime}<0
).
Assuming
-\varepsilon_{\beta}\beta^{\prime}\approx\mathcal{O}(1)
, we then have
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{pk}}/\varepsilon_{\beta}\approx\mathcal{O}(1)
(generally
2/3\lesssim\varepsilon_{\mathrm{pk}}/\varepsilon_{\beta}\lesssim 10/7
), e.g.,
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{pk}}/\varepsilon_{\beta}\approx 0.75
(or
\approx 1
) by adopting
\varepsilon_{\beta}\beta^{\prime}\approx-1
(or
\varepsilon_{\beta}\beta^{\prime}\approx-3/16
) and
\Delta_{\beta}\approx 0
; so the turning-point of the curvature
\beta(\varepsilon)
from being positive to negative is strongly correlated with the peak position in
s^{2}

Marczenko et al. (2024)
; moreover we have
s_{\mathrm{pk}}^{2}\approx 0.60
by adopting
\Delta_{\beta}\approx 0
and
\varepsilon_{\beta}\beta^{\prime}\approx-1
(a minimum of
s_{\mathrm{pk}}^{2}=1/2
occurs at
\varepsilon_{\beta}\beta^{\prime}=-3/16
according to (
6.67
)).
Therefore, the maximum SSS (
s_{\mathrm{pk}}^{2}
) in NSs may slightly be larger than 1/2
Ecker and Rezzolla (2022)
;
Cao et al. (2022)
.
In addition, magnitude of the second term in the bracket of (
6.66
) is smaller than about
1.8\%
(compared with 1) by using
\varepsilon_{\beta}\beta^{\prime}\approx-1
and the maximum
\Delta_{\beta}\approx 1/3
.
The
\varepsilon
-dependence of the curvature
\beta
from a very specific parametrization for
\Delta(\varepsilon)
, i.e.,
\Delta(\varepsilon)\approx 3^{-1}[1-[1+\exp(-3.45(\eta-1.2))]^{-1}][1-2/(20+\eta^{2})]

Fujimoto et al. (2022)
with
\eta=\ln(\varepsilon/\varepsilon_{0})
, is shown in the left panel of FIG.
122

Marczenko et al. (2024)
, from which one can obtain three peak positions
\varepsilon_{\beta}\approx 790\,\mathrm{MeV}/\mathrm{fm}^{3}
,
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{pk}}\approx 597\,\mathrm{MeV}/\mathrm{fm}^{3}
and
\varepsilon_{\beta}^{\ast}\approx 453\,\mathrm{MeV}/\mathrm{fm}^{3}
, therefore
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{pk}}/\varepsilon_{\beta}\approx 0.76
,
\varepsilon_{\beta}^{\ast}/\varepsilon_{\beta}\approx 0.57
and
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{pk}}/\varepsilon_{\beta}^{\ast}\approx 1.31
.
Furthermore, the
\Delta
curve in the left panel also indicates
\Delta_{\beta}^{\prime\prime}>0
(black dotted line).
Similarly, the peak position
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{deriv,pk}}
in the derivative part of SSS is

\frac{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{deriv,pk}}}{\varepsilon_{\beta}}\approx\frac{3}{4}\frac{10-32\varepsilon_{\beta}\beta^{\prime}}{7-48\varepsilon_{\beta}\beta^{\prime}}\left[1+12\Delta_{\beta}\cdot\frac{3+8\varepsilon_{\beta}\beta^{\prime}}{(5-16\varepsilon_{\beta}\beta^{\prime})(7-48\varepsilon_{\beta}\beta^{\prime})}\right];

(6.69)

so
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{pk}}>\varepsilon_{\mathrm{deriv,pk}}
.
The relation between
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{pk}}
and
\varepsilon_{\beta}^{\ast}
could be obtained by using Eq. (
6.66
) and
\varepsilon_{\beta}^{\ast}\approx\varepsilon_{\beta}-\beta^{\prime}/\beta^{\prime\prime}
:

\frac{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{pk}}-\varepsilon_{\beta}^{\ast}}{\varepsilon_{\beta}}\approx\frac{3+16\varepsilon_{\beta}\beta^{\prime}}{7-48\varepsilon_{\beta}\beta^{\prime}}+\frac{\beta^{\prime}}{\varepsilon_{\beta}\beta^{\prime\prime}}.

(6.70)

As long as
\beta^{\prime}/\varepsilon_{\beta}\beta^{\prime\prime}>1/3
(a quasi-linear shape near
\varepsilon_{\beta}
) or equivalently
\mathrm{d}\ln\beta^{\prime}/\mathrm{d}\ln\varepsilon_{\beta}>3
, we always have
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{pk}}>\varepsilon_{\beta}^{\ast}
, i.e., the peak position in
s^{2}
is on the right side of that in the curvature
\beta
.

In the right panel of FIG.
122
, the
\varepsilon
-dependence of the curvature obtained from Ref.
Marczenko et al. (2024)
is shown.
They found that
\varepsilon_{\beta}\approx 678\,\mathrm{MeV}/\mathrm{fm}^{3}
and
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{pk}}\approx 500\,\mathrm{MeV}/\mathrm{fm}^{3}
, so
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{pk}}/\varepsilon_{\beta}\approx 0.74
.
We can also find from the figure that
\varepsilon_{\beta}^{\ast}\approx 445\,\mathrm{MeV}
and thus
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{pk}}/\varepsilon_{\beta}^{\ast}\approx 0.89
.
Under the assumption
\Delta\geq 0
, Ref.
Marczenko et al. (2024)
further found
s_{\beta}^{2}\equiv s^{2}(\varepsilon_{\beta})\leq 1/2
and
\gamma_{\beta}\equiv\gamma(\varepsilon_{\beta})\geq 3/2
, based on the relation
s_{\beta}^{2}+\gamma_{\beta}=2
and the expressions for
\Delta_{\beta}

Marczenko et al. (2024)
:

\Delta_{\beta}=\frac{4}{3}\frac{s_{\beta}^{2}-1/2}{s_{\beta}^{2}-2}=\frac{4}{3}\frac{\gamma_{\beta}-3/2}{\gamma_{\beta}},~~\mbox{or}~~s_{\beta}^{2}=\frac{2-6\Delta_{\beta}}{4-3\Delta_{\beta}},~~\gamma_{\beta}=\frac{6}{4-3\Delta_{\beta}}.

(6.71)

If these inequalities are violated, then a negative
\Delta
is implied
Marczenko et al. (2024)
.
The polytropic index
\gamma
around the transition point
\varepsilon_{\beta}
could be obtained as:

\gamma(\varepsilon)\approx\frac{6}{4-3\Delta_{\beta}}\left[1-\frac{\delta\varepsilon}{\varepsilon_{\beta}}\frac{(2-9\Delta_{\beta})-8\varepsilon_{\beta}\beta^{\prime}(4-9\Delta_{\beta})}{(1-3\Delta_{\beta})(4-3\Delta_{\beta})^{2}}\right],~~\beta^{\prime}<0.

(6.72)

The second term in the square bracket is negative for
\varepsilon>\varepsilon_{\beta}
, indicating the
\gamma
index decreases with
\varepsilon
above
\varepsilon_{\beta}
, which may eventually approach the conformal limit.
Taking
\Delta_{\beta}\approx 0
and
-\varepsilon_{\beta}\beta\approx-1
, we have
\gamma\approx(3/2)[1-(17/8)\delta\varepsilon/\varepsilon_{\beta}]
.
The approaching of
\gamma
to the conformal limit is a direct consequence of the assumption that
\beta
experiences a transition from positive to negative (so
\varepsilon_{\beta}
exists and
\beta^{\prime}<0
).
Similarly, the SSS
s^{2}
for
\varepsilon
near
\varepsilon_{\beta}
is:

s^{2}/s_{\beta}^{2}\approx 1+\frac{2}{s_{\beta}^{2}}\frac{\delta\varepsilon}{\varepsilon_{\beta}}\frac{3(2-3\Delta_{\beta})+8\varepsilon_{\beta}\beta^{\prime}(4-9\Delta_{\beta})}{(4-3\Delta_{\beta})^{3}},~~\beta^{\prime}<0,

(6.73)

so
s^{2}/s_{\beta}^{2}<1
for
\varepsilon>\varepsilon_{\beta}
as
3(2-3\Delta_{\beta})+8\varepsilon_{\beta}\beta^{\prime}(4-9\Delta_{\beta})
may probably be negative for
-\varepsilon_{\beta}\beta^{\prime}\approx\mathcal{O}(1)
and
\Delta_{\beta}\approx 0
.

However, we notice that the curvature
\beta
in realistic NSs may have no turning-point from positive to negative (so the zero point
\varepsilon_{\beta}
is not well defined).
For example, Ref.
Perego et al. (2022)
found that
K_{\mathrm{NM}}^{\mathrm{TOV}}\equiv[9\mathrm{d}P/\mathrm{d}\rho]_{\mathrm{TOV}}\approx 10\,\mathrm{GeV}
using
\xi_{\max}\approx 0.28
(see their FIG. 4), taking
\rho_{\mathrm{TOV}}\approx 6\rho_{0}
,
P_{\mathrm{TOV}}\approx 400\,\mathrm{MeV}/\mathrm{fm}^{3}
,
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{TOV}}\approx 1\,\mathrm{GeV}/\mathrm{fm}^{3}
, one obtains
\mu_{\mathrm{TOV}}\approx(\varepsilon_{\mathrm{TOV}}+P_{\mathrm{TOV}})/\rho_{\mathrm{TOV}}\approx 1.5\,\mathrm{GeV}
and
\phi_{\mathrm{TOV}}=P_{\mathrm{TOV}}/\varepsilon_{\mathrm{TOV}}\approx 0.4
.
Consequently
\beta_{\mathrm{TOV}}=K_{\mathrm{NM}}^{\mathrm{TOV}}/9\mu_{\mathrm{TOV}}-2/(1+\phi^{-1}_{\mathrm{TOV}})\approx 0.19
.
Without requiring
\beta^{\prime}<0
(used in the above discussion) and in the mean while introducing a constant term
B
in the expansion of
\beta
over
\varepsilon
as
\beta\approx B+\beta^{\prime}\delta\varepsilon+2^{-1}\beta^{\prime\prime}\delta\varepsilon^{2}
, we can similarly work out

\displaystyle\Delta(\varepsilon)\approx

\displaystyle\Delta_{\beta}-\frac{\delta\varepsilon}{3\varepsilon_{\beta}}\frac{2+12B-(3+9B)\Delta_{\beta}-9\Delta_{\beta}^{2}}{4-3\Delta_{\beta}}

\displaystyle-\frac{\delta\varepsilon^{2}}{2\varepsilon_{\beta}^{2}}\left[\varepsilon_{\beta}\beta^{\prime}-\frac{2}{3}\frac{(7-3\Delta_{\beta})(1-3\Delta_{\beta})[2+12B-(3+9B)\Delta_{\beta}-9\Delta_{\beta}^{2}]}{(4-3\Delta_{\beta})^{3}}\right],

(6.74)

and

\displaystyle\gamma(\varepsilon)\approx

\displaystyle\frac{6}{4-3\Delta_{\beta}}\left[1-\frac{2(1+6B)-9(1+5B)\Delta_{\beta}-8\varepsilon_{\beta}\beta^{\prime}(4-9\Delta_{\beta})}{(1-3\Delta_{\beta})(4-3\Delta_{\beta})^{2}}\frac{\delta\varepsilon}{\varepsilon_{\beta}}\right],

(6.75)

\displaystyle s^{2}/s_{\beta}^{2}\approx

\displaystyle 1-\frac{1}{s_{\beta}^{2}}\frac{18(1+3B)\Delta_{\beta}-12(1+6B)-16\varepsilon_{\beta}\beta^{\prime}(4-9\Delta_{\beta})}{(4-3\Delta_{\beta})^{3}}\frac{\delta\varepsilon}{\varepsilon_{\beta}},~~s_{\beta}^{2}=B+\frac{2-6\Delta_{\beta}}{4-3\Delta_{\beta}}.

(6.76)

Then
\Delta(\varepsilon)
develops the local minimum
\Delta_{\min}(B)=\Delta(\varepsilon_{\Delta})\approx-2(1+6B)^{2}/[21(1+6B)-144\varepsilon_{\beta}\beta^{\prime}]
at
\varepsilon_{\Delta}/\varepsilon_{\beta}\approx[15(1+6B)-48\varepsilon_{\beta}\beta^{\prime}]/[7(1+6B)-48\varepsilon_{\beta}\beta^{\prime}]
with
\Delta_{\beta}\approx 0
; the dependence
B(\varepsilon_{\beta}\beta^{\prime})
can then be obtained via, e.g.,
\Delta_{\mathrm{min}}(B)\approx\Delta_{\mathrm{GR}}\approx-0.041
.
By comparing the relations of (
6.64
) we find
\Delta_{\min}(B)<\Delta_{\min}(0)<0
for
B>0
and
\varepsilon_{\beta}\beta^{\prime}<0
.
Moreover, we have
B\lesssim s_{\beta}^{2}\lesssim B+2^{-1}
for
1/3\gtrsim\Delta_{\beta}\gtrsim 0
;
the basic condition
0\leq s_{\beta}^{2}\leq 1
requires

\frac{6\Delta_{\beta}-2}{4-3\Delta_{\beta}}\leq B\leq\frac{2+3\Delta_{\beta}}{4-3\Delta_{\beta}}.

(6.77)

According to (
6.75
), we find that as long as

2(1+6B)-9(1+5B)\Delta_{\beta}-8\varepsilon_{\beta}\beta^{\prime}(4-9\Delta_{\beta})<0,

(6.78)

the correction in the square bracket of (
6.75
) would be greater than zero for
\delta\varepsilon/\varepsilon_{\beta}>0
, implying
\gamma
increases after
\varepsilon_{\beta}
.
Specifically, by rewriting (
6.78
) we have
\gamma/\gamma_{\beta}>1
for
\delta\varepsilon/\varepsilon_{\beta}>0
if
B
satisfies:

0<B<\min\left[\frac{8\varepsilon_{\beta}\beta^{\prime}(4-9\Delta_{\beta})-(2-9\Delta_{\beta})}{12-45\Delta_{\beta}},\frac{2+3\Delta_{\beta}}{4-3\Delta_{\beta}}\right],~~\mbox{where}~~\varepsilon_{\beta}\beta^{\prime}>0.

(6.79)

The
\varepsilon
-dependence of such
\beta
function is sketched in the right panel of FIG.
121
.
Under the condition (
6.78
):

s^{2}/s_{\beta}^{2}\gtrsim 1+\frac{\delta\varepsilon}{\varepsilon_{\beta}}\frac{1}{s_{\beta}^{2}}\frac{16(1+6B)-36(1+4B)\Delta_{\beta}}{(4-3\Delta_{\beta})^{3}},

(6.80)

which may be greater than 1, indicating the
s^{2}
is an increasing function of
\varepsilon
around
\varepsilon_{\beta}
.
For example, if we take
\Delta_{\beta}\approx 0
for
\varepsilon_{\beta}\approx 1\,\mathrm{GeV}/\mathrm{fm}^{3}
and
\varepsilon_{\beta}\beta^{\prime}\approx 0.2
, then
B\approx 0.1
satisfies the condition (
6.79
), so
[16(1+6B)-36(1+4B)\Delta_{\beta}]/(4-3\Delta_{\beta})^{3}\approx 0.4
,
s_{\beta}^{2}\approx 2^{-1}+B\approx 0.6
and
s^{2}/s_{\beta}^{2}\gtrsim 1+2\delta\varepsilon/3\varepsilon_{\beta}
.

These discussions imply that
B\approx 0
and
\beta^{\prime}<0
(as shown in the left panel of FIG.
121
)
construct two basic conditions (assumptions) for generating a
\beta
function with zero point and forcing the
\gamma
parameter to approach its conformal limit at energy densities above this zero point.

7 Conclusions, Caveats and Future Perspectives

We have studied the EOS of supra-denser matter in NS cores and many related issues listed in the INTRODUCTION by analyzing perturbatively the scaled TOV equations and using only NS observational data in a largely nuclear EOS-model independent manner. Our main conclusions are summarized below (corresponding to the questions listed in the INTRODUCTION).

(a)

By perturbatively analyzing the dimensionless TOV equations (
2.3
), we established the scaling between NS mass
M_{\mathrm{NS}}
and
\Gamma_{\mathrm{c}}=\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}^{-1/2}\Pi_{\mathrm{c}}^{3/2}
and that between NS radius
R
and
\nu_{\mathrm{c}}=\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}^{-1/2}\Pi_{\mathrm{c}}^{1/2}
, here
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}
is the central energy density and
\Pi_{\mathrm{c}}=\mathrm{X}/(1+3\mathrm{X}^{2}+4\mathrm{X})
with
\mathrm{X}=P_{\mathrm{c}}/\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}
and
P_{\mathrm{c}}\equiv P(\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}})
is the central EOS, see the left panel of FIG.
16
, the right panel of FIG.
28
and FIG.
29
.
The scalings are verified quantitatively by using more than a hundred microscopic/phenomenological nuclear EOS models and about
10^{5}
randomly generated meta-model nuclear EOSs satisfying all contemporary terrestrial and astrophysical constraints.
The mass and radius scalings can be effectively mapped onto power-law forms, from which we deduce that
P_{\mathrm{c}}\sim M_{\mathrm{NS}}^{\max,3}/R_{\max}^{5}
and
\mathrm{X}\sim(M_{\mathrm{NS}}^{\max}/R_{\max})^{2}
for TOV NSs.
On the other hand, for Newtonian stars,
\Pi_{\mathrm{c}}\approx\mathrm{X}
and so
M_{\mathrm{NS}}\sim P_{\mathrm{c}}^{3/2}\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}^{-2}
and
R\sim P_{\mathrm{c}}^{1/2}\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}^{-1}
, as well as
\xi\approx 2\mathrm{X}
; inversely one obtains
P_{\mathrm{c}}\sim M_{\mathrm{NS}}^{2}/R^{4}
.

(b)

Centers of NSs at the maximum-mass configuration (TOV configuration) on the M-R curve contain the stable densest visible matter existing in our Universe. If either the mass
M_{\mathrm{NS}}^{\max}\equiv M_{\mathrm{TOV}}
or the radius
R_{\max}\equiv R_{\mathrm{TOV}}

is available/observed, one can extract the EOS of the densest visible matter using either the
M_{\mathrm{NS}}
-
\Gamma_{\mathrm{c}}
or
R
-
\nu_{\mathrm{c}}
scaling at the TOV configuration. If both the mass and radius of a NS are measured we can determine individually the
P_{\mathrm{c}}
and
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}
at the core of this NS, see FIG.
17
.
In particular, for PSR J0740+6620 we found that
P_{\mathrm{c}}\equiv P(\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}})\approx 0.012\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}^{4/3}\cdot(1+0.047\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}^{1/3}+0.0026\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}^{2/3}+0.00016\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}+\cdots)
for roughly
640\,\mathrm{MeV}/\mathrm{fm}^{3}\lesssim\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}\lesssim 1210\,\mathrm{MeV}/\mathrm{fm}^{3}
.
Our predictions are independent of any input nuclear EOS.

(c)

Because the General Relativity for strong-field gravity is a nonlinear theory and the TOV equations inherit this nonlinearity, the EOS inferred from the latter is also essentially nonlinear
Cai and Li (2024)
.
This means a linear EOS of the form
P=\zeta\varepsilon+\mathrm{const.}
with
\zeta
being a constant is fundamentally inconsistent with the TOV equations.
Specifically, using such linear EOS leads to singularities for the pressure and energy density at NS centers. In addition, a polytropic index
\gamma
of 1 of such linear EOS is basically in conflict with the fact that
\gamma\geq 4/3
by analyzing directly the dimensionless TOV equations without using any input nuclear EOS, see Eq. (
4.17
) or Eq. (
4.15
).
Equivalently, an EOS model with a constant speed of sound (CSS) can not consistently describe the dense matter in NS cores; i.e., the widely used CSS EOS model in the literature is an approximation for describing core NS matter; one may take caution when making inference on the core NS EOS using such model.

(d)

The NS compactness
\xi=M_{\mathrm{NS}}/R
is found to scale with
\mathrm{X}=\phi_{\mathrm{c}}={\mathrm{X}}=P_{\mathrm{c}}/\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}
according to
\xi\approx 2\mathrm{X}/(1+3\mathrm{X}^{2}+4\mathrm{X})=2\Pi_{\mathrm{c}}
; this scaling is verified quantitatively in the left panel of FIG.
28
using
10^{5}
randomly generated meta-model EOSs.
The
\xi
-
\Pi_{\mathrm{c}}
scaling enables us to extract directly the central ratio
\mathrm{X}
and thus the trace anomaly
\Delta
from NS observational data independent of any input EOS model.

(e)

The SSS
s^{2}=\mathrm{d}P/\mathrm{d}\varepsilon
is closely related to the compactness
\xi
via
s^{2}=g(\xi)
with
g
being a dimensionless function. In particular, the central SSS can be written as
s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}\approx 3^{-1}\tau^{-1}(4+\Psi)\xi+(4/3)\tau^{-2}(5+2\Psi)\xi^{2}+\cdots
, where
\tau\approx 2
and
\Psi\equiv 2\mathrm{d}\ln M_{\mathrm{NS}}/\mathrm{d}\ln\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}>0
, see Eq. (
5.39
). This means the central stiffness (characterized by
s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}
) also characterizes the physics of compactness
\xi
of NSs, see Eq. (
5.40
), or

\displaystyle\mbox{NS compactness }\xi={M_{\mathrm{NS}}}/{R}\leftrightarrow

\displaystyle\mbox{central ratio }\mathrm{X}=\phi_{\mathrm{c}}=\widehat{P}_{\mathrm{c}}=P_{\mathrm{c}}/\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}

\displaystyle\leftrightarrow

\displaystyle\mbox{average SSS from surface to center }\mathrm{X}=\langle s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}\rangle=\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}}\int_{0}^{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}}\mathrm{d}\varepsilon^{\prime}s^{2}(\varepsilon^{\prime})

\displaystyle\leftrightarrow

\displaystyle\mbox{central stiffness }s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}=\mathrm{X}\left(1+\frac{1+\Psi}{3}\frac{1+3\mathrm{X}^{2}+4\mathrm{X}}{1-3\mathrm{X}^{2}}\right).

(7.1)

However, it does not mean
s^{2}
is physically equivalently to
\xi
since
s^{2}
is not a monotonic function of the radial distance from the NS center, i.e., there is no one-to-one correspondence between the stiffness at places away from NS centers (characterized by
s^{2}
instead of
s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}
) and the compactness.
Similarly, although
\mathrm{X}
characterizes the same physics of
\xi
when describing the compactness, i.e., a larger
\mathrm{X}
corresponds to a larger
\xi
, the ratio
\phi
is not generally equivalent to
\xi
.
This is because a NS with larger
\mathrm{X}
(and therefore a larger
\xi
) may have a smaller
\phi
at finite
\widehat{r}
, see the relevant discussion given at the end of Subsection
5.7
.

(f)

Due to the nonlinearity of the EOS in NS cores and the fact that the equivalence between
s^{2}\leq 1
(causality principle) and
\phi\equiv P/\varepsilon\leq 1
only holds for the linear EOS
P\sim\varepsilon
, the gravitational upper limit for
\phi
is expected to be smaller than 1.
Using the expression for the central SSS as
s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}=\mathrm{X}[1+3^{-1}(1+3\mathrm{X}^{2}+4\mathrm{X})/(1-3\mathrm{X}^{2})]
and
s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}\leq 1
, we found that
\phi\leq\mathrm{X}\lesssim 0.374
, see Eq. (
4.17
).
Though the Principle of Special Relativity requires
P\leq\varepsilon
, the strong-field gravity in General Relativity and the induced nonlinearity of the EOS render the upper bound for the ratio
\phi
to be much smaller than 1
Cai and Li (2024)
.
As a direct consequence, the dimensionless trace anomaly
\Delta=1/3-\phi=1/3-P/\varepsilon
is lower bounded by GR as
\Delta\gtrsim\Delta_{\mathrm{GR}}\approx-0.041
.
The GR effects significantly reduce the factor
\Pi_{\mathrm{c}}^{\mathrm{N}}=\mathrm{X}
with
\mathrm{X}\leq 3/4
in Newtonian case being upper bounded as
\Pi_{\mathrm{c}}^{\mathrm{N}}\lesssim 0.75
to
\Pi_{\mathrm{c}}=\mathrm{X}/(1+3\mathrm{X}^{2}+4\mathrm{X})\lesssim 0.128
; and it has apparent influence (since
\Pi_{\mathrm{c}}^{3}/\Pi_{\mathrm{c}}^{\mathrm{N},3}\approx 200
) on estimating the upper bound on
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}
(and that on
M_{\mathrm{TOV}}
) which involves
\Pi_{\mathrm{c}}^{3}
, see Eq. (
4.72
).
In addition, the dependence of
s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}
on
\mathrm{X}
can be effectively mapped onto a power-law form of
\mathrm{X}^{2}
, therefore
s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}\sim(M_{\mathrm{NS}}^{\max}/R_{\max})^{4}
, namely the central SSS for TOV NSs roughly scales as the NS compactness to the fourth.
Similarly for Newtonian stars,
s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}\sim(M_{\mathrm{NS}}/R)^{1}
so
s_{\mathrm{c}}\sim(M_{\mathrm{NS}}/R)^{1/2}
.

(g)

Using the upper bound for
\mathrm{X}
as
\mathrm{X}\lesssim 0.374
as well as the mass and radius scalings of Eq. (
4.31
) and (
4.30
), we can obtain an inequality which lower bounds the radius
R_{\max}
for NS at TOV configuration as
R_{\max}/\mathrm{km}\gtrsim 4.73M_{\mathrm{NS}}^{\max}/M_{\odot}+1.14
, see (
6.35
). Because NSs at the maximum-mass configuration are the densest, this gives a new causality boundary for NSs. It is shown to be consistent with observational data of several NSs including those in GW170817
Abbott et al. (2017)
;
Abbott et al. (2018)
and the PSR J0740+6620
Riley et al. (2021)
albeit in certain tension
with predictions using a few empirical EOS for NS matter in the literature. As a direct corollary, the radius of PSR J0952-0607
Romani et al. (2022)
with a mass about
2.35M_{\odot}
is found to be
\gtrsim 12.25\,\mathrm{km}
, see FIG.
113
.
Similarly, for massive NSs (
M_{\mathrm{NS}}^{\max}\gtrsim 2M_{\odot}
), the radii should be greater than about 10.6 km.

(h)

Rewriting the causality boundary
R_{\max}/\mathrm{km}\gtrsim 4.73M_{\mathrm{NS}}^{\max}/M_{\odot}+1.14
gives
\xi_{\mathrm{TOV}}\equiv\xi_{\max}\equiv M_{\mathrm{NS}}^{\max}/R_{\max}\lesssim 0.313(1-1.14\,\mathrm{km}/R_{\max})
;
therefore an absolute upper bound for
\xi_{\max}
about
0.313
is obtained. Considering
R_{\max}\approx 12.5\pm 1\,\mathrm{km}
further limits the
\xi_{\max}
to be about
\xi_{\max}\lesssim 0.283\pm 0.014
, see (
6.32
) and FIG.
116
for the summary on existing upper bounds on the compactness.
This bound for
\xi
is consistent with all observed NSs and is much tighter than existing bounds for NS compactness (the right eight points of FIG.
116
).
Similarly, using the mass scaling of Eq. (
4.31
) alone allows us to obtain an inequality for the ultimate central energy density as
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}\lesssim\varepsilon_{\mathrm{ult}}\approx 6.32(M_{\mathrm{NS}}^{\max}/M_{\odot}+0.106)^{-2}\,\mathrm{GeV}/\mathrm{fm}^{3}
; therefore the observations of massive NSs may essentially upper limit the ultimate energy density existing in our Universe, see Eq. (
6.41
).
For instance, taking
M_{\mathrm{NS}}^{\max}/M_{\odot}\approx 2.3
gives us
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{ult}}\approx 1.1\,\mathrm{GeV}/\mathrm{fm}^{3}
.
By using the bound
\mathrm{X}\lesssim 0.374
once again, we obtain
P_{\mathrm{c}}\lesssim P_{\mathrm{utl}}\approx 2.36(M_{\mathrm{NS}}^{\max}/M_{\odot}+0.106)^{-2}\,\mathrm{GeV}/\mathrm{fm}^{3}
, see Eq. (
6.42
), so
M_{\mathrm{NS}}^{\max}/M_{\odot}\approx 2.3
induces
P_{\mathrm{ult}}\approx 408\,\mathrm{MeV}/\mathrm{fm}^{3}
.
In establishing these bounds, only the mass scaling (
4.31
) is used.

(i)

Using the radius scaling of (
4.30
), we obtain an estimate on the central baryon density of NSs at the TOV configuration as
\rho_{\mathrm{c}}/\rho_{\mathrm{sat}}\approx 7350\mathrm{X}/(1+3\mathrm{X}^{2}+4\mathrm{X})\cdot(R_{\max}/\mathrm{km}-0.64)^{-2}
, see Eq. (
4.50
). Taking
R_{\max}\approx 12.5\,\mathrm{km}
leads to
\rho_{\mathrm{c}}/\rho_{\mathrm{sat}}\lesssim 6.7
. This relation is useful for estimating the highest central baryon density in NSs when future radius observation/measurement become eventually more accurate.

(j)

The conformal bound
s^{2}\leq 1/3
is likely violated in NSs at the TOV configuration with
M_{\mathrm{NS}}^{\max}\gtrsim 1.9M_{\odot}
, see FIG.
54
.
It is obvious from Eq. (
4.17
) that
s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}\to 1/3
occurs much earlier than
\mathrm{X}\to 1/3
, implying the violation of
s^{2}\leq 1/3
occurs at a mild
\mathrm{X}
being smaller than
1/3
.
In fact, if one lets
\mathrm{X}\leq 1/3
, then Eq. (
4.17
) predicts that
s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}\leq 7/9\approx 0.778
.
For a canonical NS with
M_{\mathrm{NS}}\approx 1.4M_{\odot}
and
R\approx 12\pm 1\,\mathrm{km}
, the
\mathrm{X}
is found to be about
\mathrm{X}\approx 0.15\pm 0.02
via the compactness-
\Pi_{\mathrm{c}}
scaling (see TAB.
3
) and the factor
\Psi\equiv 2\mathrm{d}\ln M_{\mathrm{NS}}/\mathrm{d}\ln\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}\approx 2.85\pm 0.29
via the
\Psi
-
M_{\mathrm{NS}}
relation of FIG.
32
; therefore
s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}\approx 0.47\pm 0.09
using the formula (
4.15
), see TAB.
5
. Therefore, the conformal bound for
s^{2}
breaks down even for light NSs. Moreover,
\Psi\approx 2.85
implies that the upper limit
\phi\leq\mathrm{X}\lesssim 0.24
for canonical NSs via the SSS formula (
4.15
).
The relation between
\Psi
and the polytropic index
n
appearing in the EOS
P\sim\varepsilon^{1+1/n}
is established as
n=3/(1+\Psi)
, see Eq. (
2.49
); which provides an alternative viewpoint on
\gamma=1+n^{-1}\geq 4/3
.

(k)

Consider some recent studies showing that
s^{2}
is perhaps upper bounded to a lower value different from 1
Hippert et al. (2024)
, the reduction on the stiffness
s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}
correspondingly reduces the compactness
\xi
as we established in the above; therefore the radius
R
for a given NS mass is expected to increase (in order to reduce
\xi
).
By adopting
s^{2}\lesssim 0.781

Hippert et al. (2024)
from a new development of transport theories for nuclear matter, in particular, we find that the radius
R
for a
2M_{\odot}
NS may increase by about 0.8 km, see the upper panel of FIG.
38
; future astrophysical observations on
R
have the potential to tell whether such lower bound on
s^{2}
is reasonable.
Similarly, if the curvature term
\beta=-\varepsilon\mathrm{d}\Delta/\mathrm{d}\varepsilon+(3^{-1}-\Delta)[1+2/(\Delta-4/3)]
of the energy per particle develops a zero point
\varepsilon_{\beta}\approx 1\,\mathrm{GeV}
(defined by
\beta(\varepsilon_{\beta})=0
) with
-\varepsilon_{\beta}\beta^{\prime}\approx\mathcal{O}(1)
and
\Delta_{\beta}\lesssim 0.1
, the maximum SSS
s_{\mathrm{pk}}^{2}
is found to be smaller than about
0.5\mbox{-}0.6
, see (
6.67
), i.e.,
s_{\mathrm{pk}}^{2}\lesssim 0.5\mbox{-}0.6
.

(l)

Since
s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}>0
as implied by Eqs. (
4.17
) and (
4.15
), a sharp phase transition (PT) signaled by the vanishing of
s^{2}
is basically excluded; while a continuous crossover characterized by a smooth reduction of
s^{2}
is allowed in cores of massive NSs.
This means massive NSs may have soft cores (
s^{2}(r)>s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}\equiv s^{2}(r=0)
).

(m)

The strong-field gravity in GR compresses NS matter and thus makes
\phi\sim\mathrm{X}=P_{\mathrm{c}}/\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}
sizable (
\sim\mathcal{O}(0.1)
), while simultaneously extrudes a possible peak in the density/radius profile of
s^{2}
whence
\mathrm{X}\gtrsim 0.1
, see FIG.
42
. The peak eventually reduces as
\mathrm{X}
increases and finally disappears in the GR causal limit
\mathrm{X}\to 0.374
.
The position of the peak
r_{\mathrm{pk}}
is at about
(30\%
-
40\%)R
from the NS center and the relative enhancement
\Delta s^{2}=s_{\max}^{2}/s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}-1
is generally smaller than about 10%, here
s_{\max}^{2}\equiv s^{2}(r_{\mathrm{pk}})
, see the left panel of FIG.
66
and FIG.
74
.
Moreover, using the compactness scaling one can extract the
\mathrm{X}
directly from observed NS masses/radii; the dimensionless trace anomaly
\Delta
as well as its energy density dependence could then be evaluated. As a result, we find using the trace anomaly decomposition of SSS that the currently available NS data (masses and radii) can not invariably generate a peaked
s^{2}
profile,
see FIG.
106
and FIG.
109
.

(n)

For Newtonian stars, the ratio
\phi
or
\mathrm{X}
is generally small, and we have in this case
s^{2}\approx s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}-4\widehat{r}^{2}/15+\widehat{r}^{4}/{60s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}}
, here
\widehat{r}
is the dimensionless distance from the star center. It monotonically decreases when going outward from the center, see Eq. (
5.26
).
In particular, it vanishes at
\widehat{r}^{2}=6s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}
and
\mathrm{d}s^{2}/\mathrm{d}\widehat{r}\approx-(8\widehat{r}/15)(1-\widehat{r}^{2}/8s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2})
is always negative for
0\leq\widehat{r}^{2}\leq 6s_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}
, see FIG.
52
.
In addition, we have generally for Newtonian stars that
s^{2}\approx 2\phi+\cdots
around
\phi\approx 0
, see (
5.22
), therefore Newtonian stars have
\gamma_{\mathrm{N}}\approx 2
.

(o)

The dense matter in NS cores could fundamentally not be conformal under the basic definition
s^{2}/\phi\to 1
since a linear EOS of the form
P=\zeta\varepsilon+\mathrm{const.}
(with
\zeta
being constant) is excluded by the TOV equations.
Using some empirical criterion such that
\Theta\lesssim 0.2
with
\Theta=[(3^{-1}-\phi)^{2}+(s^{2}-\phi)^{2}]^{1/2}
, the core matter maybe conformal however the likelihood is very low, see the right panel of FIG.
103
, TAB.
6
and Eqs. (
6.12
), (
6.13
), (
6.14
) and (
6.15
).
In particular, we find the central
\Theta_{\mathrm{c}}
for
\widehat{r}=0
is probably greater than 0.19 and the
\Theta
for finite
\widehat{r}
is even larger.
The
\Theta_{\mathrm{c}}
parameter for a canonical NS with
R\approx 12\pm 1\,\mathrm{km}
is found to be about
0.38\pm 0.05
.
Similarly, the polytropic index at NS centers is obtained as
\gamma_{\mathrm{c}}\approx 4/3+0.55\xi_{\max}
, see Eq. (
6.6
), therefore
\gamma_{\mathrm{c}}>4/3
; for a NS at the TOV configuration with radius about 12 km to have
\gamma_{\mathrm{c}}\lesssim 1.75
its mass should be smaller than about
1.88M_{\odot}
, see FIG.
101
, implying massive NSs could hardly have a
\gamma_{\mathrm{c}}\lesssim 1.75
.
Moreover, Eq. (
4.59
) gives
s^{2}/\phi=(4/3+4\mathrm{X}/3+32\mu/15)\mathrm{X}/\phi
, which is generally greater than 4/3 near NS centers, here
\mu=\widehat{\varepsilon}-1
is small negative in NS cores.
Furthermore, we have
s^{2}
as a function of
\phi
near NS centers as
s^{2}(\phi)/\mathrm{X}\approx 4/3+(32/5)(1-19\mathrm{X}/4)(\phi/\mathrm{X}-1)-(876/25)(1-3439\mathrm{X}/219)(\phi/\mathrm{X}-1)^{2}
, which is non-monotonic (see Eq. (
4.63
)), implying
s^{2}
and
\phi
are basically two different attributes of NSs.
For Newtonian stars,
s^{2}\approx 2\phi
(see (
5.22
)) and so
\Theta_{\mathrm{N}}\approx 3^{-1}-\phi+3\phi^{2}/2\gtrsim 0.2
considering
\phi\approx 0
.

(p)

The mass scaling of Eq. (
4.7
) implies that the upper bound for NS central energy density
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}
decreases with increasing
M_{\mathrm{NS}}
and roughly
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}\sim M_{\mathrm{NS}}^{-2}
, reflecting the self-gravitating nature of NSs.
On the other hand, the stability condition
\mathrm{d}M_{\mathrm{NS}}/\mathrm{d}\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}>0
implies that the
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}
increases with
M_{\mathrm{NS}}
.
Therefore, a maximum mass for stable NSs unavoidably exists by considering these two different tendencies in varying the
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}
, see FIG.
35
for the relevant sketch.
Using the specific mass, radius and compactness scalilngs given in this review, the maximum mass
M_{\mathrm{NS}}^{\max}\equiv M_{\mathrm{TOV}}
is estimated to be about
M_{\mathrm{TOV}}\approx 2.28\pm 0.28M_{\odot}
, see FIG.
40
and FIG.
41
for the summary of the status on
M_{\mathrm{TOV}}
.

(q)

The derivative
\mathrm{d}R/\mathrm{d}M_{\mathrm{NS}}
depends essentially on
\Psi
as
\mathrm{d}R/\mathrm{d}M_{\mathrm{NS}}\sim(1-2\Psi^{-1})M_{\mathrm{NS}}^{-(2/3)(1+\Psi^{-1})}
or
\mathrm{d}R/\mathrm{d}M_{\mathrm{NS}}\sim(1-2\Psi^{-1})R^{-2(1+\Psi^{-1})/(1-2\Psi^{-1})}\sim\Psi-2
, see Eq. (
4.77
) and Eq. (
4.78
). Therefore, a weak dependence of
R
on
M_{\mathrm{NS}}
or the empirical evidence of a “vertical” shape on the M-R curve could be traced back to the quasi-linear growth of
M_{\mathrm{NS}}
with
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}
around
M_{\mathrm{NS}}/M_{\odot}\approx 1.4\mbox{-}2.2
, i.e.,
M_{\mathrm{NS}}\sim\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}^{\Psi/2}\approx\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}
for
\Psi\approx 2
.
This approximation has been verified quantitatively using
10^{5}
samples of our meta-model EOSs, see FIG.
32
.
For a super-liner growth rate for low-mass NSs with
\Psi>2
, we have
\mathrm{d}R/\mathrm{d}M_{\mathrm{NS}}>0
or equivalently
\mathrm{d}M_{\mathrm{NS}}/\mathrm{d}R>0
(from the lower-left corner to the upper-right corner on the NS M-R curve).

(r)

By considering generally
\Delta(\overline{\varepsilon})\approx\Delta_{\ell}+2^{-1}\Delta_{\ell}^{\prime\prime}(\overline{\varepsilon}-\overline{\varepsilon}_{\ell})^{2}+6^{-1}\Delta_{\ell}^{\prime\prime\prime}(\overline{\varepsilon}-\overline{\varepsilon}_{\ell})^{3}
where
\Delta_{\ell}\equiv\Delta(\overline{\varepsilon}_{\ell})
,
\Delta^{\prime\prime}_{\ell}=\Delta^{\prime\prime}(\overline{\varepsilon}_{\ell})
and
\Delta^{\prime\prime\prime}_{\ell}=\Delta^{\prime\prime\prime}(\overline{\varepsilon}_{\ell})
,
\overline{\varepsilon}_{\ell}
is the peak position in
\Delta(\overline{\varepsilon})
so that
\Delta_{\ell}^{\prime\prime}
is negative, we can work out that
\overline{\varepsilon}_{\ell}^{\ast}/\overline{\varepsilon}_{\ell}\equiv\mathrm{H}(k)\equiv(3/4)(1-k^{-1})+(4k)^{-1}\sqrt{k^{2}-2k+9}
with
k\equiv\overline{\varepsilon}_{\ell}\Delta_{\ell}^{\prime\prime\prime}/\Delta_{\ell}^{\prime\prime}=\mathrm{d}\ln\Delta_{\ell}^{\prime\prime}/\mathrm{d}\ln\overline{\varepsilon}_{\ell}
, see Eq. (
5.87
),
\overline{\varepsilon}_{\ell}^{\ast}
is the peak position in the SSS
s^{2}
.
Therefore
1/2\lesssim\overline{\varepsilon}_{\ell}^{\ast}/\overline{\varepsilon}_{\ell}\lesssim 1
for all possible
k
, see Eq. (
5.93
) and FIG.
94
.
A ratio
\overline{\varepsilon}_{\ell}^{\ast}/\overline{\varepsilon}_{\ell}
deviating from 1/2 or 1 indicates a non-negligible kurtosis coefficient
\Delta_{\ell}^{\prime\prime\prime\prime}\equiv\Delta_{\ell}^{(4)}
.

We summarize schematically in panel (a) of FIG.
123
the most important results and the main steps used to obtain them in this review, using the IPAD-TOV approach.
In panel (b) of FIG.
123
, we give an example on the similarity between microscopic and macroscopic quantities involving the dimensionless quantities characterizing NS properties.
While the ratio
\phi
of the pressure over energy density of Eq. (
1.3
) is monotonic (near
\widehat{r}\approx 0
), the SSS
s^{2}
of Eq. (
1.1
) may not be monotonic; similarly, although the compactness
\xi
of Eq. (
1.2
) is positive-definite, the slope
\mathrm{d}M_{\mathrm{NS}}/\mathrm{d}R
of the NS M-R curve may either be positive or negative depending on the magnitude of
\Psi
.

FIG. 123:
(Color Online). Upper panel: a schematic summary of the main steps and most important results of the IPAD-TOV approach.
Lower panel: an example of the similarity between microscopic and macroscopic quantities characterizing NS properties.

There are certainly caveats in our analyses. As in solving any differential equations using polynomials, one has to truncate the series of expansions somewhere. In our case studied so far,
the scalings for NS mass, radius and compactness are obtained by truncating the perturbative expansion of
P
and
\varepsilon
to low orders in reduced radius
\widehat{r}
. While the results on the EOS and related quantities are quite consistent with existing constraints from state-of-the-art simulations/inferences, their refinement by including even higher-order
\widehat{r}
terms would be important for studying more accurately the radius profile of
\widehat{P}
,
\widehat{\varepsilon}
,
\Delta
and
s^{2}
.
In the Appendix of Ref.
Cai and Li (2024)
, an effective correction to the mass scaling is investigated, where
\Gamma_{\mathrm{c}}
is replaced by
\Gamma_{\mathrm{c}}(1+18\mathrm{X}/25)
, see also Ref.
Cai and Li (2024)
for some related discussions and implications of this correction term.
Such correction is natural from the viewpoint of the perturbative expansion based on
\mathrm{X}
.
Generally, expanding to even high-order terms inevitably make the analytical analyses more involved, and the determinations of the high-order coefficients will need more observational data beyond just the NS masses and radii. Nevertheless, solving the TOV equations using polynomials has a clear step-by-step procedure as we have demonstrated in this review.
Moreover, more accurate data of different observables beyond the masses and radii are expected as more progress is being made in this era of multi-messenger astronomy.

In addition, to check the accuracy of our expansions and/or determine the minimum truncation order, we used some information about the NS EOS predicted by nuclear theories or constraints from nuclear experiments. Thus, most of the novel scaling properties of NS properties revealed in our analyses are largely instead of absolutely independent of nuclear EOS models.

Given the caveats mentioned above, the quantitatively verified novel scalings of NS properties and the gained new insights into several critical issues are clearly very useful. They enabled us to reveal fundamental physics directly from NS observations with little biases due to the still very uncertain predictions of nuclear many-body theories on supra-dense matter EOS. Since solving differential equations using polynomials is a standard method in mathematics, the fruitful analyses presented in this review justify further applications of this approach in solving more unresolved fundamental issues in NS physics. Looking forward, as examples we list below a few future perspectives in this regard.

1. The (dimensionless) tidal deformability
\Lambda=2k_{2}/3\xi^{5}

Hinderer (2008)
provides very useful constraint on NS EOS
Abbott et al. (2017)
;
Abbott et al. (2018)
.
Here the quadrupolar tidal Love number
k_{2}
is determined by (
\xi=M_{\mathrm{NS}}/R
is the NS compactness)

\displaystyle k_{2}=

\displaystyle\frac{8}{5}\xi^{5}(1-2\xi)^{5}\left[2-y_{R}+2\xi\left(y_{R}-1\right)\right]\times\big\{6\xi\left[2-y_{R}+\xi\left(5y_{R}-8\right)\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt+4\xi^{3}\left[13-11y_{R}+\xi\left(3y_{R}-2\right)+2\xi^{2}\left(1+y_{R}\right)\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt+3\left(1-2\xi\right)^{2}\ln\left(1-2\xi\right)\left[2-y_{R}+2\xi\left(y_{R}-1\right)\right]\big\},

(7.2)

and the function
y(r)
satisfies the differential equation (prime is taken with respect to
r
):

\boxed{\nu y^{\prime}+y^{2}+ye^{\lambda}\left[1+4\pi r^{2}(P-\varepsilon)\right]+r^{2}Q=0,~~Q=4\pi e^{\lambda}\left(5\varepsilon+9P+\frac{\varepsilon+P}{\mathrm{d}P/\mathrm{d}\varepsilon}\right)-\frac{6e^{\lambda}}{r^{2}}-\nu^{\prime 2}.}

(7.3)

In this differential equation,
\nu(r)
and
\lambda(r)
are the time and space components of the spacetime metric; the function

y(r)
satisfies the boundary condition
y(0)=2
and
y_{R}
is its value at the radius
R
.
It would be interesting to investigate if the Love number
k_{2}
scales with some combination of NS internal properties, thus building possibly new routes towards understanding NS EOS, compared to what we have already learned from the mass and radius scalings.

In addition, the frequencies and damping times of various oscillation modes of isolated NSs or the remnant of their mergers are also controlled by the NS EOS in differential equations, see, a recent review in Ref.
Li et al. (2019)
. Some of these observables, e.g., the frequencies of f and g modes will be measured by future GW detectors. Solving the differential equations determining them using polynomials may reveal also largely nuclear EOS-model independent information about the nature and EOS of NSs.

FIG. 124:
(Color Online). Connections among nuclear characteristic quantities
\rho_{0}
,
E_{0}(\rho_{0})
,
K_{0}
,
J_{0}
,
S
,
L
,
K_{\mathrm{sym}}
and
J_{\mathrm{sym}}
, etc., defined in Eqs. (
3.1
) and (
3.2
), and the observational masses and radii of NSs.

2. Since the TOV equations couple only the system pressure
P
and energy density
\varepsilon
, and in order to investigate how certain nuclear properties
Li et al. (2008)
, e.g., the nuclear symmetry energy and the nucleon effective mass
Li et al. (2018)
, or quantities characterizing quark matter
Baym et al. (2018)
, may correlate with the NS observations (e.g., masses, radii, tidal polarizabilities and oscillation frequencies), the energy per nucleon/baryon expressed as a function of baryon density
\rho
and isospin asymmetry
\delta
, namely
E(\rho,\delta)
is necessary to be introduced directly into the dimensionless TOV equations. It is worth studying whether (nearly) EOS-model independent predictions may emerge, e.g., how does
M_{\mathrm{NS}}
or
M_{\mathrm{NS}}^{\max}
depend on the skewness parameter
J_{0}
of symmetric nuclear matter of Eq. (
3.1
)? Can we reveal such dependence similar as (
4.7
) or (
4.6
)?
See FIG.
124
for a sketch on the connections among the relevant quantities. Such investigation may extend the analyses in this review. In particular, we used in the main text the approximation
\varepsilon(\rho)\approx M_{\mathrm{N}}\rho
to estimate the central nucleon density
\rho_{\mathrm{c}}
using the
\widehat{\varepsilon}_{\mathrm{c}}
obtained from the mass and radius scalings, and some improvements along this line are needed.

3. The slow rotation of NSs could be treated as a type of perturbations
Hartle (1967)
;
Hartle and Thorne (1968)
;
Hartle and Thorne (1969)
;
Friedman and Stergioulas (2013)
; and the related stellar equations become the Hartle–Thorne (HT) equations. We can similarly analyze perturbative structures of the HT equations and find the connection between certain characteristic quantities and the NS EOS.
Obviously, the HT equations are much more involved than the TOV equations.
A related quantity is the moment of inertia
I
(defined in Eq. (
4.11
)); the crustal fraction
\Delta I/I
is strongly correlated with the core-crust transition density
\rho_{\mathrm{t}}
and the pressure
P_{\mathrm{t}}
there. They may have important impact on certain NS properties
Lattimer and Prakash (2007)
.
